# Author’s Introduction

This book grew out of something I witnessed while working with young people training for ministry.

During their practical ministry, the students taught through the first eight chapters of Romans. They worked verse by verse, phrase by phrase, examining words within the argument of the passage. They were learning to test familiar explanations against what they were reading, including explanations they had accepted for years.

Some churches welcomed them. In others, trouble began when their conclusions differed from what the congregation had traditionally been taught.

The sharpest disagreements concerned the gospel itself.

As the students explained Christ’s death for our sins and His resurrection, they also examined what people were being told they must do to be saved. Familiar expressions and practices came under scrutiny. Although the students had not deliberately selected examples to target their host churches, some of those examples exposed the very teaching being used there.

The objections included “too much grace” and “not enough works.”

That troubled me deeply. The students were teaching that salvation rests on what Christ has done, received through faith, rather than on what we promise, perform, or contribute. Yet churches that regularly preached about salvation resisted that distinction.

The conflict also became personal. Students were accused of being proud, arrogant, and know-it-alls. Some were marginalized within their own churches and no longer permitted to serve in ministry. Churches aligned with other churches against the training center.

These were young people still learning. Their questions and conclusions deserved careful examination, and they needed help to keep growing. Instead, they were expected to answer at the level of the church leadership, with their opportunities to learn and serve becoming increasingly restricted.

I could not leave those events unexplored.

How could a church preach the gospel and teach from the Bible, yet reject the message those passages proclaimed? How could serious study of Scripture lead to young believers being pushed aside by the people who should have been helping them grow?

Those questions prompted a theological investigation. The novel that follows explores that investigation through the fictional lives of four longtime friends and their relationships with the churches they attend.

# Chapter 1
## The Café

Adrián was already talking before anyone else had even arrived.

Standing at the counter.

One elbow on the wood, coffee in one hand, explaining something to Anna behind the register like they had known each other for years.

She was laughing while trying not to spill the coffee she was pouring.

It was Thursday morning at Deb’s Place, a small diner in Windsor, Ontario.

“I’m serious,” Adrián said. “If you burn this one too I’m switching cafés.”

“You say that every week.”

“And yet here I am.”

She slid the cup across the counter.

Adrián lifted it, inspected it carefully, then nodded.

“Well look at that. You *can* learn.”

She rolled her eyes.

“You’re impossible.”

“That’s what my mother says.”

A man at a nearby table chuckled quietly.

“You talk to everyone like you’ve known them forever?”

Adrián turned toward him.

“No,” he said. “Some people I wait at least five minutes.”

The man laughed.

Adrián finally took a sip.

He closed his eyes for a second.

“Alright,” he said softly. “That’s better.”

Just then the bell above the diner door rang.

Adrián looked up immediately.

“Elías!”

Elías stepped inside, closing the door behind him and pausing a moment while his eyes adjusted to the light in the room. He looked around the diner the way some people do when they like to understand a place before sitting down in it.

When he saw Adrián he shook his head slightly.

“You’ve already made friends.”

“Of course.”

Adrián lifted his cup.

“You want coffee?”

“I’ll get my own.”

“Suit yourself. But this place is dangerous.”

Elías looked at him over his glasses.

“Dangerous?”

“Yes.”

Adrián lowered his voice.

“If you come here too often you might start enjoying life.”

Elías allowed himself a small smile and walked to the counter.

A few minutes later he joined Adrián at the table by the window.

“You’re late,” Adrián said.

“I’m not late.”

“You are if I’m already on my second cup.”

“You’re always on your second cup.”

“That’s because life requires preparation.”

“How are you, my friend? It’s nice to see you,” Adrián said.

“’Bout the same, can’t complain,” Elías replied.

The bell rang again.

A man stepped inside carrying a small travel bag.

He paused just inside the door, scanning the room quickly before spotting them.

Adrián lifted his hand.

“There he is.”

Marcos nodded once and walked over.

“You two started without me?”

“We were discussing important matters,” Adrián said.

“Coffee?”

“No,” Adrián said. “Civilization.”

Marcos set his bag down and tucked his glasses into his shirt pocket.

“I’ll take coffee.”

“Good decision.”

Marcos went to the counter.

Adrián leaned back in his chair.

“You know what’s strange?” he said.

“What?”

“You can tell a lot about a person by how they order coffee.”

Elías looked at him.

“I’m sure you can.”

“If they add too much sugar they’re hiding something.”

“You just made that up.”

“Yes.”

“But it sounded convincing.”

“That’s a gift.”

Marcos returned with his coffee and sat down.

Before anyone said anything else, the bell above the door rang again.

Matías stepped inside.

He spotted the table.

Adrián raised both hands.

“Finally.”

Matías walked over and set his bag down.

“Sorry I got delayed.”

“We’ve been here ten minutes.”

“I’m two minutes late.”

“That was emotional time.”

Matías sat.

Anna brought his coffee. He added a little cream and left the sugar alone. One cup was enough for him.

Marcos and Elías drank theirs black. Adrián reached for the cream again; two empty sugar packets lay beside his cup.

“Traditional breakfast?” Anna asked Matías.

“Yes, please.”

Adrián was still studying the menu.

“Something different today.”

“You said that last week.”

“And I meant it then too.”

Anna turned to Marcos.

“Raspberry or strawberry with your toast?”

“Anything red.”

Now the four of them were there.

They had met for Thursday breakfast at the same table for years, sometimes returning on a Tuesday evening. This was their man-time. They would often just talk about whatever matters of life came to mind. Sometimes, however, they got into some pretty heavy discussions. They loved it!

For a moment nobody spoke.

Not awkward.

Just the quiet that comes when people who know each other well sit down at the same table again.

Adrián finally broke it.

“You know what I noticed on Sunday?”

“That you talk too much?” Marcos said.

“That too.”

Adrián took another sip of coffee.

“We had a great morning at church.”

“Really?” Marcos said.

“Yeah, it was impressive.”

“Impressive how?” Elías asked.

Adrián shrugged.

“I don’t know. Alive, I guess.”

“Alive,” Elías repeated.

“Yes.”

“That’s a technical term.”

“Very technical.”

Matías leaned forward slightly.

“What made it feel that way?”

Adrián thought for a moment.

“The people I guess.”

“In what sense?”

“They were… into it.”

“Into it, that doesn’t explain much.”

“I know.”

Adrián laughed.

“That’s the problem.”

Marcos stirred his coffee slowly.

“So did we.”

“At your church?” Adrián asked.

“Yes.”

“And?”

Marcos shrugged.

“It was good.”

Elías raised an eyebrow.

“Well then...that clears everything up.”

Matías spoke next.

“Ours was good too.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“See? This is what I mean.”

“What?”

“We go to different churches.”

“Yes.”

“And we all keep saying the same thing.”

“What’s that?” Elías asked.

Adrián leaned forward slightly.

“I think it was good.”

Marcos smiled.

“That’s true.”

“But we never really explain why.”

“No,” Elías said.

They sat quietly for a moment while the sounds of the diner moved around them. Cups clinked. Someone laughed near the door.

Adrián looked around the table.

“Let me ask something.”

Nobody answered yet.

“What actually makes a church healthy?”

Elías folded his hands.

“That depends what you mean by healthy.”

“Well, that’s my point. We all say it was good. That isn’t much of a qualifier.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“If we really want to put a standard to it, we should base it on Scripture, not our own opinions.”

“Of course,” Adrián said.

“Because if you asked ten people you’d get ten different answers.”

“That’s true,” Marcos said.

Adrián stared into his coffee for a moment.

Then he looked back up.

“You know something I’ve been wondering?”

“What?” Marcos asked.

Adrián looked at the three of them.

“Lately I've been wondering, what exactly is a *carnal* church?”

Nobody answered.

The word hung there for a moment.

Matías frowned slightly.

Elías looked down at the table.

Marcos tapped his spoon gently against his cup.

Finally Elías spoke.

“You know,” he said slowly,

“Paul uses that word in First Corinthians.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Carnal?”

“Yes.”

“Where?”

Elías nodded.

“Chapter three.”

Adrián turned his cup slowly in his hands.

“That’s interesting, are you certain?”

“Yes,” Elías said. “I recently heard a message on it.”

Matías spoke quietly.

“And as I understand it, Paul wasn’t talking about unbelievers.”

“No, you are correct,” Elías said.

“He was talking about the church.”

Silence settled over the table.

Marcos finally spoke.

“If that’s true…”

He looked around at the others.

“Maybe you’re on to something, Adrián. Maybe we’ve been asking the wrong question.”

Adrián tilted his head.

“What do you mean?”

Marcos shrugged slightly.

“Maybe the question isn’t what a good church looks like.”

He paused.

“Maybe the question is this…”

He looked at the others.

“What does Scripture say a **carnal church** actually is?”

No one answered.

But now they all wanted to know.

# Chapter 2
## Why Corinth?

The coffee had cooled a little.

Adrián turned the cup slowly in his hands while the others sat quietly for a moment.

Finally he looked up.

“So… Corinth.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Marcos leaned back slightly.

“Why Corinth?”

Elías replied.

“Paul calls believers *carnal* there.”

Matías frowned.

“That’s a strong word.”

“Yes.”

“Are you sure he was talking about believers?”

“Yes.”

Adrián sat up a little.

“Well good sir, now I’m curious.”

Marcos looked at Elías.

“Which verses?”

Elías answered.

“The first verses of chapter three.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“If Paul actually called a church carnal…”

He paused.

“That would be serious.”

“Yes,” Elías said quietly.

Marcos stirred his coffee again.

“But before we go chasing a word…”

He looked at the others.

“Maybe we should ask something first.”

“What?”

“What do we mean by carnal?”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Good question.”

“Everyone uses the word.”

“Yes.”

“But what does it actually mean?”

Matías answered first.

“Fleshly.”

Adrián smiled.

“Well that clears everything up.”

Matías ignored him.

“I mean controlled by the flesh.”

Elías nodded slightly.

“That would make sense.”

Marcos looked around the table.

“But how would you know?”

No one answered immediately.

Adrián finally said,

“I’ve heard people say it means worldly.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“That’s common.”

Matías added,

“Living in sin.”

Elías shook his head slightly.

“That seems too simple.”

“Why?”

“Because Paul was writing to churches.”

“So?”

“So if that’s all he meant,” Elías said, “then every church that struggles with sin would be carnal.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“That might actually be true.”

Marcos laughed softly.

“That would make things interesting.”

Matías looked uneasy.

“Then what would calling a church carnal actually tell us?”

“What do you think he meant?” Adrián asked.

Matías took a moment before answering.

“Maybe he meant believers who are not spiritually mature?”

Elías nodded slowly.

“That could be closer.”

Marcos looked back at Elías.

“But you said something else.”

“What?”

“That Paul actually used the word.”

“Yes.”

“Then maybe we should see what he meant instead of guessing.”

Adrián lifted his hands.

“I like that plan.”

“Of course you do.”

“Why wouldn't I?”

“Because it might prove you wrong.”

Adrián grinned.

“That has never stopped me before.”

Matías leaned forward slightly.

“If we’re going to do that…”

He paused.

“Then we should actually read the passage.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes. Our answer needs to come from Scripture.”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Anyone have a Bible?”

Three hands went to their bags almost at the same time.

Marcos laughed.

“Well that was predictable.”

Elías opened his well-underlined Bible and turned to First Corinthians 3.

The others waited.

The diner noise hummed quietly around them.

He turned the Bible so the others could see.

“Starting at verse 1.”

Matías leaned closer.

“What does it say?”

Elías read slowly.

“Brothers, I couldn’t speak to you as to spiritual, but as to fleshly, as to babies in Christ.”

Adrián sat back slightly.

“Wait.”

He pointed.

“Did he say *brothers*?”

“Yes.”

“So believers.”

“Yes.”

“But then he calls them fleshly.”

Matías looked at the page carefully.

“Keep reading.”

Elías continued.

“I fed you with milk, not with meat; for you weren’t yet ready.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“Immaturity.”

“Apparently.”

Elías kept reading.

“Indeed, not even now are you ready, for you are still fleshly.”

Adrián whistled quietly.

“He said it twice.”

“Yes.”

Matías leaned closer.

“What comes next?”

Elías read again.

“For insofar as there is jealousy, strife, and factions among you, aren’t you fleshly, and don’t you walk in the ways of men?”

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Finally Marcos said,

“So he tells us how he knows.”

“How?”

Marcos pointed to the page.

“Jealousy.”

“And strife,” Adrián added.

Matías frowned slightly.

“That seems… relational.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Not the kind of wrongdoing we normally associate with carnality.”

“Not exactly,” Marcos said.

Adrián leaned back in his chair.

“So let me get this straight.”

He pointed toward the Bible.

“Paul is talking to believers.”

“Yes.”

“And he calls them fleshly.”

“Yes.”

“And the evidence is…”

He paused.

“Jealousy and strife.”

Elías nodded.

“That’s what it says.”

Matías sat quietly for a moment.

Finally he said,

“That’s… interesting.”

Adrián laughed.

“That’s one way to say it.”

Marcos tapped the table gently.

“If that’s the case…”

He looked at the others.

“Then carnality might not mean what we think it means.”

Elías looked up from the Bible.

“Yes.”

“That’s what I’m starting to wonder.”

Adrián leaned forward again.

“Well now I have another question.”

“What?” Elías asked.

Adrián looked around the table.

“What exactly is the *flesh*?”

This time no one answered.

And that bothered them more than they expected.

# Chapter 3
## What Is the Flesh?

For a few seconds no one spoke.

The diner hummed quietly around them. Cups clinked somewhere behind the counter. Someone near the door laughed too loudly at something on their phone.

But at the table by the window the mood had shifted.

Adrián finally broke the silence.

“Well that’s awkward. Anybody want another coffee?”

Marcos looked at him.

“What's awkward?”

“We just discovered we’ve all been using a word we apparently don’t understand.”

Matías leaned back slightly.

“I wouldn’t say we don’t understand it.”

Adrián smiled.

“Alrighty then.”

He gestured toward Matías.

“You go first.”

Matías hesitated.

“Define the flesh.”

Matías looked at the table for a moment before answering.

“The flesh is our sinful nature.”

“That’s what most people say,” Marcos said.

“Yes.”

“And?”

Matías shrugged slightly.

“It’s the part of us that wants to disobey God.”

Adrián nodded.

“That’s how I’ve heard it explained too.”

Elías had been quiet.

He spoke slowly.

“But does that explain what we just read?”

Matías looked at him.

“Why not?”

Elías tapped the open Bible gently.

“He points to jealousy and strife.”

Marcos leaned forward slightly.

“He used jealousy.”

“And strife,” Adrián added still perplexed by the term.

“Yes,” Elías said.

“That’s relational conflict.”

Matías folded his arms.

“That’s still sin.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“But it’s not the kind of sin most people think about when they hear the word flesh.”

Adrián laughed quietly.

“That’s true.”

“What do people usually think of?” Marcos asked.

Adrián raised his eyebrows.

“Oh you know.”

“What?”

“The obvious stuff.”

Matías shook his head slightly.

“That’s part of the flesh.”

“But apparently not the only part,” Elías said.

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“So maybe the flesh is bigger than we think.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“That sounds dangerous.”

“Why?”

“Because if the definition gets bigger…”

He leaned back in his chair.

“More people get included.”

Marcos smiled.

“That might be the problem.”

Matías wasn’t smiling.

“The flesh is serious.”

“No one said it wasn’t,” Adrián said.

“But how do you define it?”

Matías thought for a moment.

“Another explanation I’ve heard is human effort.”

The others looked at him confused.

“Explain,” Marcos said.

Matías spoke carefully.

“The flesh is when a person tries to live the Christian life by their own strength instead of depending on God.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“That’s closer.”

Adrián tilted his head.

“But hold on.”

“What?”

“If that’s true…”

He gestured toward the Bible.

“Then those people in Corinth weren’t trying too hard.”

Matías shook his head.

“I didn’t say too hard. I said in their own strength.”

Elías spoke again.

“There might be something else here that we're missing.”

“What?”

“The flesh might not just be immoral behavior.”

Marcos nodded.

“And it might not just be effort either.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Well that narrows it down.”

Marcos ignored him.

“Maybe the flesh is simply human life operating independently from God.”

Elías looked at him.

“That would explain a lot.”

Matías frowned slightly.

“But if that’s the case…”

He paused.

“Then the flesh could appear very different depending on the situation.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“In one place it might look like sin.”

“And in another place,” Marcos said,

“It might look like religion.”

Adrián leaned forward slowly.

“Now that’s interesting.”

The table went quiet again.

After a moment Adrián spoke.

“Let me tell you how I’ve always thought about it.”

The others waited.

“I figured the flesh meant the obvious stuff.”

“What obvious stuff?” Marcos asked.

“You know.”

Adrián waved his hand vaguely.

“Drinking too much. Sleeping around. That kind of thing.”

Matías nodded.

“That’s part of it.”

“Right.”

Adrián shrugged.

“So as long as I wasn’t doing those things…”

He grinned.

“I figured I was doing alright.”

Marcos smiled.

“That’s probably how most people think.”

“Yes,” Elías said quietly.

“But that doesn’t seem to be Paul’s definition.”

Matías leaned forward again.

“Then what is?”

Nobody answered immediately.

Finally Marcos said,

“If we’re serious about this…”

He pointed toward the Bible.

“Then maybe we should find out how Scripture uses the word.”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“I like that.”

Elías agreed.

“Yes. Definitely.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“How would we do that?”

Marcos shrugged.

“We could trace it.”

“Where it appears?” Elías said.

“Yes.”

“And see what Scripture says about it.”

Adrián smiled.

“Well now.”

He lifted his cup again.

“This investigation is getting interesting.”

Matías wasn’t smiling.

He was staring quietly at the open Bible.

Because something had begun to bother him.

If the flesh meant more than obvious sin…

Then avoiding obvious sin might not solve the problem at all.

And that thought was harder to ignore than he expected.

# Chapter 4
## Tracing the Flesh

The diner looked the same as always.

Same tables.

Same smell of coffee.

Same quiet noise of people talking.

But the conversation at the table by the window felt different now.

The question from the previous week had stayed with them.

Adrián arrived first again.

He had already finished half his coffee by the time Elías walked in.

“You started without me.”

“I always start without you.”

Elías sat down.

“Well?”

“Well what?”

“You said you were going to look into it.”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“I did.”

“And?”

Adrián leaned back in his chair.

“I’m confused.”

Elías smiled slightly.

“That didn’t take long. Did you study it thoroughly?”

Adrián pointed at him.

“No, listen.”

He leaned forward again.

“I thought this was going to be simple.”

“What did you expect?”

“I expected to find a few verses about people doing bad things.”

“And?”

“And that’s not what I found.”

Elías waited.

“What did you find?”

Adrián rubbed the back of his neck.

“I have more questions now than when we started.”

Just then Marcos arrived and sat down.

“More questions about what?”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Perfect timing.”

“We’re talking about the flesh.”

Marcos nodded.

“Ah.”

“You looked into it too?”

“A little. I had a hard time sleeping last night.”

“And?”

Marcos shrugged.

“Same problem.”

Adrián pointed at him again.

“See?”

“What problem?”

“The word shows up in places I didn’t expect.”

Matías arrived a few minutes later and joined them.

“You started already.”

“Yes, we did in fact. Nice of you to join us,” Adrián said.

“Good.”

Matías sat down.

“What did you find?”

Adrián laughed quietly.

“That’s the problem.”

“What?”

“I’m not sure what I found.”

Elías leaned forward slightly.

“Start somewhere.”

Adrián nodded.

“Alright.”

He reached into his bag and pulled out a new Bible and a small notebook.

“I started by looking for the word flesh in the letters.”

“Paul’s letters?” Marcos asked.

“Yes.”

“And?”

Adrián flipped a few pages.

“The first thing I noticed is that Paul talks about the flesh a lot.”

“That doesn’t surprise me,” Matías said.

“Wait,” Adrián said.

“It gets stranger.”

“How?”

“In some places it clearly refers to sinful behavior.”

“That’s what we expected,” Marcos said.

“Yes.”

“But in other places…”

He looked up.

“It doesn’t.”

The others waited.

Elías finally said,

“Give an example.”

Adrián flipped another page.

“Galatians.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“That makes sense.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“But listen to this.”

He turned the notebook toward them.

“Paul says something strange.”

“What?”

Adrián read slowly.

“Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?”

Matías frowned immediately.

“That’s Galatians 3.”

“Yes.”

“What’s strange about that?” Marcos asked.

Adrián looked up.

“Think about it.”

“They began by the Spirit.”

“Yes.”

“But now they’re trying to finish by the flesh.”

“Yes.”

Matías spoke quietly.

“That sounds like effort.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“Religious effort.”

The table went quiet.

Marcos tapped the table slowly.

“So the flesh might not just mean immoral behavior.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It might also mean relying on human strength.”

Matías frowned more deeply.

“That’s not necessarily wrong.”

“What?”

“Effort.”

Marcos looked at him.

“But what exactly is Paul criticizing here?”

Matías didn’t answer immediately.

Adrián continued.

“That’s what confused me.”

He flipped another page.

“And then I saw something else.”

“What?”

“The works of the flesh.”

“Galatians 5,” Elías said.

“Yes.”

Adrián nodded.

“And some of them are exactly what we expected.”

“Like what?” Marcos asked.

“Immorality.”

“That fits.”

“Yes.”

“But then there are others.”

“What?”

Adrián looked down at the list.

“Jealousy.”

Marcos nodded.

“That matches Corinth.”

“Yes.”

Adrián continued.

“Strife.”

“Also Corinth.”

“Factions.”

Matías looked uncomfortable.

“That’s interesting.”

Adrián closed the notebook slowly.

“Do you see the problem?”

“What problem?” Marcos asked.

Adrián looked around the table.

“If jealousy and strife are works of the flesh…”

He paused.

“Then the problem in Corinth wasn’t just bad behavior.”

“No,” Elías said quietly.

“It was something deeper.”

Matías stared at the table.

“So the flesh might not always look like obvious wrongdoing.”

“No,” Marcos said.

“And it might not always look religious either.”

Adrián leaned back again.

“That’s exactly why I said I’m confused.”

He looked at the others.

“I thought the flesh meant obvious sin.”

Matías spoke quietly.

“We still haven’t explained the Galatians passage.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“But Scripture seems to be using the word differently.”

The table went silent again.

After a moment Adrián spoke.

“So now I have a question.”

“What?”

He looked around the table.

“If the flesh can look like obvious wrongdoing…”

He paused.

“And it can also look religious…”

He leaned forward slightly.

“How would we recognize it?”

No one answered.

Because suddenly the investigation felt much closer to home than any of them expected.

# Chapter 5
## Religious Flesh

No one spoke for a while.

The noise of the diner carried on around them like nothing unusual had happened. Someone at the next table was explaining a travel story with wide gestures. A spoon clinked against a cup somewhere behind them.

But at the table by the window the four men sat quietly. The sunlight was closer now to their table.

Adrián broke it first.

“Well.”

He looked around the table.

“That was not the direction I expected this to go.”

Marcos nodded.

“Same here.”

Matías leaned forward slightly.

“I’m not convinced yet. I think you're making it more than what it needs to be.”

Adrián smiled.

“I was wondering when that was coming.”

Matías ignored him.

“Just because the word appears in different contexts doesn’t mean we understand it.”

“That’s true,” Elías said.

“We need to be careful.”

Marcos looked at Matías.

“What part bothers you?”

Matías didn’t answer immediately.

Finally he said,

“This idea that the flesh could appear religious.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“You don’t think it can?”

“How can someone who is trying to do the right thing be wrong?”

Elías spoke quietly.

“But that’s exactly what Galatians sounds like.”

Matías looked at him.

“How?”

Elías opened his Bible again and turned a few worn pages.

“Look at the situation.”

The others leaned closer.

“These believers had begun with the Spirit.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“That’s what Paul said.”

“And now,” Elías continued, “they were trying to be perfected by the flesh.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“Yes.”

“But what were they actually doing?”

Adrián shrugged.

“Trying to obey God?”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“They were trying to keep the law. Look at the contrast in verse two.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“So they weren’t simply abandoning religious standards.”

“No.”

“They were becoming more serious.”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned back.

“That’s interesting.”

Matías folded his arms.

“Trying to obey God is not a problem.”

“No,” Elías said.

“But in this case, Paul calls it flesh.”

The table went quiet again.

Adrián finally spoke.

“So let me see if I understand this.”

He looked at the open Bible.

“These believers started by trusting God.”

“Yes.”

“And then later…”

He gestured slightly.

“They started relying on their own effort.”

“That seems to be the problem,” Marcos said.

Matías frowned.

“But effort is part of discipline.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“But Paul isn’t criticizing discipline.”

“What then?”

“What they’re depending on.”

Elías nodded.

“That’s the difference.”

Adrián pointed at the Bible.

“So the problem wasn’t that they wanted to obey God.”

“No.”

“The problem was how they were trying to do it.”

“Exactly,” Elías said.

Matías was quiet.

Finally he spoke.

“That’s a super dangerous idea.”

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

“Why?”

Matías answered slowly.

“Because if you tell people their effort is flesh…”

He paused.

“They might stop trying.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“I’ve heard that argument before.”

Marcos nodded.

“It still needs an answer.”

Elías checked the time; his first patient at the cardiology clinic was due at nine.

Elías spoke carefully.

“But that doesn’t change what the text says.”

Matías didn’t answer.

He was staring at the page.

Adrián leaned forward again.

“Let me ask something.”

“What?” Marcos said.

“If the Galatians were becoming more serious about religion…”

He paused.

“And Paul still calls it flesh…”

He looked around the table.

“Then what would that look like in a church today?”

No one answered right away.

Finally Marcos said,

“It might look very impressive.”

“How?”

“Very disciplined.”

“Very committed,” Elías added.

Matías spoke quietly.

“Very careful.”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“But underneath it…”

He pointed toward the Bible again.

“It would still be the flesh.”

The word hung in the air.

Matías finally looked up.

“That’s difficult to accept.”

“Why?” Adrián asked.

Matías took a long breath before answering.

“Because if that’s true…”

He paused.

“Then a church could look extremely serious about God…”

He looked back down at the page.

“And still be operating in the flesh.”

No one said anything.

Because they were beginning to realize something.

If that was true…

Then recognizing carnality might be much harder than any of them expected.

# Chapter 6
## The Works of the Flesh

The Bible was still open on the table.

No one had moved it. Now the sunlight was right on the Bible.

Adrián leaned forward again.

“So what else did you find?”

Elías turned a few pages slowly.

“Let’s go back to the list in Galatians five.”

Matías leaned closer, following the highlighted passage in his own Bible.

“What does it say?”

Elías began reading.

“Now the deeds of the flesh are obvious…”

Adrián interrupted.

“Wait.”

“What?”

“That sounds helpful.”

Marcos smiled.

“Yes.”

“If the deeds of the flesh are obvious,” Adrián said, “then maybe this will finally clear things up.”

Elías continued reading.

“adultery, sexual immorality, uncleanness, lustfulness…”

Matías nodded.

“That fits. That's what I've always thought.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“That’s what we expected.”

Elías kept reading.

“idolatry, sorcery…”

Marcos shrugged.

“Yes.”

Then Elías continued.

“Hatred…”

He paused.

Marcos looked up.

“Hatred?”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“What does that mean?”

“Hostility,” Elías said.

“Conflict between people.”

Adrián sat back.

“That’s different.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

He continued reading.

“Strife…”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“That word again.”

“Yes.”

“The same one we saw in Corinth.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías shifted slightly in his chair.

“Keep going.”

Elías read again.

“jealousies…”

Adrián pointed at the page.

“That one too.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Interesting.”

Elías continued.

“Outbursts of anger…”

Matías frowned.

“I'm not going to lie. I've seen that in church.”

Adrián laughed.

“Understatement.”

Elías kept reading.

“rivalries, divisions, heresies…”

The table went very quiet.

Marcos finally spoke.

“Divisions?”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“That sounds familiar.”

Elías looked up from the page.

“Yes.”

Matías spoke quietly.

“That’s division.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Groups forming against each other.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Well that’s uncomfortable.”

Marcos looked at the others.

“Look at the pattern.”

“What pattern?”

“These are relational fractures.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“People turning against each other.”

Matías was staring at the list.

“Jealousy.”

“Strife.”

“Factions.”

He shook his head slightly.

“That sounds just like Corinth.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“That’s what Paul said.”

Marcos tapped the table slowly.

“So these aren’t only private sins.”

“No,” Elías said.

“They destroy relationships.”

Adrián leaned forward again.

“So let me see if I understand something.”

“What?” Marcos said.

“If jealousy and strife are works of the flesh…”

He paused.

“And factions are works of the flesh…”

He looked around the table.

“Then a church could be very active…”

“Yes.”

“Very organized…”

“Yes.”

“Very serious…”

“Uh huh.”

“And still be operating in the flesh.”

Matías didn’t answer.

He was still staring at the page. His stiffness was noticeable.

Finally he said quietly,

“That’s disturbing.”

Adrián nodded.

“It is.”

Marcos spoke next.

“But it also explains something.”

“What?”

“Why churches that look strong from the outside sometimes feel unhealthy inside.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“Yes.”

“Because the flesh doesn’t always look like obvious wrongdoing.”

“No,” Marcos said.

“Sometimes it operates in a very structured church.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“That’s even worse.”

Matías finally looked up.

“There’s something else.”

“What?”

Matías pointed at the passage.

“Paul says the deeds of the flesh are *obvious*.”

“Yes.”

“What do you think he means by that?”

Marcos answered first.

“That you can see them.”

“Yes.”

“In relationships.”

Adrián leaned forward again.

“So the flesh reveals itself.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“How?”

“By what it produces.”

The four men sat quietly for a moment.

Then Adrián spoke again.

“So if we followed this idea…”

He paused.

“We might be able to diagnose a church.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“How?”

“By its fruit.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“If jealousy, strife, and factions appear…”

He finished the thought quietly.

“Then the flesh is at work.”

Elías closed the Bible gently.

“That seems to be what Paul is saying.”

Adrián looked around the table again.

“Well.”

He took a sip of his coffee, now cold.

“This investigation just became a lot more serious.”

Marcos nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías said nothing.

Because something about this passage had begun to bother him more deeply than the others realized.

If factions were works of the flesh…

Then some of the things he had always defended might not be as spiritual as he had believed.


# Chapter 7
## By Their Fruit

Adrián reopened his notebook to the list.

Jealousy.

Strife.

Factions.

The words felt heavier the longer they looked at them.

Adrián finally pushed his chair back a little.

“Well.”

He rubbed his face.

“That was cheerful.”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“It was honest.”

Matías was still staring at the passage.

“What bothers me,” he said slowly, “is how ordinary these things are.”

“What do you mean?” Adrián asked.

“They’re not shocking.”

“No,” Elías said.

“They’re common.”

Marcos nodded.

“Very common.”

Adrián leaned forward again.

“Which raises another problem.”

“What problem?” Marcos asked.

“If these things are works of the flesh…”

He tapped the page lightly.

“Then they probably appear in more churches than we’d like to admit.”

Matías frowned slightly.

“That doesn’t mean the whole church is carnal.”

“No,” Elías said.

“But it might reveal something about its condition.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“You know what this reminds me of?”

“What?” Adrián said.

“Something Jesus said.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“I was thinking the same thing.”

Adrián looked between them.

“Which thing?”

Marcos answered.

“You know a tree by its fruit.”

Adrián sat back.

“Oh.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“If the fruit appears…”

He paused.

“It tells you something about the tree.”

Matías leaned back slightly.

“Matthew 7, right?”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“Jesus said good trees produce good fruit.”

“And bad trees produce bad fruit,” Adrián finished.

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

“I heard a guy say that was talking about knowing those who are true believers,” Matías said.

“Jesus is warning about false prophets there,” Elías said. “Paul is addressing believers here. We need to keep those questions distinct.”

Adrián rubbed his chin.

“So if jealousy, strife, and factions are works of the flesh…”

He looked at the others.

“Then they are fruit.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Fruit or works of the flesh.”

Marcos added,

“And fruit reveals the source.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“So if those things appear repeatedly…”

He paused.

“They reveal what is feeding the church.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“That’s uncomfortable.”

“Why?” Marcos asked.

“Because most churches would never say they operate by the flesh.”

“No,” Elías said.

“But the fruit might say otherwise.”

Matías spoke slowly.

“There’s something else about fruit.”

“What?” Adrián asked.

“It doesn’t appear overnight.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It grows over time.”

Marcos nodded.

“Yes.”

“Fruit is the result of something being cultivated.”

The four men sat quietly for a moment.

Adrián finally spoke.

“So let me see if I understand this.”

He pointed toward the Bible.

“If we want to know whether a church is operating by the flesh…”

He paused.

“We could look at the fruit?”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“What kind of relationships exist.”

“Yes.”

“How people treat each other.”

“Yes.”

Marcos nodded.

“And whether jealousy and factions appear.”

Adrián leaned back slowly.

“Well.”

He looked around the table.

“That would make diagnosing a church possible.”

Marcos smiled.

“That’s not a bad comparison.”

Elías nodded.

“Scripture describes the condition.”

“And the fruit reveals it,” Marcos added.

Matías looked down at the table.

“That’s serious.”

Adrián tilted his head.

“Why?”

Matías answered quietly.

“Because if we applied that principle honestly…”

He paused.

“I’m not going to lie… We may discover things we don’t want to see.”

No one answered.

Adrián finally broke the silence.

“Well sir.”

He lifted his cup again.

“If we’re going to do this…”

He looked around the table.

“We might as well do it properly.”

Marcos nodded.

“How?”

Adrián smiled.

“Let’s start paying attention.”

“To what?” Elías asked.

“The fruit.”

The word hung in the air.

Because if fruit reveals the source…

Then the condition of a church might not be hidden nearly as well as people imagine.

# Chapter 8
## Diagnosing a Church

The Bible was still closed.

Not because they were finished with it.

But because none of them were quite sure what to say next. The sunlight had moved to the far corner of the diner.

Adrián finally broke the silence.

“So if fruit reveals the source…”

He looked around the table.

“Then a church should be diagnosable.”

Marcos nodded.

“That seems logical.”

Elías leaned back slightly.

“Like a physician.”

“What do you mean?” Adrián asked.

“A doctor doesn’t guess about a disease.”

“No.”

“He looks at symptoms.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“And the symptoms reveal the condition.”

“Yes.”

Adrián smiled.

“Well then.”

He spread his hands slightly.

“What would the symptoms of a carnal church be?”

Marcos answered first.

“Jealousy.”

“Strife,” Elías added.

“Factions,” Adrián said.

Matías was quiet.

Those words had begun to feel uncomfortably familiar.

Marcos spoke again.

“You know something strange?”

“What?” Adrián said.

“There’s a lot I value about our church.”

“What do you like about it?” Adrián asked.

“It’s large. Active. Lots of ministries.”

“That sounds good,” Adrián said.

“It is.”

He paused.

“But on Sunday I think I saw some of that.”

Adrián looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

Marcos shrugged slightly.

“Nothing dramatic.”

“But?”

“People were taking sides against one another.”

“Taking sides?”

“Yes.”

“Different camps.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“That sounds familiar.”

“Does it?” Adrián said.

“Yes.”

“Where?”

Elías leaned forward slightly.

“In Corinth.”

The table went quiet again.

Adrián smiled.

“You think that church was like Corinth?”

“I’m not saying that,” Elías said.

“I’m saying the fruit sounds similar.”

Matías spoke quietly.

“That’s super dangerous.”

“Why?”

“Because once you start diagnosing churches…”

He paused.

“You might discover things you don’t want to discover.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“I think we already did.”

Marcos leaned forward again.

“Actually…”

He looked around the table.

“This might explain something else.”

“What?” Adrián said.

“All those good things were there.”

“But underneath it…”

He hesitated.

“There was tension.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“Corinth looked impressive too.”

“That’s true,” Adrián said.

“They had gifts.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Many gifts.”

Matías spoke quietly.

“And yet Paul called them carnal.”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned back.

“That’s uncomfortable.”

“Why?” Marcos asked.

“Because it means a church can look impressive…”

He pointed at the table.

“And still be operating by the flesh.”

Matías looked down again.

“That brings us back to what Paul said about Corinth.”

Elías spoke carefully.

“So maybe we should do something.”

“What?”

Marcos answered.

“We should compare the churches we attend with the churches in Scripture.”

Adrián smiled slowly.

“Well sir, now that…”

He lifted his cup again.

“That sounds like an investigation.”

Matías looked uneasy.

“But we need to be careful.”

“Why?” Marcos asked.

“Because diagnosing churches is not a small thing.”

“No,” Elías said.

“But ignoring Scripture would be worse.”

No one disagreed.

Adrián finally spoke.

“Well sir.”

He looked around the table.

“Let’s start with the obvious one.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

Adrián smiled.

“The church that reminded you of Corinth.”

Marcos shook his head.

“Not yet.”

“Why not?”

“Because yours was the first one we talked about.”

Adrián laughed.

“That’s true.”

“And you said it felt…”

Marcos paused.

“Alive.”

Adrián nodded.

“Yes.”

“Well then,” Marcos said.

“Maybe we should start there.”

Adrián leaned back slowly.

“You want to hear about my church?”

“Yes.”

All three of them nodded.

Adrián took a slow sip of his coffee.

“Alright.”

He smiled slightly.

“Let me tell you what it’s like.”

# Chapter 9
## The Lively Church

Adrián leaned back in his chair and folded his arms.

“You’re going to regret asking me to describe it.”

Marcos smiled.

“Why?”

“Because I could talk about it all morning.”

“That’s allowed.”

Adrián shook his head.

“You say that now.”

Elías smiled slightly.

“Just tell us what you saw.”

Adrián nodded.

“Alright.”

He leaned forward.

“On Sunday I got there about ten minutes before the service started.”

“Large building?” Marcos asked.

“Yes. Very. Well lit, modern.”

“Full?”

“Almost.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“Actually it filled up fast.”

He continued.

“People were greeting each other everywhere. Hugging. Laughing. Kids running around.”

“That sounds healthy,” Marcos said.

“It did.”

Matías nodded slightly.

“And the service?”

Adrián smiled.

“The sound is excellent. Curved stacks of speakers hang on either side of the stage. You can hear every word, even at the back.”

“And the band?” Marcos asked.

“They’re excellent too. Our lead singer’s a recording artist.”

He continued describing it.

“People were singing with their hands raised. Some had their eyes closed. Others were clapping.”

He shrugged.

“You could tell they meant it.”

Elías listened quietly.

“And the atmosphere?”

Adrián thought for a moment.

“Warm.”

“Very warm.”

“People were excited to be there, and so was I.”

“That’s good,” Marcos said.

“Yes.”

Adrián nodded.

“Then something interesting happened.”

“What?”

“People began sharing things.”

“What kind of things?” Elías asked.

“Verses.”

“Encouragement.”

“Prayers.”

“Someone even stood up and said they felt God had given them something to share.”

Matías raised an eyebrow.

“And how did the church respond to that?”

“They listened.”

“No chaos?”

“No.”

“It felt… free.”

Adrián paused for a moment.

“There was also food afterward.”

Marcos smiled.

“That always helps.”

Adrián laughed.

“Yes.”

“Tables everywhere.”

“People talking.”

“Kids playing.”

“It felt like a family gathering.”

Elías asked quietly,

“And the teaching?”

Adrián nodded.

“We sang for most of the service. I’d happily have kept going. Then there was a short message.”

“Short?”

“Yes.”

“And it was good.”

“How?”

“Encouraging.”

“In what sense?”

Adrián shrugged.

“The speaker talked about trusting God.”

“Living by faith.”

“Depending on the Spirit.”

“That sounds biblical,” Elías said.

“Yes.”

“That’s why I liked it.”

Adrián leaned back again.

“I walked out of there thinking…”

He paused.

“That felt good.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“And you’re glad it’s your church?”

“Absolutely.”

Elías spoke quietly.

“What made it feel alive to you?”

Adrián didn’t hesitate.

“The people.”

“They weren’t stiff.”

“They weren’t afraid.”

“They were engaged.”

“It felt real.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“That’s not a bad thing.”

“No,” Adrián said.

“It really wasn’t.”

For a moment the others sat quietly.

Marcos finally spoke.

“You know something?”

“What?”

“That description reminds me of something.”

Adrián smiled.

“What?”

Marcos looked at Elías.

Elías nodded slowly.

“Yes.”

Adrián looked between them.

“What are you two thinking?”

Elías reached for the Bible again.

“There was another church that sounded like that.”

“Which one?”

Elías turned a few pages.

“Corinth.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“Really? Why?”

“Well.”

Marcos nodded.

“They were lively too.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Hold on.”

“What?”

“You think I’d have liked Corinth too?”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Let’s just read it.”

Adrián shook his head with a grin.

“Now I want to hear this.”

Matías leaned forward again.

“What does Paul say about Corinth?”

Elías opened the Bible.

“Quite a lot.”

Adrián lifted his cup again.

“Well sir.”

He smiled.

“Let’s hear it.”

# Chapter 10
## The Corinth Question

Elías rested his hand on the open Bible.

Adrián watched him with a curious smile.

“You look too calm,” Adrián said.

“Why?”

“That’s the face you make when you’ve found something interesting.”

Marcos laughed quietly.

“He usually has.”

Matías leaned forward.

“Let’s just read it.”

Elías nodded.

“Alright.”

He turned a few pages carefully.

“First Corinthians.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“So what exactly was going on in Corinth?”

Elías began reading slowly.

“Now concerning spiritual gifts, brethren, I do not want you to be unaware…”

Adrián sat up.

“Wait.”

“What?”

“Spiritual gifts?”

“Yes.”

Adrián smiled.

“Well that already sounds familiar.”

Marcos nodded.

“Yes.”

Elías continued reading portions of the letter, moving through several passages.

Paul describing the gifts.

Prophecy.

Tongues.

Knowledge.

Healing.

Adrián shook his head slightly.

“They had everything.”

“That’s what it sounds like,” Marcos said.

Elías turned another page.

“And listen to this.”

He read again.

“You are not lacking in any gift…”

Adrián leaned back slowly.

“That reminds me of our church.”

Matías nodded thoughtfully.

“A gifted church.”

“Yes.”

Elías looked up.

“But Paul still corrects them.”

“For what?” Adrián asked.

Elías turned to chapter three.

“This.”

He pointed to the passage they had read earlier.

“For insofar as there is jealousy, strife, and factions among you, aren’t you fleshly, and don’t you walk in the ways of men?”

No one at their table spoke.

Adrián finally said,

“That’s the same passage we looked at.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“But now we’re seeing the context.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“They had gifts.”

“Yes.”

“They had activity.”

“Uh huh.”

“They had people speaking.”

“Yes.”

“And yet Paul still calls them carnal.”

“Yes.”

Adrián rubbed his chin.

“That’s strange.”

“Why?” Marcos asked.

“Because when I think of a carnal church…”

He paused.

“I’ve usually imagined the opposite.”

“What do you mean?” Matías said.

“A cold church.”

“A lifeless church.”

Marcos nodded.

“Yes.”

“But Corinth wasn’t cold.”

“No,” Elías said.

“They were energetic.”

“Very.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“So a church can look very alive…”

He gestured toward the Bible.

“And still be carnal?”

Matías spoke quietly.

“That seems to be exactly what Paul is saying.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Marcos leaned back.

“That’s important.”

“Why?” Adrián asked.

“Because activity alone doesn’t establish spiritual maturity.”

Adrián smiled slightly.

“That might disappoint a lot of people.”

Matías looked serious.

“It should make us careful.”

Elías turned another page.

“There’s something else here.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

Elías read again.

“Each one of you is saying, ‘I am of Paul,’ and ‘I of Apollos,’ and ‘I of Cephas.’”

Adrián looked confused.

“What does that mean?”

Marcos answered.

“Groups.”

“Different camps forming.”

Matías nodded.

“Factions?”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“The very thing Galatians called a work of the flesh.”

Adrián leaned back again.

“So the fruit matches.”

“Yes.”

“The same fruit.”

Matías was quiet again.

Finally he said,

“That means something.”

“What?” Adrián asked.

Matías spoke slowly.

“It means carnality isn’t measured by how active a church is.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Or how gifted it is,” Marcos added.

Adrián finished the thought.

“It’s measured by the fruit.”

The table fell quiet again.

After a moment Adrián spoke softly.

“That’s uncomfortable.”

“Why?” Marcos asked.

“Because my church felt great on Sunday.”

He shrugged.

“And those things are good.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“But feeling alive isn’t the same as being spiritual.”

Adrián laughed quietly.

“That’s a line right there.”

Matías leaned forward again.

“So if Corinth looked lively…”

“And still had jealousy and factions…”

He paused.

“Then recognizing carnality might be harder than we thought.”

No one disagreed.

Adrián finally lifted his cup again.

“Well sir.”

He smiled faintly.

“This investigation is making me question what I’ve been calling spiritual.”

Marcos laughed.

“That might be the point.”

Elías looked down at the open Bible.

“Yes.”

Because if Corinth could look alive and still be carnal…

Then perhaps the real question was not which churches were lively.

But which churches were truly spiritual.

And that question had only just begun to unfold.

# Chapter 11
## The Divided Church

Elías had not moved the Bible.

It was still open in front of him.

Adrián leaned forward, his elbows resting on the table.

"Keep going."

Elías looked up.

"Keep going with what?"

"Corinth."

"You want more?"

"Of course I want more. If we're going to compare that church to the ones we attend, we might as well see the whole picture."

Marcos nodded.

"That's fair."

Elías turned back a page.

"Then let's start near the beginning of the letter."

Adrián took another sip of coffee.

"You're enjoying this a little too much."

"Reading Scripture?"

"Making me rethink my favorite church."

Elías smiled faintly.

"I haven’t said anything about your church."

"Not yet."

Matías chuckled.

Elías found the passage.

"First Corinthians, chapter one."

He read aloud.

*"Now I exhort you, brethren, by the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that you all agree, and that there be no divisions among you..."*

Matías nodded.

"That's important."

Elías continued.

*"But that you be made complete in the same mind and in the same judgment."*

Marcos leaned forward.

"Why would Paul say that unless there were divisions?"

"Exactly," Elías replied.

He read the next verse.

*"For I have been informed concerning you, my brethren, by Chloe's people, that there are quarrels among you."*

Adrián raised his eyebrows.

"Well, somebody told on them."

Marcos laughed.

"Apparently."

"Imagine being Chloe's people," Adrián said. "You travel all that way, and now your name is in the Bible for reporting church arguments."

"At least they reported something that needed attention," Matías said.

"True. Still, I wouldn't want to be sitting beside them at the next church meeting."

**CLINK!**

A spoon struck the edge of a cup at the counter.

Adrián glanced over.

"See? Even the dishes are arguing."

Anna shook her head.

"Some of us are trying to work."

"And doing a wonderful job," Adrián replied.

She smiled and returned to the espresso machine.

**PSSSSHHH.**

A burst of steam briefly drowned out the conversation.

Elías waited for the noise to settle.

"Now listen to what these quarrels were about."

He read again.

*"Now I mean this, that each one of you is saying, 'I am of Paul,' and 'I of Apollos,' and 'I of Cephas,' and 'I of Christ.'"*

Marcos nodded slowly.

"Different groups."

"Different loyalties," Matías added.

"Different favorite teachers," Elías said.

Adrián tilted his head.

"Is having a favorite teacher necessarily wrong?"

"No," Elías replied. "Paul's concern was what they were doing with those preferences."

"Which was?"

"Dividing themselves."

Marcos leaned back.

"People were organizing themselves around personalities."

"That sounds familiar," Adrián said.

"Very familiar," Marcos replied.

Adrián laughed.

"Come on. Everybody has a favorite preacher."

"Having someone you enjoy listening to isn't the issue," Matías said. "It's when you begin treating everyone else as inferior."

Elías nodded.

"That's closer to what Paul is confronting."

Adrián thought about that.

"I suppose I've done that."

The others looked at him.

"What?" Marcos asked.

"Well, I've told people they should hear certain preachers. Sometimes I can't understand why they don't enjoy them as much as I do."

"That's hardly a crime," Marcos said.

"No, although I have been known to question their spiritual condition."

Matías laughed.

"You've questioned mine often enough."

"Only because you don't appreciate good worship music."

"There it is," Matías replied.

Adrián grinned.

Elías returned to the passage.

"There's something else here. Paul doesn't merely say that divisions are unfortunate."

He turned to chapter three.

"We read this earlier. Now let's hear it in light of what we've discovered."

He read slowly.

*"For insofar as there is jealousy, strife, and factions among you, aren’t you fleshly, and don’t you walk in the ways of men?"*

The table grew quiet.

Adrián looked at the page.

"That's the word."

"Yes," Elías replied.

"Carnal."

"Fleshly," Matías added.

Adrián shook his head.

"And he's talking to believers."

"Yes."

"People with spiritual gifts."

"Yes."

"People who knew Scripture."

"Certainly."

"People who were active in the church."

Marcos nodded.

"And yet Paul says they're fleshly."

Adrián stared at his coffee.

"That's what bothers me."

"Why?" Matías asked.

"Because on Sunday at my church, I saw people excited about God. People worshiping, praying, encouraging one another. I enjoyed every minute of it."

"Those are good things," Elías said.

"I know. That's what makes this difficult."

Adrián leaned back.

"I've always believed Christianity is about relationship, not religion. I can't stand churches where everyone sits around looking miserable, as though enjoying yourself is somehow unspiritual."

Marcos smiled.

"We know."

"And when people at my church are excited, when the music is alive, when you can actually feel something happening, I think, now that’s what church should be!"

Matías looked at him.

"But how do you know what you're feeling is the Holy Spirit?"

Adrián opened his mouth.

Then closed it.

"Well..."

He laughed.

"That's an annoyingly good question."

Matías smiled.

"I have those occasionally."

Adrián looked back at the Bible.

"I suppose that's what we're discovering. Feeling spiritual and being spiritual aren't necessarily the same thing."

Elías nodded.

"Paul certainly doesn't use their gifts as proof of their maturity."

Marcos added, "He points to their relationships."

"Jealousy," Matías said.

"Strife," Elías added.

"Divisions," Marcos finished.

Adrián rubbed his forehead.

"And there I was thinking they had everything."

He paused.

"Maybe they had everything except the maturity to handle it."

No one answered immediately.

The diner door opened.

**JINGLE-JINGLE!**

A couple entered, laughing as they shook the cold from their coats.

Anna greeted them, and for a moment the ordinary sounds of the diner filled the space around the four friends.

Adrián watched them settle into a booth.

Then he turned back.

"You know something? This really is embarrassing."

"What is?" Marcos asked.

"Corinth. All those gifts, all that knowledge, all those people claiming to be spiritual, and they're fighting over which teacher is better."

Matías nodded.

"That's immaturity."

"Exactly."

Adrián gestured toward the Bible.

"They needed to grow up."

"Paul certainly says they were infants in Christ," Elías replied.

"Right. And infants need to mature."

Matías reached for his coffee.

"There's something to be said for order."

Adrián turned toward him.

"Absolutely."

Matías looked surprised.

"Did you just agree with me?"

"Don't get used to it."

Marcos glanced between them.

"Well, this is unusual."

"What?" Adrián asked.

"You two agreeing on something."

"Mark the date," Elías said without looking up.

Adrián burst out laughing.

"Somebody write it down!"

Elías smiled and glanced at his watch.

"Oh! I need to get going."

"Already?" Marcos asked.

"I promised I’d drop my wife off before I head to the clinic. She’s expecting me in about fifteen minutes."

Adrián grinned.

"Well, we wouldn't want you dividing your household."

"Very thoughtful of you."

Elías closed his Bible and slipped it into his bag.

Matías looked up.

"Will you make it Tuesday evening?"

"That should work. Send me a message when you decide."

He stood, pulled on his jacket, and picked up his keys.

"And try not to solve every problem in the church before I get back."

"No promises," Adrián replied.

Elías shook his head, smiling.

"See you, gentlemen."

"Drive safely," Marcos said.

"And don't forget your wife!" Adrián called after him.

The bell above the diner door rang sharply as Elías stepped outside.

**JINGLE!**

Adrián watched him cross toward his car.

"Good man, Elías."

"Yes," Matías said.

"A little too serious sometimes."

Matías raised an eyebrow.

"Coming from you, that means very little."

Marcos laughed.

Adrián turned back toward the table.

"Anyway, where were we?"

"Corinth," Marcos replied.

"Right."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Those people needed to grow up."

Matías nodded.

"They needed discipline."

"Exactly! That's what I've been saying."

Marcos looked amused.

"Actually, Matías just said it."

"Well, I've been thinking it."

"Of course you have."

Adrián ignored him.

"Seriously. You can't have everyone doing whatever they please. Sooner or later somebody has to establish some standards."

Matías nodded approvingly.

"Accountability matters."

"Absolutely."

"And commitment."

"Of course."

"And discipline."

"Definitely."

Marcos looked between them again.

Adrián laughed.

"Maybe we've finally discovered the secret to church unity."

Matías smiled.

"That would be a first."

Adrián became thoughtful.

"No, really. Think about it. Corinth had spiritual gifts. They had knowledge. They had enthusiasm. What they lacked was the discipline to live like mature believers."

Matías nodded.

"Spiritual maturity requires commitment."

"Right. Higher expectations. Some accountability. People taking their Christian lives seriously."

Marcos stirred his coffee.

"Well, a healthy church certainly needs some structure."

"Exactly!" Adrián said.

He looked pleased with himself.

"Raise the bar. That's what Corinth needed."

Matías smiled.

For once, the two men seemed entirely in agreement.

Adrián leaned back.

"And honestly, I think some churches understand that better than others."

Marcos looked at him.

"Such as?"

Adrián turned toward Matías.

"Your church."

Matías looked up.

"What about it?"

"You said they were disciplined."

"Very."

"Serious about holiness?"

"Absolutely."

"Committed to obedience?"

"Without question."

"And they weren't just running on feelings?"

Matías gave him a look.

"Definitely not."

Adrián smiled broadly.

"Well, there you have it!"

"Have what?" Marcos asked.

"The answer! Corinth needed exactly that."

Matías sat a little straighter.

Adrián continued.

"I mean, think about it. Here's a church that takes holiness seriously. People know what's expected of them. They're committed. They're disciplined. They don't just show up on Sunday, get excited, and forget everything by Monday."

"That's true," Matías said.

"See? That's what I'm talking about."

Marcos considered the idea.

"It certainly sounds like a healthier approach."

Matías nodded.

"People need to understand that following Christ involves responsibility."

"Of course," Adrián said.

"And obedience."

"Absolutely."

"And a willingness to deny themselves."

Adrián lifted his coffee cup.

"Now that's what I call raising the bar."

He took a sip.

**SLURRP.**

Matías winced.

"Must you do that?"

"Do what?"

"Drink your coffee like that."

Adrián lowered the cup.

"Sorry. I'll try to be more disciplined."

Marcos burst out laughing.

Even Matías couldn't help smiling.

Adrián set the cup down.

"Seriously, Matías. Tell us more about that church."

Matías looked from Adrián to Marcos.

"Well, it’s certainly different from Adrián’s church."

"Good," Adrián said. "We could use something different."

"They were very committed to doing what was right."

"Now we're talking."

"And they placed a strong emphasis on holiness."

"Excellent."

Matías settled back in his chair.

For the first time that morning, he seemed completely comfortable with the direction of the conversation.

He had spent years believing that the Christian life required discipline, obedience, and serious commitment.

And now, after everything they had discovered about Corinth, his convictions seemed more reasonable than ever.

Adrián leaned forward eagerly.

"Go on. I want to hear about this church."

Matías smiled.

"Gladly."

And he began to tell them about a church that took obedience very seriously.

# Chapter 12
## The Serious Church

Matías settled into his chair.

"Well, the first thing you need to understand is that people at our church take their Christian lives seriously."

Adrián nodded enthusiastically.

"Good. That's already an improvement."

"Let him finish," Marcos said.

"I'm encouraging him."

"You're interrupting him."

"Same thing."

Matías smiled.

"No, he’s right about one thing. Our church is different."

"Different how?" Marcos asked.

"Well, for one thing, people were prepared."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"Prepared how?"

Matías leaned forward.

"They arrived with their Bibles. They had read the passages beforehand. When the pastor referred to something from the previous week, people knew what he was talking about."

Marcos nodded approvingly.

"That's impressive."

"It was."

"And the teaching?" Adrián asked.

"Excellent. Very practical. The pastor didn't spend the entire morning trying to make everyone feel good. He challenged them."

"Challenged them to what?"

"To live differently."

Matías opened his hands.

"Look, if we're Christians, shouldn't our lives reflect that?"

"Of course," Adrián replied.

"Then why are we so surprised when a church actually expects it?"

Marcos smiled.

"That's a fair question."

Matías continued.

"The pastor talked about integrity. Honesty. Faithfulness in marriage. Raising children. Being responsible with money. Controlling your temper. Keeping your word."

Adrián nodded slowly.

"All good things."

"Exactly."

"And people listened?"

"They did more than listen. They were expected to put it into practice."

"How?"

"Accountability."

Marcos leaned forward.

"Now that's interesting."

Matías looked at him.

"They had small groups where people encouraged one another, discussed the teaching, and talked about how they were applying it."

Marcos smiled.

"We do something similar."

"I thought you might."

"Actually, we're developing a whole leadership structure around our small groups."

Adrián looked at him.

"Of course you are."

"What does that mean?"

"Nothing. I just knew you'd find a way to organize the conversation."

Marcos laughed.

"Someone has to."

Matías continued.

"What impressed me was their commitment. They weren't content to attend a service and go home unchanged."

Adrián sat back.

"Now that I can appreciate."

Matías looked surprised.

"Really?"

"Of course. I'm all about a real relationship with God. I don't want people just going through religious motions."

"Neither do I," Matías replied.

Adrián grinned.

"Well, look at us. We agree again."

"Don't make a habit of it."

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

"Refills?"

"Please," Marcos said.

She filled his cup.

Matías placed his hand over his.

"No, thank you."

Adrián held his cup out.

"Absolutely."

The coffee splashed against the bottom of his nearly empty mug.

**GLUG-GLUG-GLUG.**

"Easy," Adrián said. "I was going to stop after the last one."

Anna looked at him.

"Then why are you having another?"

Adrián considered the question.

"Because I lack discipline."

Matías shook his head.

Anna laughed and moved to the next table.

Adrián turned back toward his friend.

"Alright. Tell me something. What was the atmosphere like?"

"Respectful."

"Quiet?"

"At times."

"Music?"

"Yes, they sang."

"Good music?"

Matías thought about it.

"That depends on what you mean by good."

Adrián groaned.

"Oh, here we go."

"The congregation sang together. The words were meaningful. People paid attention to what they were singing."

"So no smoke machines?"

"No."

"Colored lights?"

"No."

"Electric guitars?"

"Adrián."

"What? I'm asking important questions."

Marcos chuckled.

Matías smiled.

"The music wasn't the center of the gathering."

Adrián looked thoughtful.

"I suppose that's not necessarily a bad thing."

"No, it isn't."

Matías took a sip of water.

"What struck me was how seriously they treated Scripture."

"That's important," Marcos said.

"Very important. The pastor expected people to read their Bibles during the week."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"Expected?"

"Yes."

"What if they didn't?"

"Well, the idea was that Christians should be growing in their understanding of God's Word."

"That's reasonable," Marcos replied.

Matías nodded.

"And they had a reading plan."

Adrián smiled.

"Ah. A plan."

"Yes."

"With assignments?"

"Of course."

"Homework?"

"If you want to call it that."

Adrián looked at Marcos.

"I thought I left school years ago."

Marcos laughed.

Matías wasn't offended.

"Tell me something, Adrián. How much time do you spend listening to Christian music?"

"A lot."

"Every day?"

"Pretty much."

"In your car?"

"Absolutely. You should hear my stereo."

"We have," Marcos said.

"From three streets away," Matías added.

Adrián laughed.

"Quality equipment."

Matías smiled.

"And why do you spend so much time listening?"

"Because I enjoy it. It encourages me. It keeps my mind on God."

"Exactly."

Matías leaned forward.

"Now imagine someone who feels the same way about reading Scripture."

Adrián paused.

"Alright. That's a fair point."

"Nobody thinks it's strange when someone spends hours enjoying music. Why should we think it's strange when someone spends an hour each morning studying the Word of God?"

Marcos nodded.

"That's a good comparison."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"An hour every morning?"

"Yes."

"Every morning?"

"Every morning."

"Before breakfast?"

"Before breakfast."

Adrián stared at him.

"You're telling me you don't eat until you've finished reading?"

"That's my routine."

"What if you're hungry?"

"I usually am."

Marcos laughed.

Adrián shook his head.

"You're a remarkable man."

Matías smiled.

"Hardly. It's just discipline."

"And you actually enjoy doing that?"

"Very much."

Matías paused.

"Not every morning is exciting, of course. Some mornings I'd rather stay in bed. Sometimes my mind wanders. Sometimes I have to read a passage twice."

"That sounds more like me," Adrián said.

"But I've learned that consistency matters. I don't want my relationship with God to depend on whether I wake up feeling inspired."

Adrián considered that.

"Well, I can't argue with that."

Matías continued.

"I also keep a prayer list—people, situations, specific needs. I write them down so I don't forget."

Marcos nodded.

"That's actually a good idea."

"And when I finish praying, I check them off."

Adrián smiled.

"You check off your prayers?"

"Yes."

"With a pen?"

"Usually."

"Do you have a spreadsheet?"

Marcos looked up immediately.

"I could make him one."

Adrián pointed at him.

"See? This is why we can't take you anywhere."

All three laughed.

Matías shook his head.

"You fellows can laugh all you want. At least I know I've prayed for the people I promised to pray for."

That quieted Adrián.

He looked at his friend for a moment.

"Actually, that's a good point."

Matías nodded.

"I've told people I would pray for them and then forgotten. I didn't like that. So I started writing things down."

"That's thoughtful," Marcos said.

"It helps me keep my word."

Adrián nodded slowly.

"I can respect that."

For a moment the conversation drifted toward people they knew, families who needed encouragement, and friends who had been going through difficult circumstances.

Matías mentioned a man he had been praying for over several months.

Adrián knew him too.

"How's he doing?"

"Better. He called me last week."

"Really?"

"Yes. We talked for almost an hour."

Adrián smiled.

"That's good to hear."

"It is."

Matías looked down at his cup.

"That's why these things matter to me. People matter. Their lives matter. I don't want to promise someone I'll help and then forget about them."

Marcos nodded.

"Consistency is a form of faithfulness."

"Exactly."

Adrián looked at Matías.

"You know, I tease you about your routines."

"I hadn't noticed."

"But I appreciate that about you. When you say you're going to do something, you usually do it."

Matías smiled.

"Thank you."

"Usually."

"There it is."

They laughed again.

Outside, a motorcycle accelerated past the diner.

**VRRROOOOM!**

Adrián turned toward the window.

"Now that's a beautiful sound."

Matías closed his eyes.

"Please don't."

"What?"

"Tell us about the engine."

"I wasn't going to."

Marcos raised an eyebrow.

"You were."

"Well, since you brought it up—"

"No," both men said together.

Adrián laughed.

"Fine. Back to holiness."

Matías took another sip of water.

"There's something else I admired about the church."

"What's that?" Marcos asked.

"They weren't afraid to talk about sin."

Adrián nodded.

"Good."

"They didn't pretend that everything was fine simply because people attended church."

"That's important," Marcos said.

"Yes. They believed that following Christ should affect how people live."

Matías looked toward Adrián.

"You said earlier that Corinth needed to grow up."

"I did."

"Well, how does a person grow if nobody expects anything from them?"

Adrián thought about that.

"That's a reasonable question."

"How does someone learn responsibility if they're never given responsibility? How do people develop faithfulness if no one encourages them to be faithful?"

Marcos nodded.

"That applies to almost everything in life."

"Exactly," Matías said.

"Families. Work. Relationships. Even raising children."

Adrián laughed.

"Especially raising children."

Matías smiled.

"Imagine telling your children that because you love them, nothing is expected of them."

"That would be a disaster," Marcos replied.

"And yet," Matías continued, "sometimes we act as though expecting something from Christians is somehow unloving."

Adrián leaned back.

"I hadn't thought about it quite that way."

Matías continued.

"Now, I'm not saying we earn God's love. We don't. Salvation is by grace. I believe that completely."

"Of course," Marcos said.

"I'm talking about what happens afterward. How we live. Whether we take our calling seriously."

Adrián nodded.

"And that’s what you see in your church?"

"Yes. People who believed their faith should make a difference."

Marcos looked thoughtful.

"You know, when you put it that way, it sounds like a church with a clear sense of direction."

"Very much so."

"And people understood what was expected of them?"

"They did."

"That alone can prevent a lot of confusion," Marcos said.

Matías nodded.

"Precisely."

Adrián looked between them.

"Well, I think we've found something here."

"What?" Marcos asked.

"A church that actually expects Christians to behave like Christians."

Matías smiled.

"That's a simple way of putting it."

"Sometimes simple is good."

Adrián glanced at the empty chair beside them.

"Elías would probably have a twelve-point explanation."

Marcos chuckled.

"With references."

"And footnotes," Adrián added.

Matías laughed.

"He'd probably want us to define what we mean by Christian behavior."

"Exactly!"

Adrián leaned forward, imitating Elías's careful manner.

"'Before proceeding, gentlemen, we must establish the precise theological meaning of discipline.'"

Marcos laughed so hard he nearly spilled his coffee.

Matías shook his head.

"You're terrible."

"I know. That's why I need a church with higher standards."

They laughed again.

Then Marcos became thoughtful.

"There's something I appreciate about what you're describing."

Matías looked at him.

"What's that?"

"It sounds sustainable."

"How do you mean?"

"Well, enthusiasm can come and go. People have good days and bad days. Some Sundays are exciting. Others aren't."

Adrián nodded reluctantly.

"That's true."

"But a church with clear expectations and committed people can keep moving forward."

Matías smiled.

"That's exactly what I appreciated."

Marcos continued.

"People know their responsibilities. They develop good habits. They encourage one another. There's a sense of purpose."

"Yes."

"And it isn't dependent on everyone feeling excited all the time."

Matías nodded.

"Exactly."

Adrián looked at his coffee.

"Well, when you say it like that, my church sounds a little exhausting."

Matías smiled.

"I didn't say that."

"No, I know."

Adrián paused.

"Listen, I still love worship music. I still think there's something wonderful about people being excited about Jesus."

"And there is," Matías said.

"I'm not giving that up."

"Nobody asked you to."

"Good. Because I have concert tickets next month."

Marcos laughed.

"Of course you do."

Adrián grinned.

"Front row, if all goes well."

Matías shook his head, smiling.

Adrián continued.

"Still, I can see what you're saying. Excitement by itself isn't enough. People need to learn how to live."

Matías nodded.

"That's all I'm saying."

Marcos leaned forward.

"And your church seems to understand that."

"Very well," Matías replied.

Adrián looked around the table.

"Well, gentlemen, I think we may have found the kind of church Corinth needed to become."

Matías looked pleased.

"Perhaps."

"Think about it. Corinth had gifts, enthusiasm, and knowledge. What they needed was maturity."

"Yes."

"And maturity doesn't just happen because everyone gets excited on Sunday."

"Exactly."

"It takes commitment."

"Yes."

"Obedience."

"Yes."

"Discipline."

Matías smiled.

"Now you're beginning to sound like me."

Adrián laughed.

"Don't tell anybody."

Marcos checked his watch; one of his company’s Learjet crews was due to call.

"We should probably get going soon."

Adrián looked surprised.

"Already?"

"We've been here almost two hours."

"Really?"

Matías glanced around the diner.

The breakfast crowd had thinned.

"He's right."

Adrián sighed.

"Well, that's unfortunate. I was just beginning to become disciplined."

Marcos stood and reached for his jacket.

"There's always tomorrow."

Matías gathered his things.

Adrián remained seated for a moment.

"Actually, Matías, I’d like to hear more about your church."

Matías looked at him.

"Really?"

"Yes. Especially how they teach people to live that way."

"There's quite a bit I could tell you."

"Good. Let's continue next time."

Marcos nodded.

"That works for me."

Adrián stood, picked up his phone, and checked the screen.

A notification appeared.

**DING!**

He smiled.

"What now?" Matías asked.

"Another concert announcement."

Matías groaned.

Adrián slipped the phone into his pocket.

"Relax. I'm not buying tickets."

Marcos raised an eyebrow.

"Yet."

"Yet," Adrián admitted.

They laughed as they made their way toward the door.

Matías walked beside Adrián.

"You know, there's something I've been meaning to ask you."

"What's that?"

"Have you ever considered setting aside a regular time each morning to read Scripture?"

Adrián looked at him.

"Before breakfast?"

"Yes."

Adrián thought for a moment.

"How early are we talking?"

Matías smiled.

"We'll discuss that."

The bell above the diner door rang as they stepped outside.

**JINGLE-JINGLE!**

The three men went their separate ways.

Matías was delighted.

For years he had believed that Christians needed to take obedience more seriously.

Now his friends were beginning to see it too.

He had enjoyed the conversation more than he expected.

And as he walked toward his car, he was already thinking about what he wanted to share with them next time.

# Chapter 13
## The Question of a Changed Life

On Tuesday evening, Adrián was standing at the counter when Elías arrived.

"Well, look who decided to join us."

Elías glanced at his watch.

"I'm early."

"Exactly. Very suspicious."

Elías smiled.

"Where are the others?"

"Marcos is parking. Matías is on his way."

Adrián lifted his coffee.

"You missed quite a conversation after you left on Thursday."

"So I heard."

"Who told you?"

"Matías."

Adrián laughed.

"Of course he did."

Elías set his bag beside the table.

"He seemed rather pleased."

"He should be. We agreed with practically everything he said."

Elías raised an eyebrow.

"Everything?"

"Well, almost everything."

"That must have been quite a breakfast."

"It was historic. You should have been there."

Elías smiled and went to order his coffee.

A few minutes later Marcos arrived, followed by Matías.

They settled around their usual table.

"How's your wife?" Marcos asked Elías.

"Good, thank you. She appreciated the ride."

Adrián nodded solemnly.

"A wise man knows when to leave a theological discussion."

"Especially when his wife is waiting," Elías replied.

Matías laughed.

Anna brought their coffees, and for a few minutes the conversation drifted toward work, family, and the weather.

Then Adrián turned toward Matías.

"Alright. I have a question."

Matías smiled.

"Only one?"

"For now."

"Go ahead."

"I’ve been thinking about what you said on Thursday."

"About discipline?"

"Yes. And about your church."

Matías nodded.

"What about it?"

"Well, I understand why you liked it. People were committed. They took Scripture seriously. They wanted to live differently."

"Exactly."

"And I agree with you. Christians shouldn't be content to live as though nothing has changed."

Matías looked pleased.

"I'm glad you see that."

Adrián paused.

"Although I have been wondering something."

"What's that?"

"How much change should we expect?"

Matías looked at him.

"What do you mean?"

"Well, when someone becomes a Christian, how different should that person be?"

Matías answered without hesitation.

"Completely different."

Marcos looked up.

"Completely?"

"Yes. They're a new creation, aren't they?"

Elías reached for his Bible.

"Second Corinthians five."

Matías nodded.

"Exactly."

Adrián leaned forward.

"So you're saying that when someone gets saved, the old life should be gone."

"That's what Scripture says."

"Immediately?"

Matías considered the question.

"Well, obviously people still have things to learn."

"Of course."

"But their direction changes. Their desires change. Their priorities change. They no longer want the life they lived before."

Adrián nodded slowly.

"Alright."

Matías continued.

"Let me ask you fellows something."

The others looked at him.

"Have any of you ever watched someone destroy his family?"

Marcos set down his cup.

"Unfortunately."

"I mean someone who claimed to be a Christian," Matías continued. "Someone who attended church, knew Scripture, spoke about loving God, and then made decisions that nearly destroyed his marriage and his children."

Adrián's expression became serious.

"Yes. I've seen that."

“So have I,” Elías said.

Matías looked around the table.

"Now let me ask you something else. Do you believe that man was actually saved?"

Marcos considered the question.

"That's difficult to know."

"Is it?"

"Well, we don't know everything about a person's relationship with God."

Matías nodded.
