Anna approached with a fresh mug.

"Coffee, Adrián?"

"Please."

She filled it and glanced toward the empty chair.

"Still missing your fourth musketeer?"

Elías nodded.

"He's in Argentina."

"I remember. How's his mother?"

"Very ill, last we heard."

Her expression softened.

"That's difficult."

"Yes."

"Well, I hope he's doing all right."

She gave a small nod and moved toward the next table.

Adrián wrapped his hands around the mug.

"Any word from Matías?"

Elías shook his head.

"Nothing new."

Adrián looked at the empty chair.

"Doesn't seem right without him."

"No," Elías said.

For a moment, the three men sat quietly.

Then Adrián turned toward Marcos.

"How are you doing?"

"Fine."

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

"Fine?"

"Yes."

"That's a very short answer."

Marcos smiled faintly.

"Would you prefer a report?"

"Now there's the Marcos I know."

Elías laughed.

Marcos looked down at his coffee.

Adrián watched him.

"Still thinking about Laodicea?"

Marcos nodded.

"Yes."

"Have you made any progress?"

Marcos didn't answer immediately.

Then he reached for his Bible.

"I've been reading it every day."

Elías looked interested.

"Revelation three?"

"Yes."

Marcos opened the Bible.

The pages made a soft rustling sound.

He found the passage quickly.

"I've been trying to understand something."

"What?" Elías asked.

"How a church could be so completely mistaken about itself."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Well, they thought they were rich."

"Yes."

"And Christ said they were poor."

Marcos nodded.

"Poor. Blind. Naked."

He paused.

"Wretched and miserable."

Elías said nothing.

Marcos looked up.

"Those aren't mild words."

"No," Elías replied.

"They're humiliating."

Adrián looked at him.

"Humiliating?"

"Imagine being told you're naked when you've been showing everyone how well dressed you are."

Adrián gave a small, uncomfortable laugh.

"That's quite a picture."

"That's the picture Christ gives."

Marcos looked back at the page.

"And the worst part is that they didn't know."

He read the words quietly.

"'and don’t know...'"

A spoon struck the rim of a mug at the next table.

Marcos waited until the sound faded.

"Do you realize what that means?"

Elías looked at him.

"Tell us."

"They weren't merely poor. They believed they were rich."

Marcos tapped the passage.

"And they weren't pretending. At least, the text doesn't say they were."

Elías nodded.

"They believed their own assessment."

"Exactly."

Marcos leaned back.

"They could have been completely sincere."

Adrián considered that.

"And completely wrong."

Marcos nodded.

"That's what frightens me."

The admission surprised both men.

Marcos noticed their expressions and gave a small, embarrassed smile.

"I don't usually say things like that."

"No," Adrián said gently. "You don't."

Marcos looked at his Bible.

"I've spent years telling people how well our church is doing."

Elías waited.

"And I had reasons. Good reasons."

"Certainly."

"Our finances are healthy. Our people are involved. We're helping families. We're growing."

Adrián nodded.

"All true."

Marcos looked at him.

"Yes. All true."

He paused.

"That's what makes this so difficult."

Elías leaned forward slightly.

"Go on."

Marcos looked down at the verse.

"Christ doesn't tell the Laodiceans that their wealth is imaginary."

"No."

"He tells them that, despite what they possess, they're poor."

Elías nodded.

"And naked," Marcos added.

"And blind."

He looked at the two men.

"Suppose someone came to our church and said that."

Adrián shifted in his chair.

"Said what?"

"That we were spiritually poor. Blind. Naked."

Marcos gave a short laugh.

"Do you know what I'd do?"

Neither answered.

"I'd ask them what evidence they had."

Elías smiled faintly.

"That's understandable."

"I'd show them our reports. I'd tell them about the people we've helped. I'd explain our ministries, our finances, our leadership."

Marcos stopped.

He looked down at the table.

"That's exactly what I did with you."

Adrián was quiet.

Marcos looked at Elías.

"You asked me how I knew our church was spiritually healthy."

"Yes."

"And I gave you figures."

Elías nodded.

"You did."

Nobody spoke.

From the kitchen came the sound of plates being stacked. A waitress called out an order. Someone near the window laughed loudly at something on a phone.

Marcos stared at the open Bible.

"I've been thinking about that all week."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Marcos, nobody is saying your church hasn't done good things."

"I know."

"And nobody is saying your work was worthless."

"I know that too."

Marcos looked at him.

"That's not what troubles me."

"What does?"

Marcos rested his hand on the page.

"What if I've been using all those good things to reassure myself about something they cannot prove?"

Elías looked at him.

Marcos continued.

"I know what we've accomplished. I know what we possess. I know what we're capable of doing."

He swallowed.

"What I don't know is whether any of that tells me what Christ sees when He looks at us."

Adrián was silent.

Marcos glanced at the empty space beside his coffee, where his folder usually sat.

"I've spent years helping our church become strong."

He looked down again.

"And now I'm not even sure I've understood what strength means."

Elías spoke gently.

"Marcos, Christ's words to Laodicea don't establish that your church is in the same condition."

"I know."

"And we shouldn't assume they do."

"I agree."

Marcos nodded slowly.

"That's why I'm not accusing anyone."

He looked at Elías.

"I'm questioning myself."

Elías said nothing.

Marcos looked back at Revelation.

"I've always believed that if something was wrong, I could identify it. And once I identified it, I could do something about it."

Adrián smiled faintly.

"That's certainly been your approach."

"Yes."

Marcos traced the words with his finger.

"'and don’t know...'"

He shook his head.

"How do you fix blindness when you're the one who can't see?"

Elías considered the question.

"Christ tells them to come to Him for sight."

"I know."

Marcos closed his eyes briefly.

"I've read that too."

He opened them.

"All my life, I've known what to do next."

His voice had grown quieter.

"Now I'm wondering whether I've been looking at our church through the very blindness Christ was confronting."

Elías started to answer.

Marcos shook his head.

"And if that's true..."

He stopped.

Adrián waited.

Marcos looked from one friend to the other.

"How would I even know?"

Neither man answered.

Marcos stared at the words again.

Then he looked directly at Elías.

"Tell me something."

Elías leaned forward.

"Go ahead."

Marcos hesitated.

For the first time that afternoon, he seemed almost afraid to finish his question.

"If Christ says I'm blind..."

His voice faltered.

"...how can I trust what I see?"

The bell above the door rang.

Adrián glanced toward the entrance.

He froze.

A man stood just inside, carrying a small travel bag over one shoulder.

His dark jacket was creased, and he looked thinner than Adrián remembered.

He was searching the room.

Adrián slowly put down his cup.

"Matías?"

Elías turned.

Marcos followed his gaze.

For a moment, nobody moved.

Then Adrián pushed back his chair so quickly that its legs scraped across the floor.

"MATÍAS!"

Several people looked up.

Matías broke into a smile.

"Adrián!"

Adrián crossed the diner in a few long strides and wrapped both arms around him.

"Look at you!"

Matías laughed as Adrián nearly lifted him off the floor.

"Careful! I'm still carrying my bag!"

"Who cares about your bag?"

"I do!"

Elías was already approaching.

He embraced Matías warmly.

"It's good to see you, brother."

"Good to see you too."

Marcos came around the table.

Matías turned toward him.

"Marcos."

They embraced.

Marcos held him a moment longer than usual.

"Welcome home."

"Thank you."

Adrián stepped back and looked him over.

"You've lost weight."

"A little."

"Have you been eating?"

"Yes."

"Properly?"

Matías smiled.

"You sound like my mother."

The smile remained for a moment.

Then his eyes lowered.

Adrián's expression changed.

He rested a hand on Matías's shoulder.

"Come on. Sit down."

Anna appeared from behind the counter.

"Well, look who's back!"

Matías smiled.

"Hello."

"I was beginning to think those three had frightened you away."

"Not yet."

"Good. We were missing one of the sensible ones."

Adrián looked offended.

"One of?"

She ignored him.

"Your usual coffee?"

Matías nodded.

"Please."

"Coming right up."

He set his bag beside the fourth chair and sat down.

For a moment, the four friends simply looked at one another.

Then Adrián spread his hands.

"Well?"

Matías smiled.

"Well what?"

"You're back!"

"Apparently."

"How long have you been in Windsor?"

"About two hours."

"Two hours?"

"The train got in this morning. I dropped a few things at home and thought I'd see whether you were here."

"You came straight from Toronto?"

"Yes. I flew in yesterday and stayed overnight."

Adrián shook his head.

"Toronto, Windsor, and straight to Deb's Place."

Matías looked around the table.

"Where else would I go?"

Nobody answered immediately.

Anna returned with his coffee.

"Here you are, dear."

"Thank you."

She set a small plate of toast beside it.

Matías looked up.

"I didn't order this."

"I know."

She gave his shoulder a gentle pat.

"Welcome back."

He smiled.

"Thank you."

As she walked away, Adrián pointed at the plate.

"Now that's favoritism."

"Clearly," Elías said.

Matías took a piece of toast.

"I see nothing has changed."

"Nothing at all," Adrián replied.

He smiled.

Then his expression softened.

"How's your mother?"

Matías stopped chewing.

He placed the toast on his plate.

For a moment, he looked down.

Then he folded his hands.

"She passed away."

The noise of the diner continued around them.

Elías lowered his eyes.

Adrián's hand came to rest on his friend's arm.

"I'm so sorry."

Matías nodded.

"Thank you."

"When?" Elías asked quietly.

"Nine days ago."

"I meant to tell you. I couldn’t do it over the phone."

He paused.

"My sister and I were with her."

Marcos reached across the table and took his hand.

"I'm glad you were able to get there."

"Yes."

Matías looked toward the window.

"I'm very grateful for that."

His voice was steady, although his eyes had filled.

Adrián waited.

Matías drew a slow breath.

"She was very weak toward the end. Some days she could barely speak."

He gave a small smile.

"Although she still managed to tell me what to do."

Adrián smiled through his own sadness.

"That sounds like a mother."

"Yes."

Matías looked down at his hands.

"She kept asking whether I'd eaten."

Adrián glanced at the toast.

"Well, there you are."

Matías laughed softly.

"Exactly."

He wiped his eyes.

"Even on the last day, she was worried about whether everyone else was comfortable."

Elías smiled.

"She sounds like a remarkable woman."

"She was."

Matías took a moment before continuing.

"The funeral was difficult. There were so many people. Relatives I hadn't seen in years. Friends from her church. Neighbours."

He shook his head.

"I had forgotten how many people loved her."

Elías nodded.

"That must have meant a great deal."

"It did."

Matías looked at the three of them.

"I wish you could have known her better."

Adrián squeezed his shoulder.

"So do I."

For a while, nobody spoke.

A server passed with two plates of eggs and toast. Someone called for more coffee. The bell rang again as a couple entered from the street.

Matías picked up his mug.

He held it between both hands.

Then he looked at his friends.

"Thank you for praying for us."

Elías nodded.

"Of course."

"My sister appreciated your messages too."

"How is she doing?" Adrián asked.

"She's tired. There was so much to arrange."

"And your father?" Elías asked.

Matías looked down.

"It's hard for him."

He paused.

"They were together a long time."

Elías nodded slowly.

"That kind of absence must be difficult to imagine."

"It is."

Matías stared into his coffee.

"Sometimes I still reach for my phone to call her."

He swallowed.

"Then I remember."

Adrián looked away for a moment.

Matías drew a breath.

"You know, gentlemen, there's something I've been wanting to tell you."

Adrián nodded.

"Of course. Did your whole family come?"

"Most of them. Even my uncle from Córdoba."

"The one you told us about?"

"Yes."

Adrián smiled.

"How's he doing?"

"Very well. He's getting older, of course."

Matías paused.

"Actually, seeing everyone again made me think about—"

"Did you get to spend much time with your father?" Elías asked.

"Yes. Quite a bit."

"Good."

"We talked a great deal."

Matías smiled faintly.

"And during those conversations, I began realizing something about myself."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Did your father tell you anything about your mother that you hadn't known?"

Matías stopped.

Then he laughed.

"Adrián."

"What?"

"Would you let me finish a sentence?"

Adrián blinked.

"Of course."

Matías looked at Elías.

"And you too."

Elías laughed.

"What did I do?"

"You've both been interviewing me since I arrived."

"We're interested!" Adrián protested.

"I know. And I'm grateful."

Matías smiled.

"But I've been trying to tell you something for the last fifteen minutes."

Adrián sat back.

"Well, sir. The floor is yours."

"Thank you."

"Would you like us to take notes?"

"No."

"Record the proceedings?"

"Adrián."

"Sorry."

They laughed.

Matías took a sip of coffee.

Then he set the mug down.

"Gentlemen, I wasn't doing well when I left Windsor."

Adrián nodded.

"Naturally. You'd just heard about your mother."

"No. Before that."

Elías looked at him.

"Before?"

Matías nodded.

"Remember our conversations about Galatia?"

"Of course."

"Especially that passage about beginning by the Spirit and trying to be perfected by the flesh?"

Elías nodded slowly.

"Galatians three."

"Yes."

Matías looked down at his hands.

"I went home after those conversations feeling as though the ground had disappeared beneath my feet."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Why?"

"Because I was beginning to recognize myself."

He paused.

"I've spent years believing that if I was sincere enough, disciplined enough, determined enough, I could become the Christian God wanted me to be."

Adrián nodded.

"And you were sincere."

"Absolutely."

Matías looked at him.

"That's what made it so difficult."

He took another sip of coffee.

"I wanted to obey God. I wanted to live a holy life. I believed those things mattered."

"They do," Elías said.

"Yes. They do."

Matías nodded.

"Yet somewhere along the way, I began depending on my determination to produce them."

He looked at Adrián.

"I thought that if I kept trying, eventually I'd become what I was supposed to be."

Adrián considered that.

"And Galatians challenged that."

"More than challenged it."

Matías looked at Elías.

"Paul asked whether people who began by the Spirit could be perfected by the flesh."

Elías nodded.

"And?"

"I couldn't get away from that question."

Matías gave a small, rueful smile.

"Especially because I'd spent so many years answering it the wrong way."

Anna passed with the coffee pot.

"More, gentlemen?"

Matías covered his cup with his hand.

"No, thank you."

"Good to have you back."

"Thank you."

She moved away.

Matías waited until she had gone.

"I began seeing how much of my confidence rested in myself."

Elías listened.

"My standards. My discipline. My seriousness."

He paused.

"And when I began examining what those things had actually produced in me, I didn't like what I saw."

Adrián looked at him.

"What did you see?"

"Someone who was always trying to become better and never quite arriving."

He smiled faintly.

"Someone who could explain obedience very well and was often frustrated with himself and everyone else."

Adrián lowered his eyes.

Matías continued.

"I was beginning to see myself differently. Not according to how sincerely I wanted to obey, but according to what Scripture said about the flesh."

He looked around the table.

"I was bankrupt, gentlemen."

Nobody spoke.

"I had sincerity. I had determination. I had standards. I had years of trying to do better."

He paused.

"And none of those things could produce the life I was trying so hard to live."

Elías watched him carefully.

"That must have been difficult to admit."

Matías smiled faintly.

"You have no idea."

He looked down at his coffee.

"And then my sister called."

The smile disappeared.

"My mother was dying."

For a moment, he couldn't continue.

"Everything I'd depended upon was already being questioned. Then suddenly I was facing something I couldn't change."

Adrián watched him.

"I couldn't make her better. I couldn't promise my father she'd recover. I couldn't even tell my sister everything would be all right."

Matías swallowed.

"And I couldn't make myself strong enough to handle it."

He looked toward the window.

"That's the condition I was in when I left."

The table was quiet.

A car passed outside, its tires hissing against the damp pavement.

Elías leaned forward.

"So what happened when you got there?"

Matías considered the question.

"At first, I did what I've always done."

"What was that?" Adrián asked.

"I tried to be strong."

He gave a small smile.

"For my father. For my sister. For my mother."

"That's understandable," Elías said.

"Yes. They needed me."

Matías nodded.

"And there were things I could do. I helped with appointments. I made phone calls. I took care of practical matters."

He paused.

"Those things were good. I'm glad I could help."

Adrián nodded.

"Of course."

"But inside, I was still trying to make myself into someone who could handle everything."

He looked down.

"And I couldn't."

Elías waited.

"Some nights I cried. Some mornings I woke up frightened. There were days I couldn't even think clearly."

He gave a small, embarrassed laugh.

"Not exactly the picture of the disciplined Christian I'd spent years trying to become."

Adrián smiled gently.

"Matías, you were losing your mother."

"I know."

Matías looked at him.

"That's something else I've begun to understand."

"What?"

"That I didn't have to pretend I wasn't grieving."

He paused.

"Or make myself feel something I wasn't feeling."

Elías nodded.

"Go on."

Matías looked at the open Bible beside him.

"I kept remembering our conversations."

"About Galatia?" Elías asked.

"Yes. About the flesh. About what it can and cannot produce."

He thought for a moment.

"Then I began reading Romans again."

Adrián smiled.

"Romans?"

"Yes."

"That letter is becoming quite popular around this table."

Matías laughed.

"It is."

He opened his Bible.

The pages rustled as he turned toward chapter eight.

"There's a passage I've known for years."

He found it.

"'There is therefore now no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus.'"

Elías nodded.

"Romans eight, verse one."

"Yes."

Matías looked at the words.

"I've quoted that verse many times."

He paused.

"But I began asking myself whether I actually believed it."

Adrián frowned.

"Of course you believed it."

"Did I?"

Matías looked at him.

"I believed it was true. Yet I kept living as though my standing before God depended on how well I was doing."

Elías nodded slowly.

"I see."

Matías continued.

"If I was disciplined, I felt confident. If I failed, I felt condemned. If I was doing well, I thought my standing before God had improved."

He looked down at the passage.

"And then Paul says there's no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus."

He paused.

"Not because they've finally become sufficiently disciplined."

Adrián nodded.

"Because they're in Christ."

"Exactly."

Matías looked at him.

"That began to mean something different to me."

Elías leaned forward.

"How so?"

"I started thinking about what was actually true."

He looked at the verse again.

"Christ had accomplished something for me that my obedience could never accomplish."

Adrián was quiet.

"And my failure to be strong enough wasn't going to undo what He had done."

Matías closed his Bible halfway.

"That gave me something to trust."

Elías nodded.

"Something outside yourself."

"Yes."

Matías looked at him.

"That's exactly it."

He sat back.

"And the more I thought about it, the more I began to understand how much of my Christian life I'd spent looking at myself."

Adrián rubbed his chin.

"At your progress?"

"Yes. My progress. My failures. My determination. My obedience."

He smiled faintly.

"I was always examining Matías."

Adrián laughed softly.

"Well, he's a very interesting fellow."

Matías laughed.

"Apparently."

Then he grew quiet again.

"And while I was doing all that, I was paying surprisingly little attention to what Christ had already accomplished."

Elías considered that.

"So your confidence began to change."

"Yes."

Matías nodded.

"Slowly."

He looked toward the window.

"Some days were harder than others. There were mornings when I woke up and everything felt unbearable."

His voice grew softer.

"Especially near the end."

Adrián listened.

"But I kept returning to what I knew was true. Not to convince myself that everything was fine. Everything wasn't fine."

He paused.

"My mother was dying."

Nobody spoke.

"Yet Christ hadn't changed. What He had accomplished hadn't changed. My place in Him hadn't changed."

Matías looked at his friends.

"And I began finding comfort in that."

Elías nodded.

"Not because your circumstances improved."

"No."

"Because your confidence was resting somewhere different."

"Exactly."

Matías smiled slightly.

"And the more convinced I became of those things, the more settled I found myself."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Settled?"

"Yes."

Matías thought for a moment.

"Not happy about what was happening. Not free from sorrow."

He looked down.

"I miss my mother terribly."

His eyes filled again.

He waited a moment.

"But I wasn't being carried along by every fear the way I had been before."

Elías nodded slowly.

"And you understood why."

"Yes."

Matías looked at him.

"I had reasons to be at peace. Reasons that were still true even when everything around me was painful."

Adrián was quiet for a long moment.

Then he asked, "And your mother?"

Matías looked toward the window.

"She knew the Lord."

He smiled faintly.

"She'd trusted Him for many years."

He paused.

"Toward the end, when she was very weak, I used to sit beside her and read."

"What did you read?" Elías asked.

"Different passages. Sometimes the Psalms. Sometimes John."

Matías looked down at his hands.

"One afternoon I read from First Thessalonians."

Elías nodded.

"About those who have fallen asleep?"

"Yes."

Matías swallowed.

"About not grieving as those who have no hope."

He paused.

"I used to hear that verse and think Christians were supposed to grieve less."

Adrián looked at him.

"And now?"

"Now I notice Paul doesn't say we shouldn't grieve."

Matías looked up.

"He says we don't grieve without hope."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

"That's a very different thing."

Matías sat quietly.

"My mother died. I cried when she died. I cried at her funeral. I'll probably cry again tonight."

He gave a small smile.

"And I have hope."

Nobody answered immediately.

Then Adrián said quietly, "I think I'm beginning to understand what you've been trying to tell us."

Matías smiled.

"Finally."

Adrián laughed.

"Well, you could have said something sooner."

"I tried!"

Elías burst out laughing.

"You did."

"For twenty minutes!"

"At least," Adrián admitted.

They laughed together.

Matías wiped his eyes.

For a moment, he looked almost surprised to find himself laughing.

Then he shook his head.

"My mother would have enjoyed this."

Adrián smiled.

"She would have liked me."

"She would have fed you."

"Even better."

They laughed again.

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

"Everything all right over here?"

Adrián looked up.

"Yes. Our friend has been telling us about Argentina."

She smiled at Matías.

"Good to have you back, dear."

"Thank you."

She topped up Adrián’s cup.

"And you three, don't keep him here all afternoon. He looks like he could use some rest."

Matías smiled.

"She's right."

Adrián shook his head.

"Now she's taking his side."

"Someone has to," Elías said.

She laughed and moved away.

Matías picked up his coffee.

Adrián watched him for a moment.

"Can I ask you something?"

"Of course."

"You said you left Windsor feeling bankrupt."

"Yes."

"And now?"

Matías considered the question.

He looked down at his Bible.

"I'm still learning."

Adrián waited.

"But I'm not looking at myself the way I used to."

Elías leaned forward.

"What do you mean?"

Matías smiled faintly.

"I used to think the Christian life was something I had to produce."

He paused.

"Now I'm beginning to understand how much depends on what Christ has already done."

Marcos looked up.

"And that changes how you live?"

"It certainly changes what I'm trusting."

Matías looked at him.

"And I think that's beginning to change how I live."

For a moment, the table was quiet.

Then Elías smiled.

"You know, Matías, you sound different."

Matías looked at him.

"Do I?"

"Yes."

"How?"

Elías considered the question.

"I'm not entirely sure."

Adrián nodded.

"I was thinking the same thing."

Matías laughed softly.

"Well, I hope it's an improvement."

Adrián grinned.

"We'll need a few weeks to evaluate."

Elías laughed.

"Perhaps a written report."

"Don't encourage him," Adrián said.

Matías shook his head.

"I see I've returned to a very serious group of theologians."

"Extremely serious," Adrián replied.

They stayed longer than usual.

Matías told them about his father, his sister, and the relatives who had gathered after the funeral. Adrián asked about the food, naturally, and Matías described an aunt who had insisted on feeding everyone whether they were hungry or not.

"She kept bringing out more empanadas," Matías said.

Adrián leaned forward.

"Now we're getting to the important part."

"Adrián," Elías protested.

"What? I'm interested in Argentinian culture."

Matías laughed.

"Very interested, apparently."

"Deeply."

The conversation wandered from one memory to another.

At one point, Matías described a cousin who had insisted on driving him everywhere and had somehow managed to get lost in his own neighbourhood.

"And he wouldn't admit it," Matías said.

Adrián laughed.

"Naturally."

"He kept saying he knew a shortcut."

"That's always a dangerous sentence."

"After the third shortcut, my sister told him to pull over."

Elías was laughing now.

"And did he?"

"Eventually. Although he insisted the GPS was wrong."

"Of course it was," Adrián said.

Matías laughed again.

It was an easy, unguarded laugh.

Then, almost as suddenly, his eyes grew moist.

He looked down at his coffee.

"My mother used to say the same thing about my father."

Adrián smiled gently.

"About getting lost?"

"Yes."

Matías wiped his eyes.

"She said he could get lost between the kitchen and the living room."

They laughed softly.

Matías smiled through his tears.

Outside, the afternoon light had begun to fade.

Anna brought their bill.

"Together or separate?"

Adrián looked at Matías.

"His is on me."

Matías shook his head.

"You don't have to."

"I know."

"Adrián—"

"Don't argue. You're outnumbered."

Elías smiled.

"Three to one."

Matías looked around the table.

"Some things really haven't changed."

Adrián paid the bill.

They gathered their coats and headed toward the door.

Matías picked up his travel bag.

Adrián immediately reached for it.

"Here. Let me."

"It's not heavy."

"Neither am I."

Elías laughed.

"That's debatable."

They stepped outside into the cool Windsor afternoon.

For a moment, the four men stood together beside the parking lot.

Matías looked at each of them.

"It's good to be home."

Adrián smiled.

"It's good to have you home."

Elías nodded.

"Very good."

Marcos embraced him once more.

"Take care of yourself, brother."

"I will."

They said their goodbyes.

As Matías walked toward his car, Adrián called after him.

"Matías!"

He turned.

"What?"

"Next time, call before you arrive!"

Matías smiled.

"Where's the fun in that?"

Adrián laughed.

Matías waved and continued across the parking lot.

The bell above Deb's Place jingled as another customer entered.

Inside, Anna began clearing their table.

Four empty mugs.

Four chairs.

And a small plate with one forgotten piece of toast.

# Chapter 23
## The Correct Church

Adrián was studying the menu when Elías arrived.

"Good afternoon, professor."

Elías smiled as he removed his coat.

"Must you call me that?"

"Would you prefer Doctor?"

"Neither."

"Your Eminence?"

"Adrián."

"Professor it is."

Elías laughed and took his seat.

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

"Good afternoon, gentlemen."

"The usual, please," Elías said.

She filled his mug.

"He's been studying that menu for ten minutes," she told him.

Elías looked at Adrián.

"Having trouble deciding?"

"No. I'm waiting for divine guidance."

Anna shook her head.

"Well, tell Him the lunch special ends at two."

She returned to the counter.

Elías laughed.

Adrián closed the menu.

"So, what have you been doing?"

"Reading."

"Naturally."

"Revelation, actually."

Adrián leaned back.

"Ah. We're still in trouble."

"Not necessarily."

"That's encouraging."

"I've been looking at Ephesus."

Adrián considered that.

"Ephesus."

"Yes."

"Now there's a church I don't remember hearing much criticism about."

"There's quite a bit to appreciate."

"Such as?"

"Doctrinal discernment, perseverance, fidelity to apostolic teaching."

Adrián stared at him.

"Good afternoon to you too."

Elías laughed.

"What?"

"You've been here thirty seconds and already used three theological expressions."

"They're not particularly complicated."

"I asked about a church. You're giving me a lecture title."

The bell above the door jingled.

Matías entered, carrying his Bible beneath one arm.

Adrián raised his hand.

"Look who's becoming a regular again!"

Matías smiled.

"Good afternoon, gentlemen."

Elías stood to embrace him.

"How are you?"

"Well, thank you."

"Getting settled?"

"Slowly."

Adrián pointed toward the empty chair.

"Sit down. The professor has begun."

Matías laughed.

"Already?"

"I'm afraid so."

"What's the subject?"

"Ephesus," Elías replied.

Matías nodded.

"Ah. An interesting church."

"Precisely."

Adrián looked between them.

"Oh, wonderful. Now there are two of you."

Anna arrived with another mug.

"Good afternoon, Matías."

"Hello."

"Coffee?"

"Please."

"And some toast?"

Matías smiled.

"Not today, thank you."

She looked disappointed.

"Are you sure?"

"Quite sure."

Adrián shook his head.

"Argentina has changed him."

Matías laughed.

"Apparently."

She filled his cup and moved away.

Elías opened his Bible.

"Before we look at Revelation, there's some historical context worth considering."

Adrián sighed.

"Of course there is."

"Paul spent considerable time in Ephesus."

Matías nodded.

"And wasn't that where he taught in the school of Tyrannus?"

Elías looked pleased.

"Exactly."

He turned to Acts nineteen.

"Paul initially reasoned in the synagogue. Then, when opposition developed, he withdrew with the disciples and began teaching daily in the school of Tyrannus."

Adrián looked up.

"The school of Tyrannus?"

"Yes. A lecture hall, apparently, where Paul was able to teach."

"A Bible school?"

"Not necessarily in the modern sense. Luke identifies the place where Paul taught, not an institution organized along the lines of our Bible colleges."

Adrián grinned.

"Thank you for clarifying that before I made the mistake."

Elías smiled.

"You're welcome."

Matías laughed.

"How long did Paul teach there?"

"Two years."

"Two years?" Adrián repeated.

"Yes."

"Imagine having Paul as your Bible teacher for two years."

Matías smiled.

"And attending his classes regularly."

Elías looked down at the passage.

"Listen to the result."

He read from Acts nineteen.

"'So that all who dwelt in Asia heard the word of the Lord Jesus, both Jews and Greeks.'"

He looked up.

"That's an extraordinary statement."

Matías nodded.

"Quite an influence."

"Indeed. The ministry extended far beyond the city itself."

Adrián leaned back.

"Well, that would be difficult to improve upon."

Elías smiled.

"There's more."

"Naturally."

"Paul later met with the Ephesian elders at Miletus and warned them about false teachers."

Matías nodded.

"The wolves."

"Exactly."

Elías turned to Acts twenty.

"He warned them that savage wolves would enter among the flock and that men would arise even from among themselves, speaking perverse things."

Adrián frowned.

"From among themselves?"

"Yes."

"That's troubling."

"Very. Which is why Paul urged them to remain watchful."

Matías looked thoughtful.

"And Timothy was there too, wasn't he?"

Elías smiled.

"Yes. Paul urged Timothy to remain in Ephesus and charge certain people not to teach false doctrines."

He turned to First Timothy.

"Chapter one, verse three."

Adrián looked impressed.

"So they had Paul teaching there, Timothy confronting false doctrine, and elders who had been personally warned about false teachers."

"Precisely."

Adrián shook his head.

"Well, gentlemen, this church certainly wasn't lacking biblical instruction."

"No," Elías replied.

"And notice the continuity. Paul warned the elders about false teaching. Timothy was instructed to confront it. Then, in Revelation, Christ commends the church for testing those who claimed to be apostles."

Matías nodded.

"That's quite a history."

Elías smiled.

"Apostolic instruction, doctrinal vigilance, and perseverance."

Adrián laughed.

"Three more theological terms."

"All perfectly appropriate."

"Of course."

Elías looked down at his Bible.

"Few churches could claim such a remarkable foundation."

The bell rang again.

Marcos entered, carrying his coat over one arm.

"Good afternoon."

"Marcos!" Adrián said. "You're just in time."

"For what?"

"A theological examination."

Marcos smiled.

"Should I be worried?"

"Only if you give the wrong answer."

Elías laughed.

"We're studying Ephesus."

Marcos took his seat.

"Ah."

Adrián gestured toward Elías.

"Apparently they had Paul, Timothy, and enough biblical instruction to fill a library."

Elías smiled.

"That's a slight exaggeration."

"Only slight?"

Marcos opened his Bible.

"So what are we looking at?"

"Revelation chapter two," Elías replied.

He waited while Marcos found the passage.

Then he began reading.

"'I know your works, and your toil and perseverance, and that you can’t tolerate evil men,'"

He paused.

"'and have tested those who call themselves apostles, and they are not, and found them false.'"

Matías nodded.

"Hmm."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Wait a minute."

Elías looked up.

"What?"

"Paul warned them about false teachers."

"Yes."

"And now Christ commends them for recognizing false apostles."

"Exactly."

Adrián smiled.

"Well, they certainly listened."

Elías nodded.

"They exercised discernment."

He looked at the passage.

"More precisely, they demonstrated fidelity to apostolic teaching by refusing to accept unauthorized claims to apostolic authority."

Adrián stared at him.

"You could have just said they didn't believe everybody."

Matías laughed.

Elías smiled.

"That would be less precise."

"Naturally."

Elías continued.

"There's an important principle here. The church wasn't expected to accept every teacher simply because he claimed to speak for God."

Marcos nodded.

"And Christ commends their judgment."

"Exactly."

Adrián leaned back.

"So they knew their doctrine."

"They knew enough to recognize false claims," Elías replied. "We shouldn't assume more than the text says."

Adrián pointed at him.

"There he goes again."

"What?"

"Correcting the question before anyone has answered it."

Elías laughed.

"I'm correcting the assumption."

"Of course you are."

Elías returned to Revelation.

"And there's more."

He read verse three.

"'You have perseverance and have endured for my name’s sake, and have not grown weary.'"

He stopped.

For a moment, the four men were quiet.

Then Marcos nodded.

"That's impressive."

Matías looked at the passage.

"They had endured difficulties."

"Yes," Elías said.

"And continued serving."

"Exactly."

Adrián rubbed his chin.

"So let me see whether I've got this straight."

Elías smiled.

"Go ahead."

"They worked hard."

"Yes."

"They persevered."

"Yes."

"They rejected evil."

"Yes."

"And they recognized false apostles."

"Correct."

Adrián looked around the table.

"Well, gentlemen, I think we may have found our winner."

Matías laughed.

"Your winner?"

"After the last three churches, somebody has to win."

Elías smiled.

"I wouldn't approach it as a competition."

"Of course you wouldn't."

Marcos laughed.

Adrián turned toward him.

"You're unusually quiet today."

Marcos smiled.

"I'm listening."

"Well, that's a pleasant change."

Marcos shook his head, amused.

Elías returned to the passage.

"There's something else I appreciate here."

"What?" Matías asked.

"Christ doesn't merely acknowledge their discernment. He commends their labor and perseverance as well."

Matías nodded.

"Yes."

"These people weren't content with knowing the truth. They worked."

"That's important," Marcos said.

"Very."

Elías looked at the passage.

"And they labored for His name's sake."

Adrián smiled.

"Not a bad reason to work."

"Certainly not."

Matías looked down at his Bible.

"Hmm."

Elías noticed.

"What is it?"

Matías read the verse again.

"'for my name’s sake,'"

He paused.

"That's interesting."

"Why?" Adrián asked.

Matías considered his answer.

"Because they had a reason for what they were doing."

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

"And Christ acknowledges it."

"Exactly."

Matías continued looking at the verse.

"There's something I appreciate about that."

"What?" Elías asked.

"That Christ doesn't overlook their work."

Elías nodded.

"No. He doesn't."

Matías looked up.

"After what we've been discussing, I think that's important."

Adrián leaned forward.

"How so?"

"Well, we've spent a considerable amount of time examining the limitations of human effort."

"Yes."

"And rightly so."

Matías looked at Elías.

"Yet here Christ commends labor."

Elías nodded immediately.

"There's no contradiction."

Matías smiled.

"I didn't say there was."

"Of course not."

Elías rested his hands beside the Bible.

"We're dealing with the distinction between the necessity of good works and the source from which spiritual life proceeds."

Adrián looked at him.

"Could you say that in English?"

Elías laughed.

"Good works matter. The flesh cannot produce the life of the Spirit."

"Much better."

Matías nodded.

"That's exactly what I've been thinking about."

Elías continued.

"I'm distinguishing justification from sanctification, and both from the evidence of genuine faith."

Adrián blinked.

"There are three now?"

"Three what?"

"Words I'm going to have to look up."

Marcos laughed.

Elías smiled.

"Justification concerns our standing before God. Sanctification concerns the believer's growth in holiness. Good works are important expressions of faith, although they aren't the basis of our justification."

Matías nodded.

"Yes. I understand those distinctions."

"Of course."

Matías looked down at his Bible.

"Although understanding the distinctions wasn't my difficulty."

Elías turned toward him.

"What do you mean?"

"Well, I could have explained justification and sanctification before I left for Argentina."

"Certainly."

"I knew the definitions."

Matías paused.

"And yet I was still depending on myself."

Elías considered that.

"Yes. Knowing a doctrine and living consistently with it aren't necessarily the same thing."

Matías smiled.

"Exactly."

Adrián leaned forward.

"Now that's interesting."

Elías looked at him.

"What is?"

"Matías knew the right answer before he discovered he wasn't living by it."

Elías nodded.

"That happens."

"Frequently?"

"Unfortunately."

Adrián smiled.

"Well, at least we've identified the problem."

Elías laughed.

Matías looked at the passage again.

"There's something else."

"Go ahead," Elías said.

"The Ephesians had been working for Christ's name."

"Yes."

"And they persevered."

"Yes."

Matías hesitated.

"I suppose what interests me is what was happening within them as they worked."

Elías nodded immediately.

"That's the distinction between outward conduct and inward disposition."

Matías looked at him.

"Yes. Although I was thinking—"

"And it's an important distinction," Elías continued. "Scripture never reduces spiritual maturity to external conformity."

Adrián smiled.

"There's a term for that too, I suppose."

"Externalism."

Adrián threw up his hands.

"Naturally!"

The others laughed.

Matías smiled.

"That's a useful term."

"Thank you," Elías said.

"Although I wasn't really looking for a term."

Elías looked at him.

"Oh?"

"I was wondering about the people themselves."

Elías considered that.

"What about them?"

"Well, we know what they did. Christ tells us."

"Yes."

"And what they did was commendable."

"Certainly."

Matías looked down at the passage.

"I suppose I was wondering whether we know anything about their experience of Christ while they were doing it."

Elías leaned back.

"That's a different question."

"Yes."

Elías thought for a moment.

"Well, we should avoid speculating about their inner lives beyond what the text reveals."

Matías nodded.

"Agreed."

"And Christ explicitly says they labored for His name's sake."

"Yes."

"So we have good reason to recognize their devotion."

"Certainly."

Matías smiled faintly.

"All right."

Adrián looked between them.

"Did we settle that?"

Elías smiled.

"I think so."

Matías took a sip of coffee.

"Perhaps."

Adrián laughed.

"That's not the same answer."

Anna arrived with their food.

"Who ordered the club?"

Adrián raised his hand.

"That would be me."

She set the plate before him.

"And the soup?"

"Mine," Elías said.

She placed it beside his Bible.

"Careful with the pages."

"I will."

She set Marcos’s plate beside his Bible.

She looked at Matías.

"Still nothing?"

"I'm fine, thank you."

She shook her head.

"Four men, three orders. You're ruining my averages."

Adrián laughed.

"Ask Marcos. He can help you with the figures."

Marcos smiled.

"Only if you have a spreadsheet."

She rolled her eyes and returned to the counter.

Adrián picked up half his sandwich.

"So, professor, let me ask you something."

"Go ahead."

"What makes Ephesus so different from the others?"

Elías considered the question.

"Well, Corinth had serious divisions and moral disorders."

Adrián nodded.

"Among other things."

"Galatia was being drawn toward reliance on the flesh through the controversy over the Law."

Matías nodded.

"And Laodicea had a profoundly mistaken assessment of its own condition."

Elías turned back to Revelation.

"Now look at Ephesus."

He tapped the passage.

"Doctrinal vigilance. Moral seriousness. Perseverance. Labor for Christ's name."

Adrián nodded.

"And Christ commends them."

"Exactly."

Elías smiled.

"There's something particularly reassuring about that."

"What?" Matías asked.

"These aren't qualities the church merely claims to possess. They're qualities Christ Himself acknowledges. Those are the things I've always valued in our own church."

Matías looked at the passage.

"That's true."

Adrián leaned back.

"Well, gentlemen, perhaps we've finally found the correct church."

Elías laughed.

"I wouldn't go quite that far."

"Why not?"

"Because no church is beyond examination."

"Naturally."

Elías smiled.

"Although there's a great deal here to admire."

Matías nodded.

"Certainly."

Adrián looked at him.

"You sound less enthusiastic."

"Do I?"

"A little."

Matías smiled.

"I admire everything Christ commends."

"Then what's troubling you?"

"Nothing, exactly."

He looked down at the Bible.

"I've just been thinking about how easily I used to assume that knowing the right thing meant I was living the right way."

Elías nodded.

"That's a familiar problem in Christian theology."

Adrián grinned.

"Another one?"

"The distinction between knowledge and practice."

"Ah. That one I understand."

Elías laughed.

Matías smiled.

"Yes. Although for me, it went deeper than practice."

Elías looked at him.

"How so?"

Matías thought for a moment.

"I could practice a great many things while still relying on myself."

Elías nodded.

"Certainly. We've already established that from Galatians."

"Yes."

Matías looked at the passage.

"And I suppose I'm still discovering what that means."

Elías smiled.

"That's understandable."

Adrián leaned back.

"Well, I'm glad somebody around here is still discovering things."

Elías looked at him.

"What does that mean?"

"Nothing, professor."

The others laughed.

Elías shook his head.

"You enjoy this far too much."

"Immensely."

They ate for a while.

The diner grew busier as the afternoon progressed. A family settled into the booth behind them, and a child insisted loudly that he hadn't ordered the vegetables on his plate.

Adrián glanced over his shoulder.

"There's a young man with strong doctrinal convictions."

Matías laughed.

"About vegetables?"

"Absolutely. He knows what he believes."

Elías smiled.

"Knowledge without obedience."

Adrián pointed at him.

"See? Everything has a theological category."

They laughed.

Elías wiped his hands and returned to Revelation.

"There's one more thing I want to emphasize."

Adrián groaned.

"Of course there is."

Elías smiled.

"Christ praises their perseverance, their refusal to tolerate evil, and their ability to distinguish true apostles from false ones."

Elías looked at his friends.

"These are not minor accomplishments. A church that neglects doctrine can do enormous harm. A church that tolerates evil can injure its people. A church that abandons perseverance will accomplish very little."

Matías nodded.

"I agree."

Marcos looked up.

"So do I."

Adrián smiled.

"Unanimous agreement. We should mark the occasion."

Elías laughed.

"All I'm saying is that we should take Christ's commendation seriously."

Matías nodded.

"We should."

He paused.

"And whatever else we discover about Ephesus, we shouldn't take any of that away."

Elías smiled.

"Precisely."

Adrián reached for his coffee.

"Although there's something I don't understand."

Elías looked at him.

"What?"

"Why haven't we read the rest?"

"The rest?"

"The letter."

Elías glanced down at the page.

"We've been examining Christ's commendation."

"I know. And it's impressive."

Adrián pointed at the Bible.

"There's another verse."

"Of course."

"Verse four."

Elías glanced down.

"Ah, yes."

He read aloud.

"'But I have this against you, that you left your first love.'"

He nodded.

"Yes. An important exhortation."

Adrián looked at him.

"An exhortation?"

"Certainly. Christ commends their faithfulness and then addresses an area in which they need correction."

Matías looked up.

"Hmm."

Elías continued.

"That's quite consistent with the way Scripture addresses believers. Commendation where it's warranted, correction where it's needed. No church is perfect."

Adrián smiled.

"Well, that's reassuring."

"It should be."

Matías looked at the passage again.

"Although He doesn't merely say they could improve."

Elías turned toward him.

"What do you mean?"

"He says, 'I have this against you.'"

"Yes. That's the language of rebuke."

Matías nodded.

"And they've left their first love."

"Correct."

Matías paused.

"That sounds rather serious."

"Certainly. Love is fundamental to Christian life."

Elías took a sip of coffee.

"That's why Christ calls them to remember, repent, and return to their first works."

Adrián looked at him.

"So you don't find this particularly troubling?"

"Any rebuke from Christ deserves serious attention."

Elías smiled slightly.

"Although I don't think we should allow one area requiring correction to erase everything He has just commended."

Matías nodded.

"No. We shouldn't."

He looked down at the page.

"Still..."

Elías waited.

"What?"

Matías read the words quietly.

"'you left your first love.'"

He looked up.

"I wonder what that meant to Him."

Elías answered immediately.

"Well, are we speaking of their love for Christ, their love for one another, or both?"

Adrián smiled.

"Of course there's a theological discussion."

Elías laughed.

"Naturally."

Matías continued looking at the verse.

"And which interpretation do you prefer?"

Elías settled back.

"I think the broader biblical teaching on love gives us good reason to understand both dimensions as closely related."

Matías nodded.

"That makes sense."

Elías closed his Bible halfway.

"And whatever position one takes, the practical exhortation remains clear."

Adrián looked at him.

"Which is?"

"Remember, repent, and return to the works they did at first."

He smiled.

"An important lesson for every church."

Matías looked down.

"Yes."

Then he turned the page back.

"But there's something else He says."

Elías looked at him.

"What's that?"

Matías ran his finger along the next verse.

"Here."

He read quietly.

"'or else I am coming to you swiftly, and will move your lamp stand out of its place, unless you repent.'"

He stopped.

Adrián lowered his sandwich.

"Remove their lampstand?"

Elías nodded.

"Yes."

"What does that mean?"

"Well, the lampstands represent the churches. Revelation chapter one establishes that symbolism."

Adrián looked at the passage.

"So Christ is threatening to remove their lampstand?"

"Yes."

Matías looked up.

"That sounds considerably more serious than an ordinary area for improvement."

Elías considered the statement.

"Certainly. The warning is solemn."

Adrián leaned forward.

"How solemn?"

Elías folded his hands.

"I understand it as a threat to the church's continued existence as a witness."

Marcos looked at the passage.

"That's severe."

"Yes," Elías replied. "It is."

Matías looked at him.

"And this is the same church Christ has just commended."

"Exactly."

"For its labor."

"Yes."

"And perseverance."

"Yes."

"And discernment."

"Correct."

Matías looked down at the page.

"Yet He threatens to remove its lampstand."

Elías nodded.

"Unless they repent."

Matías looked up.

"Yes."

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then Adrián said, "Well, that certainly changes the atmosphere."

Elías smiled faintly.

"It introduces the seriousness of Christ's correction."

Adrián stared at him.

"Introduces?"

Elías laughed.

"What would you prefer I call it?"

"I don't know. Something with a little more alarm in it."

Marcos chuckled.

Elías smiled.

"Warnings in Scripture are meant to be taken seriously, Adrián. They don't need to be exaggerated to have their proper force."

Matías nodded.

"That's true."

He looked at the passage again.

"Although I don't think we've exaggerated anything."

Elías turned toward him.

"No?"

"We've simply read what Christ said."

Elías considered that.

"Fair enough."

Matías continued looking at the verse.

Then he closed his Bible gently.

"I think I'd like to spend some more time with this."

Elías nodded.

"An excellent idea."

Adrián leaned back.

"Well, I'm glad we found the correct church."

The others laughed.

Elías smiled.

"Perhaps we should be a little more careful with that description."

"Why? They were correct about a great many things."

"Yes."

"And Christ Himself said so."

"Yes."

Adrián looked at the passage.

"And He still had something against them."

Elías nodded.

"That's true."

Anna approached with the bill.

"Together or separate?"

Adrián looked around the table.

"Together."

She smiled.

"Good. I was afraid you'd learned something from those fourteen volunteers."

Marcos laughed.

"Apparently not."

She placed the bill beside Adrián.

"Try not to stay until closing."

"No promises," he replied.

They gathered their belongings.

Outside, the air had turned cooler.

Matías pulled his coat closer.

"Breakfast next Thursday?"

"Of course," Adrián said.

Elías nodded.

"I'd like to continue with Ephesus."

Adrián smiled.

"I suspected you might."

Marcos said goodbye and headed toward his car.

Matías lingered beside Elías.

"There's something I'd like to ask you next time."

"What's that?"

Matías glanced at the Bible beneath Elías's arm.

"About Ephesus."

"Go ahead."

Matías smiled.

"Next time."

Elías laughed.

"All right."

Adrián called from a few steps away.

"Are you two planning another meeting without us?"

"Not yet," Matías replied.

"Good. I want advance notice."

They said goodbye.

Elías walked toward his car, thinking about the passage.

*I know your works, your labor, your patience.*

He had spent much of his life encouraging Christians to take Scripture seriously.

To examine what they heard.

To distinguish truth from error.

To persevere in what was right.

And here was a church Christ Himself commended for doing precisely those things.

The rebuke was serious, of course. He had never denied that.

Love was essential. Every Christian knew that. Every church needed to be reminded of it.

There were well-established biblical categories for all these matters.

He had taught them many times.

Elías unlocked his car.

He was looking forward to continuing their examination of Ephesus.

There was a great deal to admire about that church.

And he was quite confident that he understood what Christ was saying to it.

# Chapter 24
## The First Works

“Before anybody says anything,” Adrián announced, “I have a question.”

Matías looked up from his coffee.

“That's never stopped you before.”

“Very funny.”

It was another Thursday morning at Deb's Place. The rain had finally given way to a bright November sky, although the wind coming off the river was reminding Windsor that winter wasn't far away.

Marcos was removing his coat. Elías had already arranged his Bible, notebook, and reading glasses beside his plate.

Adrián pointed at him.

“And no Greek words until I've finished.”

Elías smiled.

“That's a rather unreasonable restriction.”

“Five minutes.”

“I'll do my best.”

Matías glanced at his watch.

“Starting now.”

Adrián opened his Bible.

“Last week we kept coming back to something. Christ commended the Ephesians for their works, and then He told them to do the first works.”

“Correct,” Elías said.

“So I've been wondering what the difference was.”

Marcos pulled out his chair.

“That's what I was thinking about too.”

Elías nodded.

“It's a worthwhile question.”

Adrián looked at Matías.

“See? We haven't needed any Greek.”

“Four minutes and thirty seconds,” Matías replied.

Elías laughed.

“Are we actually going to study?”

“Eventually.”

Adrián read Revelation 2:2 aloud.

“‘I know your works, and your toil and perseverance,’”

He moved down to verse five.

“‘Remember therefore from where you have fallen, and repent and do the first works;’”

He looked up.

“Christ knows what they're doing. He praises them for it. Then He tells them to return to something.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

“What were they doing before that they weren't doing now?”

Elías considered the question.

“The passage doesn't specify every activity involved in the first works. We should be careful not to invent a list.”

“Agreed,” Matías said.

“Although the connection with first love is unmistakable,” Elías continued. “The command to do the first works follows the charge that they've left their first love.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“So they hadn't stopped working.”

“Clearly not,” Elías replied.

“They were working hard.”

“Christ says so.”

“And persevering.”

“Yes.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Yet something about their earlier works was missing.”

Elías nodded thoughtfully.

“That's a reasonable way of putting it.”

Adrián smiled.

“Now that's interesting.”

“What's interesting?” Elías asked.

“Christ doesn't tell them to work harder.”

“No.”

“He tells them to remember.”

“Yes.”

“And repent.”

“Correct.”

“And return.”

Elías raised a finger.

“More precisely, do the first works.”

Adrián laughed.

“I knew you'd find something to correct.”

“Accuracy is a virtue.”

“So is letting someone finish a sentence.”

Matías grinned.

Elías surrendered with a small wave of his hand.

“Proceed.”

Adrián looked down at the passage again.

“I just wonder whether we've been looking for the answer in the wrong place.”

“What do you mean?” Marcos asked.

“We keep asking what they were doing. Maybe we should be asking what they were participating in.”

Elías looked up.

“Participating?”

“Yes.”

Adrián turned several pages.

“I came across something yesterday.”

He found 1 Corinthians 1:9 and began reading.

“‘God is faithful, by whom you were called into the fellowship of His Son, Jesus Christ our Lord.’”

He paused.

“Fellowship.”

Elías nodded.

“An important word.”

“What's the Greek?”

Matías burst out laughing.

Elías looked delighted.

“Ah! The prohibition has expired?”

“Apparently,” Matías said.

Adrián waved a hand.

“Go ahead.”

“*Koinōnia.* Sharing, communion, participation.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“That last one.”

“Participation?”

“Yes.”

Elías nodded.

“It's a legitimate sense of the word.”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Listen to what Paul says. God called us into participation with His Son.”

Matías leaned forward.

“That's quite a statement.”

“Isn't it?”

Marcos took the Bible and read the verse for himself.

“And he says that to Corinth.”

“Exactly!” Adrián said.

“The Corinthians we've been studying.”

“The very ones.”

Matías smiled.

“Wow.”

Adrián sat back.

“We've spent weeks examining their divisions, their boasting, their gifts, their problems. And right near the beginning of the letter Paul tells them what God called them into.”

“Fellowship with His Son,” Marcos said.

“Participation with His Son!”

Elías nodded.

“It's a beautiful description of the Christian calling.”

Matías was still looking at the passage.

“I've always thought of fellowship as something Christians have with one another.”

“That's certainly one aspect,” Elías replied.

“Yes,” Matías said. “Yet here Paul speaks about fellowship with Christ Himself.”

Adrián smiled.

“That's so cool.”

Marcos looked toward Elías.

“Then fellowship isn't merely attending meetings or spending time together.”

“No,” Elías said. “It's a much richer biblical concept.”

Adrián looked at their table.

“Although I hope this counts for something.”

Matías lifted his coffee.

“It counts for breakfast.”

“Very important ministry.”

They laughed.

Elías reached for his notebook.

“Actually, John develops this subject quite extensively in his first epistle.”

Matías looked up.

“First John?”

“Yes. Chapter one.”

Adrián turned there immediately.

“Let's read it.”

Elías smiled.

“I'm pleased to see such enthusiasm.”

“Don't ruin it with a lecture.”

“I wouldn't dream of it.”

Marcos laughed.

“That's not a promise he can keep.”

Elías placed a hand over his heart.

“Such confidence in my character.”

Adrián began reading.

“‘That which we have seen and heard we declare to you, that you also may have fellowship with us; and truly our fellowship is with the Father and with His Son Jesus Christ.’”

He stopped.

Matías leaned forward.

“Read that last part again.”

Adrián did.

“‘And truly our fellowship is with the Father and with His Son Jesus Christ.’”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“That's amazing.”

Elías smiled.

“Yes.”

Matías looked at Adrián.

“John is inviting them to share in something he already knows.”

“Exactly.”

“And the fellowship is with the Father and His Son.”

Adrián shook his head.

“Amen.”

For a moment, the men were quiet.

Then Elías turned the page slightly.

“Notice how John develops the argument.”

He pointed to verse five.

“This is where the central message is stated.”

Matías read aloud.

“‘This is the message which we have heard from him and announce to you, that God is light, and in him is no darkness at all.’”

Adrián looked up.

“Wait.”

Matías stopped.

“Read the beginning again.”

“‘This is the message which we have heard from him and announce to you,’”

“And the message is?”

Matías continued.

“‘God is light, and in him is no darkness at all.’”

Adrián stared at the page.

“Wow.”

Marcos leaned closer.

“What?”

“We've been talking about fellowship, and John says this is the message.”

Elías nodded.

“God is light.”

“Not that we need to become better at producing light,” Adrián said.

Elías considered that.

“The passage certainly begins with a declaration concerning God's nature.”

“Exactly!”

Adrián looked at Matías.

“The center is God Himself.”

Matías smiled.

“That's amazing.”

Elías continued.

“And John then explains the implications of claiming fellowship with Him.”

“Let's see,” Marcos said.

Matías read verse six.

“‘If we say that we have fellowship with him and walk in the darkness, we lie, and don’t tell the truth.’”

He paused.

Then he read verse seven.

“‘But if we walk in the light, as he is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus Christ, his Son, cleanses us from all sin.’”

Marcos sat back.

“That's quite a contrast.”

“Light and darkness,” Adrián said.

Elías nodded.

“John uses those categories repeatedly.”

Matías looked at him.

“What does it mean to walk in darkness?”

Elías answered readily.

“To live contrary to the truth and character of God. John is concerned with sin, false claims, and the inconsistency between profession and conduct.”

Matías nodded.

“That makes sense.”

“Walking in the light involves living openly before God, in accordance with His revelation.”

“Right.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“So we can't claim participation with God while walking contrary to who He is.”

“That's the force of John's warning,” Elías replied.

Marcos was still thinking.

“Then you can't walk in darkness and expect the results of the light.”

The others looked at him.

He glanced around the table.

“Isn't that what John is saying?”

Matías smiled.

“That's a good question.”

Elías considered it.

“In the sense that fellowship with God cannot be separated from walking in His light, yes.”

Adrián looked at Marcos.

“Say that again.”

“What?”

“What you just said.”

Marcos smiled.

“You can't walk in darkness and expect the results of the light.”

Adrián tapped the table.

“That's it.”

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“What's it?”

“We've discovered something,” Adrián announced.

“Again?”

“Yes.”

She topped up Marcos’s cup.

“Must be quite a book you've got there.”

“It is,” Matías said.

She looked at Elías.

“And you're letting the others do some talking today?”

Elías laughed.

“I'm attempting to.”

“Good for you.”

She moved to the next table.

Adrián watched her go.

“She notices everything.”

“Occupational hazard,” Marcos said.

Matías was turning the pages of his Bible.

“There's another passage.”

He found chapter two.

“Listen to this.”

He read verse nine.

“‘He who says he is in the light, and hates his brother, is in darkness until now.’”

He continued.

“‘He who loves his brother abides in the light, and there is no cause for stumbling in him.’”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Wow.”

Marcos read the verses again.

“So John connects walking in the light with loving your brother.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And darkness with hatred.”

“That's the contrast.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“There's no middle category in the way John presents it.”

Elías nodded.

“John often uses sharp contrasts to expose false claims.”

Adrián looked down.

“And here we are trying to decide whether Ephesus had enough good things to balance out leaving its first love.”

Elías looked at him.

“I wouldn't describe our discussion quite that way.”

Adrián smiled.

“I know you wouldn't.”

Matías intervened gently.

“Let's stay with John for a moment.”

He read the verses again, more slowly.

“John isn't asking whether the person can explain the light correctly.”

“No,” Elías said.

“He's examining the claim by what is present in the person's life.”

“Certainly.”

Marcos nodded.

“By the fruit.”

Elías looked at him.

“That's a reasonable connection, although John's immediate concern is the relationship between profession and conduct.”

“Right,” Marcos said.

He looked back at the passage.

“Yet the conduct tells us something about the claim.”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned back, smiling.

“That's amazing.”

Elías looked amused.

“You've said that several times today.”

“Because it keeps being amazing.”

Matías laughed.

Adrián turned to chapter four.

“And here's another one.”

He read aloud.

“‘Beloved, let us love one another, for love is of God.’”

He looked up.

“Love is of God.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

“Then verse nineteen.”

Adrián found it.

“‘We love Him because He first loved us.’”

Matías closed his eyes briefly.

“Amen.”

Marcos looked from one passage to the other.

“So John begins by saying God is light. Then he says love is of God.”

“Yes,” Elías replied.

“And the love begins with Him.”

“Correct.”

Adrián smiled broadly.

“Do you see what we're finding?”

Elías looked around the table.

“I believe so.”

“It's all coming from Him!”

Matías nodded.

“And we were called into fellowship with His Son.”

Marcos was smiling now too.

“Participation.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Exactly.”

For several minutes, the conversation moved quickly.

Matías found another passage.

Marcos compared it with something Paul had written.

Adrián kept returning to the word *participation*.

Elías supplied references, clarified a distinction, and reminded them that John's teaching about love could not be separated from his teaching about truth and obedience.

The others agreed.

Then Adrián would discover another connection, and the conversation would take off again.

At one point Matías laughed so freely that Adrián stopped speaking.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

“Why are you looking at me?” Matías asked.

Adrián grinned.

“I was just wondering who you are and what you've done with Matías.”

Marcos laughed.

Matías shook his head.

“Very funny.”

Elías smiled.

It was good to see his friend enjoying himself again.

And yet something about the morning puzzled him.

He knew these passages.

He had studied them carefully. He had taught from First John on several occasions. He understood the theological relationships between light, fellowship, love, truth, and obedience.

Everything his friends were discovering was familiar.

Yet they seemed to be encountering it as though they had never seen it before.

Their excitement was genuine.

He found himself enjoying it.

He also found himself wondering what, precisely, had changed.

The conversation eventually returned to Revelation.

Matías turned back to the passage concerning Ephesus.

“Maybe this helps us understand why Christ's warning is so serious.”

Elías looked up.

“How so? Are we identifying the first works, or exploring why they mattered?”

“Perhaps the difference wasn't the activities themselves, but the influence under which they were done. Ephesus had works Christ commended. That's not in question.”

“Agreed.”

“And sound discernment.”

“Yes.”

Matías looked at the words.

“Yet Christ says they've left their first love.”

Adrián nodded.

“And John keeps bringing us back to love as something that comes from God.”

Elías folded his hands.

“Certainly.”

Marcos looked at him.

“Then perhaps the question isn't simply whether a church is doing good things.”

Elías waited.

“Perhaps we also need to ask whether the life of Christ is being expressed through what the church is doing.”

Elías considered the statement.

“That's a substantial question.”

Adrián smiled.

“It's a wonderful question.”

Elías nodded.

“Although we should avoid assuming that every commendable work in Ephesus was somehow invalid.”

“Of course,” Matías said. “Christ Himself commended them.”

“Exactly.”

Matías looked at the passage again.

“Yet the commendations didn't settle the matter.”

Elías said nothing for a moment.

“No,” he replied. “They didn't.”

Adrián looked up.

“You know what I'm wondering?”

Marcos smiled.

“Here we go.”

Adrián ignored him.

“What does mature love actually look like?”

Elías smiled.

“That's a very important question.”

“Because we keep talking about first love. And I don't think Christ is simply asking the Ephesians to recover their early enthusiasm.”

“No,” Elías said. “I wouldn't reduce it to that.”

Matías nodded.

“Neither would I.”

Elías considered the matter.

“Actually, marriage provides a useful illustration.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Go ahead.”

“When a couple first falls in love, there's often considerable excitement. Strong emotions, constant attention, the desire to spend every possible moment together.”

Marcos smiled.

“I remember.”

“Yet marriage cannot remain permanently in that initial emotional state,” Elías continued. “Love matures. It becomes steadier, more dependable, more deeply rooted in commitment.”

Matías nodded.

“That's true.”

“Responsibilities increase. There are bills, children, work, obligations. Mature love expresses itself through faithfulness, sacrifice, and perseverance.”

Adrián smiled.

“Sounds like you've been married a while.”

“Twenty-seven years.”

“Congratulations.”

“Thank you.”

Elías continued.

“My wife and I have built a good life together. We've remained faithful to one another. We've raised our children, supported one another through difficulties, and fulfilled our responsibilities.”

He smiled warmly.

“I wouldn't trade those years for anything.”

Matías looked at him.

“That's wonderful, Elías.”

“It is.”

Adrián nodded.

“Twenty-seven years is something to be thankful for.”

“Absolutely.”

Elías took a sip of coffee.

“And I would say that kind of steady commitment is a much better measure of mature love than the intensity of one's initial emotions.”

Matías considered that.

“Can I ask you something?”

“Of course.”

“Does your wife experience your relationship that way?”

Elías looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

“You've described what your marriage means to you. I'm wondering whether she would describe it the same way.”

Elías smiled.

“Well, I assume so. We've never really discussed it.”

Matías nodded.

Adrián glanced at him.

It was a brief glance, hardly noticeable.

Elías continued.

“Of course, every marriage has its ordinary difficulties. We've certainly had ours. Yet we've always managed them responsibly.”

Marcos nodded.

“Marriage takes work.”

“Indeed,” Elías said.

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Have you ever asked her what she enjoys most about your relationship?”

Elías considered the question.

“Not in those precise terms.”

“You should.”

Elías smiled.

“Perhaps I will.”

The conversation moved on.

They discussed the difference between affection and commitment, the importance of faithfulness, and the danger of treating love as nothing more than a passing emotion.

Elías contributed several helpful observations.

The others listened appreciatively.

Nobody challenged his marriage.

Nobody suggested that his wife was unhappy.

And Elías saw no particular reason to be concerned.

Still, as they gathered their things to leave, Matías returned briefly to the morning's discoveries.

“I want to spend more time in First John.”

“Me too,” Adrián said immediately.

Marcos nodded.

“There's a lot there we haven't really considered.”

Elías smiled.

“It's a rich epistle.”

Adrián laughed.

“Rich? It's incredible!”

Elías shook his head affectionately.

“I'll have to find a more enthusiastic theological adjective.”

“Try awesome.”

“I'll consider it.”

They laughed and headed outside.

The November air was cold enough to make Adrián pull his jacket tighter.

“Next Thursday?” Marcos asked.

“Absolutely,” Matías said.

Adrián looked at Elías.

“And bring your Greek.”

Elías raised an eyebrow.

“I thought it was prohibited.”

“Only for the first five minutes.”

“Very generous.”

They said goodbye.

Elías drove home thinking about the morning.

He was pleased by his friends' enthusiasm. Their growing interest in Scripture was encouraging, and he was grateful to have contributed to their investigation.

He also remembered Matías's question about his marriage.

*Does your wife experience your relationship that way?*

It was an unusual question.

He had never considered that there might be much difference between his assessment and hers.

They had been married twenty-seven years.

Surely that spoke for itself.

When he arrived home, his wife was sitting at the kitchen table with a cup of tea.

She looked up and smiled.

“Hello.”

“Hello, dear.”

He kissed her cheek and hung up his coat.

“How was your morning?”

“Very interesting.”

“Your Bible study?”

“Yes. We're examining the relationship between fellowship, love, and spiritual maturity.”

She smiled.

“That sounds like something you'd enjoy.”

“It was quite stimulating.”

He poured himself a glass of water.

Then he remembered the question.

“May I ask you something?”

“Of course.”

He sat across from her.

“Would you say we've had a good marriage?”

She looked at him.

For a moment, she didn't answer.

Elías smiled.

“I know it's a rather broad question.”

She lowered her eyes to her tea.

“Yes,” she said quietly. “It is.”

He waited.

She looked toward the window.

“Why do you ask?”

“Something came up in our discussion today.”

“Oh.”

“We were talking about mature love. I used our marriage as an illustration.”

She looked back at him.

“You did?”

“Yes.”

He smiled.

“Twenty-seven years. Faithfulness, commitment, raising a family together. I thought it was a rather good example.”

She gave a small smile.

Then she looked down again.

Elías noticed.

“Is something wrong?”

She shook her head.

“Not exactly.”

He waited.

After a moment she said, “Elías, do you really think that's all there is to a good marriage?”

He looked surprised.

“Of course not.”

She nodded.

He continued.

“There's mutual respect, companionship, shared convictions, responsibility—”

“Elías.”

He stopped.

She was looking at him now.

“I'm not asking for a definition.”

He sat quietly.

She took a breath.

“I'm asking about us.”

He looked at her.

For the first time that afternoon, he wasn't sure what to say.


# Chapter 25
## Remember

Elías had been looking for a photograph.

It was Saturday morning, and he had gone into the spare bedroom to find an old family album. His wife had mentioned a picture of their youngest son wearing an enormous winter coat on his first day of school.

She wanted to send it to him.

Elías remembered the photograph.

What he hadn't expected was to find an entire box of things he had forgotten.

There were birthday cards, school photographs, letters from relatives, and several envelopes containing pictures from the early years of their marriage.

He sat on the edge of the bed and opened one.

His wife was standing beside him at a picnic.

They were both laughing.

He couldn't remember what had been so funny.

In another photograph, she was wearing his oversized jacket. He had his arm around her shoulders, and she was looking up at him with an expression he hadn't seen in a long time.

He smiled.

They had been so young.

He found a photograph taken at a church gathering. Several couples were sitting around a picnic table. Someone had apparently told a joke, and Elías was laughing with his head thrown back.

He stared at it.

He couldn't remember the last time anyone had taken a picture of him laughing like that.

His wife appeared in the doorway.

“Did you find it?”

“Not yet.”

She stepped inside.

“What have you got there?”

“Some old photographs.”

She came over and sat beside him.

He showed her the picnic.

She laughed.

“Oh, I remember that day.”

“You do?”

“Of course.”

“What was so funny?”

“You tried to convince everyone you could cook.”

Elías smiled.

“I can cook.”

“You burned the sausages.”

“That was an equipment problem.”

She laughed again.

He looked at her.

It was a lovely sound.

He turned to another photograph.

“Look at this one.”

She smiled.

“We used to have so much fun.”

The words were spoken without accusation.

That somehow made them harder to hear.

He studied the photograph.

“Yes,” he said quietly. “We did.”

She touched the corner of the picture.

“You were always making me laugh.”

Elías looked at her.

“Was I?”

“Oh, yes. You were quite ridiculous sometimes.”

He laughed.

“Thank you.”

“I mean that affectionately.”

“I should hope so.”

She smiled.

Then she looked at the photograph again.

“You were so fun-loving.”

Elías repeated the expression.

“Fun-loving.”

“Yes.”

He looked down at the younger man in the picture.

He remembered those years.

They had little money, a small apartment, and furniture donated by friends. Their car was unreliable. They argued occasionally about bills and laughed afterward at how seriously they'd taken things that now seemed insignificant.

They prayed together.

They read Scripture together.

Sometimes they stayed up late talking about something they'd discovered in the Bible.

He remembered how excited he had been when he first began to understand the grace of God.

He hadn't known very much.

He had wanted to know everything.

His wife leaned against his shoulder.

“What are you thinking?”

He considered the question.

“I'd forgotten how much we laughed.”

She nodded.

“So had I, for a while.”

He turned toward her.

“I'm sorry.”

She looked at him.

“Thank you for saying that.”

“I know.”

She smiled gently.

“I'm glad you're listening.”

He looked back at the photographs.

He wanted to promise that things would change.

He wanted to tell her he would arrange more outings, spend less time studying, and become more attentive.

He had already begun making a list.

Yet something about that list troubled him.

It was familiar.

When he discovered a problem, he identified the necessary correction and developed a plan.

That was how he had approached nearly everything in life.

He looked at his wife.

“Would you mind if we just sat here for a while?”

She smiled.

“I'd like that.”

So they did.

And for once, Elías didn't feel the need to improve the moment.

On Thursday, Matías arrived at Deb's Place carrying a small notebook.

Adrián noticed immediately.

“Oh, no.”

Matías looked up.

“What?”

“You've become Elías.”

Elías, already seated, smiled.

“I consider that a compliment.”

Adrián pointed at the notebook.

“First the notes. Next he'll start correcting my Greek.”

Matías sat down.

“Your Greek needs considerable correction.”

Marcos laughed.

“Apparently the transformation is complete.”

Adrián shook his head.

“I'm surrounded.”

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“Same as usual?”

“Yes, please,” Matías said.

She filled his cup.

“You boys look cheerful.”

“We're enjoying ourselves,” Adrián announced.

Anna glanced at Elías.

“All of you?”

Elías laughed.

“We're working on it.”

She raised an eyebrow.

“Sounds exhausting.”

Marcos nearly spilled his coffee.

Anna smiled and continued toward the counter.

Adrián pointed after her.

“See? She understands.”

Matías opened his notebook.

“I found something this week.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Let's hear it.”

“It's in John fifteen.”

Elías nodded.

“The vine and the branches.”

“Yes.”

Matías read.

“‘Remain in me, and I in you. As the branch can’t bear fruit by itself, unless it remains in the vine, so neither can you, unless you remain in me.’”

He paused.

Then he continued.

“‘I am the vine. You are the branches. He who remains in me, and I in him, the same bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.’”

Adrián smiled.

“Wow.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Read the part about the branch again.”

Matías did.

Marcos looked at him.

“The branch can’t bear fruit by itself.”

“Exactly.”

Adrián grinned.

“That's awesome.”

Elías nodded.
