“It's one of the clearest illustrations of dependence upon Christ in the New Testament.”

Matías smiled.

“I know you've taught this passage.”

“Several times.”

“Then let me ask you something.”

“Certainly.”

“What is the branch responsible for producing?”

Elías considered the wording.

“Christ says the branch bears fruit through abiding in the vine.”

“Right.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Yet the branch doesn't possess an independent source of life.”

“No.”

“Then the fruit comes from the life of the vine.”

“That's the imagery.”

Marcos sat back.

“That's amazing.”

Adrián looked toward him.

“What?”

“Think about what we've been discussing.”

He opened First John.

“John commands us to love one another.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And he tells us that love is of God.”

“Exactly.”

Marcos looked at John fifteen.

“Now Jesus says the branch cannot bear fruit of itself.”

Adrián's face brightened.

“Wow!”

Matías smiled broadly.

“That's what I've been thinking about all week.”

Elías looked at the passages.

“There's an important distinction between the imagery of the vine and John's direct commands concerning love.”

“Of course,” Matías replied.

“We shouldn't assume the two passages are interchangeable.”

“Agreed.”

Matías looked at him.

“Yet they both direct our attention to the source.”

Elías paused.

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“That's what keeps getting me.”

“What?” Elías asked.

“We're commanded to love.”

“Yes.”

“And love comes from God.”

“Yes.”

“And Jesus says we can't bear fruit apart from Him.”

“Correct.”

Adrián spread his hands.

“Then why do we keep going back to ourselves to produce what only comes from Him?”

Matías laughed.

“Amen!”

Marcos shook his head.

“That's a wonderful question.”

Elías looked at the open Bible.

He knew how to explain abiding.

He knew the debates surrounding the passage, the different interpretations of the branches, and the theological implications of fruitfulness.

Yet Adrián's question stayed with him.

*Why do we keep going back to ourselves?*

He thought of the list he had begun making after his conversation with his wife.

More time together.

More affection.

More attentive listening.

Regular evenings without distractions.

None of those things was wrong.

He still intended to do them.

Yet he wondered whether he had once again moved immediately from recognizing a need to organizing a response.

Matías was speaking.

“I think I've spent much of my Christian life trying to become what I believed I should be.”

Marcos nodded.

“I understand that.”

“And I assumed the answer was greater determination.”

Adrián smiled.

“You were certainly determined.”

Matías laughed.

“Yes, I was.”

“You could make determination nervous.”

They all laughed.

Matías shook his head.

“Thank you, Adrián.”

“Always happy to help.”

Matías looked back at John fifteen.

“I'm beginning to see something different.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

“Christ doesn't tell the branch to try harder to become fruitful.”

Elías nodded.

“He tells His disciples to abide in Him.”

“Exactly.”

Matías smiled.

“And the branch bears fruit.”

Adrián sat back.

“That's so cool.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“So if we're commanded to love, and love doesn't originate with us, the question becomes where we go for that love.”

Matías nodded.

“To Christ.”

“Amen,” Adrián said.

Elías looked at his friends.

Their excitement was contagious.

And yet he was quieter than usual.

Not because he disagreed.

Because something about their discoveries was beginning to reach beyond the explanations he had always given.

Later, as the breakfast plates were cleared, Adrián returned to Revelation two.

“I've been thinking about Ephesus again.”

Elías smiled.

“I suspected you might.”

“Christ tells them to remember.”

“Yes.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“Remember from where you've fallen.”

Matías nodded.

“Then repent.”

“And do the first works,” Marcos added.

Elías looked at the words.

He had explained those commands many times.

Remember.

Repent.

Do.

He had usually treated them as a straightforward sequence of spiritual correction.

Recognize the problem.

Change one's mind.

Correct the conduct.

There was nothing inherently wrong with that explanation.

Yet lately the first command had begun to trouble him.

*Remember.*

He thought of the photographs.

His wife's laughter.

Their little apartment.

The evenings when they had read Scripture together without an agenda.

He remembered the delight he had once experienced simply discovering something about Christ.

Then he remembered the years that followed.

He had studied diligently.

He had learned to distinguish sound doctrine from error.

He had become a dependable teacher.

People trusted his judgment.

He was grateful for those opportunities.

He still believed the truth mattered enormously.

Yet somewhere along the way, the way he approached Scripture had changed.

He had become increasingly concerned with organizing what he knew.

He remembered preparing lessons in which every passage had its proper place, every theological category was carefully distinguished, and every possible objection had an answer.

He had enjoyed that work.

He still did.

But he couldn't remember when he had last opened his Bible simply because he wanted to enjoy what it said about Christ.

“Elías?”

He looked up.

Adrián was watching him.

“Sorry. What did you say?”

“I asked what you think they needed to remember.”

Elías looked down at Revelation.

For once, he didn't answer immediately.

“The text says to remember from where they've fallen.”

“Yes.”

He paused.

“I've been thinking about that.”

Matías smiled.

“So have we.”

Elías looked at him.

“I wonder whether remembering sometimes takes longer than we allow.”

The others grew quiet.

Adrián leaned forward.

“What do you mean?”

Elías considered his words.

“We tend to recognize a problem and immediately look for a solution.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's familiar.”

“Yet Christ begins by telling them to remember.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“Before repent.”

“Yes.”

“And before doing the first works.”

“Exactly.”

Elías looked down.

“Perhaps there's something they need to see before they can meaningfully reconsider where they are.”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“That makes sense.”

Matías smiled.

“That's worth thinking about.”

Elías looked at his friends.

He had not intended to say quite so much.

And he wasn't ready to explain why the command had become personal.

Adrián grinned.

“See? This is why we keep you around.”

Elías laughed.

“For my exceptional insight?”

“For your Greek.”

Marcos shook his head.

“Some things never change.”

That evening, Elías returned to the box of photographs.

His wife had gone to visit a friend, and the house was quiet.

He spread several pictures across the kitchen table.

He found one taken during a Bible conference many years earlier.

He remembered the occasion clearly.

A speaker had been teaching from Galatians about the believer's freedom in Christ.

Elías had been deeply moved.

He remembered walking home afterward with his wife, talking excitedly about what they'd heard.

He remembered saying that grace was more wonderful than he'd ever imagined.

They had stayed awake until nearly midnight reading passages together.

He smiled.

Then he remembered another occasion.

Several years later, he had been asked to teach a class on Christian maturity.

He had prepared carefully.

His outline was thorough.

The students had appreciated it.

He remembered emphasizing the importance of discipline, responsibility, and doctrinal stability.

All worthwhile subjects.

He wondered when his emphasis had begun to change.

Not all at once.

There had been no deliberate decision to leave anything behind.

He had simply continued learning, organizing, teaching, and correcting.

Over time, he had become very good at it.

He thought about his marriage.

The early years had been full of affection and laughter.

Later came children, work, ministry responsibilities, and countless obligations.

He had responded by becoming increasingly organized.

He made sure things were done.

He kept his commitments.

He provided.

He taught.

He served.

And gradually, the fun-loving young man in the photographs had become someone who always seemed to have an explanation.

Elías sat back.

He was not prepared to conclude that his theological education had been a mistake.

It hadn't.

Nor did he believe that faithfulness and responsibility were somehow inferior virtues.

They weren't.

Yet he could no longer ignore the possibility that something had changed in the way he understood and lived those virtues.

His wife had experienced the consequences.

Now those consequences were beginning to challenge his beliefs.

He opened his Bible to Revelation two.

*Remember therefore from where you have fallen.*

He read the words several times.

Then he turned to First John.

*God is light, and in him is no darkness at all.*

He remained there for a long time.

He was remembering.

And some of what he remembered was beginning to hurt.

When his wife returned, she found him at the table.

“Still looking at photographs?”

He smiled.

“Yes.”

She came closer.

He held up the picture from the picnic.

“Do you remember this?”

She laughed.

“The sausages?”

“The equipment was defective.”

“Of course it was.”

He laughed with her.

Then he looked at the picture.

“I miss those days.”

She sat beside him.

“So do I.”

He turned toward her.

“I don't think I realized how much.”

She rested her head against his shoulder.

For a while, neither spoke.

Then she said quietly, “You know, Elías, I don't need you to become the young man in that picture again.”

He looked at her.

“I know.”

“I just miss being close to you.”

He nodded.

This time he didn't explain why life had become complicated.

He didn't tell her that mature love was different from youthful affection.

He simply put his arm around her.

And stayed there.

The following morning, Elías opened his Bible before breakfast.

He had no lesson to prepare.

No argument to settle.

No theological question he intended to resolve.

He turned to Second Corinthians eleven.

A phrase had been on his mind since the previous evening.

Paul's concern that believers might be led away from the simplicity and purity of devotion to Christ.

Elías read the passage slowly.

He knew its context.

He knew Paul's concern about deception and false apostles.

He had taught it before.

Yet this morning he found himself lingering over the thought of devotion to Christ.

He remembered when the Scriptures had first become precious to him.

He remembered the delight of discovering grace.

He remembered the freedom he had felt when he began to understand what Christ had accomplished.

He remembered sharing those discoveries with his wife.

He looked toward the kitchen, where she was making coffee.

He had spent years learning how to explain Christ accurately.

Now he was beginning to wonder whether he had allowed the explanations to become more familiar to him than the delight that had once accompanied them.

He wasn't certain.

And he wasn't ready to draw conclusions.

For now, he wanted to remember.

He closed his eyes briefly.

Then he opened the passage again.

There was no hurry.

For once, Elías was willing to let the words remain with him without immediately deciding what to do about them.

# Chapter 26
## From Where You Have Fallen

“Wait a minute.”

Adrián had been reading quietly for nearly ten minutes, which was unusual enough that Matías looked up immediately.

“What?”

“Listen to this.”

It was Thursday morning at Deb's Place. A cold wind had followed the Detroit River overnight, and the windows were fogged along their lower edges. Someone near the counter was complaining about the price of winter tires.

Marcos had arrived first. Matías came shortly afterward, carrying the notebook Adrián had been teasing him about. Elías was already seated when Adrián walked in, though he had hardly spoken beyond greeting his friends.

Adrián turned his Bible toward the center of the table.

“Revelation two, verse five.”

Matías leaned closer.

“Ephesus again?”

“Yes. I keep coming back to how serious this is.”

Marcos smiled.

“That's becoming a regular occurrence.”

“Isn't it wonderful?”

“Depends what we've missed.”

Adrián read aloud.

“‘Remember therefore from where you have fallen, and repent and do the first works;’”

He stopped.

Then he read it again.

Matías looked at him.

“What are you seeing?”

Adrián pointed to the page.

“Fallen.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“While they were still working?”

“Yes!”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Christ says they've fallen!”

Matías read the verse for himself.

“Wow.”

Marcos opened his Bible.

“That's stronger than saying they had something to improve.”

“Exactly!” Adrián said.

He turned back to the beginning of the letter.

“Look at everything Christ says about them. Their works. Their labor. Their perseverance. Their refusal to tolerate evil. Their testing of false apostles.”

Matías nodded.

“And they hadn't grown weary.”

“Right.”

Adrián looked up.

“Then Christ says they've fallen.”

Marcos stared at the passage.

“That's remarkable.”

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then Matías said, “They hadn't stopped working.”

“No.”

“They hadn't abandoned doctrinal discernment.”

“No.”

“They were still persevering.”

Adrián spread his hands.

“And they'd fallen!”

Marcos shook his head slowly.

“Wow.”

Elías was looking at the same verse.

He had read it many times.

He knew the wording.

He knew the grammatical construction.

He had explained it in lessons and conversations.

Yet this morning the word seemed to stand apart from everything surrounding it.

*Fallen.*

He thought of the photographs on his kitchen table.

He thought of his wife.

He said nothing.

Adrián looked toward him briefly, then returned to Matías.

“Here's what I'm wondering. How could a church be doing all those things and not recognize what had happened?”

Matías considered the question.

“Perhaps because those things were still happening.”

Marcos nodded.

“That would make it difficult.”

“Difficult to see what?” Adrián asked.

“That something had changed.”

Matías looked down at the passage.

“They could look at their labor and perseverance and see evidence that things were going well.”

“Because those things really were good,” Marcos added.

“Exactly.”

Adrián leaned back.

“And Christ saw something they weren't seeing.”

Elías lowered his eyes.

*Christ saw something they weren't seeing.*

He could not remember the last time a sentence spoken at this table had unsettled him so deeply.

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

“Who ordered the eggs over easy?”

“Me,” Marcos said.

She set the plate before him.

“And the omelet?”

“Here,” Elías replied.

She looked at Adrián.

“Club sandwich for breakfast?”

“I'm broadening my horizons.”

“It's breakfast.”

“A man has to start somewhere.”

She shook her head and placed the sandwich before him.

She set Matías’s traditional breakfast beside his mug.

Matías was laughing.

Anna looked at him.

“You've been in a good mood lately.”

“Have I?”

“Every time I come over here, you're laughing at something.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“That's because he's becoming fun-loving.”

Matías groaned.

“Not this again.”

Anna smiled.

“Good. The world could use a few more fun-loving people.”

She left the coffee pot on the table.

“Especially before nine in the morning.”

When she'd gone, Marcos looked at Matías.

“She has a point.”

“Don't encourage him.”

Adrián grinned.

“Too late.”

They ate for a while, occasionally returning to Revelation.

Elías listened.

He was grateful for the ordinary conversation, the laughter, the familiar sounds of the diner.

Yet he found himself unusually tired.

He hadn't slept well.

For several nights he'd been remembering conversations with his wife.

Some were small, almost insignificant moments.

A request to spend an evening together.

A disappointed expression when he'd chosen to finish preparing a lesson.

A conversation in which she'd tried to describe feeling distant from him.

He remembered his answers.

They had been sensible.

Sometimes they had been supported by Scripture.

He had believed them.

That was what troubled him most.

He hadn't knowingly dismissed her pain.

He had misunderstood it.

And his misunderstanding had allowed it to continue.

He looked toward his friends.

Adrián was laughing at something Marcos had said.

Matías had nearly spilled his coffee.

For a moment, Elías remembered the young man in the picnic photograph.

He had laughed like that once.

He still laughed, of course.

He wasn't an unhappy man.

Yet something about those earlier years had been different.

He couldn't quite identify it.

“There's something else,” Matías said.

They had finished eating, and he had returned to Revelation.

“Christ doesn't simply say they've fallen. He tells them to remember from where.”

Adrián nodded.

“Yes.”

“Why would they need to remember?”

Marcos thought for a moment.

“Because they might not recognize the difference between where they were and where they are.”

Matías smiled.

“That's what I was thinking.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“So remembering isn't simply thinking about the good old days.”

“No,” Matías said.

“It's recognizing something about their present condition.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“By remembering what has changed.”

Adrián smiled.

“That's awesome.”

Matías glanced at him.

“You've been saying that a lot.”

“Because you keep finding awesome things.”

Marcos laughed.

Elías looked at the verse.

*Remember therefore from where you have fallen.*

He had been remembering.

Yet the more he remembered, the less certain he became about what had happened.

His wife had told him she missed their closeness.

He remembered when they had been close.

He remembered how much they'd enjoyed one another.

Then he remembered years of increasing responsibility.

Work.

Children.

Church commitments.

Teaching.

Study.

Meetings.

All good things.

He had believed he was becoming a better husband.

More dependable.

More disciplined.

More responsible.

And somewhere in those years, his wife had begun feeling lonely.

He didn't know exactly when.

He wasn't certain why.

He only knew that the condition of their marriage was not what he had believed it to be.

“Elías?”

He looked up.

Marcos was watching him.

“Sorry?”

“I asked whether you wanted more coffee.”

“Oh.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Yes, thank you.”

Marcos poured from the pot Anna had left.

He didn't ask anything further.

Adrián was turning to another passage.

“There's something in Second Corinthians.”

Matías looked up.

“Where?”

“Chapter eleven.”

Elías felt a slight tightening in his chest.

He had been reading that chapter himself.

Adrián found verse three.

“Listen.”

He read slowly.

“‘But I fear, lest somehow, as the serpent deceived Eve by his craftiness, so your minds may be corrupted from the simplicity that is in Christ.’”

Matías leaned forward.

“Read that again.”

Adrián did.

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“The simplicity that is in Christ.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“That's beautiful.”

Matías smiled.

“Amen.”

Elías looked at the passage.

He knew that Paul was warning the Corinthians about deception and false teaching.

He knew the surrounding argument.

He knew that the verse could not simply be detached from its context and made to mean whatever someone wished.

Yet he was no longer eager to explain all of that.

He listened as his friends continued.

Adrián looked down at the verse.

“I wonder how complicated we've made the Christian life.”

“Paul is warning about deception,” Elías said. “What is drawing them away from Christ?”

Matías looked back at the verse.

“That's what I'm trying to understand about myself. I've certainly made it complicated.”

Marcos smiled.

“You?”

Matías laughed.

“I had a plan for everything.”

Adrián grinned.

“You probably had a plan for your plans.”

“Usually.”

They laughed.

Matías grew thoughtful.

“I thought spiritual maturity meant becoming increasingly capable of doing what God required.”

Marcos nodded.

“That sounds familiar.”

“And when I failed, I assumed I needed more determination.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Then Galatia happened.”

Matías smiled.

“Yes.”

He paused.

“And then Argentina.”

The table grew quiet.

Matías looked down at his coffee.

“When my mother was dying, there wasn't anything I could do to make the situation right.”

Marcos nodded.

“I remember.”

“I couldn't produce peace by trying harder.”

He looked at his friends.

“I had to trust what Christ had accomplished.”

Adrián smiled.

“Amen.”

Matías continued.

“And now I'm beginning to wonder how much of my Christian life I've spent trying to produce what I was supposed to receive from Him.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“That's a big question.”

“Yes.”

Adrián looked delighted.

“That's an incredible question.”

Elías watched them.

He remembered teaching about the sufficiency of Christ.

He had taught it accurately.

He believed it.

Yet Matías was speaking about something that had become deeply personal through his mother's death.

And Elías found himself wondering whether he had always treated the sufficiency of Christ primarily as a doctrine to defend.

He didn't know.

He wasn't ready to decide.

He simply listened.

The conversation turned to First John.

Marcos had been reading the letter again.

“I keep coming back to chapter one.”

Adrián smiled.

“God is light.”

“Yes.”

“And in Him is no darkness at all,” Matías added.

Marcos nodded.

“That's the message John announces.”

He looked at his notebook.

“Then he begins examining claims about fellowship.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Right.”

Marcos continued.

“If someone claims fellowship with God while walking in darkness, John says the claim isn't true.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And if we walk in the light, there's fellowship.”

Adrián nodded.

Marcos looked around the table.

“Then chapter two brings in love.”

He read verses nine and ten.

When he finished, Matías smiled.

“Wow.”

Adrián laughed.

“There's that word again.”

“It's appropriate.”

Marcos was still looking at the passage.

“John doesn't give us a comfortable middle category.”

“Light or darkness,” Adrián said.

“And he connects love with abiding in the light.”

Matías nodded.

“Then chapter four tells us where love comes from.”

Adrián turned there.

“‘Love is of God.’”

“Amen,” Marcos said.

Adrián continued.

“‘We love because He first loved us.’”

Matías leaned back.

“That's amazing.”

For a few moments, they sat enjoying the connection.

Then Marcos said, “Here's what I'm wondering.”

Adrián grinned.

“I love those words.”

Marcos smiled.

“John commands believers to love.”

“Yes.”

“And he tells us that love comes from God.”

“Right.”

“So if the command is real, and the love doesn't originate with us, what are we supposed to do?”

Matías looked at him.

Adrián leaned forward.

Marcos continued.

“Where do we go?”

Matías smiled.

“To Christ.”

Adrián slapped the table lightly.

“Amen!”

Anna looked over from the counter.

“Everything all right?”

“Wonderful!” Adrián called.

She shook her head.

“You're going to frighten the other customers.”

“Sorry!”

Matías laughed.

Marcos was smiling broadly.

Elías smiled too.

He had taught those passages.

He had explained the commands to love.

He had explained that love comes from God.

Yet he was beginning to recognize how quickly he had often moved from the command to the believer's responsibility.

He hadn't denied the source.

He had simply emphasized the response.

He thought of his wife.

When she told him she was lonely, he had immediately begun considering what he should do.

He had made a list.

He had wanted to become more attentive, more affectionate, more available.

Those were reasonable desires.

Yet now he wondered whether he had been approaching love as another responsibility to fulfill.

He looked down at First John.

*Love is of God.*

He remained quiet.

When the men finally gathered their things, Adrián noticed Elías had hardly touched his second cup of coffee.

“You're quiet today.”

Elías smiled.

“I suppose I am.”

“Everything okay?”

Elías considered the question.

“Yes,” he said. “I'm just thinking about some things.”

Adrián nodded.

“Good things?”

Elías looked at him.

“Important things.”

Adrián smiled.

“Well, don't think too hard. You'll hurt yourself.”

Elías laughed.

“I'll try to be careful.”

They walked outside together.

The wind had eased, and the morning sun was reflecting off the windows across the street.

Matías stopped beside Adrián.

“You know, you've changed.”

Adrián looked surprised.

“Me?”

“Yes.”

“How?”

Matías smiled.

“You seem less interested in proving something and more interested in discovering something.”

Adrián considered that.

Then he grinned.

“Maybe I'm becoming fun-loving.”

Matías laughed.

“Perhaps.”

Marcos shook his head.

“We're never going to hear the end of that expression.”

“Not a chance,” Adrián said.

Elías stood beside them, smiling.

He enjoyed their friendship.

He was glad they were finding such joy in Scripture.

Yet he couldn't escape the feeling that something important was happening among them.

Something he wasn't fully sharing.

Not because they were excluding him.

They weren't.

They welcomed every contribution he made.

They valued his knowledge.

They loved him.

And perhaps that made the feeling more difficult to explain.

He had spent years helping others understand Scripture.

Now his friends were discovering things that seemed to fill them with delight, and he was struggling to understand why those same passages had become so familiar to him without producing the same response.

He wasn't resentful.

He was uncertain.

And uncertainty was not a condition he was accustomed to.

That evening, Elías found his wife sitting by the living room window.

“Mind if I join you?”

She looked up from her book.

“Of course.”

He sat beside her.

For a while, neither spoke.

Then she said, “I've been thinking about something.”

Elías turned toward her.

“What's that?”

“Do you remember when we used to go for walks after dinner?”

He smiled.

“Yes. We walked almost every evening.”

“Even when it was cold.”

“You insisted.”

She laughed.

“I did not.”

“You certainly did.”

“Well, perhaps occasionally.”

He smiled.

She looked toward the window.

“I used to look forward to those walks all day.”

“Really?”

“Yes.”

He waited.

“Sometimes we'd talk about Scripture. Sometimes about the children. Sometimes about absolutely nothing.”

She smiled at the memory.

“You were funny, Elías.”

“Were?”

She laughed.

“There you are.”

He grinned.

Then her smile faded a little.

“I missed that.”

Elías looked at her.

“The walks?”

She shook her head.

“You.”

He felt the familiar impulse to explain.

Life had become busy. Their responsibilities had increased. There had been good reasons why those evenings gradually disappeared.

He knew all of them.

And for the first time, he realized she probably knew them too.

He remained quiet.

She continued.

“I remember asking you once if we could start walking again.”

He nodded slowly.

“I remember.”

“You told me evenings weren't practical anymore.”

“Yes.”

“You explained that we were entering a different season of life.”

He remembered those words.

They had sounded reasonable at the time.

“I think I even agreed with you,” she said.

Elías looked at her.

“Did you want to?”

She considered the question.

“No.”

He waited.

“I just didn't know what else to say.”

Something tightened in his chest.

She looked down at her hands.

“You always had such good reasons.”

Elías took a breath.

“I thought I was being sensible.”

“I know.”

She looked at him.

“That's what made it difficult.”

He didn't answer.

“Sometimes I wanted to tell you that I didn't need another explanation. I just wanted my husband to want to spend time with me.”

Elías lowered his eyes.

He remembered that evening now.

Not every word.

Enough.

He had been preparing a lesson. She had appeared in the doorway and suggested they go for a walk.

He had explained that he needed to finish.

She had said she understood.

He remembered feeling grateful that she was so reasonable.

He had never considered what it might have cost her to say those words.

“I'm sorry,” he said quietly.

She nodded.

“I know.”

He looked at her.

“I remember that evening.”

She seemed surprised.

“You do?”

“Yes.”

He paused.

“I remember thinking how understanding you were.”

Her eyes filled.

“I was trying to be.”

Elías reached for her hand.

She took it.

For a while they sat quietly.

Then she said, “I don't want you to feel that everything you've done was wrong.”

“I know.”

“You've been a good husband in so many ways.”

He looked at her.

“Thank you.”

“I just wanted you to know there were things I was missing.”

He nodded.

“I'm beginning to understand.”

She smiled faintly.

“Are you?”

Elías considered the question.

He could have reassured her.

He could have explained what he'd learned about communication, companionship, and the importance of affection in marriage.

Instead, he said, “Not nearly as much as I thought.”

She looked at him for a long moment.

Then she leaned against his shoulder.

He put his arm around her.

After a while she said, “You know, I don't even need us to go walking every evening.”

He smiled.

“No?”

“No.”

She looked up at him.

“I just want us to enjoy being together again.”

Elías nodded.

He understood the words.

He wasn't yet certain he understood everything behind them.

So he stayed beside her.

And listened when she began telling him another memory.

Later, Elías returned to his study.

He opened his Bible to Revelation two.

He read the commendations.

The works.

The labor.

The perseverance.

The discernment.

Then the words that had occupied his thoughts all week.

*But I have this against you, that you left your first love.*

And then:

*Remember therefore from where you have fallen.*

Elías sat back.

He had always regarded Ephesus as an admirable church with a serious problem.

Now he was beginning to wonder whether that description had allowed him to underestimate what Christ was saying.

Christ hadn't merely identified an area needing improvement.

He had told them they had fallen.

Elías looked at the passage again.

The Ephesians had continued doing many things Christ approved.

Yet something essential had been left behind.

He thought of his marriage.

He had continued doing many things a faithful husband should do.

Yet his wife had been lonely for years.

He wasn't prepared to equate the two situations.

He knew the comparison had limitations.

Still, the resemblance troubled him.

He had measured the health of his marriage by the things he was doing.

He had measured the health of Ephesus by the things Christ commended.

And in both cases, something important had escaped his attention.

He looked at the word again.

*Fallen.*

He had not expected it to describe a church like Ephesus.

He certainly had not expected it to make him think about himself.

He closed his Bible.

Then he opened it again.

For a long time he sat without writing anything.

He was beginning to understand why Christ had commanded the Ephesians to remember.

And he was discovering how difficult remembering could be when the things one had left behind were hidden beneath so many things one had continued to do.

He wasn't ready to change his mind.

He wasn't even certain what needed to change.

Yet he could no longer return comfortably to the explanations that had once satisfied him.

Something had shifted.

Elías let the uncertainty remain.

# Chapter 27
## Nothing Left to Prove

The first snow hadn't arrived, although Windsor seemed to be preparing for it.

A hard wind moved along the streets, pushing the last brown leaves against curbs and storefronts. The sky was pale and low, and the windows at Deb's Place had begun collecting little clouds of condensation around their edges.

Adrián arrived with his collar turned up and his hands buried in his pockets.

“It's freezing!”

Matías looked up from the table.

“It's November.”

“Thank you. I wasn't sure.”

“You're welcome.”

Adrián dropped into his chair.

“I think the wind came directly from the North Pole.”

Marcos smiled.

“It's supposed to get colder next week.”

“Wonderful. Something to look forward to.”

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“Coffee?”

“Immediately,” Adrián said. “Before I become a permanent part of the furniture.”

She laughed and filled his cup.

“Where's your coat?”

“On the chair.”

“Then perhaps you should try wearing it outside.”

Matías laughed.

Adrián looked at him.

“Everybody's a comedian this morning.”

Anna moved around the table, filling cups.

Elías sat beside Marcos, his Bible open in front of him.

“Coffee, Elías?”

He didn't answer.

Anna waited.

“Elías?”

He looked up.

“Oh. Sorry.”

“Coffee?”

He glanced at his cup.

“Perhaps later.”

“All right.”

She moved toward the counter.

Adrián was already opening his Bible.

“I've got something.”

Matías smiled.

“Of course you do.”

“No, seriously. I've been thinking about what we read in John fifteen.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“The vine?”

“Yes. And what you said about the branch.”

Matías nodded.

“That it cannot bear fruit of itself.”

“Exactly.”

Adrián looked down.

“And then I started thinking about something Paul says in Colossians.”

He found the passage.

“Chapter two, verse ten.”

He read slowly.

“‘And you are complete in Him, who is the head of all principality and power.’”

He looked up.

“Complete in Him.”

Matías smiled.

“Amen.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Read it again.”

Adrián did.

“‘You are complete in Him.’”

Marcos shook his head.

“Wow.”

Adrián grinned.

“Isn't that amazing?”

Matías looked down at the passage.

“The completeness is in Christ.”

“Yes!”

“Not something we're gradually manufacturing.”

“Exactly.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Although we're certainly still growing.”

“Of course,” Matías said.

He pointed to the words.

“Yet our growth doesn't make Christ's work more complete.”

Adrián sat back.

“That's awesome.”

Elías looked at the passage.

He knew it.

He had taught Colossians two.

He knew the context concerned the sufficiency of Christ against teachings that would persuade believers they needed something additional.

He could have explained it.

He didn't.

Matías was still looking at the verse.

“There's something here that I don't think I appreciated before.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

“When I was studying Galatia, I kept thinking about what Paul said in chapter three.”

Adrián nodded.

“Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?”

“Yes.”

Matías smiled.

“I understood the argument. At least I thought I did.”

“And now?” Marcos asked.

“Now I'm beginning to see how much of my Christian life was built around trying to become complete.”

He tapped the passage.

“And Paul says we're complete in Christ.”

Adrián looked delighted.

“Wow!”

Matías laughed.

“I know.”

“No, think about that!”

“I am thinking about it.”

“Then think louder!”

Marcos burst out laughing.

Even Elías smiled.

Anna returned with their breakfasts.

“Eggs and toast?”

“Here,” Matías said.

“Omelet?”

Marcos raised his hand.

“French toast?”

Adrián grinned.

“You remembered.”

“You finally ordered breakfast.”

She placed the plate in front of him.

Then she looked toward Elías.

“Your oatmeal.”

He looked up.

“Thank you.”

She set it down.

He didn't reach for it.

Anna hesitated, then continued toward the counter.

Marcos was turning to another passage.

“Listen to this.”

He found Hebrews ten.

“Verse fourteen.”

He read aloud.

“‘For by one offering He has perfected forever those who are being sanctified.’”

Matías stopped eating.

“Read that again.”

Marcos smiled.

“‘For by one offering He has perfected forever those who are being sanctified.’”

Adrián stared at him.

“Forever?”

“That's what it says.”

“By one offering?”

“Yes.”

Matías leaned forward.

“That's incredible.”

Marcos looked down at the verse.

“Notice that it speaks of those who are being sanctified.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“Does ‘being sanctified’ mean the growth we were just discussing?”

“We need to look at how Hebrews uses it,” Marcos said.

“Yet the offering has already accomplished something definitive.”

Marcos smiled.

“Exactly.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Wow.”

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then Matías laughed softly.

“What?” Adrián asked.

“I've spent years thinking that if I could just become disciplined enough, obedient enough, and consistent enough, I'd finally arrive somewhere.”

Marcos nodded.

“And now?”

Matías looked at Hebrews.

“Christ has accomplished something I could never accomplish.”

Adrián smiled broadly.

“Amen!”

Marcos turned to another passage.

“Romans eight.”

Matías looked up.

“Oh, I love this one.”

“Verse one.”

Marcos read.

“‘There is therefore now no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus, who don’t walk according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit.’”

Matías sat back.

His expression changed.

Adrián noticed.

“What?”

Matías looked at him.

“I held on to that verse when I was in Argentina.”

The table grew quiet.

“After my mother died, there were moments when I felt I should have done more. Been there sooner. Called more often.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's understandable.”

“I know.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“And I kept coming back to what Christ had accomplished.”

He paused.

“I couldn't change what had happened. I couldn't make myself into the perfect son.”

Adrián listened.

“Yet my standing before God wasn't resting on how well I'd handled everything.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“It was resting on Christ.”

Marcos nodded.

“Amen.”

Adrián reached across the table and squeezed his friend's shoulder.

Matías laughed.

“Don't start.”

“Too late.”

They smiled at one another.

Elías watched quietly.

He remembered when Matías had stormed out of the diner during their discussions of Galatia.

He remembered the determination in his voice.

The frustration.

The insistence that obedience must count for something.

Now Matías was speaking about Christ with a freedom Elías had rarely heard from him.

And he was laughing.

Not nervously.

Not to cover embarrassment.

He was simply enjoying himself.

Elías looked down at his oatmeal.

It had gone cold.

Anna approached Adrián while the others were comparing passages.

She leaned slightly toward him.

“Can I ask you something?”

“Of course.”

She glanced toward Elías.

“Is he okay?”

Adrián followed her eyes.

Elías was looking out the window.

“I think so. Why?”

“He's usually very friendly.”

“He is.”

“He hardly said anything when I asked about coffee. He thanked me for breakfast, but he hasn't touched it.”

Adrián looked at his friend again.

“He's had some things on his mind.”

Anna nodded.

“I figured.”

She lowered her voice.

“He said ‘perhaps later’ about coffee. Would you check whether he'd like some now?”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“I'll ask him.”

“Thanks.”

She moved away.

Adrián turned toward Elías.

“Elías?”

His friend looked up.

“Yes?”

“You okay?”

Elías smiled.

“Yes. Just a little distracted.”

“Anna was wondering whether you wanted coffee.”

Elías glanced at his cup.

“Oh.”

He looked toward the counter.

“Tell her I'm sorry. Yes, I'd like some.”

Adrián nodded.

“Everything all right?”

Elías hesitated.

“I'm thinking through some things.”

Adrián studied him for a moment.

Then he smiled.

“Well, you're in good company.”

Elías gave a small laugh.

“Yes. I suppose I am.”

Adrián didn't press him.

He signaled Anna for coffee.

Then he returned to the conversation.

Marcos had found another passage.

“Second Corinthians five.”

Matías looked up.

“Which verse?”

“Twenty-one.”

Marcos read.

“‘For He made Him who knew no sin to be sin for us, that we might become the righteousness of God in Him.’”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Wow.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“That's extraordinary.”

Marcos looked at him.

“Think about what Paul is saying.”

Matías smiled.

“I am.”

“No, I mean—”

Adrián laughed.

“Now you're doing it!”

Marcos grinned.

“All right. Think louder.”

They laughed.

Then Marcos continued.

“Christ was made sin for us. And in Him we become the righteousness of God.”

Matías looked down.

“Not a righteousness we establish ourselves.”

“No.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Romans ten.”

Matías nodded.

“Exactly.”

He turned there.

“Paul says Israel had zeal for God, yet sought to establish their own righteousness rather than submit to God's righteousness.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Zeal wasn't the problem by itself.”

“No,” Matías replied. “Paul acknowledges their zeal.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Then the question is where their confidence was resting.”

Matías nodded.

“Yes.”

Marcos looked at Colossians again.

“And here Paul says we're complete in Christ.”

Adrián smiled.

“It's everywhere!”

Matías laughed.

“What is?”

“Christ!”

Marcos grinned.

“Well, that is rather the point.”

Adrián shook his head.

“No, I mean we keep finding the same thing in different places.”

He gestured toward their open Bibles.

“Galatians. Romans. Colossians. Hebrews. First John. John fifteen.”

Matías nodded.

“Different passages. Different questions.”

“And the same Person,” Marcos said.

Adrián sat back.

“That's awesome.”

For a few moments, the three men simply enjoyed what they were seeing.

Then Matías looked at Marcos.

“You know something?”

“What?”

“I think we're beginning to experience the same thing.”

Marcos smiled.

“What do you mean?”

“You've been talking about how much you relied on accomplishment.”

Marcos nodded.

“And I've been talking about how much I relied on discipline.”

Adrián laughed.

“And I had my own ideas about spiritual maturity.”

Matías smiled.

“Yes, you did.”

“Thank you.”

“But look at us now.”

Marcos glanced around the table.

Matías continued.

“We came from different places. We had different ideas about what made us spiritual.”

Adrián nodded.

“And now we're all getting excited about the same thing.”

Marcos smiled.

“Christ.”

“Amen,” Matías said.

Adrián raised his coffee and caught Elías’s eye.

“To the One who did what we couldn't.”

Marcos lifted his cup.

Matías joined him.

Their mugs touched.

Elías watched.

He smiled politely.

Then he looked back toward the window.

Outside, a few small flakes of snow had begun drifting through the air.

Adrián noticed first.

“Look!”

Matías turned.

“Snow.”

“Already?”

Marcos laughed.

“It's nearly December.”

Adrián shook his head.

“I refuse to accept this.”

“You can file a complaint,” Matías said.

“With whom?”

“Environment Canada.”

“Do they accept theological objections?”

“Probably not.”

Marcos laughed.

Elías smiled.

He was glad to hear them laughing.

He was glad they were enjoying Scripture.

Yet he felt strangely removed from the conversation.

He knew the passages they were reading.

He knew the doctrines they were discussing.

He believed Christ's work was sufficient.

He believed justification was by grace.

He believed the believer's completeness was in Christ.

He had defended those truths for years.

And still, he couldn't shake the feeling that something was missing.

His friends were speaking about freedom.

He was remembering the loneliness of his wife.

He didn't know how to explain the difference.

And for once, he wasn't certain he wanted to try.

When they finally gathered their things, Adrián noticed Elías had barely eaten.

“Not hungry?”

Elías looked at his bowl.

“Apparently not.”

“You sure you're okay?”

“Yes.”

Adrián waited.

Elías smiled faintly.

“I've been remembering some things.”

Adrián nodded.

“Good memories?”

“Some.”

He looked down.

“Some difficult ones.”

Adrián rested a hand on his shoulder.

“Well, if you want to talk, we're here.”

Elías looked at him.

“Thank you.”

“I mean it.”

“I know.”

They walked toward the door.

Anna was wiping down a nearby table.

“Have a good week, gentlemen.”

“You too,” Matías said.

She looked at Elías.

“Take care of yourself.”

He smiled.

“Thank you, Anna.”

Outside, the snow had stopped almost as soon as it began.

The pavement remained wet and dark.

Marcos pulled his coat tighter.

“Next Thursday?”

“Absolutely,” Adrián said.

Matías nodded.

“I wouldn't miss it.”

Elías looked at his friends.

“I'll be here.”

They said goodbye.

Adrián and Matías walked toward their cars, still talking about Hebrews ten.

Marcos joined them, pointing out another connection he'd noticed.

Their voices carried across the parking lot.

Elías stood beside his car for a moment.

He watched them laughing.

Then he opened the door and sat behind the wheel.

He didn't start the engine immediately.

He thought about the morning.

His friends had been so excited.

And he understood why.

Everything they'd read was true.

Christ's work was finished.

The believer was complete in Him.

The offering had accomplished what no human effort could accomplish.

Elías knew those things.

He had known them for years.

Yet he couldn't remember when he'd last felt the freedom his friends were describing.

He looked at the Bible on the passenger seat.

Then he thought of his wife.

Her words returned to him.

*I just want us to enjoy being together again.*

He closed his eyes briefly.

He had spent years trying to be the kind of husband he believed he should be.

And she had spent years feeling lonely.

He had spent years teaching the sufficiency of Christ.

And now three friends were discovering a freedom in that sufficiency that he struggled to recognize in his own experience.

He didn't know what to do with those two realities.

He wasn't ready to draw conclusions.

He wasn't even certain what question he should ask.

For now, all he knew was that the explanations which had once made him feel so secure no longer seemed sufficient.

He started the engine.

The heater came on slowly.

Outside, the wind lifted a few remaining leaves from the pavement.

Winter was coming.

And Elías, who had spent so many years helping others find their way through Scripture, was beginning to realize how uncertain he had become about his own.

# Chapter 28
## Whose Church Is It?

Elías had never dreaded a church meeting before.

He had attended hundreds of them. Some had been tedious, others encouraging, and a few had continued so long that he wondered whether the participants intended to remain until the Lord returned.

Generally, he enjoyed them.

He liked serious discussion. He appreciated careful preparation. And he respected men who took responsibility for the teaching and well-being of a congregation.

This Wednesday evening was different.

A young man named Daniel had been asked to attend a special meeting with several elders and teachers.

Elías had known him for almost six years.

Daniel was twenty-five, married, and the father of two small children. He taught an occasional adult Bible class, helped with the sound system, and had a habit of staying afterward to ask questions.

Sometimes difficult questions.

Elías had always liked that about him.

The trouble had begun with First John.

Daniel had been preparing a lesson on fellowship when he noticed something that didn't seem to fit the church's established teaching.

He had asked whether First John 1:9 actually taught that a Christian who sinned lost fellowship with God until he confessed that particular sin.

It was a question Elías had answered many times.

The church distinguished between positional union with Christ and experiential fellowship with God. Sin interrupted the latter. Confession restored it.

The distinction was familiar.

It was carefully explained in their teaching materials.

Daniel wasn't convinced that the author was making that distinction in the passage.

And now they were meeting to discuss it.

Elías took his seat near the end of the conference table.

Seven men were present.

A thick memorandum had been distributed.

The heading read:

**THE CONDITIONAL STRUCTURE OF FIRST JOHN 1:9 AND THE RESTORATION OF EXPERIENTIAL FELLOWSHIP**

Elías looked at the title.

Ordinarily, he would have appreciated its precision.

Tonight he found himself wondering whether anyone had asked Daniel what had led him to the question.

The meeting began with prayer.

Then one of the elders, Harold, opened his folder.

“We've asked Daniel to join us because of concerns arising from his recent Bible-study discussions.”

Daniel sat quietly.

His Bible was open.

Harold continued.

“Our established position is that confession of known sin is necessary for the restoration of fellowship following a believer's departure from walking in the light.”

Daniel nodded.

“I understand that's our position.”

“Good.”

Harold adjusted his glasses.

“The difficulty is that you've continued questioning it after receiving instruction.”

Daniel looked down at his Bible.

“Yes. I still have questions.”

Daniel looked back at the passage. He read aloud from chapter one, verse five, through chapter two, verse two.

“‘This is the message which we have heard from him and announce to you, that God is light, and in him is no darkness at all.’”

“‘If we say that we have fellowship with him and walk in the darkness, we lie, and don’t tell the truth.’”

“‘But if we walk in the light, as he is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus Christ, his Son, cleanses us from all sin.’”

“‘If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us.’”

“‘If we confess our sins, he is faithful and righteous to forgive us the sins, and to cleanse us from all unrighteousness.’”

“‘If we say that we haven’t sinned, we make him a liar, and his word is not in us.’”

“‘My little children, I write these things to you so that you may not sin. If anyone sins, we have a Counselor with the Father, Jesus Christ, the righteous.’”

“‘And he is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not for ours only, but also for the whole world.’”

Another elder, Peter, leaned forward.

“Perhaps we should begin with the grammatical issue.”

He opened the memorandum.

“Verse nine states a condition and a result. The question is whether the conditional construction is procedural, establishing the prerequisite for restoration, or descriptive of the believer's proper acknowledgment of sin.”

Elías followed the argument easily.

Daniel nodded.

“That's part of what I'm asking.”

Peter continued.

“The traditional distinction between judicial forgiveness and parental forgiveness resolves the apparent tension between the believer's settled standing in Christ and the necessity of maintaining experiential communion.”

Daniel waited until he finished.

“Could I ask something?”

“Certainly.”

“Where does the author make that distinction in this passage?”

Peter looked at him.

“It's a theological distinction drawn from the broader teaching of Scripture.”

“I understand.”

Daniel turned a page.

“Which passages establish it? I'm asking whether we might be reading that distinction into the author's argument rather than drawing it from what he's saying.”

The room grew quiet.

Harold folded his hands.

“Daniel, we've discussed this before.”

“Yes.”

“And we've explained why the distinction is necessary.”

Daniel looked at him.

“I know. I'm still trying to understand the passage.”

Elías watched him.

There was no anger in Daniel's voice.

He seemed tired.

Another elder leaned forward.

“You are denying that Christians should confess their sins! You're changing sanctification altogether. This is heresy!”

Daniel looked startled.

“No. I'm not denying confession.”

“Then what are you doing?”

“I'm asking whether the passage teaches that confession is the means by which a believer re-enters fellowship after every sin.”

Peter adjusted his glasses.

“Then you're questioning the relationship between positional union and experiential communion.”

“I'm questioning whether that's what the author is explaining here.”

The elder shook his head.

“You're undermining an essential aspect of sanctification.”

Daniel looked down at his Bible.

“I don't want to undermine anything Scripture teaches.”

He paused.

“I want to understand what it teaches.”

Elías looked at him.

The young man was struggling to remain composed.

And for the first time, Elías noticed something about the conversation that troubled him more than the disagreement itself.

They had explained the distinction. He still hadn't heard them show Daniel where John made it.

The discussion continued for another twenty minutes.

They examined the conditional clauses.

They compared interpretations of the present tense.

They discussed the relationship between fellowship, confession, cleansing, and forgiveness.

They considered whether the author's first-person plural included believers, false claimants, or both within the sequence of hypothetical statements.

Elías knew the arguments on both sides.

He could have contributed substantially.

He said very little.

Then Harold closed his folder.

“I think we've reached the central issue.”

Daniel looked up.

Harold continued.

“We cannot have teachers introducing uncertainty concerning doctrines the church has already examined and established.”

Daniel sat very still.

“I'm not teaching against the church.”

“You've raised questions in your Bible class.”

“Yes.”

“And those questions have caused confusion.”

Daniel looked troubled.

“One of the men asked me whether confession was necessary to restore fellowship. I told him I was studying the passage because I wasn't certain our explanation accounted for everything the author says.”

Peter leaned forward.

“Do you see how that could undermine confidence in our teaching?”

Daniel hesitated.

“I suppose it could make people examine it.”

A few men shifted in their chairs.

Harold's expression tightened.

“This is a direct shot at our authority as church leaders. You're trying to sway others against us.”

Daniel stared at him.

“I'm not trying to turn anyone against you.”

“Then why are you continuing to question what we've taught?”

“Because I'm still trying to understand the passage.”

Peter closed his memorandum.

“Our doctrine is settled on the matter.”

Daniel looked at him.

“I know.”

“Then why can't you accept it?”

Daniel glanced at his Bible.

“Because I'm not yet convinced that's what the author is saying.”

Harold waited a moment.

“We cannot allow every established doctrine to be reopened indefinitely.”

Daniel nodded slowly.

“I understand the concern.”

“Then are you prepared to affirm our position?”

Daniel looked at his Bible.

“I'd like to keep studying it.”

The room was silent.

Finally Peter spoke.

“Then we may need to suspend your teaching responsibilities.”

Daniel nodded.

“I understand.”

Harold continued.

“And if you persist in promoting uncertainty about our doctrine, we may need to consider whether you can remain in fellowship with this congregation.”

Elías looked up sharply.

Daniel's face changed.

He hadn't expected that.

“Remain in fellowship?”

Harold spoke firmly.

“We have a responsibility to protect the church.”

Elías looked around the table.

Then he looked at Daniel.

“May I ask something?”

Harold turned toward him.

“Of course.”

Elías hesitated.

He had a dozen theological questions he could have asked.

Instead, he looked at Daniel.

“Have we understood what you're asking?”

Daniel seemed surprised.

“I hope so.”

Elías looked toward Harold.

“I'm not certain we have.”

Peter frowned.

“What do you mean?”

Elías looked down at his Bible.

“I've heard Daniel say several times that he isn't rejecting confession. He's questioning whether our explanation of its relationship to fellowship comes from this passage.”

Harold nodded.

“Our doctrine is settled on the matter.”

Elías was quiet.

Then he asked, “Are we considering removing him from fellowship because he's asking us to examine our interpretation?”

Peter leaned forward.

“We're considering the danger of doctrinal instability and insubordination to the elders.”

Elías looked at him.

“Insubordination?”

“Yes. Daniel has been instructed concerning our doctrinal position, and he continues questioning it.”

Elías turned toward Daniel.

The young man looked bewildered.

He had come to ask about Scripture.

Now he was being accused of rebellion.

Elías felt something tighten in his chest.

He looked back at the elders.

“Isn't he part of the fellowship we're trying to protect?”

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Harold sighed.

“Elías, you know how seriously Scripture treats false teaching.”

“I do.”

“You've taught us that yourself.”

Elías lowered his eyes.

“Yes,” he said quietly. “I have.”

The words troubled him more than Harold could have known.

Then Peter spoke.

“Elías, are you suggesting that Daniel's position is acceptable?”

Elías looked up.

“No. I haven't reached a conclusion about his interpretation.”

“Then why are you defending it?”

“I'm not defending his interpretation.”

He paused.

“I'm asking whether we've understood his question.”

Peter exchanged a glance with Harold.

It was brief.

Elías noticed.

Harold leaned back.

“Perhaps we should discuss your concerns separately.”

Elías felt the words settle over him.

Separately.

He knew what that meant.

He had attended enough meetings to recognize the shift.

A moment earlier, he had been one of the men responsible for examining a doctrinal concern.

Now he was beginning to wonder whether he would be the next concern.

“Of course,” he replied quietly.

Harold nodded.

“We can arrange that.”

Elías looked down at the table.

For years, he had been respected for his doctrinal precision.

He had helped develop teaching materials.

He had defended the church's positions.

Now, simply asking whether they had understood a brother's question seemed enough to place his own loyalty under suspicion.

Harold looked toward Daniel.

“We're not making a final decision tonight.”

Daniel nodded.

“We'll give you time to consider what we've discussed.”

“Thank you.”

Daniel gathered his Bible and papers.

He looked around the table.

“Thank you for meeting with me.”

Nobody seemed quite certain how to respond.

Harold nodded.

“We'll be in touch.”

Daniel left.

The door closed quietly behind him.

For several moments, nobody spoke.

Then Peter exhaled.

“Well, that was difficult.”

Harold nodded.

“I don't enjoy meetings like this.”

“Neither do I,” another elder said. “Daniel's a good young man.”

“He is,” Harold agreed. “That's what makes this so unfortunate.”

Elías looked toward the closed door.

Peter began collecting the memoranda.

“I think we needed to be firm. If we allow this kind of questioning to continue in the classes, people won't know what to believe.”

Harold nodded.

“Exactly. We've spent years establishing a consistent doctrinal foundation. We can't have individual teachers introducing uncertainty whenever they encounter a difficulty.”

“Especially when we've already addressed it,” another man added.

Peter placed the papers into his folder.

“I think Daniel understands now that we're serious.”

Elías looked up.

“What do you mean?”

“He knows there are consequences if he continues.”

Harold nodded thoughtfully.

“And that may be enough. He might decide it's wiser to leave the question alone.”

One of the elders smiled faintly.

“I suspect the others will think twice before bringing these matters into their classes.”

There were a few nods around the table.

“That's probably a good thing,” Peter said. “We need stability.”

Elías looked from one man to another.

He knew them.

He knew their devotion to the congregation. He knew the hours they spent visiting the sick, preparing lessons, counselling families, and helping people through difficult circumstances.

They were relieved.

They believed a threat to the church's doctrinal stability had been contained.

And perhaps Daniel would stop asking questions.

Perhaps the others would too.

The room would grow quieter.

Elías looked at the memorandum.

He wondered what the silence would mean.

Harold turned toward him.

“Elías, I appreciate your concern for Daniel.”

Elías looked up.

“Thank you.”

“Still, I was surprised by your questions tonight.”

Elías said nothing.

“You've always understood the importance of maintaining doctrinal consistency.”

“I still do.”

“Then I hope you'll appreciate why we can't allow this to continue.”

Elías looked at him.

“I understand your concern.”

Harold waited.

Elías didn't add anything.

Peter leaned forward.

“Do you agree with our doctrinal position?”

Elías hesitated.

He had affirmed it for years.

He had taught it.

He knew the supporting arguments.

Yet tonight he had heard a question he wasn't certain they had answered.

“I'd like to examine the passage again.”

The room became quiet.

Peter looked toward Harold.

Harold's expression changed.

“Elías, are you reconsidering the doctrine?”

“I'm reconsidering whether we've answered Daniel's question.”

Peter folded his arms.

“We've already explained our position.”

“Yes.”

“And you know the theological basis for it.”

“I do.”

Harold leaned forward.

“Then what exactly is troubling you?”

Elías looked toward the door.

“The way we've treated him.”

Harold sighed.

“We've been patient.”

“I know you've tried to be.”

“We've given him opportunities to reconsider.”

“Yes.”

“And we haven't made a final decision.”

Elías nodded.

He could hear the familiar reasoning.

He had used it himself.

Harold studied him.

“I think we should meet again, Elías. Just the elders and you.”

Elías felt a heaviness settle over him.

“All right.”

“We don't want misunderstandings developing among the leadership.”

“Neither do I.”

Harold nodded.

“Good.”

The men began gathering their belongings.

Someone mentioned an upcoming outreach event.

Another reminded Harold about a hospital visit scheduled for the next morning.

The conversation returned to familiar responsibilities.

Coats were collected.

Chairs were pushed under the table.

One by one, the men left.

Elías remained seated.

The room was quiet.

The fluorescent lights hummed overhead.

Seven chairs stood around the conference table, one slightly apart from the others where Daniel had been sitting.

The memorandum lay open before Elías.

Seven pages.

Carefully organized.

Thoroughly referenced.

Every objection anticipated.

Every conclusion supported.

He knew how much work had gone into it.

He knew because he had written documents just like it.

He remembered meetings where someone had questioned an established interpretation. He remembered the concern that followed, the discussions about protecting the congregation, the careful explanations offered to settle the matter.

And he remembered the satisfaction of reaching a conclusion.

The doctrine had been defended. The matter had been settled. The church could move forward.

He had considered that faithfulness.

Tonight, listening to Harold and Peter, he had heard arguments he might easily have made himself.

He knew the concern behind them. He understood the responsibility they felt. He even remembered the uneasiness that arose when someone questioned something he had spent years teaching.

There had been times when a question felt less like an invitation to examine Scripture and more like a threat to everything he had carefully established.

He had never thought of it that way before.

He remembered the relief that followed when the questioning stopped.

No further objections.

No more uncomfortable discussions.

The congregation could move forward together.

He had called it unity.

Now he wondered whether some people had simply learned to remain silent.

He looked at the empty chair where Daniel had been sitting.

The young man had not demanded that anyone agree with him.

He had simply wanted to keep studying.

And that was not all Elías remembered.

He remembered being just like Daniel.

Years earlier, when he was beginning to study Scripture seriously, he had asked questions about everything.

He remembered sitting with an open Bible late into the evening, following references from one passage to another, sometimes discovering that something he'd always believed wasn't quite what the passage said.

Those discoveries had delighted him.

He hadn't been troubled when an explanation proved inadequate. It meant there was more to discover.

He remembered asking older believers questions they couldn't immediately answer.

Some had welcomed them.

Others had seemed uncomfortable.

He hadn't understood their discomfort.

He wasn't trying to challenge anyone.

He wanted to know what God had said.

Sometimes he had been wrong. Sometimes he had misunderstood the passage entirely. And sometimes an older believer had patiently shown him something he had overlooked.

He remembered the pleasure of discovering that he had been mistaken.

There was something wonderful about finding that Scripture said more than he had understood.

He smiled faintly.

He had forgotten that.

Somewhere along the way, something had changed.

He had become the man with the answers.

People came to him with questions, and he knew what to tell them. He had studied diligently, learned from capable teachers, and developed explanations that brought difficult passages together.

Much of that learning had been valuable.

He was grateful for it.

Yet he could no longer remember when a question had stopped being something exciting to investigate and started becoming something he felt responsible to settle.

Or when his confidence in Scripture had become so closely tied to his confidence in his own explanations.

He looked again at Daniel's empty chair.

*I'd like to keep studying it.*

Elías remembered saying things like that himself.

He remembered meaning them.

He looked around the conference room.

The same table.

The same chairs.

The same Bibles and carefully prepared documents.

Nothing in the room had changed.

Yet he was no longer certain he understood what had happened there.

He closed the memorandum.

For years, he had admired men who could defend what they believed.

Tonight he found himself remembering a young man who had been delighted simply to discover what God had said.

And he wondered when he had stopped being that young man.

The next morning, Elías arrived at Deb's Place before anyone else.

Anna was wiping down the counter.

“Morning, Elías.”

“Good morning.”

“Coffee?”

“Yes, please.”

She filled his cup.

“You're early.”

“I suppose I am.”

She studied him for a moment.

“Everything all right?”

He smiled faintly.

“Long evening.”

“Those are no fun.”

“No.”

She left him with his coffee.

Outside, snow had begun falling.

Elías watched it gather along the edges of the sidewalk.

He had barely slept.

He kept remembering Daniel's face when Harold mentioned removing him from fellowship.

He remembered the relief in the room after Daniel left.

He remembered the conversation about discouraging further questions.

And he remembered Harold suggesting that they might need to meet with him separately.

Adrián arrived first.

He came through the door brushing snow from his shoulders.

“Beautiful!”

Elías looked up.

“The snow?”

“No, the coffee.”

Adrián dropped into his chair.

“Although the snow isn't bad either.”

Matías arrived a minute later, followed by Marcos.

They were laughing about something that had happened in the parking lot.

Apparently Adrián had attempted to clear his windshield with a loyalty card from a grocery store.

“It worked,” Adrián insisted.

“For about six inches,” Marcos replied.

“Six very important inches.”

Matías laughed.

“Buy a scraper.”

“I have one.”

“Where?”

“At home.”

“Very useful.”

They laughed again.

Elías smiled.

He was glad to see them.

For several minutes they talked about the weather, their families, and the possibility that Adrián might eventually purchase proper winter equipment.

Then Matías noticed that Elías hadn't joined much of the conversation.

“Elías?”

He looked up.

“Yes?”

“You seem tired.”

Elías nodded.

“I am.”

“Everything okay?”

He looked at his friends.

He had spent most of the night trying to understand what had happened.

He still didn't know.

“There was a meeting at church last night.”

Marcos set down his cup.

“A difficult one?”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“What happened?”

Elías hesitated.

Then he began telling them.

He explained the disagreement over First John 1:9.

He described Daniel's questions.

He explained the church's established position.

He told them about the memorandum, the grammatical arguments, and the concern that Daniel's questions could undermine doctrinal stability.

The others listened.

When he reached the possibility of removing Daniel from fellowship, Adrián stopped him.

“Wait.”

Elías looked up.

“They're going to remove him?”

“Nothing has been decided.”

“Why?”

Elías looked down.

“Because he hasn't agreed to stop questioning the interpretation.”

Matías leaned back.

Marcos was quiet.

Adrián looked from one friend to another.

“That's serious.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

Then Elías told them what happened after Daniel left.

He described the relief among the elders.

The expectation that Daniel would stop raising questions.

The hope that others would be discouraged from doing the same.

“And then,” Elías added, “they suggested I might need a meeting of my own.”

Matías looked at him.

“Because you asked whether they'd understood Daniel?”

Elías nodded.

“I think so.”

For a while, nobody spoke.

Then Marcos asked, “Elías, how are you doing with all this?”

Elías looked surprised.

“I don't know.”

Matías leaned forward.

“You've been carrying this by yourself?”

“I've been trying to understand what happened.”

“That's not what I asked.”

Elías looked at him.

Matías smiled gently.

“We're your friends.”

Elías lowered his eyes.

“You don't have to figure everything out before talking to us.”

Adrián nodded.

“Besides, you've listened to our problems for months. It's about time you gave us something to do.”

Marcos laughed.

“He has a point.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“I wasn't aware you were looking for additional responsibilities.”

“We're not,” Adrián replied. “We're looking after you.”

Elías looked at him.

For a moment, he couldn't speak.

Then he nodded.

“Thank you.”

Matías reached across the table and rested a hand on his arm.

“We'll work through it together.”

Elías looked down.

He hadn't expected that.

He had expected questions.

Arguments.

Perhaps disagreement.

Instead, his friends seemed more concerned about him than about resolving the controversy.

Something in his chest loosened.

Only slightly.

Enough that he noticed.

Marcos opened his Bible.

“Can I ask something?”

“Of course,” Elías said.

“Whose church is it?”

Elías looked at him.

“Christ's.”

Marcos nodded.

“Then perhaps we should begin there.”

Matías turned to Matthew sixteen.

He found verse eighteen.

“‘I will build My church.’”

Adrián smiled.

“His church.”

“And He's the builder,” Marcos said.

Matías nodded.

Elías looked at the words.

He had taught that passage many times.

Yet today he was grateful that someone else had opened it.

Adrián leaned forward.

“Then what does Christ want us to do when someone is struggling to understand Scripture?”

Marcos considered the question.

“We should probably distinguish between someone asking questions and someone determined to spread false teaching.”

Matías nodded.

“Those aren't the same thing.”

Elías looked up.

“No,” he said quietly. “They're not.”

Adrián turned to Acts seventeen.

“Here's something.”

He read about the Bereans examining the Scriptures daily to determine whether what they heard was true.

Marcos smiled.

“They examined what Paul taught.”

“Yes.”

“And Luke commends them for it.”

Matías nodded.

“That's remarkable.”

Adrián looked at Elías.

“Daniel is asking whether the church's explanation comes from the passage.”

Elías considered the comparison.

“Yes.”

Matías turned to Acts eighteen.

“There's Apollos too.”

He read about Priscilla and Aquila explaining the way of God more accurately to him.

Marcos leaned forward.

“They didn't treat his incomplete understanding as rebellion.”

“No,” Matías said.

“They helped him.”

Adrián smiled.

“Amen.”

Elías looked at the passage.

He remembered teaching about Apollos.

He remembered emphasizing the importance of accurate doctrine.

He had spent comparatively little time considering the manner in which Priscilla and Aquila had helped him.

He said nothing.

Marcos found Second Timothy two.

“Listen to this.”

He read slowly.

“‘And a servant of the Lord must not quarrel but be gentle to all, able to teach, patient, in humility correcting those who are in opposition.’”

Matías looked up.

“Wow.”

Adrián smiled.

“Read that again.”

Marcos did.

Elías looked down at his hands.

Gentle.

Patient.

Humble.

He remembered Daniel sitting at the end of the conference table.

He remembered the seven-page memorandum.

He remembered the warning about fellowship.

Matías looked toward him.

“Was that how the meeting went?”

Elías was quiet.

Then he shook his head.

“No.”

Nobody rushed to fill the silence.

After a moment, Elías added, “I'm not sure we ever asked whether he might be right.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

Adrián leaned forward.

“Then perhaps that's where we begin.”

Elías looked at him.

“How?”

“By listening.”

Matías smiled.

“To Daniel.”

“And to Scripture,” Marcos added.

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Four men, a Bible, and a difficult question.”

Matías laughed.

“Sounds familiar.”

“It's worked pretty well so far.”

Marcos smiled.

“We haven't solved everything.”

“Of course not,” Adrián said. “We're still waiting for Elías to explain the Greek.”

Elías laughed.

A real laugh.

For the first time that morning.

Adrián grinned.

“There he is.”

They continued reading for another hour.

They examined passages concerning false teaching, church discipline, gentleness, and the responsibility of leaders.

They considered Titus three and the warning concerning a divisive person.

They discussed the difference between a teacher who persistently promotes error and a believer who is honestly struggling with a passage.

They didn't settle every question.

And nobody seemed troubled by that.

At one point Marcos said, “We should be careful not to assume Daniel is right simply because he's asking questions.”

Matías nodded.

“Absolutely.”

“And we shouldn't assume the elders are wrong simply because they're concerned about doctrine.”

“Agreed,” Adrián said.

Elías looked at them.

He appreciated that.

He loved the elders.

He knew their families.

He had prayed with them, served beside them, and watched them care for people through difficult years.

He didn't want to turn against them.

Marcos continued.

“Perhaps the question isn't which side we're on.”

Adrián smiled.

“Then what is it?”

“What does Christ say?”

Matías nodded.

“And how do we respond to everyone involved in a way that's consistent with Him?”

Elías looked down at Matthew sixteen.

*I will build My church.*

For years he had felt responsible for protecting the church.

He still believed leaders had a genuine responsibility to guard the flock.

Yet something about Christ's words was beginning to relieve a pressure he hadn't known how to name.

The church belonged to Christ.

He was its builder.

Elías didn't have to possess every answer before he could trust Him.

He looked at his friends.

They were studying.

Laughing occasionally.

Asking questions.

Disagreeing without becoming defensive.

And caring for him without making his uncertainty an issue.

He felt something he hadn't experienced in a long time.

Not certainty.

Not yet.

Something gentler.

He was no longer carrying the problem alone.

Then he thought of Daniel.

For a moment, he imagined the young man sitting at their table, his Bible open, asking the questions that had caused so much concern.

He wondered whether Daniel might enjoy meeting the others.

The thought stayed with him briefly.

Then Adrián said something about breakfast, and the conversation moved on.

When they finally prepared to leave, the snow had stopped.

A thin white covering lay across the parked cars.

Adrián looked outside.

“Wonderful.”

Matías smiled.

“Forgot your scraper again?”

Adrián closed his eyes.

“Don't start.”

Marcos laughed.

“I have one in my car.”

Adrián looked at him.

“A real friend.”

“I'll lend it to you.”

“An even better friend.”

They gathered their coats.

Elías remained seated for a moment.

Matías noticed.

“You coming?”

“Yes.”

He stood.

Then he looked at the three men.

“Thank you.”

Adrián smiled.

“For what?”

Elías hesitated.

He wasn't sure how to explain it.

“For listening.”

Matías nodded.

“Of course.”

Marcos picked up his Bible.

“We're in this together.”

Adrián grinned.

“Unfortunately for you.”

Elías laughed.

They walked outside.

The cold air struck their faces, and Adrián immediately began complaining about the wind.

Matías reminded him that winter had barely begun.

Marcos went to retrieve the scraper.

Elías stood beside his car and watched them.

He had come to Deb's Place carrying a problem he didn't know how to resolve.

He was leaving without a resolution.

Yet the burden felt different.

He had not been required to defend himself.

He had not been expected to provide the answer.

His friends had simply come alongside him.

He didn't know how to describe what that meant to him.

He wasn't ready to try.

For now, he was grateful.

Adrián called across the parking lot.

“Elías! You need a scraper?”

Elías looked at his windshield.

A thin layer of snow covered the glass.

He smiled.

“Yes, actually.”

Adrián raised the borrowed scraper triumphantly.

“Excellent! Today we serve!”

Matías laughed.

Marcos shook his head.

Elías began laughing too.

And for a few moments, standing in the cold beside his friends, he forgot that he was supposed to have everything figured out.

# Chapter 29
## The Freedom to Ask

Adrián had brought a windshield scraper.

He placed it in the middle of the table at Deb's Place as though presenting an important scientific discovery.

Matías stared at it.

“Well, look at that.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“An actual scraper.”

“Gentlemen,” Adrián announced, “I am prepared for winter.”

Elías examined it.

“Did you buy it?”

“Of course.”

“Or did Marcos lend it to you again?”

Adrián looked offended.

“I cannot believe the suspicion in this room.”

Matías picked it up.

There was still a price tag attached.

“Three dollars and ninety-nine cents.”

“An investment in my future.”

“That's awesome,” Marcos said solemnly.

Adrián grinned.

“Finally, someone appreciates progress.”

Anna arrived with their coffee.

She looked at the scraper.

“Planning to clean the table?”

“Personal development,” Matías explained.

Anna nodded.

“Good. We encourage that here.”

She filled their cups and moved on.

Elías laughed.

He had been looking forward to this morning.

The situation at church remained unresolved. Harold had not yet arranged the separate meeting he'd suggested, and Daniel's teaching responsibilities were still under consideration.

Elías had spent much of the week thinking about what had happened.

The questions remained.

Yet something about them was different.

He no longer felt quite so alone.

Adrián moved the scraper aside.

“So,” he said, “I've been thinking about your friend Daniel.”

Elías looked up.

“Our friend, actually.”

Adrián frowned.

“What do you mean?”

“Daniel Mercer. You met him at the men's retreat a few years ago.”

Matías stopped stirring his coffee.

“Wait. Daniel Mercer?”

“Yes.”
