“That because people seemed excited, they must be spiritual.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“And Corinth challenged that assumption.”

“It certainly did.”

Adrián shook his head.

“I still remember reading that they weren't lacking in any gift.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And then Paul calls them fleshly.”

“Because of jealousy and strife,” Matías added.

“Right.”

Adrián looked at his cup.

“That was quite a surprise.”

Marcos smiled.

“You defended them for a while.”

“I did.”

“Rather enthusiastically.”

“I'm an enthusiastic person.”

“Even when you're wrong?”

“Especially then.”

They laughed.

Matías looked thoughtful.

“Can I tell you something?”

Adrián nodded.

“Of course.”

“When we were studying Corinth, I wasn't nearly as troubled as you were.”

“I remember.”

“Actually, I felt rather comfortable with Paul's criticism.”

Adrián raised an eyebrow.

“Comfortable?”

“Yes.”

“That's an unusual response.”

Matías smiled.

“I know.”

He looked down at his Bible.

“I thought Corinth was a good example of what happens when Christians don't take their lives seriously enough.”

Adrián nodded.

“And you thought they needed discipline.”

“Exactly.”

“Standards.”

“Yes.”

“Accountability.”

“Certainly.”

Adrián grinned.

“And a reading plan.”

Matías laughed.

“Possibly.”

Marcos smiled.

“Preferably before breakfast.”

“Now you're both being ridiculous.”

Adrián lifted his hands.

“We learned from the best.”

Matías shook his head, smiling.

Then he became thoughtful again.

“The point is, when I heard about a church emphasizing obedience and holiness, I was impressed.”

“Your congregation,” Marcos said.

“Yes.”

Matías nodded.

“I thought those people understood something Corinth had missed.”

“And you admired them,” Elías said.

“Very much.”

He paused.

“More than I realized.”

Adrián looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

Matías considered his answer.

“I suppose I saw in that church the kind of Christian life I'd been trying to live.”

The others listened.

“Serious. Disciplined. Committed. Careful about sin. Intentional about obedience.”

He smiled faintly.

“It was familiar.”

Adrián nodded.

“It sounded like you.”

“Yes.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“And when we began studying Galatia, I didn't want to hear that their attempt to become more complete through the flesh was a problem.”

Marcos nodded.

“You were defending something important to you.”

“Very important.”

Matías paused.

“Although I wasn't simply defending the church.”

He looked at Adrián.

“I was defending the way I'd been trying to live.”

Adrián's expression softened.

“I remember.”

Matías smiled.

“I think you understood that before I did.”

“Only because Corinth had already given me a little practice.”

They laughed quietly.

For a moment, nobody spoke.

Then Marcos reached for his coffee.

“Well, I suppose my turn is coming.”

Adrián grinned.

“Oh, absolutely.”

Marcos smiled.

“Go ahead.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Community Impact Week.”

Marcos laughed.

“I knew it.”

“Three thousand two hundred and eighty participants.”

“You remember the number?”

“Of course.”

“I'm impressed.”

“Eighty-six professionals.”

Marcos shook his head.

“Please don't.”

“Forty-two partner organizations.”

Matías was laughing now.

“And,” Adrián continued, raising one finger, “one very tired organizer.”

“That part is accurate,” Marcos admitted.

Elías smiled.

“You certainly accomplished a great deal.”

“We did.”

“And people were genuinely helped,” Matías added.

Marcos nodded.

“Yes. That's still something I'm grateful for.”

Adrián looked at him.

“And you loved every minute of organizing it.”

“Not every minute.”

“The air conditioning?”

“Especially not the air conditioning.”

They laughed.

Marcos leaned back.

“I did enjoy seeing everything come together.”

“We noticed,” Adrián said.

Marcos smiled.

“I know.”

He looked at the three men.

“You know, when I first heard about the church at Laodicea, I didn't immediately think about the rebuke.”

“What did you think about?” Elías asked.

“The resources.”

Adrián nodded.

“Of course.”

“The ability to accomplish things. The financial stability. The confidence.”

Marcos paused.

“I understand why a church would appreciate those things.”

“Certainly,” Elías said.

Marcos continued.

“When you have resources, you can help people. You can plan ahead. You can respond to needs. You can support worthwhile work.”

Matías nodded.

“All true.”

“And when you see those things happening, it's encouraging.”

“Of course,” Adrián said.

Marcos looked down at his coffee.

“That's why Christ's assessment of Laodicea bothered me so much.”

Elías waited.

“They said they were rich and needed nothing.”

“Yes.”

“And He said they were poor and blind.”

Marcos shook his head.

“I couldn't get away from that.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Because they were wrong about themselves?”

“Yes.”

Marcos paused.

“And because I recognized how easily I could have made the same judgment.”

He looked at his friends.

“If you'd asked me a few months ago whether our church was healthy, I could have given you a very impressive report.”

Adrián smiled.

“You did give us a very impressive report.”

“Several,” Matías added.

Marcos laughed.

“Yes. Several.”

Then he grew thoughtful.

“And every figure could have been correct.”

Elías nodded.

“That's important.”

“Yet those figures couldn't tell me whether we were depending on Christ.”

“No,” Elías said.

Marcos looked at the Bible.

“I think that's what I found so unsettling.”

He paused.

“I knew how to evaluate the success of an event. I wasn't nearly as certain how to evaluate the spiritual condition of a church.”

Adrián nodded slowly.

“And Laodicea made you question the difference.”

“Exactly.”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“I suppose I've always been impressed by churches that get things done.”

Adrián grinned.

“We had noticed.”

“Yes, thank you.”

They laughed.

Then Marcos looked toward Elías.

“And I think we know which church impressed you.”

Elías raised an eyebrow.

“Do we?”

Adrián sat up.

“Oh, we certainly do.”

Matías smiled.

“Ephesus.”

Elías looked at the three of them.

“Apparently my preferences haven't been particularly well concealed.”

“Not at all,” Adrián said.

Elías laughed.

“Well, I did admire Ephesus.”

“Did?” Adrián asked.

“I still admire what Christ commended.”

Matías nodded.

“As we should.”

Elías looked at his Bible.

“Think about their history. Paul taught there. Timothy was instructed to confront false doctrine there. The elders were warned about false teachers.”

Marcos smiled.

“And Christ commended their discernment.”

“Yes.”

Elías nodded.

“They had persevered. They had labored for His name. They had tested those claiming to be apostles and found them false.”

Adrián looked at him.

“You're beginning to sound excited.”

Elías smiled.

“I suppose I am.”

“Careful. That's my department.”

They laughed.

Elías continued.

“Those commendations are genuine. Christ Himself gives them.”

“Of course,” Matías said.

“And I don't want us to diminish them simply because we know what comes afterward.”

Marcos nodded.

“Neither do I.”

Elías rested his hand on the Bible.

“Yet what comes afterward is impossible to ignore.”

Adrián looked at him.

“They had fallen.”

“Yes.”

“And Christ told them to repent.”

“Yes.”

“And warned that He would remove their lampstand.”

Elías nodded.

“Unless they repented.”

He sat quietly for a moment.

“I've been thinking about that warning all week.”

Matías looked at him.

“What particularly troubles you?”

Elías considered the question.

“I suppose it's how easily I would have assumed that a church with those commendations was spiritually healthy.”

Marcos nodded.

“You would have trusted its doctrine.”

“Yes.”

“Its discernment.”

“Yes.”

“And its perseverance.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“All of it.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“You'd have joined that church in a heartbeat.”

Elías laughed.

“Quite possibly.”

“Then you'd have volunteered to teach.”

“Perhaps.”

“And within six months you'd have corrected everyone.”

Matías burst out laughing.

Elías shook his head.

“Adrián.”

“What?”

“You're enjoying this.”

“Immensely.”

Elías laughed too.

“Unfortunately, you may have a point.”

Elías waited for the laughter to settle.

Then he looked down at Revelation.

“I've spent much of my Christian life valuing doctrinal accuracy.”

Matías nodded.

“And rightly so.”

“Yes. Truth matters.”

Elías paused.

“Yet I've also enjoyed being the person who knew the answer.”

Adrián smiled.

“We know.”

Elías looked up.

“Thank you.”

“You're welcome.”

They laughed again.

Elías continued.

“I've been thinking about how quickly I could recognize an error in someone else's teaching.”

Marcos nodded.

“You've always been good at that.”

“Perhaps.”

Elías looked at him.

“Although being able to identify an error doesn't tell me everything about the source of my own confidence.”

Matías leaned forward.

“That's interesting.”

Elías smiled.

“Isn't it?”

Adrián glanced between them.

“Now both of you are becoming dangerous.”

Matías laughed.

Elías continued.

“When I read Christ's message to Ephesus, I see a church whose religious record I would have admired.”

He paused.

“And Christ says it had fallen.”

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Then Adrián looked around the table.

“Well.”

He set down his coffee.

“I think we've discovered something rather embarrassing.”

Marcos smiled.

“What's that?”

Adrián pointed toward Matías.

“He liked the disciplined church.”

Matías nodded.

“Yes.”

He turned toward Marcos.

“You liked the successful church.”

“Certainly.”

Then toward Elías.

“And you liked the doctrinally correct church.”

Elías smiled.

“Very much.”

Adrián leaned back.

“And I liked the exciting church.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's accurate.”

Adrián looked at the three of them.

“So we spent all this time trying to figure out what makes a church healthy…”

He paused.

“…and we each had a favorite before we finished reading.”

Matías laughed.

“Apparently.”

Elías smiled.

“That's an interesting observation.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Don't turn it into a theological term.”

“I wasn't going to.”

“You were thinking about it.”

Elías laughed.

“Perhaps.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“There's something else.”

Adrián turned toward him.

“What?”

“We didn't simply admire different churches.”

“No?”

“We were using different things to judge them.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“That's true.”

Adrián smiled.

“And each of us thought his preferred church had something the others needed.”

Matías laughed softly.

“That's certainly how I felt about Corinth.”

Adrián looked at him.

“You were going to fix my church.”

“Someone had to.”

They laughed.

“And I thought Corinth had something your church needed,” Adrián replied.

Matías raised an eyebrow.

“What?”

“A little joy.”

Matías laughed.

“Of course.”

Marcos smiled.

“And I thought both of you needed better organization.”

Adrián turned toward him.

“There's a surprise.”

Elías laughed.

Marcos looked at Elías.

“And you probably thought all three of us needed better teaching.”

Elías smiled.

“I may have entertained that possibility.”

Adrián threw up his hands.

“Gentlemen, we've solved it!”

Matías laughed.

“Have we?”

“Of course. All four churches needed to become more like the one each of us preferred.”

Marcos burst out laughing.

Elías shook his head.

“That's rather revealing.”

Adrián smiled.

“Isn't it?”

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“More coffee?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She began pouring.

“What are you gentlemen laughing about?”

“Our remarkable ability to recognize what's wrong with everyone else.”

Anna smiled.

“Well, you've had plenty of practice.”

Marcos laughed.

“Apparently.”

She looked at Adrián.

“Anything wrong with your coffee?”

“Nothing whatsoever.”

“That's a first.”

“I'm growing, Anna.”

“Keep it up.”

She moved to the next table.

The four men were still smiling when Matías looked down at his Bible.

“There's something I keep coming back to.”

Elías turned toward him.

“What is it?”

“Galatians three.”

Adrián smiled.

“Your favorite passage.”

Matías laughed.

“Not exactly my favorite when we first studied it.”

He opened the Bible.

“Paul asks whether, having begun by the Spirit, they were now being perfected by the flesh.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías looked around the table.

“The Galatians were looking to something that couldn't complete what the Spirit had begun.”

“Right,” Marcos said.

Matías continued.

“And we've been discussing how easily we look at things that impress us and assume they establish spiritual health.”

Adrián nodded.

“Gifts.”

“Discipline.”

“Results,” Marcos added.

“Knowledge,” Elías said.

For a moment, they sat quietly.

Then Adrián looked at the four of them.

“Those are all rather different things.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“And none of them is necessarily wrong.”

“Certainly not,” Elías replied.

Adrián leaned back.

“Then I suppose the question is what we're expecting them to tell us.”

Matías nodded.

“That's worth considering.”

Marcos looked at his Bible.

“Especially if we're using them to answer a question they can't answer.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Such as whether we're depending on Christ.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

Adrián looked toward the window.

A couple was walking past, laughing about something neither of them could hear.

He watched them for a moment.

Then he turned back.

“You know what I find interesting?”

Matías smiled.

“There's more?”

“Always.”

Adrián looked at the open Bibles.

“We've spent weeks examining what the Corinthians were doing, what the Galatians were trying to do, what the Laodiceans had accomplished, and what the Ephesians knew and defended.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Adrián paused.

“And I keep thinking about what Jesus said about the vine.”

Matías looked up.

“John fifteen?”

“Yes.”

Elías opened his Bible.

“Verse five?”

Adrián smiled.

“That's the one.”

Elías found the passage and read.

“‘I am the vine. You are the branches. He who remains in me, and I in him, the same bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.’”

Marcos looked at the verse.

“Apart from Me.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías leaned forward.

“He doesn't say the branches need to become more impressive.”

Adrián smiled.

“No.”

“Or that they need to prove how much they can accomplish,” Marcos added.

Elías looked at the passage.

“The branch bears fruit because of its relationship to the vine.”

Matías nodded.

“Because its life comes from the vine.”

“Yes.”

Adrián sat quietly for a moment.

Then he smiled.

“Imagine a branch boasting about how many grapes it produced.”

Marcos laughed.

“Adrián.”

“No, seriously.”

He lifted one hand as though addressing an audience.

“Ladies and gentlemen, I'd like to thank everyone who made this harvest possible.”

Matías began laughing.

Adrián continued.

“First, my personal commitment to fruitfulness.”

Marcos covered his face.

“And of course, my outstanding record of perseverance.”

Elías was laughing now.

“Don't forget your doctrinal accuracy,” he said.

Adrián pointed at him.

“Absolutely. I have always maintained the correct position on grapes.”

They laughed so loudly that Anna looked over from the counter.

“Everything all right?”

“Perfectly!” Adrián called.

She shook her head, smiling.

When the laughter subsided, Marcos looked at the open Bible.

“That's actually quite a picture.”

Adrián smiled.

“A ridiculous one.”

“Yes.”

Marcos paused.

“Although it does make the point.”

Elías nodded.

“The branch has no independent life.”

Matías looked at the verse again.

“And yet it really does bear fruit.”

“Certainly,” Elías said.

Adrián leaned back.

“So the fruit is real.”

“Yes.”

“And the branch isn't the source.”

“No.”

He smiled.

“I think I like that.”

For a while, nobody spoke.

The diner continued around them.

Anna carried plates to a nearby table. Someone called for a refill. A child laughed from one of the booths.

Marcos looked at his friends.

“There's something encouraging about that passage.”

Matías nodded.

“What?”

“Christ doesn't tell the branches to manufacture life.”

Elías smiled.

“No.”

“He identifies Himself as the vine.”

“Yes.”

Marcos looked down.

“I've spent so much time thinking about what a church can accomplish.”

Adrián smiled.

“We know.”

Marcos laughed softly.

“Yes, you do.”

Then he looked at the passage again.

“It's good to be reminded that Christ is the source of spiritual life.”

Matías nodded.

“Very good.”

Elías sat quietly, his Bible open before him.

His phone vibrated.

He glanced at the screen.

Adrián noticed.

“Trouble?”

Elías smiled.

“No.”

“Another theological question?”

“Not this time.”

“Then what?”

“A message from my wife.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Ah.”

Elías read it and laughed softly.

Matías smiled.

“What did she say?”

“She wants to know whether I'd like to go for a walk this evening.”

Adrián nodded solemnly.

“A very serious invitation.”

Elías smiled.

“Apparently.”

“Does she have a destination?”

“No.”

Adrián looked at Marcos.

“Gentlemen, we may have a problem.”

Marcos laughed.

“What problem?”

“Our friend is about to participate in an activity with no measurable objective.”

Elías shook his head.

Matías smiled.

“And no reading assignment.”

“Or doctrinal examination,” Adrián added.

Elías laughed.

“I think I'll survive.”

He typed a reply and put the phone away.

Adrián watched him.

“What did you tell her?”

“That I'd love to.”

Adrián smiled.

“Good answer.”

Elías looked down at his Bible for a moment.

Then he closed it.

“I'm looking forward to it.”

The others smiled.

Anna approached with the bill.

“Together or separate?”

Adrián looked around the table.

“Together.”

Marcos raised an eyebrow.

“Again?”

“I'm feeling generous.”

Matías smiled.

“Are you sure?”

“Absolutely.”

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

He examined it.

Then looked up.

“Anna.”

“Yes?”

“Is this the correct amount?”

She folded her arms.

“Why?”

“I just want to make sure we're not being charged for all the theological discoveries.”

She laughed.

“Those are free.”

“Wonderful.”

“The coffee isn't.”

Adrián sighed.

“There's always a catch.”

They laughed as they gathered their things.

Outside, the morning was cool, with a light wind moving along the street.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars, reluctant to end the conversation.

Matías looked at the others.

“I enjoyed today.”

“So did I,” Marcos said.

Adrián smiled.

“Even though we didn't finish comparing the churches?”

Elías looked at him.

“Did you expect us to?”

“Not really.”

Matías laughed.

“I think we discovered something important.”

Marcos nodded.

“We each had our own reasons for admiring the churches we admired.”

“And those reasons weren't necessarily the same as Christ's assessment,” Elías added.

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“No.”

He glanced at the others.

“That's something to think about.”

Matías smiled.

“Next Thursday?”

“Of course,” Marcos said.

Elías nodded.

“I'll be here.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Provided you haven't become too busy walking.”

Elías laughed.

“I think I can manage both.”

They said their goodbyes.

As Adrián walked toward his car, he thought about the morning.

Four friends.

Four churches.

Four very different ideas of what made a church worth admiring.

He had always assumed that the things which excited him were the things that mattered most.

Matías had valued discipline.

Marcos had valued accomplishment.

Elías had valued knowledge and discernment.

And each of them had found something in Scripture that challenged the measure he trusted.

Adrián opened his car door, then paused.

He remembered the laughter about the branch boasting of its grapes.

He smiled.

It really was a ridiculous picture.

Then he thought about how naturally the four of them had defended the things they admired.

Perhaps the picture wasn't quite as ridiculous as he'd thought.

He climbed into the car.

Next Thursday, they would have more to discuss.

For now, he was glad they'd begun to see the difference between the things that impressed them and the life that came from Christ.

And he was particularly glad that none of them had suggested a fifth church.

Four were proving quite enough.

# Chapter 33
## The Trouble with Being Somebody

Matías had been looking forward to Thursday.

He had also been putting off something.

The two feelings had accompanied him all week, usually when he was driving, occasionally while working, and once in the middle of brushing his teeth. That last occasion had irritated him. He had stood before the bathroom mirror, toothbrush in hand, thinking about the Galatians.

His wife had found him there.

“Are you all right?”

“Yes.”

“Then why are you staring at yourself?”

“I'm thinking.”

“While brushing your teeth?”

He had looked at the toothbrush.

“I suppose I should finish.”

She had left him to it, smiling.

Now, seated at Deb's Place, he was wondering how to explain what had been troubling him.

Marcos was telling the others about a conversation he'd had with a friend who was considering retirement. The man had built a successful business and was struggling with the idea of leaving it behind.

“He told me he wouldn't know what to do with himself,” Marcos said. “Not because he needs the money. He's been financially secure for years. He simply doesn't know who he'd be without the company.”

Adrián was examining the breakfast menu.

“Tell him to come and work for me. I have several problems he can occupy himself with.”

“He's trying to retire, Adrián.”

“Then I'll give him fewer problems.”

Marcos laughed.

“The interesting thing is that I understood him. I've spent so many years making decisions, solving problems, and keeping things moving that I sometimes wonder how I'd manage without all of it.”

“You'd manage,” Elías said.

“Probably. Although I'm not sure I'd enjoy it.”

“That's because you haven't discovered fishing,” Adrián said.

“You don't fish.”

“Exactly. I've discovered how much trouble it is.”

Anna arrived with their coffee and took their orders. She reminded Adrián that the kitchen would not prepare his eggs three different ways simply because he couldn't decide.

He settled for two.

When she left, Matías opened his Bible.

“I've been thinking about Galatia again.”

Adrián put down the menu.

“Are we going back?”

“Not exactly. Something about it has been bothering me, and I think I finally understand why.”

He found the third chapter.

“I've always believed in discipline. You know that about me. I believe commitments should be kept, responsibilities should be taken seriously, and a person ought to exercise some control over his appetites. I haven't changed my mind about any of that.

“When I became a Christian, I brought the same attitude into my Christian life. I established a time for prayer, a schedule for reading Scripture, and certain expectations for my conduct. If I failed to keep them, I corrected myself and tried harder the following day.

“And I thought that was how a Christian became spiritual.”

He looked at the open page.

“I'm beginning to wonder whether I had confused two entirely different things.”

Elías waited.

Matías continued.

“I know discipline can produce results. I've seen it in my work. A man who studies consistently generally learns more than one who doesn't. A man who plans carefully can accomplish things that would otherwise never happen. If someone wants to improve his physical condition, he has to train. That's how much of life works.

“So I assumed spirituality worked the same way. Apply the right disciplines, maintain them long enough, and eventually you become a spiritual man.

“I never would have said that I could produce the life of Christ. I knew better than that. At least I thought I did. Yet when I look at how I actually approached my Christian life, that's precisely what I was expecting my efforts to accomplish.”

He turned the Bible toward the others.

“Then Paul asks the Galatians, ‘Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?’

“I've read that question for years. I've explained it to other people. This week I found myself wondering how often I still avoid what it exposes in me.”

Marcos looked at him.

“What changed?”

“I'm not entirely sure. Perhaps our discussions about the four churches. I kept thinking about how easily I recognized the problems in Corinth and Laodicea. Their mistakes seemed obvious to me. Yet I was drawn to Galatia because I admired its seriousness.”

He closed his Bible halfway.

“I wasn't attracted to the confusion Paul was correcting. I was attracted to the idea of a Christian life that could be measured by discipline. It seemed responsible. Reliable. Something I understood.

“And now I'm wondering how much of my confidence was actually in Christ.”

No one hurried to answer.

Outside, a delivery truck stopped along the curb. Two men began unloading boxes through the diner's side entrance.

Marcos watched them for a moment before turning back.

“I think I understand what you're saying. I do something similar with results. If I want to know whether a project is succeeding, I look for evidence. Did we accomplish what we intended? Did we help the people we said we'd help? Did we use our resources well?

“Those are reasonable questions when you're managing a project. The trouble comes when I use the same measurements to decide whether a church is spiritual.

“I can organize a successful outreach. I can arrange transportation, recruit volunteers, raise money, and make sure everything happens on schedule. We might genuinely help a great many people. Then I look at what we've accomplished and feel satisfied that we're doing well spiritually.

“Yet none of those results tells me whether we were depending upon Christ. They tell me what happened. They don't necessarily tell me the source of it.”

Adrián considered this while spreading butter on his toast.

“There's something rather embarrassing about our preferences,” he said. “We each managed to choose the church that made the most sense to us. Matías wanted discipline. Marcos wanted results. Elías wanted sound doctrine. And I wanted something exciting enough that I wouldn't fall asleep before the sermon.”

“You were particularly enthusiastic about Corinth,” Elías said.

“I was. I still enjoy a lively gathering. There's nothing wrong with people being happy. I just hadn't considered how easily I could mistake excitement for spiritual life.”

He took a bite of toast.

“It's remarkable, really. Four men with four different ideas of what an impressive church should look like, and every one of us found a church to admire.”

Elías had been listening with an expression Matías recognized. It was the look he wore when he was reconsidering something he had taught for years.

“I've been thinking about Ephesus,” he said. “Especially the difference between being correct and being dependent upon Christ.

“Sound doctrine matters. I don't want us to lose sight of that. Christ Himself commended the Ephesians for testing those who falsely claimed to be apostles. Their discernment was not something He dismissed.

“Yet the church had fallen. And that leaves me with a question I find increasingly uncomfortable. How much of what we regard as evidence of spiritual health can be maintained without present dependence upon Christ?

“A man can learn to interpret Scripture accurately. He can become familiar with the errors of various teachers. He can recognize inconsistencies in an argument and explain difficult passages. Those abilities can be developed and exercised through human effort. They may be used in genuine service to Christ. They can also become the basis of a man's confidence in himself.

“I've known the difference in principle for a long time. I'm beginning to recognize how easily I can lose sight of it in practice.”

He stopped, then added, “Especially when somebody tells me I'm right.”

Adrián smiled.

“I've noticed that about you.”

“I suspected you had.”

“Only because you make it so easy.”

Elías laughed.

There was no need to defend himself. The observation was accurate.

Marcos reached for his coffee.

“There's a common thread here. Each of us has been describing something that gives him a sense of worth. Matías is the disciplined man. Elías is the knowledgeable man. I'm the man who gets things done. Adrián is—”

“An extraordinary individual,” Adrián supplied.

“Thank you. I was searching for a charitable description.”

“Always happy to help.”

Marcos continued.

“We can become attached to those identities. Not necessarily because the things themselves are wrong. We enjoy being the person who possesses them.”

Matías considered that.

“And when those identities become religious, they're even harder to question. We can defend them with Scripture. We can tell ourselves that we're simply trying to be faithful.”

Elías looked at him.

“That's what makes the flesh so difficult to recognize. It doesn't always announce itself through something obviously sinful. It can take satisfaction in being the most disciplined person in the room, the most knowledgeable, the most active, or the most visibly spiritual.”

Adrián sat back.

“So in every case, the flesh gets to be somebody.”

The words seemed to settle naturally among them.

Marcos looked toward the window.

“Yes. I think that's it.”

Matías opened his Bible again.

“Paul had something to say about being somebody.”

He turned to Philippians 3.

The others knew the passage, and he didn't rush through it.

Paul had been circumcised on the eighth day. He belonged to Israel, to the tribe of Benjamin. He was a Hebrew of Hebrews. Concerning the law, a Pharisee. Concerning zeal, a persecutor of the assembly. Concerning righteousness in the law, found blameless.

Matías rested his finger on the page.

“That's an extraordinary religious record. He possessed the qualifications he described. Some were inherited privileges; others reflected his zeal and conduct.

“Then he says that whatever things were gain to him, he counted as loss for Christ.

“I used to read that mainly as a warning against trusting religious accomplishments for justification. And it certainly speaks to that. Now I'm struck by something else. Paul had a great deal in which he could take personal satisfaction. He knew what it meant to be somebody in the religious world.

“And he had found Christ to be worth more.”

Elías leaned forward.

“Notice that Paul doesn't replace his old record with a new list of Christian achievements. He speaks about knowing Christ, being found in Him, and having a righteousness that is through faith. His confidence is no longer established by what he can present on his own behalf.”

Marcos turned to the passage.

“That's very different from trying to become a more impressive Christian.”

“It is,” Matías said.

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Although I can see another problem coming.”

The others waited.

“We're becoming quite good at recognizing the flesh. We've examined four churches. We've identified four different ways religious people can take satisfaction in themselves. We're now applying those discoveries to our own lives.”

He looked around the table.

“How long before we become proud of being the people who understand carnality better than everyone else?”

Elías began to laugh.

“Not long, I suspect.”

“I can already imagine it,” Adrián continued. “We could organize a conference. Invite people from all over the country. Explain why their churches are carnal.”

Marcos grinned.

“And charge admission?”

“Of course. We have expenses.”

“Who would be speaking?” Matías asked.

“The four of us. Naturally.”

Elías shook his head.

“The flesh doesn't mind studying the flesh, provided it gets to be the expert.”

That amused them more than any of the morning's earlier jokes.

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“What's so funny?”

Adrián looked up.

“We've discovered that we're capable of turning almost anything into an opportunity to admire ourselves.”

She considered him.

“You've only just discovered that?”

Marcos laughed.

Anna refilled Adrián’s coffee and returned to the counter, leaving him to contemplate the efficiency of her diagnosis.

When the laughter subsided, Matías remained looking at Philippians.

“There's something else,” he said.

He waited until the others were listening.

“We've been talking about the flesh as though the solution is to become better at recognizing it. And understanding it matters. Paul certainly wanted the Galatians to understand what they were doing.

“Yet I don't think I can overcome dependence upon myself simply by becoming more knowledgeable about it. That could become another accomplishment. Another discipline. Another thing I do well.

“I spent years trying to produce spirituality through effort. I don't want to replace that with an equally determined effort to become the man who never depends upon himself.”

He looked toward Elías.

“Do you understand what I mean?”

“I do. You're asking whether even our attempt to stop relying on ourselves could become another form of self-reliance.”

“Precisely.”

Marcos closed his Bible.

“Then perhaps we've reached the limit of what diagnosing the problem can tell us.”

Adrián looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

“We began by asking what makes a church carnal. We've discovered that the flesh can imitate or pursue many things we associate with spiritual health. We've also discovered that the same problem can appear in our own lives.

“Now we need to understand something else. If discipline, religious activity, and doctrinal knowledge cannot themselves produce the life of Christ, how is that life expressed through us?”

Elías did not answer immediately.

He had spent years teaching believers how to study Scripture, how to recognize error, and how to conduct themselves faithfully. Those were worthwhile subjects. He still believed they were worthwhile.

Yet he was beginning to see how easily a discussion of the Christian life could become a discussion of almost everything except the life itself.

He opened his Bible to Galatians 2.

“I think we've been looking at one part of Paul's argument without giving enough attention to another.”

He found the twentieth verse.

“I have been crucified with Christ, and it is no longer I that live, but Christ lives in me.”

He left the Bible open.

Matías read the words for himself.

Then he looked up.

“Christ lives in me.”

There was something different about hearing the statement after everything they had discussed.

It wasn't an instruction to establish a better routine.

It wasn't a call to become more impressive.

It was a declaration about the believer's life.

Adrián looked from Matías to Elías.

“I think we're going to need another Thursday.”

Marcos smiled.

“Probably several.”

“That's fine,” Adrián said. “I wasn't planning to stop coming.”

Across the room, Anna called out that she was glad to hear it, since she'd already ordered extra coffee for the following week.

They laughed.

Matías remained seated after the others began gathering their things.

For years, he had asked himself whether he was living the Christian life well enough.

Now another question had begun to occupy him.

What exactly was the Christian life he had been trying so hard to live?

He looked once more at the words before closing his Bible.

*Christ lives in me.*

He wanted to understand them.

Not as another principle to master.

He wanted to understand what Paul was actually saying.

And for the first time in a long while, he wasn't thinking about what he needed to accomplish before he could begin.

# Chapter 34
## Christ, Our Life

Matías had written three passages on a sheet of paper.

He hadn't intended to prepare anything for Thursday. He had simply opened his Bible after breakfast, found Galatians 2:20, and followed a reference to Colossians. From there he had gone to Philippians.

By the time he finished, the paper was lying beside his coffee cup.

He read the passages again.

*Christ lives in me.*

*Christ, our life.*

*To me to live is Christ.*

He had known every one of them for years.

He wasn't sure he'd ever considered them together.

His wife came into the kitchen and noticed the paper.

“Studying?”

“A little.”

“Anything interesting?”

He smiled.

“I think so.”

She glanced at the passages.

“Are you preparing something for the men?”

“No. At least I don't think so.”

She sat across from him.

“Then what are you doing?”

Matías considered the question.

“Trying to understand something I thought I already understood.”

She smiled.

“That should keep you busy.”

He laughed.

It probably would.

At Deb's Place, Adrián was telling Elías about a conversation he'd had with his grandson.

The boy had asked whether God needed people to help Him.

“I told him God doesn't need anything,” Adrián said. “Then he asked why everybody at church keeps telling us God needs workers.”

Elías smiled.

“What did you say?”

“I told him that was a very good question.”

“And?”

“And he asked me to answer it.”

Marcos laughed.

“Reasonable child.”

“Unreasonably persistent. I explained that God gives us opportunities to serve Him, and the boy wanted to know why we keep talking as though He's short of employees.”

Elías laughed.

“He sounds like his grandfather.”

“Much more troublesome. I was a delightful child.”

Matías arrived while they were still discussing it. He greeted them, ordered coffee, and placed his Bible and folded paper on the table.

Adrián noticed the paper.

“You've brought evidence.”

“Only a few passages.”

“That's how it always begins.”

Matías smiled and unfolded the sheet.

“I've been thinking about our conversation last week. We ended by asking how Christ's life is expressed through us. I've spent so much time thinking about the things a Christian ought to do that I realized I needed to return to what Scripture says about the life we have.”

He showed them the paper.

“Three passages kept bringing me back to the same thing.”

Elías looked at the references.

“Galatians, Colossians, and Philippians.”

“Yes. And I want to begin with Galatians because that's where my difficulty started.”

He opened to the second chapter.

He read verses nineteen through twenty-one:

> For I, through the law, died to the law, that I might live to God.

> I have been crucified with Christ, and it is no longer I that live, but Christ lives in me. That life which I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me, and gave himself up for me.

> I don’t reject the grace of God. For if righteousness is through the law, then Christ died for nothing!”

“I've always understood that as an important statement about our identification with Christ. Yet I wonder whether I've treated it as a doctrine I needed to affirm without really considering what Paul was declaring.

“He says Christ lives in him.

“Not that Christ has provided him with a collection of spiritual principles. Not that Christ has given him a program through which he can manufacture spiritual life. Christ Himself lives in Paul.

“And I began to wonder whether I've been treating the Christian life as something separate from Christ, something I must produce after believing the gospel.”

Elías read verse twenty carefully.

“Notice the contrast in Paul's words. Paul says, in effect, ‘I live—yet no longer I; Christ lives in me.’ He doesn't deny that he's living. In the rest of the verse he speaks about the life he now lives in his earthly body. He's still Paul. He thinks, chooses, works, travels, suffers, and speaks.

“Yet he identifies Christ as the One living in him. His life is no longer understood as an independent undertaking.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“So Paul isn't saying he has stopped acting.”

“Certainly not,” Elías said. “The same apostle describes extraordinary labor in the service of Christ. The question is not whether Paul is active. It's what accounts for his life and upon whom he depends.”

Matías looked at the next reference on his paper.

“Now listen to Colossians 3:4.”

He read:

“When Christ, our life, is revealed, then you will also be revealed with him in glory.”

He looked at his friends.

“Christ, our life. Paul doesn't merely say that Christ gives us life, although that's true. He identifies Christ Himself as our life.

“I kept reading those words, trying to understand why they seemed so different from the way I usually think about Christianity.

“When someone asks how my Christian life is going, I instinctively begin reviewing my activities. Have I been praying regularly? Have I been reading Scripture? Have I been faithful in my responsibilities? Have I behaved as I should?

“Those questions have their place. Yet none of them answers the question of who my life is.

“I can report on everything I've done this week and never mention Christ except as the reason I was supposed to do it.”

He set down the paper.

“I think that's what has been troubling me. I've made the Christian life into a life of Christian activities.”

Elías listened without interrupting.

He knew the temptation. His own years of teaching had often involved explaining the things believers ought to understand and practice. It was possible to teach those things faithfully. It was also possible to become so occupied with them that Christ Himself receded into the background.

Marcos reached for the paper.

“What's the third passage?”

“Philippians 1:21.”

Marcos found it in his Bible.

“For to me to live is Christ, and to die is gain.”

He read it a second time.

“That's quite a statement.”

“It is,” Matías said. “Paul doesn't say, ‘To me, living is performing Christian duties.’ He says, ‘To me to live is Christ.’”

Adrián had been unusually quiet.

“I'm beginning to see why you brought these together,” he said. “In Galatians, Christ lives in Paul. In Colossians, Christ is our life. In Philippians, to live is Christ.”

He looked at the three references.

“It's almost as though Paul is determined not to let us separate the life from the Person.”

Elías nodded.

“And John speaks in much the same way. In his first letter, he says that God has given us eternal life and that this life is in His Son. Then he makes the statement that whoever has the Son has the life.”

He turned to 1 John 5.

“Notice how directly he connects the two. Life is not presented as an independent possession that can be separated from Christ. It is in the Son.”

Marcos considered that.

“We often talk about eternal life as something we'll enjoy after we die.”

“Yes,” Elías said. “And Scripture certainly speaks about its future fullness. Yet John is describing something believers already possess. They have the Son, and they have life in Him.”

Anna arrived with breakfast.

Adrián moved his Bible to make room for his plate.

“Anna, we're discussing life.”

“That's nice.”

“Would you say you're enjoying yours?”

She looked at the crowded table, the open books, and the coffee cups.

“At the moment, I'm trying to keep four grown men fed.”

“A noble calling.”

“Especially one of them.”

She set down his breakfast.

He looked at the plate.

“Only two eggs?”

“That's what you ordered.”

“I was hoping you'd be generous.”

“Then you should have ordered three.”

She returned to the counter.

Adrián watched her go.

“She's remarkably consistent.”

Marcos smiled.

“Perhaps you could learn something from her.”

“I'm already learning a great deal. Apparently, I need to order more eggs.”

After breakfast, Elías returned to the passage in Galatians.

“There's something I want to be careful about,” he said. “When Paul says Christ lives in him, we shouldn't imagine that Paul has disappeared or that his personality has been replaced. He still speaks of living by faith. The relationship is real, and so is his participation.

“Nor should we reduce Christ's indwelling to a metaphor for adopting His teachings. Paul is saying something far greater than that he has learned to imitate Jesus. He speaks of Christ living in him.

“And this is where I think we need to distinguish the life from its expression.”

He turned to John 15.

“Jesus describes Himself as the vine and His disciples as the branches. The branch bears fruit because it remains in the vine. It doesn't create the vine's life through its own effort. The fruit is the expression of a life it receives.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“That changes the place of discipline.”

“How so?” Marcos asked.

“For years, I treated discipline as though it could produce spirituality. I believed that if I established the right practices and maintained them faithfully, spiritual life would be the result.

“Now I'm seeing that no amount of discipline can create Christ's life in me. I can train myself to follow a schedule. I can learn to control certain behaviors. I can become more knowledgeable and more consistent. Those things are within the range of human effort.

“I cannot produce Christ.

“And if Christ is already my life, then I don't need to produce Him. I need to understand what it means to live by faith in the One who dwells in me.”

Marcos listened carefully.

“There's something reassuring about that,” he said. “And something that challenges the way I've approached ministry.

“I tend to think in terms of accomplishing things. If we want to help a neighborhood, we organize people and resources. We make plans. We do the work. That's appropriate for the practical responsibilities involved.

“Yet I've sometimes carried that same thinking into my understanding of spiritual life. I assume that if we organize enough good activities, spiritual life will somehow be the outcome.

“Now I wonder whether we've confused the expression of life with the life itself.

“A church can have a full calendar without those activities proving its dependence upon Christ. A believer can have an equally full calendar. The number of things accomplished doesn't establish the source from which they came.”

Adrián looked at him.

“So the answer isn't to cancel everything.”

“No. That would be another way of making the activities themselves the issue.”

“Good,” Adrián said. “Because I rather enjoy some of them.”

Elías smiled.

“The point is not that activity is wrong. Think of Paul's own ministry. He worked tirelessly. He endured suffering, traveled extensively, taught, prayed, and cared for the churches. He was anything except inactive.

“Yet when he spoke about the source of his labor, he directed attention to the grace of God working with him. And when he described his life, he said Christ lived in him.

“We should be careful not to turn this into another rule: ‘A spiritual Christian must do less.’ That would miss the point just as surely as believing that doing more creates spirituality.”

Matías nodded.

“I think I understand. The life comes first. Activity may express that life. Activity doesn't manufacture it.”

The conversation wandered for a while.

Marcos mentioned a man he knew who had become exhausted by his responsibilities at church. The man was involved in nearly everything, and whenever he considered stepping away from a task, he felt guilty.

“He once told me he didn't know whether he was serving the Lord or simply afraid of disappointing people,” Marcos said.

Elías knew several people who could have said the same thing.

Matías thought about his own routines. He remembered mornings when he had prayed with genuine pleasure and mornings when he had hurried through the same prayers because he couldn't bear the thought of failing to complete them.

The activity had looked identical.

He had not experienced those mornings in the same way.

He was still considering that when he turned to Adrián.

“I've been thinking about something your grandson said,” Matías told him.

Adrián looked surprised.

“My grandson?”

“The question about God needing workers.”

“Oh, yes. That young man has a promising future in making adults uncomfortable.”

Matías smiled.

“I think it's connected to what we're discussing. We speak so often about what we must do for Christ that we can begin to imagine He's waiting for us to supply something He lacks.”

Elías turned toward him.

“That's an important distinction. Christ certainly calls believers to serve. He gives gifts to His people, and Scripture speaks plainly about good works. Yet He isn't dependent upon our independent ability to produce His life. We are dependent upon Him.”

Adrián considered this.

“It's a strange thing. We believe Christ is sufficient to save us, and then we spend years behaving as though the rest depends on what we can manage.”

Matías looked at Galatians 3.

“Which brings us back to Paul's question.”

He read it again.

“Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?”

The words no longer sounded to him like a challenge to become more disciplined.

They exposed the inadequacy of discipline as the source of spiritual life.

He thought of the years he had spent trying to improve himself into the kind of Christian he wanted to become.

He wasn't ashamed of every effort he'd made. Many of his habits had been useful. He had learned Scripture, kept commitments, and served people he loved.

Yet he could see how readily he had treated those accomplishments as the means of producing spirituality.

He looked across the table.

“I've spent much of my Christian life trying to live the Christian life.”

Adrián smiled.

“That sounds reasonable.”

“It always did to me.”

Matías looked at the open Bible.

“Then I read Paul saying that Christ lives in him. That Christ is our life. That to live is Christ.”

He rested his hand on the page.

“All these years I've been trying to produce the Christian life. And the Christian life is Christ.”

The words needed no immediate answer.

Outside, the afternoon light had shifted across the pavement. A couple came through the door, shaking the cold from their coats. Anna greeted them and pointed toward an empty table.

Elías thought about his wife.

They had been walking together again, often without any particular destination. At first he'd felt the need to suggest somewhere to go or something useful to discuss. Gradually he'd stopped.

He enjoyed her company.

He didn't need their walks to accomplish something in order for their time together to matter.

He knew the comparison had limits. His marriage was not the same as the believer's union with Christ. Yet he was beginning to recognize how readily he measured relationships by what they produced.

He wondered how often he had approached Christ with the same instinct.

What should I learn?

What should I accomplish?

What should I improve?

All worthwhile questions in their proper place.

He had been slower to consider the wonder of the One who was already his life.

They stayed longer than usual.

No one seemed particularly interested in ending the conversation, although there was no attempt to resolve every question before leaving.

As Marcos put on his coat, he said, “I think this changes how we'll have to look at the churches.”

Elías glanced toward him.

“In what way?”

“We've been examining what they did and what they valued. Now we're beginning to understand why none of those things can serve as a substitute for Christ Himself.”

Matías folded his paper and slipped it into his Bible.

“And perhaps why Paul was so troubled when the Galatians began looking elsewhere for what had begun in the Spirit.”

Adrián reached for his keys.

“I still have a question.”

The others looked at him.

“If Christ is our life, how did we manage to make so much of Christianity about everything except Him?”

It was not a question anyone felt obliged to answer before leaving.

Elías thought of Daniel and the young believer's questions that had caused so much discomfort among the elders. He wondered whether there was a connection between what the four friends were discovering and the fear that sometimes arose when someone spoke too freely about grace.

He wasn't ready to explain it.

He wanted to understand it.

Outside, they said goodbye and went their separate ways.

Matías sat in his car for a moment before starting the engine.

He thought about the sheet of paper tucked inside his Bible.

Three passages.

Three statements about Christ.

He had begun the week wondering how he could become a more spiritual man.

He was ending it thinking about the extraordinary fact that Christ Himself dwelt in him.

For once, he felt no urgency to turn the discovery into a new routine.

He started the car and drove home.

# Chapter 35
## Sin Management

Adrián was halfway through a story when Matías arrived.

Apparently, a friend had invited him to attend a men's breakfast at a church across town. Adrián had agreed, partly because he knew the man and partly because he'd been promised an excellent breakfast.

“The breakfast was very good,” he told Marcos and Elías. “I want to establish that before I say anything else. They had proper coffee, fresh bread, eggs, the whole thing. Someone had clearly put a great deal of work into it.”

“And the meeting?” Marcos asked.

“Well, that's where things became interesting.”

Matías pulled out his chair.

“What happened?”

“Nothing terrible. Everyone was friendly. The speaker was a pleasant man, very sincere. He talked about the difficulties men face in their Christian lives. Temptation, anger, bad habits, that sort of thing. Then he introduced a program the church was starting.”

Adrián took a drink of coffee.

“Six weeks. Each man gets a workbook, an accountability partner, a list of daily commitments, and a weekly meeting where he reports how well he's doing.”

Matías began removing his coat.

“That sounds fairly ordinary. What did he say would make it effective?”

“It was. That's what interested me. The speaker told us the program would help us become more spiritual. He said if we followed it faithfully, we'd begin to experience victory over the flesh.”

Adrián looked at the others.

“I kept thinking about what we discussed last Thursday. So when he finished, I asked him something.”

Elías smiled.

“I imagine you did.”

“I asked how keeping the commitments would give us victory over the flesh.”

Marcos set down his cup.

“How did he answer?”

“He said the program was designed to develop spiritual habits. I told him I understood that. Then I asked whether developing spiritual habits was the same thing as becoming spiritual.”

“And?”

“He said I was making things unnecessarily complicated.”

Matías laughed.

“That sounds familiar.”

“I assured him it was a talent I've been developing for years.”

Adrián smiled, then became more thoughtful.

“I didn't want to embarrass the man. He was trying to help people. And some of the things he recommended were perfectly sensible. If a man has a drinking problem, he may need help staying away from alcohol. If he's hurting his family, somebody needs to confront that. If he has a destructive habit, practical safeguards can be useful.

“What troubled me was the promise that following the program faithfully would bring victory over the flesh. I kept wondering how six weeks of human effort could produce something Paul says comes from the Spirit.”

Anna arrived with Matías's coffee.

“You're late today.”

“Only a few minutes.”

“That's what he said last week.”

She pointed toward Adrián.

“He told me you'd probably been detained by a theological emergency.”

Matías smiled.

“Not quite.”

“Good. I'd hate to think there was one of those before breakfast.”

She took his order and returned to the counter.

Marcos looked toward Adrián.

“Did you sign up?”

“No. I told the man I already have three accountability partners.”

He indicated his friends.

“Unfortunately, none of them is particularly encouraging.”

Elías laughed.

“That's because we know you.”

“Exactly. A terrible disadvantage.”

Matías had been thinking about the breakfast program.

“I understand why churches create things like that,” he said. “I've been involved in similar efforts. Sometimes people are genuinely struggling, and the church wants to help them. It would be irresponsible to see someone harming himself or others and simply tell him to have more faith.

“There's a place for correction. There's a place for practical help. Paul tells believers to restore someone caught in a trespass. He gives instructions about conduct, relationships, and responsibilities. We shouldn't pretend those passages aren't there.”

He opened his Bible to Galatians 6.

“Yet notice who Paul says should restore someone, and how it should be done. He addresses those who are spiritual and calls for gentleness. He also warns the one doing the restoring to consider himself.

“That interests me now. Because I can imagine a church taking that passage and turning it into an administrative procedure. Someone fails. We investigate. We establish requirements. We monitor compliance. We decide when the person has satisfied the conditions.

“Perhaps some practical steps are necessary. Still, we've changed something important if we begin to believe that the procedure itself can produce spiritual restoration.”

Marcos had spent years designing systems that were supposed to accomplish particular results. He understood both their usefulness and their limitations.

“In business, a system is only as good as the thing it's designed to accomplish,” he said. “If I want to make sure an aircraft receives its scheduled maintenance, I establish procedures. I want inspections documented, responsibilities assigned, and nothing overlooked. That's entirely appropriate.

“Yet suppose I decide that because the maintenance records are complete, the aircraft must be airworthy. That would be foolish. The paperwork matters because it helps us attend to the actual condition of the aircraft. The paperwork is not the aircraft's condition.

“I wonder whether churches sometimes make a similar mistake. We establish procedures to encourage good conduct, then begin treating compliance as evidence of spiritual health.

“A person attends every meeting, completes every assignment, reports to his accountability partner, and follows the rules. We conclude he's doing well.

“Perhaps he is. Perhaps those activities are genuinely useful to him. Yet all we've actually established is that he has followed the program.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Which means he could graduate with honors and still be miserable.”

“Quite possibly.”

“I've known a few graduates.”

“So have I,” Marcos said.

Elías was looking at Galatians 6.

“There's another danger. A system can make the people administering it feel spiritually superior to the people receiving correction. The leaders become the ones who have mastered the Christian life. Everyone else is measured against their expectations.

“And once that happens, even a genuine desire to help can become entangled with the flesh.”

Matías considered the passage.

“I've done that,” he said.

The admission came without drama.

“I've looked at people who were struggling and wondered why they couldn't simply become more disciplined. I knew the Scriptures. I knew what they ought to do. Sometimes I was impatient with them because I thought the answer was obvious.

“I wasn't always wrong about the behavior that needed to change. I was wrong to assume that greater discipline could produce the expression of Christ's life through them.”

Anna brought their breakfasts.

Marcos moved his Bible away from the edge of the table. Adrián inspected his plate with satisfaction.

“Now this,” he announced, “is a successful program.”

Anna glanced at him.

“You haven't tasted it yet.”

“I have faith.”

“You have an appetite.”

“An important distinction.”

She smiled and went to serve another table.

They ate for a while, discussing a mutual friend who had recently undergone surgery. Elías had spoken with him earlier in the week and reported that his recovery was going well.

Eventually Marcos returned to the subject.

“I think there's something else we need to distinguish. A church can manage behavior without ever addressing the source of that behavior. And in some situations, that's exactly what happens.

“Imagine a congregation where jealousy and strife have become common. People compete for influence. Leaders become defensive. Families take sides. Everyone knows there's a problem.

“The church responds by introducing rules about how disagreements must be handled. There are procedures for complaints, restrictions on who may speak about certain matters, and expectations about maintaining unity.

“Those measures might reduce the visible conflict. People may become more careful about what they say. Meetings might become more orderly.

“Yet the jealousy may remain. The pride may remain. The desire for influence may remain. The church has learned to control how the problem appears without necessarily addressing the problem itself.”

Elías looked at him.

“And if the leaders themselves are acting by the flesh, their efforts to control the situation may produce more of the very thing they're trying to prevent.”

“Exactly.”

Matías put down his fork.

“That's what troubles me about managing the problem this way. It suggests we're dealing with something that can be kept within acceptable limits.

“We don't usually say that openly. We say we're maintaining standards or protecting the church. Those can be legitimate concerns. Yet sometimes what we're actually doing is arranging things so that the same problems can continue without becoming too disruptive.”

Adrián had been listening.

“You know what people say when someone begins asking uncomfortable questions?”

Elías looked at him.

“I can think of several things.”

“Don't cause division. Don't undermine the leadership. We need to protect the testimony of the church. These matters should be handled privately.”

He set down his fork.

“Any of those statements might be appropriate in a particular situation. I understand that. Yet I've also seen them used to stop a conversation before anyone has examined whether the concern is legitimate.”

Elías thought of Daniel.

He remembered the young man's questions about confession and fellowship. The elders had not wanted those questions discussed outside their established boundaries.

They had been concerned about protecting the congregation from confusion.

He had understood their concern.

He still did.

Yet Daniel's question had not disappeared simply because he had been told to stop asking it.

“I've seen that happen,” Elías said. “And I've participated in it.”

He did not immediately explain.

Then he continued.

“Sometimes a church becomes so concerned with preserving order that it begins to regard any challenge to its established practices as a threat. It may have sound reasons for some of those practices. Others may deserve reconsideration.

“The problem arises when protecting the arrangement becomes more important than examining the truth.

“And if the arrangement depends upon keeping people from asking questions, then maintaining it may require increasingly forceful measures.”

Matías looked at him.

“Are you thinking about Daniel?”

“Yes.”

Elías folded his hands.

“I've been thinking about him quite a lot. Not because I believe every question he raised was necessarily correct. We haven't resolved all of them. What troubles me is how quickly the discussion became a question of whether he would submit.

“We were no longer simply asking what Scripture said. We were asking whether he would accept the answer we had already established.”

Marcos considered that.

“Then the procedure became more important than the question.”

“In that instance, I think it did.”

Adrián reached for the coffee pot and discovered it was empty.

He held it up.

“This is what happens when institutions fail to plan.”

Anna, passing behind him, took the pot from his hand.

“This institution has been serving you coffee for years.”

“And I'm deeply grateful.”

“You have an unusual way of showing it.”

“I'm working on my character.”

“Try working on your bill.”

She returned to the counter.

Adrián watched her go.

“There's a woman who believes in accountability.”

The others laughed.

Matías declined a refill. As Anna filled the other cups, he returned to his Bible.

“There's a passage we should look at. It speaks directly to the kind of thing we're discussing.”

He turned to Colossians 2.

“Paul describes regulations concerning what people may handle, taste, or touch. Then he says those practices have an appearance of wisdom in self-imposed worship, humility, and severity to the body, yet they are of no value against the indulgence of the flesh.”

He let the others find the passage.

“What strikes me is that the practices appear wise. They're serious. They demand restraint. A person following them may look extraordinarily disciplined.

“Yet Paul refuses to credit them with the power to deal with the flesh.

“That's difficult for someone like me. I instinctively believe that if a problem is serious, the answer must involve a sufficiently serious program. And if the program doesn't work, perhaps it needs to become stricter.

“Paul is challenging the assumption underneath that thinking.”

Elías looked at the surrounding verses.

“And the context matters. Paul has already told the Colossians that they died with Christ to the elementary principles of the world. He's questioning why they would submit themselves to regulations as though those regulations could accomplish what their union with Christ has already changed.

“He isn't saying practical wisdom is useless. He's exposing the inability of religious regulations to deal with the flesh.”

Marcos leaned back.

“So we can restrain certain actions. We can establish order. We can even improve outward conduct. What we cannot do is produce the life of Christ through those measures.”

Matías nodded.

“And that's the mistake I made for years.”

He looked at the others.

“I thought that if I became disciplined enough, spirituality would follow. I wasn't consciously trying to replace Christ. I believed I was becoming more faithful to Him.

“Now I see how easily the things I was doing became the source of my confidence.

“I knew whether I'd completed my reading. I knew whether I'd prayed. I knew whether I'd kept my commitments. I could measure all of it.

“What I couldn't measure so easily was whether I was actually depending upon Christ.”

Adrián studied him.

“Do you regret all those years?”

Matías thought for a moment.

“No. Not in the way you might expect. I learned a great deal of Scripture. I developed useful habits. I served people. Some of those years were wonderful.

“I regret treating my accomplishments as though they could make me spiritual.

“And I regret the pressure I sometimes placed on others because I believed they needed to accomplish the same things.”

He looked down at his Bible.

“I wonder how many people have been taught to measure their Christian lives that way.”

Elías turned to Romans 8.

“There's a distinction here that I think we need to preserve carefully,” he said.

He found the ninth verse.

“Paul tells believers that they are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit, if God's Spirit dwells in them.

“That's a statement about their position. He's not telling them to achieve a new position through improved behavior. He's describing what is true of those who belong to Christ.

“Yet believers can still act by the flesh. We saw that in Corinth. Paul addressed them as brothers, as people in Christ, while confronting their jealousy and strife.

“So we need to be careful with our language. A believer doesn't move out of Christ and into the flesh every time he acts according to the flesh. His conduct may contradict the life and position God has given him. His position is not created or maintained by that conduct.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“That changes how we think about correction.”

“It should,” Elías said. “If the believer is already in Christ, correction isn't an attempt to place him there. Nor is it an attempt to manufacture Christ's life through discipline. It should direct him toward the truth of what God has done and the life he already possesses in His Son.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Then perhaps that's why some churches are so frightened of grace.”

Matías turned toward him.

“Explain.”

“If you've built a system around controlling behavior, you need people to believe the system is what keeps them spiritual. You need the rules, the pressure, the measurements, and the people who enforce them.

“Then someone comes along and says that God has already placed believers in Christ. That Christ Himself is their life. That they cannot produce spirituality through their own effort.

“What happens to your system?”

Marcos considered the question.

“It would have to acknowledge its limitations.”

“Precisely. And some systems aren't particularly good at doing that.”

Elías thought again of Daniel.

He remembered the discomfort in the elders' meeting when the young man had asked whether a familiar teaching was actually supported by the passage they were using.

The question itself had not been an act of rebellion.

Yet it had threatened something they regarded as settled.

He wondered how much more threatening it might seem when someone questioned not merely a teaching, but an entire approach to Christian living.

Matías was still thinking about Adrián's observation.

“I understand the fear,” he said. “People can misuse grace. They can use it as an excuse for conduct that harms themselves and others. I've seen that happen, and I don't want to minimize it.

“Yet if we respond by teaching people that rules and discipline are the source of spiritual life, we haven't protected the gospel. We've replaced its power with something else.

“We're telling people that Christ is sufficient to save them, then suggesting that human effort is sufficient to make them spiritual.”

The table was quiet for a while.

Anna came to collect the plates.

“Everything all right?”

“Very good,” Marcos said.

She looked at Adrián.

“And you?”

“I'm considering a complaint.”

“About what?”

“The fact that breakfast is over.”

She smiled.

“There's always tomorrow.”

“An encouraging message.”

“Don't make it theological.”

Adrián laughed.

“I wouldn't dream of it.”

They remained at the table after the plates were cleared.

Marcos had been considering something that troubled him.

“We should probably admit that churches can't simply abandon every practical responsibility,” he said. “People still need to be protected from harm. Leaders must address serious misconduct. Conflicts need attention. There are occasions when boundaries are necessary.

“The problem isn't the existence of every rule or procedure. It's the belief that those things can produce spirituality.

“And perhaps another problem is that systems are easier to administer than relationships. You can write a policy. You can establish a committee. You can measure compliance.

“It's much harder to sit with someone who's struggling, listen carefully, understand what is happening, and help him see what is true in Christ.”

Elías looked at him.

“And it requires humility from the person offering help.”

“Exactly. A procedure can be administered by someone who has no present dependence upon Christ. Genuine spiritual care cannot be reduced to the procedure.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“That's an uncomfortable thought for a man who likes procedures.”

Marcos laughed.

“I've noticed.”

Adrián looked around the table.

“So what are we calling all of this?”

“What do you mean?” Elías asked.

“The programs. The regulations. The pressure. The endless attempts to keep people behaving like spiritual Christians.”

Matías considered the question.

“Sin management.”

Adrián nodded.

“That sounds about right.”

Elías closed his Bible.

“And it leaves the central problem untouched if it never brings people back to Christ.”

Marcos looked at the closed book.

“There's something almost tragic about that. A church can become very skilled at managing behavior while losing confidence in the very life God has given His people.”

Adrián reached for his coat.

“Then I suppose we have another question.”

Matías smiled.

“I was wondering when you'd say that.”

Adrián looked at him.

“If the flesh cannot produce spiritual life, how can the flesh possibly cure itself?”

Elías stood.

“That's worth examining.”

They gathered their things and made their way toward the counter.

Anna was preparing another pot of coffee.

“Finished solving the world's problems?”

“Not quite,” Adrián said. “We've discovered that some of our solutions may be part of the problem.”

She looked at him.

“That explains a great deal.”

He laughed and paid the bill.

Outside, the air was colder than it had been when they arrived.

Matías stood beside his car, thinking about the morning.

He had spent years believing that discipline was the path to spirituality. Even now, the habit of thinking that way came naturally to him.

He knew how to establish a routine.

He knew how to work toward a goal.

He knew how to measure progress.

And he was beginning to understand that none of those abilities could produce the life of Christ.

He thought of Paul's words to the Galatians.

*Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?*

He had always wanted to be a faithful Christian.

Now he was learning that faithfulness could never be manufactured by the flesh.

As he drove home, he found himself returning to a question that had once seemed almost too simple to deserve much attention.

If Christ Himself was his life, why had he spent so many years trying to create one of his own?

# Chapter 36
## When Carnality Tries to Cure Carnality

Adrián was standing beside the table, trying to balance four coffee cups.

Anna watched from behind the counter.

“You know we have trays.”

“I know.”

“They're designed for exactly what you're doing.”

“I prefer a personal challenge.”

“You're going to spill something.”

“Your confidence in me is touching.”

He made it to the table without spilling a drop.

Then, while reaching for his chair, he knocked one of the cups with his elbow.

A small stream of coffee ran across the tabletop.

Anna handed him a cloth.

“Congratulations.”

Adrián surveyed the damage.

“I was doing very well until the end.”

“That's what makes it so impressive.”

By the time the others arrived, the table was dry, the coffee had been replaced, and Adrián had decided not to discuss the incident further.

Unfortunately, Anna had other plans.

“Your friend has already had an adventure,” she told them.

Adrián looked at her.

“I thought we agreed to move forward.”

“I didn't agree to anything.”

Elías laughed as he took off his coat.

“Some things never change.”

They talked about their families for a while.

Matías had spoken with his father again. He was doing better, although he still found the evenings difficult since his wife's death. Matías had been calling more frequently, sometimes without having anything particular to discuss.

“I used to think I needed to have something useful to say,” he told them. “Now I just call. Sometimes we talk about the weather or what he had for lunch. Last night he spent ten minutes telling me about a neighbor's dog.”

“Was it an interesting dog?” Adrián asked.

“Not especially.”

“Then you're a good son.”

Matías smiled.

“I think he simply likes having someone to talk to.”

Elías understood that better than he might have a few months earlier. His walks with his wife had become a regular part of their evenings. Sometimes they talked at length. Other times they walked beside each other without saying much.

He had stopped feeling the need to fill every quiet moment.

Eventually Marcos returned to their previous discussion.

“I've been thinking about what we said concerning sin management. Particularly the idea that a church can become very effective at controlling behavior without ever dealing with the source of it.”

Adrián reached for his coffee.

“I've seen that happen with individuals too. A man has a terrible temper. He gets into trouble, hurts people, and decides he needs to change. So he reads books, attends meetings, memorizes verses, and asks someone to hold him accountable.

“After a while he becomes much better at controlling his anger. His family is relieved. His friends notice the difference. Everyone congratulates him.

“And perhaps they should. At least people aren't being shouted at anymore.

“Then something interesting happens. He begins looking down on people who still struggle with their tempers. He becomes impatient with them. He tells them they simply need to exercise more discipline.

“So now he's not shouting at people anymore. He's quietly despising them.”

Marcos considered the example.

“The behavior changed. The source of his confidence may not have.”

“Exactly. And I wonder whether we sometimes congratulate ourselves too soon.”

Elías reached for his Bible.

“Paul describes something related to that in Galatians. He lists the works of the flesh, including jealousy, strife, rivalries, divisions, and envy. Then he describes the fruit of the Spirit.

“The distinction is more significant than a list of bad behaviors followed by a list of good ones.

“Paul identifies two different sources.”

He turned to Galatians 5 and read the familiar words about the works of the flesh.

Then he continued into the following verses.

“But the fruit of the Spirit is love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faith, gentleness, and self-control.”

Matías was following the passage in his own Bible.

“There's something I've never paid enough attention to,” he said.

“What?” Marcos asked.

“Paul calls one list the *works of the flesh* and the other the *fruit of the Spirit*.

“He doesn't call the second list the works of the disciplined Christian.”

Adrián smiled.

“That would make a rather different verse.”

“It would. Yet I think that's how I've understood it for years.”

Matías rested his hands on the table.

“I believed that becoming spiritual meant developing those qualities. I needed to become more loving, more patient, more gentle, and more self-controlled. So I worked at it.

“And when I struggled, I assumed I wasn't working hard enough.

“I don't think I ever stopped to consider what Paul was actually saying. He calls it the fruit of the Spirit because the Spirit produces it.”

Elías looked at him.

“That's an important distinction.”

Matías continued.

“I can learn to control my temper. I can discipline myself to speak politely when I'm irritated. I can train myself to be more patient with difficult people.

“Those changes may be genuinely beneficial. They may spare other people a great deal of unpleasantness.

“Yet none of them proves that the Spirit produced the behavior.

“A man can become remarkably self-controlled through determination alone. He can even become proud of his self-control.

“And if he does, he may have replaced one expression of the flesh with another.”

Marcos looked down at the passage.

“So the appearance of the behavior doesn't necessarily establish its source.”

“No,” Matías said. “And that's what I find so unsettling.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián moved his coffee cup farther from the edge of the table.

She noticed.

“Learning?”

“Constantly.”

“That's encouraging.”

“I'm hoping to be fully qualified to carry coffee by Christmas.”

“Don't rush yourself.”

She returned to the counter.

Adrián watched her go.

“There's a woman who understands gradual improvement.”

Marcos laughed.

“And yet you've been practicing for years.”

“Some of us require more grace than others.”

They began eating.

For a while the conversation turned to work. Marcos had been dealing with a scheduling problem involving one of his aircraft.
