Matías offered a suggestion, and the two spent several minutes discussing the difficulties of coordinating people who all believed their own responsibilities were the most urgent.

Adrián listened with amusement.

“It's reassuring to know you have the same problems as everyone else.”

Marcos looked at him.

“People?”

“Exactly.”

Eventually Matías returned to Galatians.

“There's another thing I want to mention. Something I think may be even more deceptive than discipline.”

Elías looked up.

“Go ahead.”

Matías considered how to express it.

“We often talk about living the Christian life with the help of the Holy Spirit.”

Adrián looked puzzled.

“What's wrong with that?”

“That's what I asked myself. It sounds perfectly spiritual. And Scripture certainly says the Spirit helps us. Romans 8 speaks directly about His help in our weakness.

“Yet I've been thinking about what I actually meant when I used that expression.

“For most of my Christian life, I believed my responsibility was to live as Christ commanded. I needed to obey, resist temptation, develop godly character, and become increasingly spiritual.

“I knew I couldn't do those things successfully on my own. So I asked the Holy Spirit to help me.

“And I thought that was dependence upon God.”

He took a sip of coffee.

“Now consider the arrangement.

“I'm still the one undertaking the Christian life. I'm the one trying to produce the results. I decide what needs to be accomplished, establish the necessary disciplines, and ask the Holy Spirit to provide the strength I lack.

“The Spirit becomes my assistant in an undertaking that remains fundamentally mine.

“And if I succeed, I feel pleased with how well I've lived. I may even thank God for helping me.”

Adrián smiled.

“Very generous of you.”

Matías laughed.

“That's precisely the problem. God receives the acknowledgment. I remain the successful Christian.

“I'm not saying every believer who asks the Spirit for help understands things that way. I'm describing how I understood it.

“I had made myself responsible for producing the Christian life, and I expected the Holy Spirit to help me produce it.

“Yet Paul doesn't describe the Christian life that way.”

He turned to Galatians 2.

“Listen to what he says.”

He read the twentieth verse.

“I have been crucified with Christ, and it is no longer I that live, but Christ lives in me. That life which I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me, and gave himself up for me.”

Matías looked at his friends.

“Christ lives in me.

“Paul doesn't describe an independent Christian receiving assistance from God so he can become spiritual.

“He identifies Christ as the One living in him.

“And that is something entirely different from the arrangement I had imagined.”

Elías remained with the passage.

“I think we need to preserve the distinction carefully,” he said. “The Holy Spirit truly does help believers. Scripture is explicit about that. We should never suggest otherwise.

“The question is whether we understand His help as God's work in and through us, or as additional strength supplied to an independent human effort.

“Paul says the Spirit of God dwells in believers. He also speaks of Christ being in them. The Christian life is grounded in God's presence and work.

“It's not an independent life that occasionally receives divine assistance.”

Marcos had been listening carefully.

“Then even asking God for help can become part of a fleshly approach to spirituality.”

“It can,” Matías said, “if what I'm asking Him to help me accomplish is a Christian life that I'm still trying to produce myself.”

He looked again at the passage.

“I don't think I understood how completely I had made myself responsible for something only Christ could be.”

Adrián leaned back.

“We've found another way for the flesh to remain somebody.”

Matías smiled.

“And this time it even gives the Holy Spirit a supporting role.”

Marcos had been turning over the implications of what Matías said.

“I think that explains something I've observed in church life. We can organize ministries entirely around human ability and then ask God to bless what we've organized.

“We identify the people with the strongest skills. We establish objectives, prepare budgets, develop schedules, and measure the results. Then we pray that the Holy Spirit will help us succeed.

“Now, none of those practical activities is necessarily wrong. I've spent my life organizing complicated undertakings. Planning is useful.

“Yet we can become so confident in our ability to produce results that dependence upon Christ becomes little more than something we acknowledge before beginning.

“We pray for His help, then proceed as though everything depends upon our competence.”

Adrián considered that.

“And when it succeeds, we call it a work of God.”

“Sometimes it genuinely may be,” Marcos replied. “That's the difficulty. The activity itself doesn't tell us enough about its source.

“We may accomplish something worthwhile through careful organization. We may even help people in significant ways.

“Yet successful organization is not the same thing as spiritual life.”

Elías turned to 1 Corinthians.

“Paul made a similar distinction when he spoke about his own labor. He said he had worked more abundantly than the other apostles, then immediately added that it was not he, but the grace of God with him.

“He wasn't denying that he had worked. He was identifying the source of what had been accomplished.

“And that's important because we're not discussing whether Christians should be active or inactive.

“We're discussing whose life is being expressed.”

Matías looked toward Elías.

“Then the question isn't how much I accomplish.”

“Not as a measure of spirituality.”

“Or how disciplined I become.”

“Not as the source of spiritual life.”

Matías looked at the passage in Galatians.

“It's the fruit of the Spirit. It cannot simultaneously be the fruit of my independent effort.”

Elías considered that.

“The fruit appears in the believer's life. The believer genuinely bears it. Jesus Himself uses that language in John 15.

“Yet the life producing the fruit comes from the vine.

“The branch doesn't possess an independent source of life. Its fruitfulness depends upon the vine.”

He turned to the passage.

“Jesus says, ‘I am the vine. You are the branches.’ Then He says that the one who remains in Him bears much fruit, because apart from Him we can do nothing.

“Notice that the branch bears fruit. It isn't absent from the picture.

“Yet it cannot manufacture the life that produces the fruit.”

Adrián smiled.

“So the branch doesn't need a course on how to become a grape factory.”

Marcos laughed.

“Apparently not.”

“It simply needs the life of the vine.”

Elías smiled.

“Yes. And that life is not something the branch produces.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“Paul doesn't call it the fruit of Matías, assisted by the Holy Spirit.

“He calls it the fruit of the Spirit.

“I've been trying to produce something that was never mine to produce.”

No one rushed to respond.

Matías remained looking at the passage.

For years, he had measured his Christian life by the things he could accomplish. The more consistent his habits became, the more confident he felt. When he failed, he returned to the same methods with greater determination.

He had believed he was depending upon God.

Now he was beginning to recognize how often he had been depending upon himself while asking God to make his efforts successful.

The conversation eventually returned to the way churches deal with sin.

Marcos spoke about a congregation he'd known where a disagreement between several families had grown into a serious division.

“The leaders were genuinely concerned,” he said. “They wanted to protect the church from further conflict. So they introduced procedures for handling complaints and told people not to discuss the matter outside the leadership meetings.

“The public arguments stopped. Services became calmer. People learned to avoid certain subjects.

“After several months, the leaders announced that peace had been restored.

“Yet when I spoke privately with people from the congregation, the resentment was still there. The families were still divided. They had simply learned not to speak about it.”

Adrián looked at him.

“So the church had become peaceful.”

“Quiet,” Marcos corrected. “I'm not sure it had become peaceful.”

Elías thought about that distinction.

“A church can use authority to produce outward conformity. It can require people to stop arguing. It can establish boundaries and impose consequences.

“Sometimes those measures are necessary to protect people from harm. We shouldn't dismiss legitimate correction.

“Yet none of those things can produce the fruit of the Spirit.

“Authority can require silence. It cannot manufacture peace.

“Discipline can restrain an angry response. It cannot manufacture love.

“Rules can require people to behave respectfully. They cannot produce the gentleness of Christ.”

Matías looked at Galatians 5.

“And if the leaders themselves are acting by the flesh, their attempts to correct others may produce even more carnality.”

He thought for a moment.

“I remember a situation years ago when a younger man in our congregation was struggling. I was asked to speak with him. I met with him several times, gave him passages to read, and helped him establish some practical boundaries.

“He improved. At least, his behavior improved.

“And I remember feeling rather pleased with myself.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Because you'd helped him?”

“Yes. And helping him was good. I don't want to pretend otherwise.

“Yet I also enjoyed being the man who had helped him. I felt competent. Spiritually mature. I knew how to handle these situations.

“Then some months later, he struggled again. I was frustrated with him. I thought he'd wasted my time.

“Looking back, I wonder how much of my frustration came from concern for him and how much came from the fact that his failure threatened my sense of having succeeded.”

Marcos understood.

“You'd made yourself responsible for producing the outcome.”

“Exactly.”

Matías turned to Galatians 6.

“Paul tells those who are spiritual to restore someone caught in a trespass with gentleness. Then he warns the person doing the restoring to consider himself.

“I've read that passage many times. Yet I don't think I appreciated how easily the person offering correction can become proud of his own spiritual qualifications.

“The flesh can take satisfaction in correcting the flesh.

“And when it does, the correction itself becomes another expression of the problem.”

Elías looked at the surrounding verses.

“Paul goes on to warn against thinking we're something when we're nothing. He also cautions against comparing ourselves with others.

“It's remarkable how naturally those things can appear in religious service.

“A man may be correct about another person's failure. He may even offer useful counsel. Yet his own confidence can be resting in the very flesh that cannot produce spiritual life.”

Anna came to clear their plates.

“Another productive morning?”

Adrián smiled.

“We've discovered that it's possible to become proud of overcoming pride.”

She looked at him.

“That sounds exhausting.”

“It is.”

“Have you tried being ordinary?”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián considered her suggestion.

“I don't think I'm qualified.”

“That's what I thought.”

She gathered the plates and returned to the counter.

Elías watched her go, smiling.

Then he looked toward the others.

“There's something I want to return to before we finish.”

He opened Romans 8.

“Last week we discussed the difference between being in the flesh and acting by the flesh. Paul tells believers that they are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit, if God's Spirit dwells in them.

“That's their position.

“Yet believers can still act by the flesh. Corinth demonstrates that plainly.

“And this is where I think many of our approaches to spiritual growth become confused.

“We begin treating the Christian life as though believers must accomplish something to become what God has already made them in Christ.

“We tell them to work harder, become more disciplined, and develop spiritual character. We may even tell them to ask the Holy Spirit for enough strength to succeed.

“Yet Paul begins with what God has done.

“The believer is in Christ. The Spirit dwells in him. Christ is his life.

“Those realities are not rewards for successful Christian conduct.”

Matías listened carefully.

“Then correction should begin with what is true of the believer in Christ.”

“It should be grounded in that truth,” Elías said.

“And we need to understand something else. If the flesh cannot produce the fruit of the Spirit, then it cannot become spiritual by being subjected to increasingly sophisticated methods of improvement.

“The problem is not that our methods are insufficiently effective.

“The problem is that we're asking the flesh to accomplish something it cannot do.”

Marcos looked at him.

“Which means the flesh cannot cure the flesh.”

“No.”

“And even if it becomes very successful at changing behavior, it remains incapable of producing Christ's life.”

“Precisely.”

Adrián sat back.

“Well, gentlemen, that eliminates quite a few of our favorite solutions.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“Nearly all of mine.”

They remained at the table longer than usual.

For a while nobody opened another passage. They talked about the week ahead, a mutual friend's birthday, and whether Adrián's latest restaurant recommendation was worth the drive.

Then Matías returned to something Elías had said.

“There's a question we haven't answered.”

Elías looked toward him.

“Which one?”

“If the flesh cannot produce spiritual life, and even our attempts to correct it can become another expression of the flesh, what has God actually done about it?”

Elías considered the question.

For years he had taught that the Christian life required believers to recognize sin, confess it, resist temptation, and pursue holiness. He had believed those instructions were sufficient to explain how Christians should live.

Yet their conversations had exposed something he could no longer overlook.

Human effort could restrain behavior.

Religious discipline could produce impressive habits.

Church authority could enforce outward conformity.

None of those things could produce the life of Christ.

And Scripture did not present the flesh as something God intended to improve until it became spiritual.

Elías opened his Bible to Romans 8.

“There's a statement here that I think we need to examine.”

He found the third verse.

He read slowly.

“For what the law couldn't do, in that it was weak through the flesh, God did, sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful flesh and for sin, he condemned sin in the flesh.”

Matías looked at the words.

“Read the beginning again.”

Elías did.

“For what the law couldn't do, in that it was weak through the flesh, God did.”

Matías repeated the final words quietly.

“God did.”

Elías left the Bible open.

“Paul doesn't say that God discovered a more effective method for the flesh to improve itself.

“He says the law could not accomplish what was necessary because of the weakness of the flesh.

“And then he tells us that God acted.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Through His Son.”

“Yes.”

Adrián was no longer smiling.

“So this isn't another program for us to follow.”

“No,” Elías said. “Paul is describing something God has accomplished.”

Matías looked again at the verse.

For years, he had approached his Christian life as an undertaking for which he was ultimately responsible.

He needed to become more disciplined.

He needed to become more obedient.

He needed to develop spiritual character.

And when he failed, he needed to try again, this time with greater determination and more help from the Holy Spirit.

Now he was reading something different.

What the law could not do because of the flesh, God did.

He wasn't being offered another method of self-improvement.

He was being directed to an act of God.

Elías turned a few pages back, to Romans 6.

“Paul says something else that belongs with this.”

He read the sixth verse.

“Knowing this, that our old man was crucified with him, that the body of sin might be done away with, so that we would no longer be in bondage to sin.”

Matías looked up.

“Was crucified.”

“Yes.”

“Not something we're being told to accomplish.”

“No. Paul is declaring what has happened in connection with Christ.”

Elías looked at the others.

“I think we've spent so much time trying to understand how Christians should deal with the flesh that we haven't given sufficient attention to what God has already done concerning it.”

Marcos considered that.

“And the cross is where Paul takes us.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

He closed his Bible gently.

“Perhaps we should begin there next Thursday.”

They gathered their things and made their way toward the counter.

Anna was preparing another pot of coffee.

“Finished for today?”

“For today,” Adrián said.

“Good. I need that table eventually.”

“We're considering applying for permanent residency.”

“Application denied.”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián looked disappointed.

“Not even an interview?”

“I've known you long enough.”

They said goodbye and stepped outside.

The morning air was cool.

Matías stood beside his car for a moment.

He thought about the passages they had read.

The works of the flesh.

The fruit of the Spirit.

Christ living in the believer.

And the words from Romans that had remained with him.

*What the law couldn't do ... God did.*

He had spent years believing that the answer to his failures was greater determination. Even when he asked the Holy Spirit for help, he had often imagined himself as the one responsible for producing the Christian life.

Now he was beginning to understand why that approach had never brought him the confidence he sought.

The flesh could not produce the fruit of the Spirit.

It could not become spiritual through discipline.

It could not cure itself.

And God had never asked it to accomplish what only He could do.

Matías opened his car door, then looked once more at the Bible in his hand.

He knew Christ had died for his sins.

He had believed that for years.

What he was beginning to discover was that Scripture spoke of the cross in connection with something far more extensive than the forgiveness of his failures.

It spoke of his own crucifixion with Christ.

He wanted to understand what that meant.

And for once, he wasn't thinking about what he needed to do to make it happen.

He was thinking about what God had already done.

# Chapter 37
## The Cross Paul Would Not Leave

Marcos had been thinking about the cross all week.

He had heard the message of Christ crucified since he was young. He knew the familiar passages. He believed that Jesus had died for his sins, that forgiveness came through His blood, and that salvation was received by faith.

Those truths were precious to him.

Yet something from their last conversation had unsettled an assumption he had carried for years.

He had always regarded the cross primarily as the place where the Christian life began.

After that, the believer had to learn how to live.

And most of the instruction he had received about living concerned what he needed to do.

He wondered whether he had been overlooking something important.

At Deb's Place, Adrián was examining the breakfast menu when Marcos arrived.

“You're early,” Marcos said.

“I'm conducting research.”

“On breakfast?”

“An important subject. They've added something new.”

Marcos glanced at the menu.

“It's an omelette.”

“Not just an omelette. A Mediterranean omelette.”

“You don't like olives.”

“I know. That's the difficulty.”

Marcos sat down.

“Then order something else.”

Adrián looked disappointed.

“You've taken all the excitement out of it.”

Elías arrived a few minutes later, followed by Matías. They greeted one another, ordered breakfast, and talked about the week.

Eventually Marcos opened his Bible.

“I've been thinking about something since our last conversation.”

Adrián looked up.

“The cross?”

“Yes.”

“I suspected we'd be returning to that.”

Marcos smiled.

“I think Paul returned to it more often than I've appreciated.”

He opened to the beginning of 1 Corinthians.

“When we started examining Corinth, we were interested in their divisions, their spiritual gifts, and the jealousy among them. We wanted to understand why Paul called them fleshly.

“Then we moved to Galatia and discovered that the flesh could also express itself through religious effort.

“We've spent a great deal of time examining the problem.

“This week I went back to the beginning of Paul's first letter to Corinth, and something struck me.”

Elías leaned forward.

“What was that?”

“Paul doesn't begin by offering them a program for correcting their behavior.

“He begins by directing their attention to Christ.”

Marcos turned the Bible so the others could follow.

“In the first chapter, he speaks about the divisions among them. Some were identifying themselves with Paul, others with Apollos, others with Cephas.

“Then he asks whether Christ has been divided and whether Paul was crucified for them.

“It's remarkable how quickly he brings the cross into the discussion.”

Matías found the passage.

“He's challenging the basis of their loyalties.”

“Yes,” Marcos said. “They were attaching importance to the men they followed. Paul directs their attention to the One who was crucified for them.”

He read further.

“For the word of the cross is foolishness to those who are perishing, but to us who are being saved it is the power of God.”

Adrián looked at the verse.

“I've heard that passage many times.”

“So have I,” Marcos said. “And I usually thought about it in connection with evangelism. The message of the cross seems foolish to those who don't believe, yet it is God's power to save.

“That's certainly part of what Paul is saying.

“Yet look at what Paul is doing in this letter.

“He brings the cross into his appeal against their divisions. He's confronting their present loyalties.

“And he tells them that the word of the cross is the power of God.”

Elías read the verse again.

“Notice that Paul says ‘to us who are being saved.’ He includes himself and the believers in that statement. The message of the cross is not something he considers useful only for bringing people to faith.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's what I've been thinking.

“We often treat the cross as the beginning of the Christian life. We preach Christ crucified so that people may be saved. Then, once they're believers, we begin teaching them how to live.

“And so much of that teaching becomes a discussion of discipline, obedience, resisting temptation, and correcting behavior.

“Yet Paul doesn't seem willing to leave the cross behind.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Perfect.”

She raised an eyebrow.

“You haven't tasted it.”

“I've learned to recognize excellence.”

“Then you should recognize your bill when it comes.”

Marcos laughed.

Anna smiled and returned to the counter.

Adrián picked up his fork.

“Continue, Marcos. I'm listening.”

Marcos turned to another passage.

“Paul says something else in the same chapter.”

He read from verse twenty-two.

“Seeing that Jews ask for signs, and Greeks seek after wisdom, but we preach Christ crucified.”

He stopped.

“Think about that for a moment.

“The Jews were looking for signs. The Greeks were seeking wisdom. Paul understood what people expected from a religious message.

“Yet he didn't adjust his message to satisfy those expectations.

“He preached Christ crucified.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Which was offensive to one group and foolish to the other.”

“Yes,” Marcos said. “Paul says that plainly.

“And then he describes Christ as the power of God and the wisdom of God.

“That's what interests me. People were looking for something impressive. Something that would satisfy their expectations of power or wisdom.

“And God presented them with a crucified Christ.”

Elías considered the passage.

“The cross leaves no room for human boasting. Paul develops that thought through the rest of the chapter.

“He reminds the Corinthians that not many of them were wise according to the flesh, mighty, or noble. Then he explains that God chose what the world regarded as foolish and weak.

“And he tells them why.

“So that no flesh should boast before God.”

Matías read the words carefully.

“That sounds familiar.”

Adrián smiled.

“Very familiar.”

Elías continued.

“Consider what we've discovered about the four churches.

“Corinth had become impressed with spiritual gifts, personalities, and human distinctions.

“Galatia was being drawn toward religious achievement.

“Laodicea was confident in its own sufficiency.

“And Ephesus, despite Christ's genuine commendations, had fallen from its first love.

“In each case, we've had to reconsider what people regard as evidence of spiritual strength.

“Now Paul tells the Corinthians that God has acted in such a way that no flesh may boast before Him.”

He rested his hand on the Bible.

“The cross is not designed to make human ability impressive.

“It removes the basis for boasting in human ability.”

Matías had been thinking about something Marcos said earlier.

“I wonder whether this explains why I've always had difficulty with the idea that discipline cannot produce spirituality.”

The others listened.

“Discipline is something I understand. I know how to work toward a goal. I know how to develop habits, overcome obstacles, and improve my performance.

“And those abilities have served me well in ordinary life.

“When I became a Christian, I assumed that spiritual growth would follow a similar pattern. I needed to apply myself, become more consistent, and develop the qualities Scripture describes.

“I knew salvation was by grace. I never believed I could earn it.

“Yet once I was saved, I thought the responsibility for becoming spiritual rested largely upon me.

“And if I couldn't accomplish it on my own, I would ask the Holy Spirit to help me.”

He looked down at the Bible.

“Now I wonder whether I was treating the cross as the means by which God saved me, while treating my own discipline as the means by which I would become spiritual.”

Marcos considered that.

“I think many believers understand the Christian life that way.”

Matías nodded.

“And it sounds reasonable. We believe Christ died for our sins. Then we assume we must do our part.

“Yet Paul keeps bringing the Corinthians back to something God has accomplished.

“He doesn't tell them that the solution to their carnality is to become more impressive Christians.

“He directs them to Christ crucified.”

Adrián leaned back.

“That's quite different from telling them to try harder.”

“It is,” Matías said.

“And it troubles me because trying harder has been my answer to almost everything.”

Elías turned to the second chapter of 1 Corinthians.

“There's another statement here that deserves our attention.”

He read:

“For I determined not to know anything among you, except Jesus Christ, and him crucified.”

He looked at his friends.

“Paul had a great deal he could have taught the Corinthians. And, of course, he did teach them many things throughout the letter.

“So he isn't saying that he refused to discuss any subject except the crucifixion.

“He's describing the governing center of his message.

“He was determined that their confidence would rest in God's power rather than human wisdom.”

Marcos looked at the following verses.

“And he says that directly.”

“Yes.”

Elías read from verse five.

“That your faith wouldn't stand in the wisdom of men, but in the power of God.”

He closed the Bible halfway.

“I've taught this passage many times. Usually I've emphasized Paul's refusal to rely upon impressive speaking or persuasive techniques.

“That's certainly present in the text.

“Yet I'm beginning to appreciate something else.

“Paul was not merely concerned about how the gospel was presented. He was concerned about where the Corinthians' faith rested.

“They had become impressed with human wisdom and human distinctions.

“Paul wanted their confidence established in God's power.

“And he directed them to Christ crucified.”

Adrián considered that.

“So the cross isn't simply the message that gets us into Christianity.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's central to understanding the life God has given us.”

The conversation continued for some time.

Marcos returned to the question that had troubled him during the week.

“I've been thinking about the way churches respond to carnality.

“When people become divided, we introduce programs to encourage unity.

“When they struggle with sin, we establish accountability systems.

“When they become careless, we emphasize discipline.

“Some of those measures may serve useful purposes. We've already discussed that.

“Yet none of them can produce spiritual life.

“And now I find it remarkable that Paul, writing to a church he explicitly calls fleshly, places such emphasis on the cross.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Are you suggesting the cross solves carnality?”

Marcos considered the question.

“I'm suggesting we need to understand what Paul is telling us before we decide how to answer that.

“We've spent years trying to manage the flesh. We've tried to restrain it, correct it, and improve its behavior.

“Yet Paul speaks about the cross as the power of God.

“I don't think he regarded it as merely the beginning of the Christian life.”

Elías opened to Romans 6.

“And there's a reason we need to be careful here.

“The cross is not another method for us to apply.

“We mustn't replace our programs for managing the flesh with a new program called ‘applying the cross.’

“Paul speaks about something God has done through Christ.

“He tells believers that their old self was crucified with Him.

“That's not an instruction to arrange their own crucifixion through greater determination.

“It's a declaration about what has happened in their identification with Christ.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“Then the cross is not something the flesh can use to improve itself.”

“No,” Elías said.

“The cross is God's answer to something the flesh could never accomplish.”

Marcos turned to Romans 8.

“That's what we read last week.”

He found the third verse.

“For what the law couldn't do, in that it was weak through the flesh, God did, sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful flesh and for sin, he condemned sin in the flesh.”

He looked up.

“God did.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“Those words have been with me all week.”

Marcos continued.

“The law couldn't accomplish what was necessary because of the flesh.

“And God acted through His Son.

“That changes the entire way we approach the question.

“We're not looking for a more effective method of managing carnality.

“We're beginning to examine what God has already accomplished.”

Adrián had been quiet for several minutes.

Then he spoke.

“You know what surprises me?”

Marcos looked toward him.

“What?”

“That we've spent so many years hearing about the cross and still managed to treat it as something we leave behind once we're saved.

“We believe Christ died for our sins. We're grateful for forgiveness. We know salvation is by grace.

“Then we spend the rest of our lives trying to become the Christians we think we ought to be.

“And when we fail, we try harder.”

He looked around the table.

“Sometimes we ask the Holy Spirit to help us try harder.”

Matías smiled.

“I recognize that approach.”

“So do I,” Adrián said.

“And now we're discovering that Paul never moved away from the cross.

“He brought the Corinthians back to it.

“He reminded the Galatians of Christ crucified.

“And when he explained the believer's relationship to sin, he spoke about being crucified with Christ.”

Elías nodded.

“The cross is not merely where forgiveness was secured. Scripture connects it with our death with Christ, our freedom from sin's dominion, and the end of boasting in the flesh.

“Those are truths we need to examine carefully.

“We shouldn't assume we understand everything the cross accomplished simply because we understand that Christ died for our sins.”

Matías looked down at Romans 6.

“Then perhaps we've been asking the wrong question.”

Adrián smiled.

“We've done that before.”

Matías laughed.

“Yes, we have.”

He continued.

“We've been asking how Christians can overcome the flesh.

“Last week we began asking what God has already done concerning it. We need to stay with that question.”

Anna brought the bill. Adrián paid it, and they gathered their things.

Outside, the afternoon was cool, with a light wind moving along the street.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars, reluctant to end the conversation.

Matías looked toward Elías.

“I'd like to spend more time in Romans 6.”

“So would I,” Elías said.

“And Galatians,” Marcos added.

Adrián smiled.

“Of course. We can't leave Galatia out of anything.”

They laughed.

Then Elías looked at his friends.

“There's something I think we should remember as we continue.

“We've spent a great deal of time examining what the flesh can and cannot do.

“Now we're turning to what God has done.

“And we need to be careful not to turn that into another discussion about what we must accomplish.”

Matías nodded.

“Because the cross is not our accomplishment.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's His.”

They said their goodbyes.

As Marcos drove home, he thought about the conversation.

For years he had regarded the cross primarily as the place where the Christian life began.

Forgiveness.

Salvation.

A new beginning.

Then came the responsibility to live as a Christian.

He had never denied the importance of the cross. He had simply assumed that the rest of the Christian life was largely a matter of learning what to do.

Now he was beginning to see why Paul had refused to leave it.

The Corinthians needed more than improved behavior.

The Galatians needed more than greater discipline.

And the flesh could never become spiritual through its own efforts.

God had acted through His Son.

Marcos thought again of Paul's words.

*For the word of the cross ... is the power of God.*

He had believed those words for years.

Now he wanted to understand their full significance.

Not as another method for becoming a better Christian.

As the declaration of what God had accomplished in Christ.

And he was beginning to suspect that understanding the cross would change far more than the way he thought about carnality.

# Chapter 38
## The Cross Does Not Bow to Carnality

Matías had been thinking about crucifixion.

Not the suffering itself. He had heard many sermons describing the physical agony of Christ's death, and he understood why the subject was treated with such seriousness.

What occupied him now was something Paul had said about believers.

*Our old man was crucified with Him.*

The words were familiar.

Their implications were becoming less so.

He had spent years approaching the flesh as something that needed correction. He understood correction. In his work, when something was wrong, he identified the problem, established a plan, and supervised the necessary improvements.

He had approached his Christian life much the same way.

Yet Paul had not said that the old man was placed under a program of improvement.

He said he was crucified.

Matías had been unable to stop thinking about the difference.

At Deb's Place, Adrián was explaining to Anna why he believed regular customers deserved certain privileges.

“After all these years, I think we should have our own table.”

“You do have your own table.”

“Officially.”

“What would that change?”

“Nothing immediately. It's a matter of recognition.”

Anna considered him.

“Would you like your name engraved on it?”

Adrián brightened.

“That's an excellent idea.”

“Then everyone would know whom to blame when the coffee gets spilled.”

Marcos laughed as he approached.

“Good morning.”

Anna smiled.

“Morning, Marcos. Your friend is negotiating again.”

“Has he made any progress?”

“None whatsoever.”

Adrián shook his head.

“She's a difficult woman.”

“And yet you keep coming back.”

“The coffee is worth the hardship.”

By the time Matías and Elías arrived, the negotiations had been suspended.

They ordered breakfast and spent a few minutes talking about their week.

Then Matías opened his Bible.

“I've been thinking about Romans 6.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Still?”

“More than ever.”

He found the sixth verse.

“Last Thursday we said something important. The cross is not another method for us to use against the flesh. It's something God has accomplished through Christ.

“I've believed that Christ died for my sins for as long as I can remember. Yet I've never given the same attention to what Paul says about my death with Him.”

He read the verse.

“knowing this, that our old man was crucified with him, that the body of sin might be done away with, so that we would no longer be in bondage to sin.”

He left his Bible open.

“That's not how I've understood the Christian life.

“I've spent years trying to correct myself. I've tried to become more disciplined, more consistent, and more obedient. When I failed, I looked for a better way to succeed.

“Yet Paul doesn't tell me that my old man needs more discipline.

“He tells me my old man was crucified with Christ.”

Elías looked at the passage.

“And notice the tense. Paul speaks about something that has happened. He isn't giving the Romans instructions for arranging their own crucifixion.

“He's explaining what is true of them in their identification with Christ.”

Matías continued.

“That's what troubles me. If God has already dealt with my old man through the cross, why have I spent so much time trying to improve him?”

Marcos had been following the passage.

“There's something about crucifixion that makes the language particularly striking,” he said.

“In ordinary life, when something isn't functioning properly, we try to repair it. We may replace a part, correct a procedure, or improve the way something operates.

“That's reasonable when you're dealing with machinery or an organization.

“Yet crucifixion isn't a repair procedure.

“You don't crucify someone in order to make him more effective.”

Adrián looked at him.

“That would be a rather unusual training program.”

Marcos smiled.

“Exactly.

“And that's why Paul's language interests me. He doesn't describe the old man as someone God intends to improve until he becomes suitable for spiritual life.

“He speaks of crucifixion.

“Something decisive has happened.”

Elías considered the observation.

“We should be careful about what we mean by the old man. Paul is speaking about who we were in Adam, under sin's dominion. He isn't saying that the believer's body has ceased to exist or that the capacity to act by the flesh has disappeared.

“The Corinthians demonstrate that believers can still act by the flesh.

“Yet Romans 6 declares that our relationship to sin has fundamentally changed through our union with Christ.”

He turned to the preceding verses.

“Paul begins by saying that those who were baptized into Christ Jesus were baptized into His death. He speaks of being buried with Him and united with Him in the likeness of His death.

“Then he tells us that our old man was crucified with Him.

“The point is not that believers must produce their own death to sin.

“It's that Christ's death has consequences for those who belong to Him.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Then God has done something concerning the very life I was trying to improve.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And Paul wants believers to know it.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián examined his plate.

“Excellent.”

She looked at him.

“You say that every week.”

“Because you keep meeting my expectations.”

“Then perhaps your expectations are too low.”

Marcos laughed.

Anna smiled and returned to the counter.

For a while they ate and discussed a problem Marcos had encountered at work. One of his employees had suggested a complicated solution to something that required a relatively simple adjustment.

“I spent twenty minutes listening to him explain how we could reorganize the entire process,” Marcos said. “Then I asked whether he'd checked the original instructions.”

“Had he?” Matías asked.

“No.”

Adrián smiled.

“That sounds familiar.”

Marcos laughed.

“Unfortunately, it does.”

When the conversation returned to Scripture, Elías opened to Galatians 6.

“There's another passage that belongs here.”

He read Paul's words.

“But far be it from me to boast, except in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, through which the world has been crucified to me, and I to the world.”

He looked at his friends.

“Paul speaks about the cross as something that has changed his relationship to the world.

“And notice what he says about boasting.

“He refuses to boast in anything except the cross of Christ.”

Matías considered that.

“Which is significant in Galatians.”

“Very,” Elías said.

“Paul has been confronting people who were placing confidence in religious observance. Circumcision had become a matter of great importance to those troubling the Galatian believers.

“And near the end of the letter, Paul exposes the desire to make a good showing in the flesh.

“He says that some wanted the Galatians circumcised so they could boast in their flesh.

“Then Paul declares that his own boasting is in the cross.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“That's quite a contrast.”

“It is.

“The cross removes the basis for boasting in religious achievement.

“Paul isn't presenting himself as a man who has become sufficiently disciplined to deserve God's approval.

“He's directing attention to what Christ has accomplished.”

Adrián leaned back.

“So the cross doesn't leave much room for becoming somebody.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“No. Not if the thing you're boasting about is yourself.”

Marcos had been thinking about the four churches.

“I wonder whether this helps explain why the cross is so uncomfortable to the flesh.

“Think about Corinth. People were attaching importance to their gifts, their knowledge, and the teachers they followed. Those things gave them reasons to regard themselves as significant.

“Then Paul speaks about Christ crucified and says that no flesh should boast before God.

“Or consider Galatia. They were being drawn toward a religious system in which human observance could become the basis of confidence.

“And Paul boasts in the cross.

“The message is the same in both situations. Human achievement cannot become the source of spiritual life.”

Elías considered the other two churches.

“We should remember that Scripture doesn't explicitly call Ephesus and Laodicea fleshly in the way Paul addresses Corinth. We've been examining their conditions in light of what Scripture teaches about the flesh.

“Yet the cross certainly challenges the kinds of confidence that can develop around religious accomplishment.

“Laodicea believed it needed nothing. Ephesus had genuine commendations from Christ, yet had fallen from its first love.

“Neither religious success nor an impressive record can substitute for the life that comes from Christ.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“It's remarkable how many ways we can find to feel important.”

Matías smiled.

“And how easily we can defend those things as spiritual.”

Elías returned to Romans 6.

“There's something else here that I don't want us to overlook.”

He read the next verse.

“For he who has died has been freed from sin.”

Then he continued through the passage, describing Christ's death to sin and His life to God.

“Paul is not describing a temporary improvement in the believer's conduct.

“He's describing a change in relationship to sin through Christ's death and resurrection.

“And then, in verse eleven, he says, ‘Thus consider yourselves also to be dead to sin, but alive to God in Christ Jesus our Lord.’”

Matías looked at the verse.

“Consider yourselves.”

“Yes.”

“That's an interesting instruction.”

“And it follows what Paul has already said about our death with Christ.

“Paul isn't telling believers to make themselves dead to sin by imagining it strongly enough.

“He's telling them to consider themselves dead to sin and alive to God in Christ.”

Matías remained looking at the passage.

“I've often treated that verse as though it were another spiritual exercise.

“I thought that if I reminded myself often enough that I was dead to sin, perhaps I would eventually become dead to sin.

“Yet Paul has already told me what happened.

“He isn't asking me to accomplish it.

“He's asking me to count on what God has accomplished.”

Marcos looked at him.

“That changes the place of faith.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“Faith isn't a method for making the cross effective.

“It's believing what God has said about what Christ has done.”

Elías smiled.

“That's an important distinction.”

Adrián had been listening quietly.

Then he said, “There's something I want to understand.

“If our old man was crucified with Christ, why do believers still act by the flesh?”

Elías considered the question.

“Paul says our old man was crucified so that we would no longer be in bondage to sin. He doesn't say believers become incapable of acting contrary to their position in Christ.

“Romans 6 itself contains instructions not to let sin reign in our mortal bodies and not to present our members to sin.

“Those instructions would make little sense if believers were incapable of acting otherwise.

“Yet the instructions follow the declaration of what God has done.

“Paul doesn't tell believers to overcome sin's dominion so that they may eventually become united with Christ.

“He tells them what has happened through Christ, then calls them to consider themselves in light of that reality.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“So the order matters.”

“Very much.”

Marcos added, “And perhaps that's where sin management gets things backward.

“It begins with the behavior that needs to change and assumes that sufficient effort will eventually produce spiritual life.

“Paul begins with what God has accomplished and addresses believers on that basis.”

Adrián considered that.

“Then the cross doesn't negotiate with the flesh.”

Elías looked at him.

“What do you mean?”

“It doesn't offer the flesh an opportunity to improve itself until it becomes acceptable.

“It doesn't say that if the flesh becomes sufficiently disciplined, God will allow it to produce spiritual life.

“The cross declares something entirely different.”

Matías looked down at Romans 6.

“Our old man was crucified with Christ.”

Adrián nodded.

“That's what I mean.”

The conversation slowed.

For a while they sat quietly, each considering what they had read.

Then Matías spoke again.

“There's something almost offensive about this to a person who has spent his life trying to improve himself.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“Why?”

“Because it leaves me no achievement of my own to rely on.

“I've always believed that if I worked hard enough, I could improve almost anything. And in many areas of life, that's true.

“Yet when it comes to producing spiritual life, the cross tells me that my own ability is not the answer.

“God hasn't offered to strengthen the flesh until it becomes spiritual.

“He has dealt with our old man through Christ's death.

“And that means the life I need cannot come from the very thing He has judged.”

Elías listened carefully.

“Paul's words in Romans 8 belong with that thought.”

He found the third verse.

“For what the law couldn't do, in that it was weak through the flesh, God did, sending his own Son in the likeness of sinful flesh and for sin, he condemned sin in the flesh.”

He looked up.

“The law could not accomplish what was necessary because of the flesh.

“God did what the law could not do.

“And He did it through His Son.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“So the cross isn't God's attempt to make the flesh more capable.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's God's decisive action concerning sin.”

Adrián looked toward Matías.

“And that's why the cross doesn't bow to carnality.”

Matías nodded slowly.

“It doesn't accommodate our confidence in ourselves.”

Anna approached with the bill.

“Together or separate?”

Adrián reached for it.

“Together.”

Marcos smiled.

“Again?”

“I'm developing a reputation for generosity.”

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

“It's a very expensive reputation.”

Adrián examined the amount.

“I've noticed.”

They laughed as they gathered their belongings.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

Matías looked toward Elías.

“I'd like to continue with Galatians next Thursday.”

“Of course.”

“And Romans 6.”

Elías smiled.

“I think we'll be spending quite a bit of time there.”

Adrián looked at them.

“Gentlemen, I have one request.”

Marcos raised an eyebrow.

“What's that?”

“Please don't make me memorize the entire book of Romans before next week.”

Matías laughed.

“I wouldn't dream of it.”

“Good. I have enough trouble remembering where I parked.”

They said goodbye.

As Matías drove home, he thought about the conversation.

For years, he had believed that spiritual growth was largely a matter of correcting what was wrong with him.

More discipline.

More effort.

More determination.

He had never questioned the importance of the cross for his salvation.

Yet he had not understood why Paul spoke of his old man being crucified with Christ.

Now he was beginning to see the difference.

The cross was not a program of improvement.

It was not an invitation for the flesh to become more spiritual.

It was God's decisive action concerning the old man and sin's dominion.

And that action did not depend upon Matías's ability to accomplish it.

He thought again of Romans 6.

*Our old man was crucified with Him.*

Those words were becoming more than a familiar doctrine to him.

They were describing what God had done.

And he was beginning to understand why the cross would never accommodate the flesh's desire to become somebody.

# Chapter 39
## The Cross Is Not Deceived by Carnality

Elías had been thinking about Daniel again.

He had not seen the young man in several weeks, although they had exchanged a few messages. Daniel was doing well. He had found a small group of believers with whom he could study Scripture, and he seemed relieved to be able to ask questions without feeling that he was creating a problem.

That last detail troubled Elías.

He had spent much of his life teaching the Bible. He believed in sound doctrine and understood the responsibility of protecting believers from error. He still believed those things.

Yet he had begun to question something about the way he had exercised that responsibility.

When Daniel had raised questions about confession and fellowship, Elías had been concerned about the teaching itself. He had wanted to prevent confusion.

He could remember explaining to the elders why the young man's questions needed to be handled carefully.

He had spoken about protecting the church.

Maintaining unity.

Preserving sound doctrine.

All of those concerns had sounded reasonable.

What he could no longer answer with the same confidence was whether they had actually examined Daniel's questions as carefully as they had examined his willingness to submit.

He had been thinking about that distinction all week.

Adrián was already at Deb's Place when Elías arrived.

He was standing beside the counter, talking to Anna.

“I'm simply saying that if you put my photograph on the wall, it might encourage other customers to become regulars.”

Anna looked unimpressed.

“We have photographs on the wall.”

“Exactly.”

“Those are photographs of Windsor from fifty years ago.”

“Then I'm offering something more current.”

She looked at Elías.

“Please take him to his table.”

Elías laughed.

“Come on, Adrián.”

“I'm trying to help the business.”

“You've been helping the business for years,” Anna said. “Mostly by eating.”

Adrián followed Elías to their usual table.

“Some people have no appreciation for marketing.”

“Perhaps she's concerned about the effect your photograph would have on the customers.”

Adrián considered that.

“You may have a point.”

Marcos and Matías arrived shortly afterward. They ordered breakfast and spent some time catching up.

Matías had been helping with plans for the community center. Marcos was dealing with an unexpected maintenance expense on one of his aircraft. Adrián had attended a family gathering that had apparently involved an argument about the proper way to prepare arepas.

He was still offended by the outcome.

“They voted against me.”

“Perhaps you were wrong,” Marcos suggested.

“That's an unnecessarily harsh conclusion.”

They laughed.

Eventually Elías opened his Bible.

“I've been thinking about something since our last conversation.”

Matías looked toward him.

“The cross?”

“Yes. Particularly what it reveals about the flesh.”

He turned to the first chapter of Corinthians.

“Last Thursday we considered how the cross refuses to accommodate the flesh's desire to improve itself. Paul doesn't present crucifixion as another means of making human effort acceptable to God.

“Yet there's something else about the cross that I think we need to examine.

“The flesh is not always obvious.”

Adrián smiled.

“We've certainly discovered that.”

“Consider the four churches we've studied. Corinth's jealousy and divisions were relatively easy to recognize once Paul identified them.

“Galatia was more difficult. The people troubling those believers weren't encouraging immorality. They were promoting religious observance.

“Laodicea believed itself rich and in need of nothing.

“And Ephesus had received genuine commendations from Christ, even though He declared that it had fallen from its first love.

“We've seen that outward appearances cannot tell us everything about the source of a church's conduct.

“Carnality can be remarkably religious.”

Matías looked at him.

“And it can sound very responsible.”

Elías nodded.

“That's what I've been thinking about.”

Matías leaned forward.

“I remember something from years ago. We had a disagreement in our church about how a particular ministry should be organized. It wasn't a doctrinal issue. It concerned who would be responsible for certain decisions.

“Several people had reasonable suggestions. Yet the discussion became increasingly difficult because two of the men involved were determined to have their own way.

“Eventually the leadership decided that the matter needed to be settled. We reminded everyone of the importance of unity and asked them to support the final decision.

“The disagreement ended publicly.

“Yet one of those men was given nearly everything he wanted, largely because we knew he would cause trouble if he didn't get it.”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“I've seen that happen in business.”

“So have I,” Matías said. “And at the time, I considered our decision a success. We'd avoided a larger conflict.

“Looking back, I'm not sure we dealt with the problem at all. We rewarded the man who was most difficult to oppose, then asked everyone else to preserve unity.

“We called it wisdom.”

Adrián considered the example.

“Perhaps it was practical.”

“It was practical,” Matías said. “And perhaps it prevented a worse situation. I don't know.

“What troubles me now is how easily we gave our decision a spiritual explanation.

“We weren't simply saying that we'd made a difficult administrative choice. We were presenting the outcome as an expression of Christian unity.

“And I wonder whether we sometimes use spiritual language to give our own preferences an authority they don't deserve.”

Elías looked at him.

“That's precisely what I've been considering.”

He took a moment before continuing.

“No one announces that he's acting by the flesh. He explains his actions in terms that seem appropriate.

“He wants to protect the church.

“He wants to preserve unity.

“He wants to maintain standards.

“He wants to defend sound doctrine.

“Those can all be legitimate concerns. Scripture gives us reasons to care about every one of them.

“Yet the language itself cannot establish the source of our actions.

“A man can defend sound doctrine because he loves the truth and wants others to know Christ.

“He can also defend sound doctrine because being correct has become central to his identity.

“The same words may be spoken. The same passages may be quoted.

“And the source may be entirely different.”

Marcos looked down at his coffee.

“Which means the flesh can use the language of spirituality to protect itself.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And it can do so without recognizing what it's doing.”

Anna arrived with breakfast.

Adrián looked at his plate and smiled.

“Now there's something that doesn't need defending.”

She glanced at him.

“Give it a minute.”

“Why are you so determined to undermine my confidence?”

“I'm trying to keep you humble.”

“It's a full-time occupation.”

She laughed and returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Marcos eventually returned to something Elías had said.

“I think there's a particular danger when an organization becomes successful.

“Success creates expectations. People become accustomed to certain results. Leaders gain confidence in the methods they've developed.

“Then someone questions one of those methods.

“The leaders may genuinely believe they're protecting something valuable. And perhaps they are.

“Yet the more their identity becomes attached to the success of the organization, the harder it becomes to distinguish protecting the people from protecting the arrangement.”

He paused to take a drink of coffee.

“I've experienced that in my company. Sometimes an employee suggests changing a procedure that I helped establish years earlier.

“My first reaction isn't always to consider whether he's right.

“Sometimes my first reaction is to defend the procedure.

“Because it's mine.”

Matías smiled.

“I understand that.”

Marcos continued.

“Now imagine that same reaction in a church.

“A leader establishes a ministry, develops a teaching approach, or introduces a program. Over the years it becomes part of the church's identity.

“Then someone asks whether the practice is actually producing what the church claims.

“The question may be entirely reasonable.

“Yet the leader hears it as a challenge to his judgment, his experience, or his authority.

“And suddenly the discussion is no longer about whether the practice is sound.

“It's about whether the person asking the question is being divisive.”

Elías had stopped eating.

He was thinking about Daniel.

“I've done that,” he said.

The others looked toward him.

“I don't mean precisely the same thing in every detail. Yet I've allowed a question about teaching to become a question about loyalty.”

He set down his fork.

“You remember Daniel?”

Matías nodded.

“The young man who questioned the teaching about confession?”

“Yes.”

Elías looked at his friends.

“When he first raised his concerns, I believed I understood the issue. I'd taught the passage for years. I knew how our church explained it.

“Daniel was asking whether First John 1:9 meant that believers had to confess each sin in order to restore fellowship with God.

“He wasn't denying the seriousness of sin. He was questioning whether the passage supported the explanation we'd been giving.

“I thought his interpretation was potentially dangerous. I was concerned about what might happen if people concluded that confession was unnecessary.

“So I advised him to be careful about discussing the matter with others.”

Elías looked down at his Bible.

“Then the elders became involved. We discussed the importance of maintaining doctrinal unity. We were concerned about confusion spreading through the congregation.

“Eventually Daniel was told that he needed to stop promoting his questions or leave.”

Adrián looked up.

“He left?”

“Yes. Peacefully.”

Elías was quiet for a moment.

“I've been thinking about what happened between his first question and that final conversation.

“At some point, our concern about the interpretation became entangled with our concern about his willingness to accept our authority.

“And I don't think we recognized the difference.”

Marcos considered that.

“Were you certain his interpretation was wrong?”

“I was certain that it differed from what we'd always taught.”

Elías gave a small, rueful smile.

“I wish those two statements had seemed as different to me then as they do now.”

Matías looked at him.

“Have you changed your understanding of the passage?”

“I'm still examining it. There are questions about the context that deserve careful attention. I don't want to replace one unexamined certainty with another.

“Yet I've become convinced of something about the way we handled Daniel.

“We should have been willing to examine the Scriptures with him without making agreement with our established explanation the measure of his submission to Christ.”

He looked at the others.

“That's difficult for me to admit.

“I believed I was defending sound doctrine.

“And I may also have been defending my reputation as someone who knew the correct answer.”

No one hurried to reassure him.

Elías continued.

“I think that's one of the reasons religious carnality is so difficult to recognize.

“It doesn't always appear as a desire to do something obviously wrong.

“Sometimes it appears as a determination to defend something right, while our confidence has shifted from Christ to ourselves.”

Matías opened to 1 Corinthians.

“There's a passage here that speaks directly to that.”

He found the first chapter.

“Paul asks where the wise man is, where the scribe is, and where the debater of the age is. Then he asks whether God has not made foolish the wisdom of the world.

“He's challenging human wisdom at the very point where people are most confident in it.”

Elías nodded.

“And that's why the cross is so offensive to human pride.

“The cross does not recognize human wisdom, religious accomplishment, or personal importance as grounds for boasting before God.

“Paul says that God has acted so that no flesh may boast in His presence.

“Then he tells the Corinthians that it is because of God that they are in Christ Jesus.”

He found verse thirty.

“That's a remarkable statement.

“God is the One who placed them in Christ.

“And Christ became wisdom from God to them, along with righteousness, sanctification, and redemption.

“Paul is not directing their confidence toward what they have made themselves.

“He's directing it toward Christ.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then the cross exposes the problem with religious pride even when the person is defending something genuinely valuable.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“A man may possess accurate knowledge. He may teach sound doctrine. He may have years of experience.

“Those things can be useful.

“Yet if he begins treating them as the source of his spiritual standing, he has placed his confidence where Paul says no flesh can boast.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“So the cross isn't impressed by our explanations.”

Matías smiled.

“Not even the really good ones.”

Adrián laughed.

“That's unfortunate. I've spent years developing mine.”

Marcos turned the conversation toward grace.

“There's another place where I think this becomes visible.

“Have you noticed how quickly people become uncomfortable when grace is emphasized?”

Matías nodded.

“I've heard people say that too much grace will encourage sin.”

“So have I,” Marcos said. “And I understand the concern. People can distort grace into permission for conduct Scripture condemns.

“Yet sometimes the response is revealing.

“Someone begins teaching what God has accomplished in Christ. He emphasizes forgiveness, the believer's position in Christ, and freedom from condemnation.

“And before anyone has examined whether he's teaching Scripture accurately, the warnings begin.

“People will take advantage.

“We need balance.

“We can't let people think obedience doesn't matter.

“Sometimes those concerns lead to a serious biblical discussion.

“Other times they become reasons to stop the discussion altogether.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Because the message threatens the system.”

“Sometimes,” Marcos said.

He considered his next words.

“Suppose a church has spent years motivating people through fear of losing fellowship with God. Its leaders have taught believers to examine themselves constantly, confess every failure, and work harder to remain spiritually acceptable.

“Then someone begins teaching that God has placed believers in Christ, that their old man was crucified with Him, and that Christ Himself is their life.

“If the leaders have built their entire approach to Christian living around maintaining the believer's spiritual standing through effort, that message challenges more than a particular teaching.

“It challenges the basis of their approach.”

Matías was listening closely.

“And the immediate response may be to call the message dangerous.”

“Yes.”

Elías thought again of Daniel.

“I've heard the expression *hyper-grace* used that way,” he said. “Sometimes it's attached to teachings that genuinely deserve correction. Yet sometimes it's used without anyone explaining precisely what error is being alleged.

“The label becomes a substitute for examining the argument.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“It's very efficient. You can dismiss a man in two words.”

Elías looked at him.

“And never discover whether he was right.”

The conversation settled into a thoughtful silence.

Then Matías spoke.

“I think we're beginning to see something important about the cross.

“It doesn't merely confront the obvious expressions of the flesh.

“It also exposes the things the flesh uses to justify itself.

“Pride can call itself conviction.

“Fear can call itself discernment.

“Control can call itself leadership.

“And self-reliance can call itself spiritual maturity.”

He looked at the others.

“Those words may describe something genuine. That's what makes the problem so difficult.

“We cannot determine whether an action is spiritual simply by the name we give it.”

Marcos nodded.

“And the cross leaves no room for boasting in any of those things.”

Elías looked at the open Bible.

“That's why Paul's message is so important.

“He doesn't tell the Corinthians to exchange their obvious pride for a more respectable form of pride.

“He directs them to Christ crucified.

“The cross exposes the inadequacy of every claim to spiritual significance apart from Him.”

Adrián leaned back.

“So carnality can survive by sounding spiritual.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And the cross exposes the deception.”

Anna approached with the coffee pot.

“More?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured carefully.

“You're quiet today.”

“We're examining pride.”

She glanced at him.

“That should keep you busy.”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián looked wounded.

“I was hoping for encouragement.”

“You'll survive.”

She moved on to the next table.

Adrián watched her go.

“Do you think she prepares those remarks in advance?”

“Not for you,” Elías said. “You've given her plenty of practice.”

They laughed, and the conversation moved briefly to other things.

When it returned to the cross, Marcos was the one who spoke.

“There's something I've been wondering.

“If the cross exposes our confidence in human wisdom and religious achievement, shouldn't that affect the way we approach church leadership?”

Elías considered the question.

“It certainly should.”

“Then perhaps one of the most important things a leader can recognize is that he has no independent spiritual strength.

“He may have experience, knowledge, and ability. He may have responsibilities that require judgment and courage.

“Yet none of those things makes him capable of producing spiritual life.

“And none of them places him beyond the danger of acting by the flesh.”

Matías looked at him.

“That would change the way correction is handled.”

“Yes. It would require a leader to examine his own confidence while addressing someone else's conduct.”

Elías was thinking about the meeting with Daniel.

“I wish I'd done more of that,” he said.

He looked toward his friends.

“I was concerned about whether Daniel would accept our teaching.

“I should also have been concerned about whether we were willing to have our teaching examined.”

When they had finished, Adrián paid the bill, despite announcing that he was becoming suspicious of how frequently the others allowed him to do so.

They gathered their coats and made their way toward the door.

Outside, the air was cool.

Elías stood beside his car, thinking about the conversation.

He had spent years believing that doctrinal accuracy was one of the strongest safeguards against carnality.

He still believed sound doctrine mattered deeply.

Yet he could no longer assume that possessing correct knowledge meant he was necessarily acting from the life of Christ.

He thought again of Daniel.

The young man had asked a question about Scripture.

Elías had been more concerned about protecting the established answer than he had realized.

He wondered how often that had happened in other areas of his life.

Across the parking lot, Adrián called to him.

“Everything all right?”

Elías smiled.

“Yes. Just thinking.”

“That's becoming a habit.”

“A useful one, I hope.”

Adrián waved and climbed into his car.

Elías remained outside for another moment.

The cross left no room for his boasting, however accurately he could explain it.

Nor did it recognize religious experience, knowledge, or authority as grounds for human boasting.

It exposed every attempt to establish spiritual confidence apart from Christ.

Even the attempts that sounded most convincing.

Elías opened his car door.

He was beginning to understand why the cross was so uncomfortable to the flesh.

**The flesh could disguise itself from others. It could even deceive the person acting by it.**

The cross left no room for its claims.

And that raised another question he intended to bring to the table.

If churches could become so skilled at protecting their methods, what happened when they began trusting those methods more than the gospel itself?

# Chapter 40
## The Gospel That Needs Help

Marcos had been invited to a church leadership meeting.

He wasn't a member of the congregation, although he knew several of its leaders. They were considering expanding their youth ministry, and someone had suggested that his experience organizing events and managing people might be useful.

The invitation had seemed reasonable enough.

The church had a substantial building, a good reputation in the community, and a leadership team that appeared genuinely concerned about reaching younger people.

Marcos had gone expecting a discussion about the gospel and discipleship.

Instead, he had spent nearly two hours listening to proposals for making church more attractive.

There had been discussions about lighting, music, social media, special events, and the length of Sunday messages.

One leader suggested that young people would be more likely to attend if the teaching portions were kept under fifteen minutes.

Another argued that Bible studies should be less demanding.

Someone else proposed replacing a weekly teaching meeting with a rotating schedule of activities.

Marcos had listened carefully.

He understood the importance of communicating effectively. He also understood that people were more likely to attend something they found welcoming and interesting.

Yet one detail had troubled him.

Throughout the meeting, almost nobody had discussed what the young people would actually learn about Christ.

At Deb's Place, Adrián was examining a small advertisement that had been left beside the cash register.

“Look at this.”

Anna glanced over.

“What?”

“Live music, food trucks, games, and prizes. Saturday afternoon.”

“It's a community event.”

“I can see that. I'm wondering whether I should attend.”

“It's for families.”

“I have a family.”

“Most of them are grown.”

“That shouldn't disqualify me from winning a prize.”

Anna took the advertisement from him.

“You're not going for the games. You're going for the food trucks.”

Adrián considered the accusation.

“I don't see why we need to question my motives.”

Marcos arrived in time to hear the exchange.

“What's happening?”

“I'm being persecuted for my interest in community activities.”

Anna handed Marcos the advertisement.

“He wants free food.”

“That's not what it says,” Adrián protested. “It says food trucks. I fully expect to pay.”

“Then perhaps there's hope for you.”

She returned to her work.

Marcos laughed and followed Adrián to their table.

By the time Matías and Elías arrived, the conversation had moved to the difficulties of organizing large public events.

Marcos mentioned the church meeting.

“I attended something this week that I think belongs in our investigation.”

Matías took off his coat.

“Another church?”

“In a manner of speaking.”

Marcos explained the invitation and described the meeting.

He didn't name the congregation. He had no desire to embarrass people who had invited him in good faith.

“They were concerned about reaching younger people,” he said. “And I believe that concern was sincere. They'd noticed that fewer young adults were attending, and they wanted to do something about it.

“One man said that young people today simply don't have the attention span for serious Bible teaching.”

Adrián smiled.

“I've heard that.”

“So have I,” Matías said.

Marcos continued.

“Another leader said we needed to accept that different generations respond to different things. He suggested that the church should concentrate on creating experiences that would make young people want to come back.

“Then someone proposed a monthly event that would have almost no teaching at all.

“The idea was to get them comfortable with the church first. They could learn the Bible later.”

Elías looked interested.

“What did you say?”

“Very little at first. I wanted to understand what they were trying to accomplish.

“Eventually I asked what they hoped the young people would know about Christ after attending for six months.

“There was a long discussion about belonging, friendships, and feeling connected.

“Those are worthwhile things. I understand their importance.

“Yet nobody could tell me what they expected the young people to understand about the gospel.”

He looked around the table.

“And I began wondering whether we'd confused attracting people to church with making disciples of Christ.”

Matías had been thinking about the statement concerning attention spans.

“I find that argument particularly strange,” he said.

“Which one?” Adrián asked.

“That young people are incapable of sustained attention.

“I have employees in their twenties who can spend hours studying complicated technical problems. Some of them are extraordinarily capable.

“My grandchildren can tell me more about the details of a video game than I could possibly remember. They know the characters, the rules, the strategies, and the history behind things I barely understand.

“Yet we're told that young people cannot concentrate long enough to study Scripture.”

Adrián laughed.

“You should hear my grandson explain the rules of the games he plays. By the time he's finished, I need a vacation.”

“Exactly,” Matías said.

“People can concentrate deeply when they believe something is worth understanding.

“Of course, not everyone has the same interests or abilities. And a teacher should communicate clearly. I'm not suggesting that we should make Bible teaching unnecessarily difficult.

“Yet I wonder whether we've accepted an assumption about young people without ever seriously questioning it.

“We tell ourselves they cannot handle substantial teaching.

“So we stop offering it.

“Then, after years of giving them very little, we observe that they don't seem interested in serious Bible study.

“And we treat that as proof that our original assumption was correct.”

Marcos looked at him.

“That's exactly what troubled me about the meeting.

“They were making decisions about what young people could receive without having given them much opportunity to receive it.”

Elías considered the problem.

“And perhaps we should distinguish between a person's attention span and the way something is taught.

“A long message can be poorly prepared. A short message can be clear, substantial, and faithful to Scripture.

“Length alone tells us very little.

“Yet if the goal becomes keeping people entertained, serious teaching will eventually appear to be an obstacle.”

Adrián leaned back.

“I enjoy a lively meeting. I've never made a secret of that. Good music, a welcoming atmosphere, people enjoying themselves. I don't see anything wrong with those things.

“And if a church wants to organize a family event, wonderful. People need opportunities to know one another.

“What I don't understand is why we assume people must be entertained before they're willing to hear about Christ.”

He thought for a moment.

“Or why we assume that once they've been entertained, they'll naturally want to know Him.”

Anna brought their breakfasts.

Adrián examined his plate.

“Excellent.”

She looked at him.

“You've become very easy to please.”

“I'm growing spiritually.”

“Is that what we're calling it?”

The others laughed.

She topped up Adrián's coffee and returned to the counter.

Marcos waited until she was gone.

“There's another part of the meeting that I found interesting.

“One of the leaders had attended a conference where the speaker discussed how churches could become more effective in reaching modern culture.

“He said that the traditional approach to preaching no longer worked with certain groups.

“People needed to be reached through experiences, relationships, and activities before they could receive biblical teaching.

“He was quite persuasive. He had statistics, examples, and stories from successful churches.

“And I understood why the others were impressed.”

Matías looked at him.

“Were you impressed?”

“With the presentation? Yes.

“With the conclusions? I wasn't convinced.”

Marcos continued.

“There's a principle in business that I've learned to be careful about. If someone shows me a successful result, I want to know what produced it and what the result actually means.

“A company may attract thousands of customers through a promotion. That's impressive.

“Yet if the customers leave as soon as the promotion ends, the company may not have accomplished what it intended.

“Likewise, a church may attract hundreds of young people to an event.

“That's a real result. It tells us that people attended.

“It doesn't tell us whether they heard the gospel, understood it, believed it, or began growing in the knowledge of Christ.

“Those are different questions.”

Elías nodded.

“And they're questions Scripture gives us reason to ask.”

He opened to Matthew 28.

“Jesus told His disciples to make disciples of all nations, baptizing them and teaching them to observe all He commanded.

“That's a much more substantial responsibility than gathering an audience.

“The commission includes all nations. It includes teaching.

“And it assumes that people from those nations can become disciples.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“That's important.”

“Very,” Elías said.

“Because we sometimes speak as though certain cultures are beyond the reach of the gospel unless we make it more suitable for them.”

Adrián had been thinking about that.

“I've heard missionaries say that certain people groups are difficult to reach.”

Elías nodded.
