“Some are. Language, history, persecution, social pressures, and religious traditions can create genuine obstacles. We shouldn't pretend those difficulties don't exist.

“Yet difficulty is not the same as the gospel being insufficient.

“People need to hear the message in a language they understand. They may need explanations that account for what they already believe. We should be patient and thoughtful.

“Paul himself adapted his approach to different audiences.

“Yet he didn't conclude that Christ crucified had lost His power because an audience found the message offensive.”

He turned to 1 Corinthians.

“We've just been studying this.

“Paul says that Jews sought signs and Greeks sought wisdom.

“Those were different expectations. Different ways of evaluating what was being presented.

“And Paul knew that Christ crucified was a stumbling block to one group and foolishness to the other.

“Yet he continued preaching Christ crucified.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“He didn't try to remove the offense by changing the message.”

“No,” Elías said.

“He communicated the message. He reasoned with people. He explained Scripture. He addressed their circumstances.

“Yet he didn't replace the gospel with something he believed would be more acceptable.”

Marcos considered that.

“Then we need to distinguish between helping people understand the gospel and trying to help the gospel become effective.”

Elías smiled.

“That's an excellent distinction.”

Marcos continued.

“Translation helps people understand. Clear explanations help people understand. Appropriate illustrations may help people understand.

“Those are matters of communication.

“Yet if we begin believing that the gospel itself lacks the power to reach a particular generation, we're saying something quite different.

“We're suggesting that God's message needs something added to make it capable of doing what God says it does.”

Adrián looked at him.

“And that's where the trouble begins.”

Elías turned to Romans 1.

“Paul makes a statement here that ought to govern our thinking.”

He read verse sixteen.

“For I am not ashamed of the Good News of Christ, because it is the power of God for salvation for everyone who believes; for the Jew first, and also for the Greek.”

He looked up.

“Everyone who believes.

“Paul doesn't restrict that statement to people of a particular generation, culture, or educational background.

“The gospel is God's power for salvation.

“The message is that Christ died for our sins and rose again.

“That doesn't mean everyone who hears it will believe. Scripture doesn't promise that.

“It means the gospel's power does not depend upon our ability to make it attractive.”

Matías considered the verse.

“Then perhaps some of our methods reveal a lack of confidence in the message.”

“They can,” Elías said.

“Especially when we begin replacing the message with the methods.”

Marcos looked down at his Bible.

“That's what I've been wondering.

“Suppose a church decides that people won't come if the Bible is taught seriously.

“So it begins emphasizing activities.

“The activities succeed. People come.

“Then the leaders become concerned that substantial teaching might cause people to leave.

“So they keep the teaching light.

“Eventually the church becomes very good at organizing events and very cautious about teaching Scripture.

“And because the events continue to succeed, the leaders conclude that their approach is working.”

He looked at the others.

“Working at what?”

No one answered immediately.

Marcos continued.

“If the objective is attendance, perhaps it's working wonderfully.

“If the objective is making disciples who know Christ and are established in His truth, the attendance figures cannot answer the question.”

Adrián was looking out the window.

“I've been thinking about something else.

“If we attract people by convincing them that Christianity is entertainment, what happens when we begin teaching them something that isn't entertaining?”

Matías set down his fork.

“If the activities are what bring them in, isn't that an opportunity to tell them about Christ?”

“Perhaps,” Adrián said.

“Yet suppose we've spent years telling them, through everything we do, that church is supposed to be exciting.

“Every meeting needs something new. Every message needs to be immediately interesting. Every activity needs to compete with whatever else they could be doing.

“Then we open Romans and begin discussing what it means to have died with Christ.

“Or we begin examining Galatians and the difference between the flesh and the Spirit.

“Or we ask them to think carefully about what Scripture actually says.

“What have we prepared them to expect?”

Elías looked thoughtful.

“We may have taught them to evaluate spiritual things by how stimulating they find them.”

“Exactly,” Adrián said.

“And that isn't necessarily their fault. We taught them to do it.”

Marcos considered the observation.

“Then the church can create the very appetite it later claims it must satisfy.”

Adrián nodded.

“And keep feeding it.”

Matías opened his Bible to Acts.

“There's something I'd like to bring into this.”

He found the second chapter.

“After Pentecost, Luke describes the believers devoting themselves to the apostles' teaching, fellowship, breaking bread, and prayers.

“I know the circumstances were different. We shouldn't imagine that every church meeting must look exactly like the earliest gatherings.

“Yet I'm struck by the importance given to teaching.

“These were new believers. They had much to learn.

“And the church didn't conclude that serious instruction should wait until they'd developed a longer attention span.”

Elías smiled.

“Nor did Paul assume that new believers should remain indefinitely on elementary teaching.”

He turned to Colossians.

“Paul describes proclaiming Christ, admonishing and teaching every person with all wisdom, so that he may present every person mature in Christ.

“That's the purpose.

“Not merely to produce an initial response.

“Not merely to gather people into an organization.

“To see believers established and mature in Christ.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Which means discipleship isn't an optional activity for churches that happen to enjoy Bible study.”

“No,” Elías said.

“And serious discipleship doesn't mean merely transferring information.

“We've already seen that a person can possess considerable biblical knowledge and still act by the flesh.

“The goal is not to produce people who can win theological arguments.

“It's to bring believers to know Christ, understand what God has accomplished in Him, and live by faith in the One who is their life.

“Knowledge matters because truth matters. Yet knowledge is not itself the life.”

Matías looked at him.

“That distinction is important. Otherwise we could simply replace entertainment with intellectual achievement and call the result spirituality.”

Elías nodded.

“Precisely.”

Marcos remained thoughtful.

“I think that may be where the church meeting went wrong.

“The leaders were discussing how to get people into the building.

“They weren't discussing how to establish those people in Christ.

“And I don't think anyone intended to neglect discipleship. It simply wasn't central to the conversation.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Did you ask about that?”

“I did, near the end.

“I asked whether the church had a plan for teaching young believers the Scriptures.

“One man said they wanted to begin with relationships and introduce deeper teaching when the young people were ready.

“I asked how they would know when they were ready.

“He said they'd know when the young people began showing interest.”

Matías looked puzzled.

“So they were waiting for people to request something they'd never been encouraged to value.”

“That was my concern.”

Elías considered it.

“There's a difference between patiently teaching people according to their understanding and deciding in advance that they cannot receive substantial truth.

“Paul adapted his instruction to the maturity of his hearers. He didn't abandon the goal of maturity.

“And the Corinthians are particularly relevant here. Their immaturity was a problem Paul confronted, not a condition he decided to accommodate indefinitely.”

Marcos nodded.

“That's what I wish I'd said.”

Adrián smiled.

“You'll have another opportunity.”

“Perhaps.”

“And next time you can bring Elías. He'll have twelve passages ready before the meeting begins.”

Elías laughed.

“Only twelve?”

Anna arrived to clear their plates.

“Another coffee?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“I think I'm going to need one.”

She poured.

“What are you discussing today?”

“Whether people need to be entertained before they'll listen to something important.”

Anna looked at him.

“Depends who's talking.”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián sighed.

“I walked into that one.”

She smiled.

“Would you like a fifteen-minute version of my opinion?”

“Apparently that's all I'm capable of handling.”

“Then I'll keep it short.”

She returned to the counter.

The men laughed, and for a few moments the seriousness of their discussion gave way to the familiar warmth of the diner.

Elías was still thinking about Romans 1.

“There's something we should be careful not to lose,” he said.

“We've been examining the danger of relying upon methods. Yet the gospel is not a formula that operates independently of God.

“Paul calls it the power of God.

“The power belongs to God.

“And the message concerns His Son.”

He looked around the table.

“When a church loses confidence in the gospel, it may begin looking for something else to produce the results it wants.

“Sometimes that something is discipline.

“Sometimes it's religious tradition.

“Sometimes it's organizational success.

“And sometimes it's entertainment, marketing, or carefully designed experiences.

“The methods differ. The underlying confidence can be the same.”

Matías considered that.

“Confidence in what man can accomplish.”

“Yes.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“Then a church can become increasingly successful at attracting people while becoming less dependent upon the gospel itself.”

Elías nodded.

“And if the message is gradually displaced, we shouldn't be surprised when the results no longer resemble what Scripture describes as spiritual life.”

Adrián looked at him.

“That's an uncomfortable thought.”

“Why?”

“Because a church might have everything people usually associate with success.

“A beautiful building.

“Excellent music.

“Plenty of activities.

“Full meetings.

“And leaders who know how to keep everyone interested.”

He paused.

“Yet people may spend years attending without ever understanding what God has done for them in Christ.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“Or that Christ Himself is their life.”

Marcos returned to the leadership meeting one last time.

“I don't want to judge those men unfairly. They were trying to reach people. They were concerned about a real problem.

“And I don't think every event they proposed was a mistake. Some could provide wonderful opportunities for families and young people to meet one another.

“What troubled me was the assumption underneath the conversation.

“They seemed to believe that the gospel, presented seriously, would not be sufficient for the generation they wanted to reach.

“So they were searching for something that would succeed where they feared the gospel might fail.”

Elías considered his words.

“And the danger is that the thing added to help the gospel eventually becomes the thing the church trusts.”

Matías looked at Marcos.

“Did anyone ask whether the young people themselves wanted to study the Bible?”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“No.”

“Perhaps that would have been a useful place to begin.”

“Perhaps it would.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Imagine that. Asking young people what they think instead of announcing what they're incapable of understanding.”

Marcos laughed.

“It might make for a very different meeting.”

Elías opened again to 1 Corinthians.

“There's a reason this discussion belongs here, among our chapters about the cross.”

He found the passage they had examined several times.

“Paul knew that the message of Christ crucified did not satisfy every audience's expectations.

“Some wanted signs.

“Others wanted wisdom.

“And the cross appeared offensive or foolish.

“Yet Paul refused to replace it with something more impressive.

“He understood that the power was God's.”

Matías considered the passage.

“Then the cross exposes this form of carnality too.”

“How?” Marcos asked.

“Because it confronts our confidence in our own ability to make the gospel effective.

“We begin thinking that if we organize the right event, create the right atmosphere, or develop the right presentation, we can produce spiritual results.

“And we may produce results.

“People may attend.

“They may enjoy themselves.

“They may become involved.

“Yet none of those accomplishments can produce the life of Christ.”

Elías nodded.

“The gospel doesn't become powerful because we make it attractive.

“It is God's power.

“Our responsibility is to communicate it faithfully and clearly, not to supply the power we imagine it lacks.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“Then perhaps we've been asking the wrong question again.”

Marcos smiled.

“We do seem to have a talent for that.”

Adrián continued.

“We keep asking what we need to add to the gospel to reach people.

“Perhaps we should be asking what happens when we begin taking things away from it.”

They sat quietly for a while.

The diner was becoming busier. A family entered with three young children, and Anna hurried to arrange a larger table near the window.

One of the children was asking his father a complicated series of questions about the engine in their car.

The father was doing his best to answer.

Adrián watched them with amusement.

“There's a short attention span for you.”

Matías laughed.

The boy continued asking questions while his father removed his coat.

Marcos looked toward the family.

“I suppose we shouldn't underestimate what people are capable of learning.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Especially when they discover that something is worth knowing.”

When they finally stood to leave, Adrián picked up the advertisement he'd been examining earlier.

Anna noticed.

“Still thinking about the food trucks?”

“I'm considering attending.”

“Take your grandchildren.”

“That's an excellent idea.”

“And let them win the prizes.”

Adrián looked disappointed.

“You drive a hard bargain.”

She laughed.

They said goodbye and stepped outside.

Marcos remained beside his car for a moment, thinking about the leadership meeting.

He understood the desire to reach people.

He shared it.

He knew how much work went into organizing an event, preparing a program, or creating an environment where people felt welcome.

There was nothing inherently wrong with those things.

Yet he could no longer assume that successful methods were evidence of spiritual effectiveness.

He thought about the leaders who had gathered around the conference table, discussing what modern culture required.

They had spoken confidently about what young people would and would not receive.

They had discussed how to make the church more appealing.

And somewhere in the conversation, the gospel had become something that needed their assistance.

Marcos remembered Paul's words.

*For I am not ashamed of the Good News of Christ, because it is the power of God for salvation for everyone who believes; for the Jew first, and also for the Greek.*

Not the power of a particular presentation.

Not the power of an event.

Not the power of an atmosphere.

The power of God.

He thought about the Corinthians, who had been impressed with human wisdom, and about the Galatians, who had been drawn toward religious achievement.

The same confidence in human ability could appear in a church that prided itself on being modern, relevant, and successful.

The flesh did not need an old-fashioned church to feel important.

It was perfectly comfortable with a contemporary one.

As Marcos opened his car door, a question occurred to him.

If the gospel was God's power, what happened to a church that gradually replaced it with the things it believed would make it effective?

It might continue attracting people.

It might continue growing.

It might even become famous for its success.

And all the while, it could be losing confidence in the very message through which God gives life.

The gospel had not become powerless.

The church had begun trusting something else.

Marcos climbed into his car.

He thought again about the cross.

Paul had refused to replace Christ crucified with the wisdom his audiences wanted.

Perhaps the church's greatest need was not to discover what could be added to the gospel.

Perhaps it was to recover confidence in the One the gospel proclaimed.

# Chapter 41
## The Cross Confronted Carnality

Adrián had been thinking about an expression he'd heard in church for most of his life.

*You need to crucify your flesh.*

He had heard it in sermons, Bible studies, and conversations with believers who were struggling with sin. Sometimes the instruction was accompanied by practical advice about prayer, fasting, or discipline. Other times it was delivered with considerable force, as though the person receiving it simply needed to become more determined.

Adrián had used the expression himself.

He could remember telling a younger believer that he needed to crucify his flesh if he wanted to overcome certain habits.

At the time, it had sounded biblical.

Now he was wondering whether he'd understood what he was saying.

The question had begun troubling him after their discussion about the cross.

They had seen that the cross did not accommodate the flesh's desire to improve itself. They had considered how religious pride could disguise itself behind perfectly respectable language. And most recently, they had examined the ways churches tried to make the gospel effective through human ingenuity.

Through it all, one thing had become increasingly clear.

The flesh was remarkably resourceful.

It could be religious or worldly, disciplined or careless, traditional or contemporary. It could defend sound doctrine or organize impressive activities. It could even speak confidently about the cross.

And now Adrián wanted to know something.

What had the cross actually done concerning the flesh?

He arrived at Deb's Place before the others.

Anna was arranging clean cups behind the counter.

“Good morning.”

She looked over her shoulder.

“Morning, Adrián.”

“I have a question.”

“I'm sure you do.”

“Have you ever tried to fix something that couldn't be fixed?”

Anna considered him.

“Are we talking about appliances or people?”

“Either.”

“I've met a few of both.”

Adrián laughed.

“What did you do?”

“With the appliances? Usually replaced them.”

“And the people?”

“I stopped trying to fix them.”

He smiled.

“That sounds rather wise.”

“Don't look so surprised.”

“I'm not surprised. I'm impressed.”

Anna pointed toward his usual table.

“Go sit down before you become too impressed.”

Adrián took his coffee and obeyed.

By the time the others arrived, he had found the passage he wanted to discuss.

They exchanged greetings, ordered breakfast, and talked for a while about the week.

Matías had spent an afternoon helping his father sort through some old photographs over a video call. His father had found a box containing pictures from Argentina, including several of Matías as a boy.

“Apparently I was quite handsome,” Matías said.

Adrián looked at him.

“What happened?”

Marcos laughed.

Matías shook his head.

“I knew I shouldn't have mentioned it.”

Elías smiled.

“Did your father enjoy looking through them?”

“Very much. We spent almost two hours talking about people I hadn't thought of in years.”

He looked down at his coffee.

“It was good for both of us.”

The conversation continued until their breakfasts arrived.

Then Adrián opened his Bible.

“I want to ask you something.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“Go ahead.”

“Have any of you ever been told to crucify your flesh?”

Matías smiled faintly.

“Many times.”

“I've taught it,” Elías said.

“So have I,” Adrián replied. “And I've been thinking about what we actually meant.”

He turned to Galatians 5.

“Listen to this.”

He read verse twenty-four.

“Those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh with its passions and lusts.”

He looked up.

“That's not quite the way I remember hearing it.”

Elías leaned forward.

“What do you mean?”

“I remember being told that I needed to crucify my flesh.

“Paul says those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh.

“He isn't telling them to begin the process. He's speaking about something that characterizes those who belong to Christ.”

Matías found the passage.

“That's an important difference.”

Adrián continued.

“And it's the same kind of language we found in Romans 6.

“Our old man *was crucified* with Christ.

“Now Paul says those who belong to Christ *have crucified* the flesh.

“Crucifixion keeps appearing in these passages.

“And it doesn't sound like a program of gradual improvement.”

Elías took some time to examine the passage.

“We need to be careful here,” he said.

“Paul uses related language in Romans and Galatians, although the expressions aren't identical.

“In Romans 6, he says our old man was crucified with Christ. He's describing what happened in our identification with Christ, with the result that we are no longer under sin's dominion as we once were.

“In Galatians 5, he says those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.

“Here Paul names the believers as those who have crucified the flesh. We need to account for that, too.

“Both passages confront the idea that the flesh can become the source of spiritual life.

“And both speak of crucifixion as something decisive.”

He looked toward Adrián.

“Yet we shouldn't conclude that believers are now incapable of acting by the flesh. Paul has just warned the Galatians about the works of the flesh. He also tells them to walk by the Spirit.

“The flesh remains something believers must not follow.

“The difference is that Paul doesn't present the flesh as something God intends to make spiritual.”

Matías had been listening carefully.

“That's the distinction I keep returning to.

“For years, I believed my flesh needed to become more disciplined so I could live the Christian life successfully.

“I knew I couldn't earn salvation. Yet I thought spiritual maturity depended upon how thoroughly I improved myself afterward.

“And when I failed, I assumed the answer was more effort.”

He looked at Galatians.

“Paul isn't telling me that the flesh needs a better training program.

“He says it has been crucified.”

Marcos considered that.

“Crucifixion is a very different answer from rehabilitation.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And that's what makes it difficult for someone like me.”

Anna arrived with fresh coffee.

Adrián looked at her.

“Can I ask you something?”

She raised an eyebrow.

“Another question?”

“Just one.”

“Go ahead.”

“Suppose you had a coffee machine that never worked properly. You repaired it, adjusted it, replaced parts, and kept trying to make it function. At what point would you stop?”

Anna considered him.

“When it cost more to repair than replace.”

Adrián smiled.

“Very practical.”

“That's generally the idea.”

She glanced at the open Bibles.

“Are you gentlemen discussing coffee machines now?”

“Not exactly.”

“I thought not.”

She moved toward another table.

Marcos watched her go.

“She's probably relieved.”

Adrián laughed.

“Perhaps.”

Then he looked back at his friends.

“That's what I've been thinking about. We keep trying to repair something God has already judged.

“We try to make the flesh more obedient, more disciplined, more religious.

“And when it doesn't work, we assume the problem is that we haven't tried hard enough.

“Yet the cross doesn't offer the flesh another opportunity to improve itself.”

Elías considered his words.

“That's right. Although we should remember that the cross is not simply a declaration that human effort has failed.

“It's God's accomplished work in Christ.

“The believer's death with Christ is not something he produces by becoming sufficiently determined.

“It's something God has done in connection with His Son.”

Adrián nodded.

“Which means I can't turn crucifixion into another achievement.”

“Exactly.”

Marcos had been reading the surrounding verses in Galatians.

“There's something interesting about the order of Paul's argument,” he said.

He turned back to verse sixteen.

“Paul tells the Galatians to walk by the Spirit, and they will not carry out the desire of the flesh.

“Then he describes the conflict between the flesh and the Spirit.

“He lists the works of the flesh and the fruit of the Spirit.

“After that, he says those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.

“And then he says that if we live by the Spirit, we should also walk by the Spirit.”

He looked at the others.

“Paul doesn't present the Christian life as a struggle to make the flesh spiritual.

“He speaks about belonging to Christ, the crucifixion of the flesh, and walking by the Spirit.

“The life is from the Spirit. The fruit is from the Spirit.

“And the flesh is not the source of either.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“That brings us back to what we discussed about the fruit of the Spirit.

“I can imitate certain qualities through discipline. I can control my temper, speak politely, and behave patiently.

“Those changes may be useful.

“Yet I cannot produce the fruit of the Spirit through my own effort.

“The flesh cannot produce what belongs to the Spirit.”

Elías nodded.

“And that's why the distinction between the works of the flesh and the fruit of the Spirit matters.

“Paul isn't simply giving believers two lists and telling them to stop doing the first and work harder at producing the second.

“He's identifying two different sources.

“The flesh produces its works.

“The Spirit produces His fruit.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then the cross confronted carnality at its source.

“It didn't merely tell the flesh to behave better.

“It declared God's judgment upon what the flesh is.”

For a while they ate quietly.

Then Matías spoke again.

“There's something personal about this that I find difficult to explain.”

The others looked toward him.

“I've spent much of my life believing that improvement was always possible if I applied myself.

“And I brought that entire approach into my Christian life.

“I believed that if I could identify my spiritual weaknesses, I could gradually eliminate them.

“I knew I needed God's help. I prayed for it. I asked the Holy Spirit to strengthen me.

“Yet underneath all of that, I was still trying to produce the Christian life through my own improvement.”

He looked down at the Bible.

“Now I find Paul saying that the old man was crucified with Christ.

“And that those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh.

“Those statements leave no room for the idea that the flesh can eventually become spiritual if I work on it long enough.”

Elías listened without interrupting.

Matías continued.

“It's humbling.

“Because I've spent years trying to accomplish something God never asked the flesh to accomplish.

“And I've sometimes judged other believers according to how successfully they appeared to be doing the same thing.”

Marcos considered that.

“Perhaps that's one reason the message of the cross is so offensive to human pride.

“It removes the possibility of taking credit for becoming spiritual through our own efforts.”

Matías nodded.

“Yes.

“And I think that's why I've resisted it without realizing I was resisting it.

“I wanted to believe there was something I could accomplish.”

Adrián turned to Romans 6.

“There's another question.”

Marcos smiled.

“Of course there is.”

Adrián ignored him.

“If our old man was crucified with Christ, why does Paul tell believers not to let sin reign in their mortal bodies?”

Elías looked at the passage.

“Because what God has accomplished concerning our relationship to sin does not mean that believers are incapable of yielding to it.

“Paul tells the Romans that they have died to sin. Then he instructs them to consider themselves dead to sin and alive to God in Christ Jesus.

“After that, he tells them not to let sin reign in their mortal bodies.

“The instructions are grounded in what God has already done.”

He turned the Bible toward Adrián.

“Notice that Paul doesn't say, ‘Do not let sin reign so that you may eventually become dead to sin.’

“He says they have died to sin, then calls them to count upon that truth.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“Then considering ourselves dead to sin doesn't make the crucifixion happen.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's believing what God has declared.”

Marcos considered that.

“And the believer's conduct should reflect what is already true of him in Christ.”

“Yes.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Then the cross isn't another method for fighting the flesh.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's God's action concerning the old man and sin's dominion.

“And Paul wants believers to understand what that means.”

The conversation turned toward the ways churches speak about spiritual growth.

Marcos recalled a sermon he'd heard years earlier.

“The speaker told us that every Christian had two natures fighting for control. He compared them to two animals, each becoming stronger according to which one we fed.

“The lesson was that if we spent enough time praying, reading Scripture, and attending church, the spiritual side would become stronger.

“If we neglected those activities, the flesh would become stronger.

“I remember finding the illustration very convincing.”

Adrián smiled.

“I've heard that one.”

“So have I,” Matías said.

Marcos continued.

“Yet I wonder whether it encouraged me to think of the Christian life as a contest between two competing sources of strength.

“My responsibility was to make sure the spiritual side received enough attention to defeat the flesh.

“And once again, everything depended upon how well I managed the process.”

Elías considered the illustration.

“Scripture certainly speaks about conflict between the flesh and the Spirit. Galatians 5 says so plainly.

“Yet your confidence was in how well you could manage the contest. Paul doesn't tell believers to produce spiritual life by managing two competing strengths.

“Nor does he describe spiritual life as something produced by strengthening the flesh until it becomes obedient.

“He tells believers to walk by the Spirit.

“And he reminds them that those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh.”

Matías looked at him.

“Then the Christian life isn't a matter of giving the Spirit enough assistance to defeat the flesh.”

“No,” Elías said.

“The Spirit is the source of spiritual life.

“And the cross is God's accomplished work concerning the flesh.”

Anna returned to clear their plates.

“Everything good?”

“Very good,” Adrián said.

She looked at him.

“You seem unusually serious today.”

“We're discussing crucifixion.”

Anna paused.

“Well, that explains it.”

She gathered the plates.

“Would you like dessert?”

Adrián looked at the others.

“Perhaps we should.”

Marcos smiled.

“Why?”

“Because we've had a difficult morning.”

Anna shook her head.

“You always find a reason.”

“It's a gift.”

She laughed and returned to the counter.

Elías had been thinking about something Marcos said earlier.

“I think we should be careful about one more thing.

“When we say the flesh has been crucified, we must not turn that into a claim that believers can no longer act by the flesh.

“Paul addressed the Corinthians as people in Christ while confronting their jealousy and strife.

“The Galatians had received the Spirit, yet they were being drawn toward religious self-reliance.

“The cross had not failed in either case.

“The problem was that their conduct was inconsistent with what God had accomplished and the life He had given them.”

Marcos nodded.

“Then the answer isn't to crucify the flesh again.”

“No.”

“Or to improve it until it becomes spiritual.”

“No.”

Matías looked down at Romans 6.

“It's to know what God has done and count upon it.”

Elías smiled.

“That's where Paul begins.”

Adrián considered the passage.

“And to walk by the Spirit rather than relying upon the flesh.”

“Yes.”

He looked around the table.

“Then perhaps the real question isn't how successfully we're managing the flesh.

“It's whether we believe what God has said about what happened through Christ.”

They remained at the table for some time.

The conversation had become quieter, not because they had run out of things to say, but because the implications were beginning to reach beyond their investigation of churches.

Each man had spent years approaching the Christian life according to assumptions he rarely questioned.

Adrián had been drawn toward spiritual experiences and visible demonstrations of power.

Matías had trusted discipline and religious seriousness.

Marcos had valued organization, measurable results, and successful activity.

Elías had placed great confidence in doctrinal accuracy.

None of those things could become the source of spiritual life.

And now the message of the cross was confronting something deeper than their preferred approaches to church.

It was confronting their confidence in themselves.

When they finally stood to leave, Anna brought the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos looked at him.

“Again?”

“I'm practicing generosity.”

Matías smiled.

“Is that the fruit of the Spirit?”

Adrián considered the question.

“I'm not prepared to make that claim.”

Elías laughed.

“Probably wise.”

Anna handed him the bill.

“Whatever it is, I appreciate it.”

They said goodbye and stepped outside.

The afternoon was cool, and a light wind moved along the street.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

Matías looked toward Elías.

“I'd like to return to Romans 6 next Thursday.”

Elías nodded.

“There's still a great deal there.”

“And Galatians 5,” Marcos added.

Adrián smiled.

“Of course. We can't leave Galatia alone.”

They laughed.

Then Matías looked down at the Bible in his hand.

“What we read in Romans 6 is still unsettling me.

“The message of the cross doesn't merely expose the flesh.

“It confronts us with what God has already done.

“And that leaves me with a very different question from the one I've been asking all my life.”

Adrián looked at him.

“What question?”

Matías considered his words.

“I've always asked how I could become a better Christian.

“Now I'm asking whether I actually believe what God has said about my death with Christ.”

No one interrupted.

The question belonged to all of them.

As Adrián drove home, he thought about the expression that had troubled him all week.

*You need to crucify your flesh.*

He had spoken those words many times.

Now he understood why they had never quite settled the matter.

He had been telling people to accomplish something through determination, while Paul was directing believers to what had already happened in Christ.

The cross was not a method for improving the flesh.

It was not another discipline to master.

It was not something the believer could accomplish through sufficient effort.

It was God's decisive action concerning the old man and sin's dominion.

And those who belonged to Christ had crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.

Adrián thought about the churches they had examined.

Corinth.

Galatia.

Ephesus.

Laodicea.

Different appearances. Different problems. Different reasons for confidence.

Yet none of them could produce spiritual life through the flesh.

The cross confronted every such attempt.

Not by offering a better way to manage carnality.

By declaring what God had already accomplished in Christ.

And Adrián was beginning to understand that the question was no longer whether the flesh could be improved.

The question was whether believers would count upon what God had done.

# Chapter 42
## The Cross Disarmed Carnality

Adrián had been thinking about a man he knew from church.

The man was a sincere believer, someone who had spent years trying to overcome a particular pattern of sin. He had attended counseling sessions, joined accountability groups, and followed several programs designed to help him gain victory.

For a while, things would improve.

Then he would fail again.

Each failure seemed to leave him more discouraged than the one before it. He would confess what he had done, promise himself that it would never happen again, and begin another period of intense discipline.

Eventually, he began speaking about himself as though defeat were inevitable.

He believed Christ had forgiven him.

He wasn't nearly as certain that Christ had freed him.

Adrián remembered a conversation they'd had several months earlier.

The man had said something that now seemed especially significant.

“I know God has forgiven me. I just don't think I'll ever be free of this.”

At the time, Adrián had encouraged him to keep trying.

He had suggested another accountability arrangement.

Now he wondered whether he had offered the man more of the very thing that had left him exhausted.

When Adrián arrived at Deb's Place, Anna was preparing coffee.

“Good morning.”

She looked up.

“Morning. You're quiet today.”

“I'm thinking.”

“That usually costs somebody money.”

Adrián smiled.

“Not this time.”

“Good. I haven't finished counting yesterday's receipts.”

He took his coffee to the table.

Marcos arrived a few minutes later.

“You're early.”

“I had something on my mind.”

Marcos sat down.

“Anything serious?”

“Possibly.”

“Then I'll wait until the others arrive.”

“Wise decision.”

By the time Matías and Elías joined them, Adrián had been looking through Romans 6 for several minutes.

They ordered breakfast and talked briefly about the week.

Then Adrián told them about his friend.

“I've known him for years,” he said. “He's a good man. He loves his family, and I believe he genuinely wants to know Christ.

“Yet he speaks about his Christian life as though sin is something that will always have the final word.

“He believes in forgiveness. He just doesn't believe he can be free.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“I've known believers like that.”

“So have I,” Marcos said.

Adrián continued.

“What troubles me is that I've often encouraged them to keep doing the same things.

“Try harder. Become more disciplined. Find someone to hold you accountable.

“Some of those things may be useful in particular situations. Yet we've spent several weeks discovering that none of them can produce spiritual life.

“And now I'm wondering whether I've been telling people to fight for something God says Christ has already accomplished.”

Elías opened his Bible.

“That's a question worth examining.”

He turned to Colossians 2.

“There's a passage here that speaks about what God accomplished through the cross.”

He began with verse thirteen.

“You were dead through your trespasses and the uncircumcision of your flesh. He made you alive together with him, having forgiven us all our trespasses,”

Matías looked at the passage.

“That's the gospel we've always believed.”

“Yes,” Elías said. “And notice how completely Paul describes the forgiveness. He doesn't present it as a temporary arrangement that must be continually reestablished through human effort.

“He says God made us alive with Christ and forgave our trespasses.”

He continued into the next verse.

“wiping out the handwriting in ordinances which was against us; and he has taken it out of the way, nailing it to the cross;”

Marcos leaned forward.

“That's decisive language.”

“It is,” Elías said.

“Something that stood against us has been removed.

“And the action belongs to God.”

Matías studied the passage.

“I've always found that comforting when thinking about forgiveness. Yet I haven't always connected it with freedom.”

Elías turned to the next verse.

“having stripped the principalities and the powers, he made a show of them openly, triumphing over them in it.”

Adrián looked up.

“Stripped.”

“Yes.”

“That's an interesting word.”

“It is. Paul is describing a victory.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Something that possessed authority has been stripped of its claim.”

Elías nodded.

“That's the picture Paul gives us concerning the rulers and authorities.”

He looked around the table.

“And we should be precise about that. Colossians 2:15 speaks specifically about rulers and authorities. It doesn't say that the flesh itself was disarmed in that verse.

“We've been examining the flesh through Romans 6 and Galatians 5. Those passages address our death with Christ and the crucifixion of the flesh.

“Colossians adds another dimension to the victory accomplished through Christ. The record against us has been canceled, and the hostile powers have been defeated.

“We shouldn't confuse those statements. Together they show us how extensive God's work through the cross is.”

Adrián looked at the passage again.

“So the cross isn't merely an announcement that God is willing to forgive us.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Paul describes forgiveness, the cancellation of what stood against us, and victory over hostile powers.

“And in Romans, he tells us that our old man was crucified with Christ so that we would no longer be enslaved to sin.”

Anna brought their breakfasts.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Excellent.”

She glanced at him.

“You haven't tasted it.”

“I have confidence.”

“You had confidence when you carried four cups of coffee.”

Marcos laughed.

“That didn't end well.”

Adrián sighed.

“Apparently my failures are being preserved for future generations.”

Anna smiled.

“Only the memorable ones.”

She returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Marcos returned to Romans 6.

“There's something I've been thinking about since our last conversation.

“Paul says that our old man was crucified with Christ so that we would no longer be slaves to sin.

“That's very different from saying that believers must become strong enough to defeat sin through their own efforts.

“Slavery describes a relationship of dominion. A slave is under another's authority.

“And Paul says something happened through Christ's death that changed that relationship.”

He turned to verse fourteen.

“Then he says that sin shall not have dominion over believers, because they are not under law, but under grace.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“That's remarkable.”

Marcos continued.

“We've spent years treating sin as though it were an enemy that still possessed unquestioned authority over us.

“We've told believers they need to become stronger, more determined, and more disciplined if they hope to resist it.

“Yet Paul begins by telling them that sin's dominion has been broken through their death with Christ.

“He doesn't describe the believer as someone who must earn freedom through successful resistance.

“He describes someone whose relationship to sin has already changed.”

Elías looked at the surrounding passage.

“And that's why Paul can tell believers not to let sin reign in their mortal bodies.

“He isn't telling them to overthrow a dominion that Christ has left intact.

“He's instructing them in light of what God has accomplished.”

Matías considered the words.

“Then sin can still be obeyed, even though it no longer has the same claim upon the believer.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Paul's instructions make that clear.

“The believer can still present his members to sin. He can still act by the flesh.

“Yet he is no longer the man he was before being united with Christ.”

Adrián was thinking about his friend.

“Then perhaps the problem is that he still sees himself as a slave.”

Elías considered the statement.

“That may be part of it. We should be careful not to assume we understand everything involved in another person's struggle.

“Some patterns of behavior involve complicated circumstances, and people may need practical help, protection, or ongoing support.

“Yet if a believer understands himself only through his failures, he may never appreciate what God has declared concerning him in Christ.”

Adrián nodded.

“That's what I mean.

“My friend talks as though sin has the final word.

“He believes Christ forgave him, yet he seems to think the rest of his Christian life depends upon his ability to become strong enough to defeat what still controls him.

“And every time he fails, he becomes more convinced that he cannot succeed.”

Matías looked at him.

“I recognize that way of thinking.

“For years, I measured my spiritual condition by how successfully I controlled my behavior.

“When I was doing well, I felt confident.

“When I failed, I felt defeated.

“And my confidence rose and fell according to my performance.”

He paused.

“Now I wonder whether I was placing my confidence in the wrong thing.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“In yourself.”

“Yes.”

Matías continued.

“Paul doesn't tell me that sin's dominion will be broken once I become sufficiently disciplined.

“He tells me that my old man was crucified with Christ.

“And he tells me to consider myself dead to sin and alive to God in Christ Jesus.

“That's a very different basis for confidence.”

Elías turned to Romans 8.

“There's another passage that belongs with this.”

He found the first verse.

“There is therefore now no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus, who don’t walk according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit.”

Matías looked at the words.

He knew them well.

They had comforted him after his mother's death, when grief and regret had left him questioning things he could no longer change.

He had returned to that verse many times.

Elías continued.

“Paul isn't describing a believer who has finally become disciplined enough to escape condemnation.

“He's describing those who are in Christ Jesus.

“The absence of condemnation rests upon their position in Him.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“And that changes the place of fear.”

“It should,” Elías said.

“Fear of condemnation can be a powerful means of controlling people.

“A church may teach believers that every failure places them under renewed condemnation, and that they must perform certain actions to restore their standing.

“Such teaching can produce tremendous anxiety.

“And anxious people can become very compliant.”

Adrián considered that.

“Which makes fear a useful tool for managing behavior.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Yet Paul directs believers to the reality that there is no condemnation for those who are in Christ.

“That doesn't make sin harmless.

“It means condemnation is not the basis upon which God calls His children to live.”

Matías remained looking at the verse.

“I've known people who lived under constant fear of displeasing God.

“They were serious believers. They wanted to do what was right.

“Yet their entire Christian life seemed to revolve around avoiding condemnation.”

Marcos considered that.

“And perhaps they were never able to enjoy the life they already possessed in Christ.”

Adrián leaned back.

“There's something almost strange about the way we approach freedom.

“We tell believers that Christ has set them free.

“Then we give them a long list of things they must accomplish before they can begin living as though they're free.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“I've probably given people some of those lists.”

“So have I,” Adrián said.

“And I meant well.

“Yet I wonder whether we sometimes make freedom sound like the reward for successful Christian living rather than something Christ has accomplished.”

Elías turned to Galatians 5.

“Paul says that Christ set us free for freedom.

“And he warns the Galatians not to become entangled again in a yoke of slavery.

“Remember their situation. They weren't being tempted primarily by obvious immorality. They were being drawn toward religious observance as a basis for their standing before God.

“Paul regarded that as a serious threat to the freedom they had in Christ.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then slavery can take more than one form.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“A believer may live as though sin remains his master.

“He may also place himself under a religious system that makes his confidence depend upon human observance.

“In both cases, he can lose sight of what God has accomplished in Christ.”

Matías considered the thought.

“And the flesh can find a place in either arrangement.”

“Certainly.”

For a while the conversation moved to other things.

Marcos mentioned a difficult conversation he'd had with an employee who was considering leaving his company. The man had received another offer, and Marcos had been disappointed.

“I wanted to convince him to stay,” he said. “I had several arguments prepared.

“Then I realized that I was more concerned about losing a good employee than about what might actually be best for him.”

Adrián smiled.

“Did you tell him that?”

“Not in quite those words.”

“What did you say?”

“I told him I valued his work and wanted him to make the decision he believed was right for his family.”

Matías nodded.

“That sounds reasonable.”

“It was. And he still hasn't decided.”

Adrián laughed.

“Then perhaps you should have offered him a larger office.”

Marcos smiled.

“I considered it.”

The conversation drifted toward their families and the coming weekend.

Eventually Elías returned to the subject of the cross.

“I think we need to be careful about the word *disarmed*,” he said.

Adrián looked toward him.

“Because of Colossians?”

“Yes. And because it's easy to turn a biblical truth into an explanation that goes beyond what Scripture says.

“Colossians tells us that the rulers and authorities were disarmed through Christ's triumph.

“Romans tells us that our old man was crucified with Christ so that we would no longer be enslaved to sin.

“Galatians tells us that those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.

“Those passages are related through Christ's accomplished work, yet each makes a particular statement.

“We don't need to force them into saying exactly the same thing.”

Marcos nodded.

“Then when we say the cross disarmed carnality, we're describing the consequence of what God has done concerning sin's dominion, not claiming that Colossians 2:15 directly identifies the flesh as one of the rulers and authorities.”

“Precisely.”

Elías continued.

“And we shouldn't imagine that the flesh has become harmless.

“Paul still warns believers against its desires and works.

“Yet the believer is no longer under sin's dominion as he once was.

“He has died with Christ.

“He belongs to Christ.

“And the Spirit of God dwells in him.”

Matías looked at Romans 6.

“Then the flesh may still make demands, yet those demands do not establish its rightful authority over the believer.”

“Yes.”

Adrián considered that.

“So the flesh can shout.”

Marcos smiled.

“Apparently.”

Adrián continued.

“Yet the cross has already spoken.”

Elías looked at him.

“And what God has accomplished does not become less true because the believer is struggling to understand it.”

The table grew quiet.

Matías had been thinking about how much of his Christian life had been governed by fear of failure.

He had never questioned whether Christ was sufficient to save him.

Yet he had often lived as though Christ's work had left the rest of his spiritual life dependent upon his own ability.

Now he was beginning to see something different.

The cross had not merely made forgiveness available.

God had dealt with the old man through Christ's death.

Sin's dominion had been broken.

The believer was no longer the person he had been in Adam.

And the life he now possessed was Christ Himself.

Matías looked toward the others.

“There's something I want to say.”

They listened.

“I think I've spent years trying to become strong enough to live the Christian life.

“And perhaps that's why I found it so exhausting.

“I believed I needed to strengthen myself against the flesh.

“Yet Paul tells me that my old man was crucified with Christ.

“He tells me that sin shall not have dominion over me.

“And he tells me that Christ is my life.”

He looked down at his Bible.

“I'm beginning to understand that the Christian life is not about becoming strong enough to overcome the flesh through my own efforts.

“It's about living in the reality of what God has already accomplished.”

Marcos considered that.

“And that changes the entire struggle.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“It does.”

Anna brought the bill, and Adrián paid it.

Outside, the afternoon was cool and bright.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

Adrián looked toward Elías.

“I think I want to speak with my friend again.”

“What will you tell him?”

Adrián considered the question.

“I'm not sure yet.

“I don't want to give him another program.

“And I don't want to make promises about how quickly his struggles will disappear.

“I think I'd like to sit with him and look at Romans 6.

“Perhaps we can begin with what God has said about him in Christ.”

Elías smiled.

“That sounds like a good place to begin.”

As Adrián drove home, he thought about the man who had told him he would never be free.

He remembered how discouraged the man had sounded.

How convinced he had become that sin would always have the final word.

Adrián had wanted to help him.

And perhaps some of the practical advice he'd offered had been useful.

Yet he was beginning to understand that freedom could not be manufactured through human effort.

The cross had already addressed something far deeper than the believer's ability to control his behavior.

God had acted through His Son.

The old man had been crucified with Christ.

Sin's dominion had been broken.

And those who belonged to Christ were no longer under condemnation.

Adrián thought again of the words from Colossians.

God had made believers alive with Christ.

He had forgiven their trespasses.

He had canceled what stood against them.

And He had triumphed over the hostile powers.

The cross was not a partial victory waiting for human determination to complete it.

It was God's accomplished work.

Adrián smiled faintly as he turned onto his street.

For years he had imagined the Christian life as an endless struggle to become strong enough to resist the flesh.

Now he was beginning to understand why Paul spoke so confidently about freedom.

The believer's confidence was not meant to rest in his own strength.

It rested in Christ.

And for the first time, the problem of carnality no longer seemed quite as overwhelming as it once had.

Not because Adrián had discovered a better way to fight it.

Because he was beginning to believe what God had already done.

# Chapter 43
## The Cross Protects from Carnality

Adrián had been thinking about his conversation with the man who believed he would never be free from sin.

They had met for coffee a few days earlier.

Adrián had brought his Bible, intending to spend some time in Romans 6. He had prepared several passages and even made a few notes about the believer's death with Christ.

The conversation hadn't gone quite as he expected.

His friend had listened carefully as Adrián explained that the old man had been crucified with Christ and that sin no longer had dominion over those who belonged to Him.

Then the man asked a question.

“If all that's true, why do I still struggle?”

Adrián had begun explaining the difference between the believer's position in Christ and his conduct.

His friend listened patiently.

Then he asked another question.

“What am I supposed to do when temptation comes?”

Adrián had opened his mouth to answer.

And stopped.

He knew what he would have said a few months earlier.

Resist it.

Pray harder.

Remember the consequences.

Call your accountability partner.

Keep yourself occupied.

Some of that advice might have been useful in particular circumstances. He understood the importance of avoiding situations where someone could harm himself or others.

Yet he was beginning to recognize the familiar pattern.

Once again, the Christian life was becoming a matter of what the believer must accomplish through his own strength to overcome the flesh.

Adrián had finally told his friend that he wanted to think about the question before answering.

He had been thinking about it ever since.

At Deb's Place, Anna was arranging menus when Adrián arrived.

“You look troubled.”

“Do I?”

“A little.”

“Perhaps I'm becoming more serious.”

She considered him.

“That would be a remarkable development.”

Adrián smiled.

“Your encouragement means a great deal.”

“Coffee?”

“Please.”

She poured him a cup.

“Whatever it is, I hope you work it out.”

“Thank you, Anna.”

He took his coffee to the table.

Marcos arrived shortly afterward, followed by Matías and Elías.

They greeted one another, ordered breakfast, and talked about the week.

Matías had received another collection of photographs from his father in Argentina. Marcos had finally resolved the scheduling problem that had been occupying him for several weeks. Elías and his wife had begun planning a short trip together.

Adrián listened, asked questions, and laughed at the appropriate moments.

Eventually Marcos noticed that he was quieter than usual.

“Something on your mind?”

“Yes.”

“Anything you'd like to discuss?”

Adrián opened his Bible.

“I think it belongs with what we've been studying.”

He told them about his conversation with his friend.

Then he repeated the question.

“What am I supposed to do when temptation comes?”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“That's a reasonable question.”

“Of course it is,” Adrián said. “And I realized that I wasn't sure how to answer without returning to the same approach we've been examining.

“I could tell him to fight harder.

“I could give him a list of things to avoid.

“I could encourage him to become more disciplined.

“Yet we've already established that the flesh cannot produce spiritual life.

“So what does Scripture actually say?”

Elías turned to Galatians 5.

“I think Paul answers that question more directly than we sometimes realize.”

He found verse sixteen.

“Paul says, ‘But I say, walk by the Spirit, and you won’t fulfill the lust of the flesh.’”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“That sounds almost too simple.”

“It does,” Elías said. “Especially when we're accustomed to complicated approaches to overcoming sin.”

He left the Bible open.

“Notice what Paul doesn't say here.

“He doesn't tell believers to make the flesh stronger so that it will become obedient.

“He doesn't instruct them to spend their lives concentrating upon the flesh and measuring its every movement.

“He directs them to walk by the Spirit.

“And he states the result: they will not carry out the desire of the flesh.”

Matías read the verse again.

“That's a very different order from the one I've followed most of my life.”

“How so?” Marcos asked.

“I've always begun with the problem.

“If I was struggling with impatience, I concentrated on becoming patient.

“If I was struggling with anger, I worked on controlling my anger.

“If I was struggling with pride, I tried to become more humble.

“I identified the behavior I wanted to change and then applied myself to changing it.

“And I asked the Holy Spirit to help me succeed.”

He looked at Galatians.

“Paul begins somewhere else.

“He tells believers to walk by the Spirit.

“The result is that they will not carry out the desire of the flesh.”

Marcos considered the distinction.

“Then Paul isn't presenting victory over the flesh as the result of becoming increasingly skilled at managing it.”

“No,” Elías said.

“He directs believers to the Spirit, who is the source of spiritual life.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Wonderful.”

She smiled.

“I'm glad you're pleased.”

“I'm beginning to think you enjoy feeding us.”

“I enjoy having customers who pay.”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián looked toward the others.

“You see? Even Anna understands the importance of results.”

“Especially financial ones,” she said.

She returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Marcos returned to Galatians.

“There's something I want to understand.

“We've been saying that the cross protects believers from carnality.

“Yet the passage we're reading speaks about walking by the Spirit.

“How do those things belong together?”

Elías considered the question.

“That's important. We shouldn't confuse what the cross accomplished with the Spirit's present work in the believer.

“Through Christ's death, our old man was crucified with Him. Romans 6 tells us that our relationship to sin's dominion has changed.

“Galatians tells us that those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh with its passions and lusts.

“And now Paul tells believers to walk by the Spirit.

“The cross is not being repeated each day.

“The Spirit is not trying to complete a victory Christ failed to accomplish.

“The believer is called to live in the reality of what God has already done.”

Matías looked at him.

“Then the cross protects us because its accomplished work is the basis of our freedom.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And the Spirit is the One by whom we walk.

“We need both truths.

“If we speak only about the cross without considering the Spirit, we may treat our death with Christ as an interesting doctrine with little connection to daily life.

“If we speak about the Spirit without understanding what God accomplished through the cross, we may begin treating Him as someone who helps us improve the flesh.

“Paul doesn't present those things as separate approaches to Christian living.

“They belong together in Christ.”

Adrián had been following the conversation carefully.

“Then perhaps I've been looking for the answer in the wrong place.”

Marcos smiled.

“Again?”

“It's becoming a specialty.”

The others laughed.

Adrián continued.

“When my friend asked what he should do when temptation comes, I immediately began thinking about actions he could take to defeat it.

“And perhaps that's why I found the question difficult.

“I was still imagining the Christian life as a struggle between the believer and the flesh.

“The believer needs to become strong enough to resist.

“And if he isn't strong enough, he needs more help.”

He looked at Galatians 5.

“Yet Paul says to walk by the Spirit.

“He isn't describing the believer becoming strong enough to defeat the flesh through his own effort.

“He's describing a life that comes from another source.”

Matías nodded.

“That's what we discovered when we discussed the fruit of the Spirit.

“Love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faith, gentleness, and self-control are not the products of our independent effort.

“They are the fruit of the Spirit.

“And if the Spirit produces the fruit, then the believer's responsibility cannot be to manufacture it.”

Elías looked at the passage.

“Exactly.

“The believer bears fruit because of the life he possesses in Christ.

“The branch doesn't produce the life of the vine.

“It bears fruit through that life.”

Marcos considered the illustration.

“Then walking by the Spirit isn't another method for producing spirituality.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's living by the One who is already the source of spiritual life.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“But what will that mean to him when temptation comes? I can't just repeat the words.”

Matías had been thinking about the word *walk*.

“There's something ordinary about that expression,” he said.

“Paul doesn't describe a special experience reserved for unusually mature believers.

“He speaks about walking.

“It's the language of daily life.”

Marcos nodded.

“And that matters because so much of our thinking about spiritual victory concerns extraordinary moments.

“A crisis.

“A temptation.

“A serious failure.

“We become intensely concerned about the flesh when something goes wrong.

“Yet Paul speaks about the ordinary course of life.”

Matías continued.

“I think I've spent years treating the Christian life as a series of problems to overcome.

“I would identify a weakness, work on it, and then move to the next one.

“My attention was constantly directed toward what needed improvement.

“And because there was always something else to correct, I rarely felt that I had arrived anywhere.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“An endless construction project.”

Matías laughed.

“Exactly. And I was both the contractor and the building.”

The others laughed.

Then Matías became thoughtful again.

“Now I wonder whether I was so occupied with improving myself that I gave very little attention to the life I already possessed in Christ.

“I knew the Scriptures.

“I believed the doctrines.

“I prayed for help.

“Yet my confidence still rested largely upon whether I was succeeding.”

Elías looked at him.

“And Paul directs your attention away from the flesh as the source of spiritual life.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“Toward the Spirit.”

Marcos turned to Romans 8.

“There's a passage here that helps me understand what you're saying.”

He found the fifth verse.

“Paul speaks about those who are according to the flesh setting their minds on the things of the flesh, and those who are according to the Spirit setting their minds on the things of the Spirit.”

He looked toward Elías.

“We should be careful here. Verse one says, ‘who don’t walk according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit.’ How do those words fit into Paul's argument?”

Elías nodded.

“Very careful.

“Paul is contrasting those who belong to the fleshly realm with those who belong to the Spirit. He makes that clear in verse nine, where he tells believers they are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit, if God's Spirit dwells in them.

“That's a positional statement.

“Believers can still act by the flesh, as Corinth demonstrates.

“Yet Paul doesn't describe them as moving in and out of their position in Christ according to their behavior.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then the believer's life is grounded in the Spirit who dwells in him.”

“Yes.”

“And the Christian life is not an attempt to enter the Spirit through successful conduct.”

“No,” Elías said.

“The believer is already in the Spirit.

“The question concerns living consistently with the life and position God has given him.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“That's a very different way of understanding the Christian life.

“We've spent years telling believers what they must do to become spiritual.

“Paul tells them what God has made true of them in Christ.”

The conversation moved toward the practical implications.

Marcos had been considering Adrián's friend.

“I think we should acknowledge something.

“A believer who is struggling with destructive behavior may need practical assistance.

“If someone is harming his family, he may need immediate boundaries.

“If someone is dealing with an addiction, he may need professional treatment or ongoing support.

“If a person has developed dangerous habits, it may be wise to avoid circumstances that make those habits easier to repeat.

“Those things can be important.

“Yet they cannot produce the life of Christ.”

Elías nodded.

“And we shouldn't confuse them with the source of spiritual freedom.

“Practical measures may restrain harm or help someone through a difficult situation.

“They do not accomplish what the cross accomplished.

“And they do not produce what the Spirit produces.”

Adrián considered that.

“Then I don't need to tell my friend that every practical step he's taken was useless.”

“Certainly not,” Marcos said.

“Some may have been helpful.”

Adrián nodded.

“Yet I do need to help him understand that those steps aren't the source of his life in Christ.”

Elías smiled.

“That's the distinction.”

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured carefully.

“You gentlemen seem very serious today.”

“We're discussing freedom.”

Anna looked at him.

“From what?”

“From trying to fix ourselves.”

She considered that.

“Sounds like a relief.”

Adrián smiled.

“It is beginning to.”

She moved on to another table.

Marcos watched her go.

“Anna has a gift for getting to the point.”

“She certainly does,” Matías said.

Elías returned to Galatians 5.

“There's something else about verse sixteen that I think we need to appreciate.

“Paul doesn't say that if believers walk by the Spirit, they might eventually become strong enough to resist the flesh.

“He says they will not carry out its desire.

“That's a definite statement.

“The protection is not found in the believer's ability to overpower the flesh.

“It's found in walking by the Spirit.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“Then the result is certain because of the source.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“The Spirit does not produce the works of the flesh.

“He produces His own fruit.”

Marcos considered the thought.

“And that's why the flesh doesn't need to be the center of the believer's attention.”

Elías nodded.

“We should recognize its works and refuse to regard them as spiritual. Paul certainly warns believers about them.

“Yet our life is not found in endlessly examining the flesh.

“Our life is Christ.

“And the Spirit is the One who gives expression to that life in us.”

Adrián leaned back.

“That's what I've been missing.

“I've spent years thinking that the Christian life was primarily about avoiding the flesh.

“Now I'm beginning to understand that it's about living by the Spirit.”

Matías opened to Galatians 2.

“I want to return to something Paul said earlier in the letter.”

He found verse twenty.

“I have been crucified with Christ, and it is no longer I that live, but Christ lives in me. That life which I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me, and gave himself up for me.”

He looked at his friends.

“We've discussed that verse several times.

“Yet I think it belongs here again.

“Paul doesn't describe the Christian life as a man who has become sufficiently disciplined to imitate Christ.

“He says Christ lives in him.

“And he says that the life he now lives, he lives by faith in the Son of God.”

Elías nodded.

“Faith is essential here.

“Not because faith makes Christ's work complete.

“Not because believing strongly enough produces a crucifixion that hasn't happened.

“Faith receives and counts upon what God has already accomplished.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then the Christian life is not something we manufacture through our own efforts.

“It's the life of Christ expressed through believers who live by faith in Him.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“That's very different from what I believed for years.”

For a while the table was quiet.

Adrián was thinking about his friend again.

He remembered the man's discouragement, his repeated promises to improve, and his conviction that sin would always defeat him.

He wondered what might happen if the man began to understand that Christ had not merely forgiven his failures.

God had placed him in Christ.

His old man had been crucified with Christ.

Sin no longer possessed the same dominion over him.

And the Spirit of God dwelt in him.

Adrián realized that he had often encouraged his friend to become stronger.

Perhaps what the man needed was to discover that his life was not found in his own strength at all.

Marcos broke the silence.

“There's something remarkable about the direction our investigation has taken.

“We began by examining churches.

“We wanted to understand why Corinth was called fleshly and whether similar expressions of carnality could appear in other congregations.

“We found religious self-reliance in Galatia.

“We examined the confidence of Laodicea.

“We considered the fallen condition of Ephesus.

“Then we began examining how churches try to manage carnality.

“And now we've arrived at something entirely different.”

Adrián looked toward him.

“What?”

“The life of Christ.”

Marcos continued.

“We began by asking how the flesh could be corrected.

“Now we're discovering that the answer was never to make the flesh spiritual.

“God has dealt with our old man through the cross.

“And the believer's life is Christ Himself.”

Matías nodded.

“Which means the Christian life isn't an endless struggle to produce something we don't possess.

“It's living by faith in the One who is already our life.”

Elías looked at the others.

“Then we can see why the cross protects believers from carnality.

“Not because the crucifixion is being repeated.

“Not because the believer must continually accomplish what God has already done.

“The cross established the reality upon which our freedom rests.

“Our old man was crucified with Christ.

“We are no longer under sin's dominion as we once were.

“And we have been given life in Christ.

“The Spirit now enables us to walk in keeping with that life.”

Adrián considered the words.

“Then the protection is not another method.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's grounded in what God has accomplished and expressed through the Spirit's work in us.”

Matías looked at Galatians 5.

“And Paul tells us the result.

“But I say, walk by the Spirit, and you won’t fulfill the lust of the flesh.”

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Again?”

“I'm becoming very consistent.”

Matías looked at him.

“Careful. You may begin taking pride in your discipline.”

Adrián laughed.

“Then perhaps one of you should pay.”

Marcos reached for his wallet.

“Gladly.”

Adrián pulled the bill away.

“Not so fast.”

Anna shook her head.

“Every week.”

They laughed as they gathered their things.

Outside, the afternoon was cool and bright.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

Adrián looked toward Elías.

“I think I know what I want to tell my friend.”

Elías smiled.

“What?”

“I want to tell him that his life isn't found in his ability to overcome the flesh.

“It's found in Christ.

“And I want to show him what Paul says about walking by the Spirit.”

Matías nodded.

“That sounds like a good conversation.”

Adrián smiled.

“I hope so.”

As Adrián drove home, he thought about the weeks they had spent examining carnality.

At first, the flesh had seemed to be everywhere.

In divisions.

In religious pride.

In self-sufficiency.

In impressive activity.

In attempts to manage sin.

Even in the belief that the Holy Spirit existed to help believers produce their own spiritual lives.

The more they examined it, the more resourceful the flesh appeared.

Yet something had changed.

The cross had confronted carnality.

The cross had disarmed its claim to dominion.

And the cross still protected believers through the freedom established in Christ.

The believer did not need to accomplish that victory again.

He needed to know what God had done, count upon it, and walk by the Spirit.

Adrián thought again of Galatians 5.

*But I say, walk by the Spirit, and you won’t fulfill the lust of the flesh.*

For years, he had regarded the Christian life as a constant effort to become strong enough to resist sin.

Now he was beginning to understand that the flesh was not meant to occupy the center of his attention.

Christ was his life.

And the Spirit was the One through whom that life was expressed.

As he turned onto his street, Adrián remembered a question he'd asked weeks earlier.

If the cross had accomplished so much, why did churches continue spending so much energy trying to manage the flesh?

He had no easy answer.

Yet he suspected that the question would take them back to some of the most uncomfortable things they'd discovered about themselves.

And about the churches they loved.

# Chapter 44
## Why Carnality Management Still Exists

The question had followed Matías home.

They had spent weeks examining carnality, tracing its expressions through four churches, and discovering what Scripture said about the cross.

Their investigation had taken them somewhere none of them had expected.

They had begun by asking why believers acted by the flesh.

Then they had asked how the flesh could be corrected.

And now they were discovering that God had never intended the flesh to become the source of spiritual life.

The old man had been crucified with Christ.

Those who belonged to Christ had crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.

Sin no longer possessed the dominion it once had over them.

And the believer's life was Christ Himself.

Matías believed those things.

He had read the passages repeatedly.

Yet another question continued troubling him.

If God had already accomplished so much through the cross, why did churches continue organizing so much of their life around controlling the flesh?

More troubling still, why had he spent so many years doing exactly that?

At Deb's Place, Adrián was standing beside the counter when Matías arrived.

Anna was listening to him with an expression of patient resignation.

“I'm only suggesting that you consider a loyalty program.”

“We have one.”

Adrián looked surprised.

“You do?”

“Yes. Loyal customers keep coming back.”

“That's not a program.”

“It's working.”

Matías laughed.

“Good morning.”

Anna smiled.

“Morning, Matías. Please tell your friend that not everything needs a program.”

Matías looked toward Adrián.

“I'm beginning to understand that.”

Adrián pointed at him.

“That's a theological answer to a business question.”

“Perhaps you've been asking the wrong person.”

“Apparently.”

They took their usual seats.

Marcos arrived a few minutes later, followed by Elías.

Breakfast was ordered, coffee poured, and the familiar conversation began.

They talked about their families and the week ahead. Marcos had been invited to speak at a business luncheon. Matías was considering a visit to Argentina. Elías had enjoyed an evening with his wife that involved no plans whatsoever, which he admitted was becoming one of his favorite ways to spend time.

Eventually Adrián looked around the table.

“There's something I want to ask.”

Marcos smiled.

“I think we know what's coming.”

Adrián opened his Bible.

“If the cross confronted carnality, if it disarmed sin's claim to dominion, and if believers are protected as they walk by the Spirit, why do churches still spend so much energy trying to manage the flesh?”

He looked toward Matías.

“We've talked about this before. Yet I don't think we've really answered it.”

Matías considered the question.

“I think part of the answer is that we don't fully believe what God has said.”

He rested his hands on the table.

“I don't mean that we consciously deny the cross. We believe Christ died for our sins. We believe salvation is by grace. We preach those things.

“Yet when we begin discussing the Christian life, our confidence often shifts.

“We start talking about discipline, rules, accountability, commitment, and effort.

“We establish expectations and measure whether people are meeting them.

“And when someone struggles, we assume the answer is to increase the pressure.”

Marcos nodded thoughtfully.

“Because we still regard the flesh as the central problem that must be controlled.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And because we believe controlling it is our responsibility.

“If we assume the flesh remains the dominant power in a believer's life, then we have to find some way to manage it.

“We cannot simply allow people to do whatever they please. We have responsibilities toward one another. Serious misconduct must be addressed. People need protection from harm.

“So we develop systems.

“And gradually, those systems can become the very foundation of our approach to Christian living.”

Elías had been listening.

“There's another assumption underneath that,” he said.
