“We may believe that Christ has forgiven the believer while treating the rest of the Christian life as something that must be produced through human effort.

“We accept the cross as sufficient for salvation.

“Then we become less certain that what God accomplished in Christ is sufficient as the basis for living.”

Matías looked at him.

“That's exactly how I understood it.”

Marcos leaned back.

“I think we should consider why systems are so attractive.

“In business, systems provide predictability. They establish responsibilities, define expectations, and help people know what to do.

“And there's nothing inherently wrong with that.

“Churches also need practical arrangements. Someone must handle finances, organize meetings, protect children, and ensure that responsibilities are carried out.

“Yet the problem begins when we confuse the ability to organize people with the ability to produce spiritual life.”

He paused.

“Suppose a church decides that its members need to become more committed.

“So it establishes attendance expectations, ministry requirements, and accountability arrangements.

“After a few months, attendance improves. More people volunteer. Meetings become better organized.

“The leaders are pleased.

“And perhaps some genuinely good things have happened.

“Yet what has actually been established?

“That people are attending more frequently.

“That more work is being accomplished.

“That the organization is functioning more effectively.

“None of those things tells us whether the people are depending upon Christ.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Yet the results are visible.”

“Exactly,” Marcos said.

“And visible results are reassuring.

“It's much easier to count attendance than to determine whether a believer is walking by the Spirit.

“You can measure how many people completed a discipleship program.

“You cannot measure the life of Christ by the number of assignments they submitted.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“And if you're someone who likes measurable progress, that can be difficult to accept.”

Marcos laughed.

“I've noticed.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián examined his plate.

“Excellent.”

She looked at him.

“Would you like me to record that in your loyalty program?”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián smiled.

“I think you're beginning to appreciate my ideas.”

“Don't get carried away.”

She returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Elías opened to Galatians.

“There's something in Paul's letter that I think explains why this problem persists.

“The Galatians had received the Spirit by hearing of faith.

“Paul reminded them of that.

“Then he asked, ‘Are you so foolish? Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?’”

He looked around the table.

“That's a remarkable question.

“Paul was not addressing people who had openly rejected Christ.

“He was addressing believers who had begun by the Spirit and were being drawn toward religious effort as the means of completion.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“That's what makes Galatia so important to our investigation.

“Religious effort can appear to be a natural continuation of faith.

“We believe Christ saved us.

“Then we assume that becoming spiritual depends on what we can produce.

“And because we want to be serious Christians, we apply ourselves.”

Elías nodded.

“Yet Paul challenges the source of that confidence.

“The flesh cannot complete what began by the Spirit.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Then carnality management survives partly because it looks like responsibility.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And sometimes because it looks like maturity.

“A church with clear rules, disciplined members, and well-organized ministries may appear very strong.

“Yet those things cannot establish that its life is being produced by the Spirit.”

Adrián had been thinking about something he'd heard repeatedly over the years.

“There's a sentence that seems to appear whenever grace is discussed.”

Matías smiled.

“Only one?”

“Several, actually. Yet this one is especially common.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“‘I believe in grace, but I would never teach it that way.’”

Marcos looked at him.

“I've heard that.”

“So have I,” Elías said.

Adrián continued.

“Usually the explanation is that people will take advantage.

“If we emphasize forgiveness, they'll become careless.

“If we tell them they're no longer under law, they'll stop obeying.

“If we teach that Christ is their life, they'll think they don't need to do anything.”

He took a drink of coffee.

“And eventually someone asks the question that seems to settle everything.”

Matías looked toward him.

“Where do you draw the line?”

Adrián pointed at him.

“Exactly.”

He continued.

“I've heard that question so many times.

“Where do you draw the line?

“How much freedom should people have?

“How much grace can you teach before people begin taking advantage?

“And somehow the entire conversation becomes a discussion about how much control the church needs to maintain.”

Marcos considered the question.

“There's a legitimate concern underneath some of that. Leaders cannot ignore harmful conduct. A church has responsibilities toward its people.

“Yet if the central question becomes how much grace we can safely allow, we've already begun treating grace as the danger.”

Matías nodded.

“And human control as the protection.”

Elías looked at Romans 6.

“Paul anticipated that objection.

“Romans 6 begins by asking whether we should continue in sin so that grace may abound.

“He doesn't avoid the question.

“Nor does he answer by placing believers back under the law.

“He directs them to their death with Christ.”

He found the passage.

“Paul's answer rests upon what God has accomplished.

“Those who died to sin are not being encouraged to continue living under its dominion.

“And those who belong to Christ have been given a new life.”

Matías looked at the verses.

“That's very different from saying grace must be balanced by enough law to keep people under control.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Paul doesn't present grace and law as two competing ingredients that must be mixed in the correct proportions.

“He tells believers that sin shall not have dominion over them because they are not under law, but under grace.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then perhaps we've been trying to use the law to accomplish something Paul says it cannot accomplish.”

Elías nodded.

“Romans 8 tells us that what the law could not do because of the flesh, God did through His Son.

“The law can identify sin.

“It cannot produce the life of Christ.”

Adrián leaned back.

“So when we become frightened of grace, we return to the very thing that couldn't solve the problem.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“And call it balance.”

For a while the table was quiet.

Then Marcos spoke.

“I think there's another reason carnality management survives.

“Control feels safer than dependence.”

Elías looked toward him.

“Explain.”

Marcos considered his words.

“If I establish a system, I know what I expect people to do.

“I can monitor whether they're doing it.

“I can correct them when they don't.

“And I can report whether the system is producing the results I intended.

“That's reassuring.

“Dependence upon Christ is different.

“I cannot manufacture spiritual life in another person.

“I cannot produce the fruit of the Spirit through administrative procedures.

“And I cannot guarantee that someone will become spiritually mature according to my schedule.”

He looked at his friends.

“For a man accustomed to managing organizations, that's uncomfortable.

“It means there are things I cannot control.”

Matías smiled.

“I understand that feeling.”

Marcos continued.

“And when leaders become uncomfortable with what they cannot control, they may begin increasing control over the things they can.

“More rules.

“More monitoring.

“More expectations.

“More pressure.

“Eventually the system itself becomes the source of confidence.”

Elías considered the thought.

“And when someone questions the system, the question can feel like a threat to the church.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“Because the system has become inseparable from what the leaders believe keeps the church spiritually healthy.”

Elías thought of Daniel.

What Marcos had said about control brought Elías back to his own part in Daniel's rejection.

Daniel had not been encouraging people to sin.

He had been asking whether their established teaching about confession and fellowship was supported by Scripture.

Yet the discussion had quickly become a matter of submission.

Elías looked toward his friends.

“I think that's part of what happened with Daniel.”

Matías nodded.

“I was wondering whether you'd bring him up.”

Elías continued.

“We believed we were protecting sound doctrine.

“And that concern was sincere.

“Yet when Daniel questioned our explanation, we began treating his willingness to accept it as evidence of whether he was properly submitted.

“We were no longer simply examining Scripture.

“We were protecting an established understanding.”

Adrián looked at him.

“And when he wouldn't stop asking questions, he became the problem.”

“Yes.”

Elías was quiet for a moment.

“It's difficult to admit how easily that can happen.

“A leader may begin with a genuine desire to protect believers.

“Over time, he develops established answers, procedures, and expectations.

“Those things become familiar.

“And familiarity can begin to feel like certainty.

“Then someone asks a question that challenges the arrangement.

“Instead of examining the question, the leader may feel compelled to defend the arrangement.”

Marcos considered that.

“Because changing it would require admitting that something might have been wrong.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And sometimes the flesh would rather protect its reputation than examine the truth.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“There's another thing I've noticed.

“When someone begins teaching grace, the labels often appear very quickly.”

Matías smiled.

“Hyper-grace?”

“That's one.”

Elías nodded.

“Antinomianism is another.”

Adrián continued.

“Now, there are genuine errors people can teach about grace. We shouldn't pretend otherwise.

“Yet I've heard those labels used before anyone explained what the person was actually teaching.

“Someone emphasizes that believers are forgiven in Christ.

“Someone questions whether the law is the means of spiritual growth.

“Someone teaches that Christ Himself is our life.

“And suddenly people are warned that the teaching is dangerous.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Sometimes the label becomes a substitute for examining Scripture.”

“Exactly,” Adrián said.

“And once the label is attached, people become afraid to listen.”

Elías considered that.

“A sound response to error requires identifying the error.

“If someone is teaching something contrary to Scripture, we should be able to explain what it is and why it is wrong.

“Simply attaching a label doesn't establish anything.”

Matías looked toward him.

“And if the teaching is biblical, the label may only protect us from having to reconsider our own assumptions.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Anna arrived with fresh coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen seem to be having a serious discussion.”

“We're talking about why people resist change.”

Anna smiled.

“That's easy.”

Adrián looked at her.

“Is it?”

“People like things the way they like them.”

Marcos laughed.

“There's probably something to that.”

Anna continued.

“Especially when they're the ones making the rules.”

Adrián looked toward Elías.

“I think she's been listening.”

“I don't need to listen,” Anna said. “I've worked with people for years.”

She moved on to another table.

Matías smiled.

“She's not entirely wrong.”

Elías returned to Galatians.

“There's a passage near the end of the letter that I think belongs here.”

He found the sixth chapter.

“Paul speaks about people who wanted to make a good impression in the flesh.

“They were promoting circumcision, and Paul says they wanted to avoid persecution for the cross of Christ.

“He also says they wanted to boast in the flesh of those who followed them.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“That's revealing.”

“It is,” Elías said.

“Because it shows how religious activity can become a means of preserving human reputation.

“The issue wasn't simply whether people were observing a particular practice.

“It involved what the teachers wanted to accomplish through that observance.”

Matías considered the passage.

“They wanted results they could claim.”

“Yes.”

“And perhaps protection from the offense of the cross.”

Elías nodded.

“Paul says that explicitly.

“Then he declares that he will boast in nothing except the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ.”

Adrián looked at the verse.

“So the cross threatens the very thing religious flesh wants to preserve.”

“What?” Marcos asked.

“Its importance.”

Adrián continued.

“If the Christian life depends upon the system, the people who manage the system become very important.

“If spirituality depends upon keeping the rules, the people who establish and enforce the rules become very important.

“If spiritual growth depends upon following the correct program, the people who designed the program become very important.

“Then someone comes along and says that God has placed believers in Christ, that their old man was crucified with Him, and that Christ Himself is their life.

“Suddenly the system isn't the source of spiritual life anymore.

“And neither are the people running it.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“That can be difficult for a man who has spent years believing he's indispensable.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Speaking from experience?”

“Unfortunately.”

Marcos had been thinking about the church meeting he'd attended several weeks earlier.

“I think the same thing can happen when churches rely upon methods for reaching people.

“Leaders may sincerely want to share the gospel.

“Yet if they become convinced that the gospel needs their methods to become effective, the methods begin occupying the center of attention.

“Then the church's success becomes tied to the abilities of the people who organize the events, develop the programs, and attract the crowds.”

Elías nodded.

“And the danger is that the church may begin trusting its methods more than the message.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“It's another form of the same problem.

“Human ability becomes the source of confidence.”

Matías considered that.

“Then carnality management isn't limited to rules about behavior.

“It includes any arrangement through which we attempt to produce spiritual life by human means.”

“Exactly,” Marcos said.

“Whether we're trying to make believers more disciplined or trying to make the gospel more attractive, the question is where our confidence rests.”

The conversation slowed.

For a while they sat quietly, each considering the implications.

Then Matías spoke.

“I think there's something we've been reluctant to admit.”

The others looked toward him.

“We've been discussing why churches continue managing carnality.

“And we've identified several reasons.

“Fear.

“Control.

“Tradition.

“Concern for reputation.

“Confidence in human ability.

“Yet I think the most difficult reason is much closer to home.”

He paused.

“We like having something to accomplish.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“That's certainly true of you.”

Matías laughed.

“Yes. Especially me.

“I like being responsible.

“I like knowing what's expected.

“I like seeing progress.

“And I like the satisfaction of accomplishing something difficult.

“Those qualities have served me well in ordinary life.

“Yet I've brought the same confidence into my relationship with God.

“I wanted to become a successful Christian.

“And I wanted to know that I had done what was necessary to become one.”

He looked down at his Bible.

“The cross removes that confidence.

“It tells me that God has already dealt with my old man through Christ.

“It tells me that my life is Christ.

“And it leaves me with nothing to boast about except Him.”

Elías listened carefully.

Matías continued.

“I think carnality management survives because it gives the flesh something to do.

“Something to measure.

“Something to accomplish.

“Something to be proud of.

“And the flesh is very reluctant to surrender its importance.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Then perhaps that's why grace sounds dangerous.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“Because it removes the system?”

“Because it removes the basis for confidence in ourselves.”

Adrián continued.

“If I believe my spirituality depends upon my discipline, then discipline is precious to me.

“If I believe it depends upon my knowledge, then my knowledge becomes precious.

“If I believe it depends upon my ministry, then my ministry becomes precious.

“And if someone tells me that none of those things can produce the life of Christ, I may feel as though he's taking something away from me.”

Elías looked thoughtful.

“Even though he's directing you toward what God has already given you.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“And perhaps that's the strangest part.

“We can become so attached to what we're trying to accomplish that we resist hearing about what God has accomplished.”

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Another opportunity to demonstrate your generosity?”

Adrián looked at him.

“I'm beginning to suspect you only come here because I pay.”

Matías laughed.

“It's certainly one of the benefits.”

Anna placed the bill on the table.

“Would you like me to divide it?”

Adrián considered the question.

“No. I'll take it.”

She smiled.

“Very generous.”

He looked toward his friends.

“You see? Someone appreciates me.”

Elías laughed.

“Careful. You may begin boasting.”

“Too late.”

Outside, the afternoon was cool.

They stood beside their cars for a few minutes, reluctant to end the conversation.

Marcos looked toward Matías.

“I think we've answered the question.”

Matías considered that.

“Perhaps we've begun to answer it.”

Elías nodded.

“I think the answer is more personal than we expected.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“It usually is.”

They said goodbye.

As Matías drove home, he thought about the years he'd spent trying to become the Christian he believed he ought to be.

He had established routines.

He had developed disciplines.

He had worked hard to become more obedient.

And he had encouraged others to do the same.

He had never intended to replace Christ.

Yet he was beginning to recognize how easily his own efforts had become the source of his confidence.

He thought about the churches they had examined.

Corinth had found reasons to boast in spiritual distinctions.

Galatia had been drawn toward religious achievement.

Laodicea had become confident in its own sufficiency.

Ephesus had received genuine commendations from Christ, yet had fallen from its first love.

Different churches.

Different circumstances.

Different expressions of the flesh.

And throughout their investigation, the same question had continued returning.

What was the source of their confidence?

Matías thought about the cross.

God had acted through His Son.

The old man had been crucified with Christ.

The believer had been placed in Him.

And Christ Himself was his life.

Those things were not accomplishments Matías could claim.

They were the work of God.

He began to understand why churches might continue relying upon systems of carnality management even while professing faith in the cross.

Systems gave people something they could control.

They offered measurable results.

They made human ability important.

And they allowed the flesh to remain occupied with its own improvement.

The cross offered no such arrangement.

It declared what God had accomplished.

And grace directed the believer's confidence away from himself and toward Christ.

As Matías turned onto his street, he thought about something Adrián had said.

*We can become so attached to what we're trying to accomplish that we resist hearing about what God has accomplished.*

He had spent years doing exactly that.

And now he was beginning to see why grace had made such a difference.

Not because grace had given him a better method for becoming spiritual.

Because grace had brought him to the One who was already his life.

# Chapter 45
## Grace Has Made the Difference

Adrián had received a message from the man he'd been meeting with.

It was short.

*I've been reading Romans 6 again. I think I'm beginning to understand something. For the first time, I'm reading about what Christ has done instead of looking for another way to fix myself.*

Adrián read it twice.

He was glad his friend had continued studying the passage. He was also relieved that the man hadn't asked for another program.

Their conversations had changed considerably.

For years, whenever his friend spoke about his struggles, Adrián had tried to offer a solution. He recommended books, suggested accountability arrangements, and encouraged greater discipline. He had prayed with him and assured him that God would help him overcome his difficulties.

He had meant every word.

Yet their conversations had almost always ended with a new commitment from his friend.

This time, the man had gone home with something else to consider.

What God had accomplished in Christ.

Adrián wondered why that distinction had taken him so many years to appreciate.

At Deb's Place, Anna was cleaning the counter when Adrián arrived.

“Good morning.”

She glanced at him.

“You're smiling.”

“Am I?”

“More than usual.”

“I received some good news.”

“That's nice.”

He took his coffee.

“Would you like to hear it?”

“Is it going to take twenty minutes?”

“Possibly.”

“Then tell me after breakfast.”

Adrián laughed.

“Fair enough.”

He joined Marcos at their usual table.

Matías and Elías arrived shortly afterward.

They ordered breakfast, talked about their families, and exchanged news from the week.

Eventually Adrián told them about the message he'd received.

Marcos listened with interest.

“That's encouraging.”

“It is,” Adrián said. “Especially because he's looking at what Christ has done.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“That's becoming a recurring discovery.”

“Yes. And I'm beginning to appreciate it.”

Elías opened his Bible.

“I think that brings us naturally to something we've been discussing for weeks.”

“Grace?” Marcos asked.

“Yes.”

Elías looked around the table.

“We've spoken about the cross, the believer's death with Christ, freedom from sin's dominion, and walking by the Spirit.

“Yet we haven't spent much time considering how Paul speaks about grace in connection with the Christian life.”

Matías nodded.

“We usually speak about grace when discussing forgiveness.”

“And rightly so,” Elías said. “Forgiveness is a wonderful expression of God's grace.

“Yet Scripture speaks of grace in ways that reach beyond forgiveness.”

Marcos turned to Titus 2.

“There's a passage here that I've been thinking about.”

He found the eleventh verse.

“For the grace of God has appeared, bringing salvation to all men,

“instructing us to the intent that, denying ungodliness and worldly lusts, we would live soberly, righteously, and godly in this present age;”

He looked up.

“That's a very different description from the way I've often heard grace discussed.

“We sometimes speak about grace as though it were God's response when the Christian life goes wrong.

“We fail.

“God forgives us.

“We begin again.

“And grace becomes the provision that allows us to recover from our mistakes.”

Adrián looked at the passage.

“A safety net.”

“Exactly,” Marcos said.

“And we need that assurance of forgiveness. Yet Paul describes grace as doing something more.

“It instructs us.

“It concerns how we live now.”

Matías considered the passage.

“I've always found that interesting. Grace seems to be presented almost as a teacher.”

Elías nodded.

“And notice what it teaches.

“It doesn't teach believers to become careless about sin.

“It instructs them to reject ungodliness and worldly desires.”

Adrián leaned back.

“That's almost the opposite of what people fear.”

“What do you mean?” Marcos asked.

“We've all heard people say that too much grace will encourage sin.

“Yet Paul says grace teaches us to deny ungodliness.

“Perhaps we've been so concerned about what people might do with grace that we haven't paid enough attention to what Scripture says grace does.”

Matías had been following the conversation carefully.

“There's something we need to distinguish,” he said.

“We've spent several chapters establishing that Christ Himself is our life.

“I don't want us to begin speaking about grace as though it were a separate power that produces spiritual life independently of Him.”

Elías smiled.

“That's important.”

Matías continued.

“When I hear that grace teaches us, I don't want to imagine another spiritual program.

“As though God has replaced the law with a new set of instructions called grace, and now I must produce spiritual life by keeping them successfully.

“That would bring us back to the same problem.

“I would still be trying to produce the Christian life.

“I would simply be using different language.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then how should we understand it?”

Matías considered his words.

“I think we need to remember what we've already discovered.

“God placed us in Christ.

“Christ is our life.

“The Spirit produces His fruit.

“And the cross has already dealt with our old man.

“Grace cannot be separated from those realities.

“It is God's gracious action toward us and His work in us through Christ.

“It doesn't establish another independent source of life.”

Elías nodded.

“Paul makes that connection throughout his letters.

“In Ephesians, he says believers have been saved by grace through faith, and that this is God's gift.

“Then he says we are God's workmanship, created in Christ Jesus for good works that God prepared beforehand.

“The works follow God's gracious action.

“They are not the means by which we create the life that makes them possible.”

Adrián smiled.

“So grace doesn't give us another way to become somebody.”

Matías laughed.

“No. It directs our confidence toward what God has done.”

Anna arrived with breakfast.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Wonderful.”

She smiled.

“You say that every week.”

“Because it's true every week.”

“Then perhaps you should write a review.”

“I've been considering it.”

“Keep it short.”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián looked toward the others.

“Apparently my reputation precedes me.”

Anna returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Elías opened to Romans 6.

“There's another passage that belongs here.”

He found verse fourteen.

“Paul tells believers that sin shall not have dominion over them, because they are not under law, but under grace.”

He looked around the table.

“That's an extraordinary statement.

“Paul connects freedom from sin's dominion with being under grace rather than under law.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“And that is exactly where our usual thinking becomes confused.

“We assume that if people are struggling with sin, they need more law.

“More rules.

“More pressure.

“More discipline.

“Yet Paul says sin shall not have dominion over believers because they are not under law, but under grace.”

Marcos considered that.

“Then grace is not simply permission to begin again after failure.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Paul is describing a different relationship to sin and to God.

“The believer has died with Christ.

“He is alive to God in Christ Jesus.

“And he is under grace.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Then perhaps that's why sin management has been so unsuccessful at producing spirituality.

“It keeps returning believers to the very framework Paul says cannot produce freedom.”

Elías nodded.

“The law can identify sin. It cannot give life.

“And the flesh cannot become spiritual through obedience to regulations.

“God has accomplished what the law could not do because of the flesh.”

Marcos had been thinking about the word *grace*.

“I wonder whether we sometimes make grace sound like God's willingness to overlook things.”

Matías looked toward him.

“Explain.”

“We speak about a gracious person as someone who is forgiving, patient, and willing to excuse mistakes.

“Those are admirable qualities.

“And when we speak about God's grace, we often emphasize His willingness to forgive us.

“Yet Paul's language seems much richer.

“Grace is connected with salvation.

“With our position in Christ.

“With freedom from sin's dominion.

“With God's work in us.

“And with the life we now possess in His Son.”

Elías nodded.

“Paul also speaks about grace in connection with his own ministry.”

He turned to 1 Corinthians 15.

“But by the grace of God I am what I am. His grace which was given to me was not futile, but I worked more than all of them; yet not I, but the grace of God which was with me.”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“That's the passage we discussed earlier.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And it deserves another look.

“Paul doesn't deny that he labored.

“He doesn't describe himself as inactive.

“Yet he refuses to identify himself as the independent source of what was accomplished.

“He attributes it to God's grace.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“That's very different from saying, ‘I worked hard, and God helped me succeed.’”

Elías considered the distinction.

“It can be. The words themselves aren't necessarily wrong. God truly helps His people.

“Yet Paul is emphasizing something deeper than assistance to independent human effort.

“He understands his life and ministry as dependent upon God's gracious work.”

Adrián had been thinking about the expression they discussed several weeks earlier.

“Living the Christian life with the help of the Holy Spirit.”

Matías smiled.

“I remember.”

“I think this passage helps me understand why that expression can be misleading.

“Paul doesn't say that he became an extraordinarily successful Christian because he worked harder than everyone else and received a little additional help from God.

“He says that by God's grace he is what he is.

“And when he speaks about his labor, he refuses to claim it as an independent accomplishment.”

Marcos considered the thought.

“Then grace doesn't merely make human effort more effective.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Grace brings us into what God has accomplished in Christ and sustains the life that comes from Him.”

Matías looked at his Bible.

“And that's why it cannot become another method.

“If I turn grace into a technique for improving myself, I've misunderstood it.

“Grace is not something I use to produce Christ.

“Christ is the life God has given me.”

The conversation moved to the fruit of the Spirit.

Matías returned to Galatians 5.

“I think this is where everything we've been discussing begins to come together.

“Paul calls them the works of the flesh and the fruit of the Spirit.

“We've already established that the flesh cannot produce the fruit of the Spirit.

“Yet I spent years trying to become more loving, patient, gentle, and self-controlled through discipline.

“And I asked the Holy Spirit to help me.

“I believed that was spiritual growth.”

He looked at his friends.

“Now I understand why I was so often frustrated.

“I was trying to produce something that belonged to another source.”

Marcos nodded.

“The fruit belongs to the Spirit.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“And that's why grace makes such a difference.

“God hasn't merely forgiven my failures and told me to try again.

“He has placed me in Christ.

“He has given me life in His Son.

“And His Spirit dwells in me.”

Elías looked at Galatians.

“That's the difference between trying to manufacture spiritual qualities and bearing fruit through the life God has given.

“The branch bears fruit.

“Yet the life producing the fruit comes from the vine.”

Adrián smiled.

“I'm beginning to think that illustration is going to stay with us.”

Matías laughed.

“I hope it does.”

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen have been very quiet.”

“We're discussing grace.”

Anna considered him.

“Is that why you're smiling?”

“Possibly.”

“Then it must be a good subject.”

“It is.”

She smiled and moved on.

Marcos watched her go.

“There's something refreshing about the way she asks questions.”

Adrián laughed.

“Especially when she isn't asking about my bill.”

Elías returned to Titus.

“I think we should examine one more thing.

“When Paul says grace instructs us to deny ungodliness and worldly desires, he's not describing a life without moral direction.

“Grace doesn't make obedience irrelevant.

“It changes the basis upon which the believer lives.”

Matías nodded.

“And that's important because people often assume that if we stop emphasizing rules and discipline, we're encouraging carelessness.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Yet the New Testament doesn't present grace as indifference to conduct.

“Paul gives believers many instructions.

“He tells them to love one another, serve one another, forgive one another, and walk by the Spirit.

“Those instructions are not attempts to create spiritual life through human effort.

“They are addressed to people who already possess life in Christ.”

Marcos considered the distinction.

“Then obedience is not the source of life.”

“No.”

“It's an expression consistent with the life God has given.”

“Yes.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“And if we reverse that order, we return to carnality management.”

Elías nodded.

“Precisely.”

Matías had been thinking about something personal.

“For years, I believed that grace was what I needed when I failed.

“I would become discouraged, confess my failures, and remind myself that God was gracious.

“Then I would begin again.

“And my confidence would return as I became more consistent.”

He paused.

“Now I realize that I was treating grace as the provision that allowed me to resume my own efforts.

“I wasn't resting my confidence in what God had accomplished.

“I was using forgiveness to begin another attempt at producing the Christian life.”

The others listened.

Matías continued.

“I don't regret learning Scripture.

“I don't regret praying.

“I don't regret serving people.

“Those things have been important in my life.

“What I regret is treating them as though they could make me spiritual.

“I was so occupied with what I was doing for God that I often failed to appreciate what God had done for me in Christ.”

Elías looked at him.

“And what He was doing in you.”

Matías nodded.

“Yes.”

He smiled faintly.

“Grace has made the difference.

“Not because it has given me a better way to improve myself.

“Because it has directed me away from myself and toward Christ.”

Marcos looked around the table.

“There's something remarkable about the direction our investigation has taken.

“We began with the problem of carnality.

“We examined its different expressions in the churches.

“We discovered how easily the flesh can become religious.

“Then we considered the cross.

“And now we're talking about grace.”

Adrián smiled.

“Which is where we probably should have begun.”

“Perhaps,” Marcos said.

“Yet I'm not sure we would have understood it the same way.”

Elías considered that.

“We might have treated grace as another subject to study.

“Now we've seen why it matters.

“The flesh cannot produce spiritual life.

“The cross has dealt with our old man.

“And God has given us life in Christ.

“Grace is not simply the answer to our failures.

“It's the character of God's work toward us in Christ.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“And it leaves no room for boasting in ourselves.”

For a while the conversation became quieter.

Adrián thought about his friend.

Matías thought about the years he'd spent measuring his spiritual progress.

Marcos considered how often he had confused successful activity with spiritual fruitfulness.

Elías remembered Daniel and the discomfort his questions about grace had caused.

Each man had spent years believing the gospel.

Yet each had also found ways to place confidence in something other than Christ.

And now they were beginning to understand why Paul spoke so confidently about grace.

Not as a concession to human weakness.

Not as permission for careless living.

Not as a new method for producing spirituality.

As God's gracious work in Christ, through whom believers had received life.

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Another demonstration of grace?”

Adrián laughed.

“Careful. You may begin expecting this every week.”

Matías looked at him.

“I thought you enjoyed being generous.”

“I do. I'm simply trying to avoid creating a system.”

Elías laughed.

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

“Whatever you call it, I appreciate it.”

They gathered their things and made their way toward the door.

Outside, the afternoon was cool and bright.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

Marcos looked toward Matías.

“I think we're approaching something important.”

Matías nodded.

“We are.”

“What do you think we should examine next?”

Matías opened his Bible to 1 Corinthians.

“Paul says that it is because of God that we are in Christ Jesus.

“We've spoken about being in Christ many times.

“Yet I wonder how many believers understand what that actually means.”

Adrián smiled.

“Then perhaps that's where we should go next.”

As Matías drove home, he thought about the conversation.

For years, he had understood grace primarily as God's forgiveness when he failed.

He had believed that grace made salvation possible.

And he had been deeply grateful for it.

Yet he had often approached the Christian life as though grace had brought him to the starting line and the rest depended upon his effort.

He needed to become disciplined.

He needed to become obedient.

He needed to develop spiritual character.

And when he failed, he needed grace so that he could begin again.

Now he was beginning to understand something different.

Grace had not merely forgiven his failures.

God had placed him in Christ.

His old man had been crucified with Him.

He had been made alive in Christ.

And the Spirit of God dwelt in him.

The Christian life was not something he needed to manufacture.

It was the life God had already given him in His Son.

Matías thought again of Paul's words.

*But by the grace of God I am what I am.*

For years, he had measured himself by what he had accomplished.

Now he was beginning to see that the most important things about him were not his accomplishments at all.

They were what God had done.

And that was why grace had made all the difference.

# Chapter 46
## In Christ

Matías had been a Christian for most of his adult life.

He knew the expression *in Christ*.

He had read it in Paul's letters, heard it in sermons, and used it in conversations with other believers. He had even taught passages in which it appeared.

Yet when he tried to explain precisely what it meant, he discovered that his understanding had been surprisingly vague.

He had generally thought of being in Christ as another way of describing someone who lived as a Christian.

A person who believed the gospel, attended church, read Scripture, and sought to obey God was living in Christ.

The more faithfully he lived, the more completely he seemed to be expressing what it meant to be in Him.

Their recent conversations had already challenged that understanding.

Being in Christ was not something the believer accomplished through Christian living.

It was something God had done.

And Matías wanted to understand that more carefully.

At Deb's Place, Adrián was examining a small sign near the entrance.

*Please wait to be seated.*

He looked toward Anna.

“Does this apply to regular customers?”

“Yes.”

“Even very regular customers?”

“Especially them.”

Adrián smiled.

“I was hoping we'd reached a higher level of relationship.”

Anna pointed toward their usual table.

“Go ahead.”

“Thank you.”

“Don't make me regret it.”

Marcos arrived just as Adrián sat down.

“What was that about?”

“Privileges.”

“Did you get any?”

“Apparently I'm allowed to sit down.”

Marcos laughed.

“An extraordinary achievement.”

Matías and Elías arrived shortly afterward.

They ordered breakfast, talked about their families, and exchanged news from the week.

Eventually Matías opened his Bible.

“There's something I've been thinking about since our last conversation.”

Adrián smiled.

“I suspected there might be.”

Matías turned to 1 Corinthians.

“Paul says something here that I think we've passed over too quickly.”

He found the first chapter.

“Listen to verse thirty: ‘Because of him, you are in Christ Jesus, who was made to us wisdom from God, and righteousness and sanctification, and redemption:’”

He looked at his friends.

“I've read that verse many times.

“Yet I don't think I've appreciated what Paul is actually saying.”

Elías leaned forward.

“What stands out to you?”

“Those words: *Because of him*.

“Paul doesn't tell the Corinthians that they are in Christ because they've become sufficiently disciplined or obedient.

“He doesn't say they're in Christ because they've developed spiritual character.

“He traces their being in Christ to God.”

Matías paused.

“And remember who he's addressing.

“The Corinthians.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“The very people Paul later calls fleshly.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“People who were divided, jealous, and impressed with human distinctions.

“Yet Paul says they are in Christ Jesus.

“That tells me something important about what it means to be in Christ.”

Elías opened to the same passage.

“Paul's language is significant.

“He's describing their position, not congratulating them on their conduct.

“And that distinction appears throughout his letters.

“Believers are described as being in Christ.

“They are not instructed to achieve that position through successful Christian living.”

Marcos considered the statement.

“Then being in Christ is not a description of how well someone is living.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's a description of where God has placed the believer in relation to His Son.”

Matías nodded.

“That's what I've been trying to understand.

“For years, I thought of being in Christ almost as though it were something I needed to maintain through obedience.

“When I was living faithfully, I felt close to Him.

“When I failed, I felt as though I'd moved away.

“And my confidence in my relationship with God rose and fell according to my conduct.”

He looked at the passage.

“Yet Paul tells the Corinthians that they are in Christ because of God.

“Their failures were serious. Paul certainly confronted them.

“Yet he didn't begin by telling them they needed to become sufficiently spiritual to enter Christ.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Because they were already in Him.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Excellent.”

She smiled.

“You're becoming very predictable.”

“Consistency is an admirable quality.”

“Especially when paying your bill.”

Marcos laughed.

Anna returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Marcos turned to Romans 8.

“There's another passage that helps me understand this.”

He found the ninth verse.

“Paul tells believers that they are not in the flesh, but in the Spirit, if God's Spirit dwells in them.”

He looked toward Elías.

“We've discussed this before, yet I think it belongs here.”

Elías nodded.

“Very much.

“Paul is describing the believer's position.

“He doesn't say that Christians move into the Spirit when they behave well and return to the flesh whenever they fail.

“He tells them that they are in the Spirit because the Spirit of God dwells in them.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Then a believer can act by the flesh without being in the flesh.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Corinth demonstrates that distinction.

“Paul addressed those believers as people in Christ, while confronting conduct that arose from the flesh.

“Their behavior was inconsistent with their position.

“It did not create or determine that position.”

Adrián leaned back.

“That's very different from the way people often speak.”

“What do you mean?” Marcos asked.

“I've heard Christians say that someone who is struggling with sin needs to get back into Christ.

“Or that a believer who has become careless is no longer in the Spirit.

“I understand what they're trying to communicate.

“Yet if Paul is speaking about position, those expressions can become confusing.”

Elías nodded.

“They can.

“We need to distinguish between the believer's position in Christ and his experience of living by faith in Him.

“Those are related realities.

“They are not identical.”

Matías had been considering the implications.

“Then being in Christ is not something I accomplish.

“It's not something I produce through obedience.

“And it's not something I maintain through discipline.”

Elías nodded.

“God is the One who placed you in Christ.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“That changes everything.

“Because if my position depends upon my conduct, then my confidence must continually return to myself.

“I have to examine whether I've done enough.

“Whether I've been obedient enough.

“Whether I've maintained the right habits.

“And when I fail, I begin questioning whether I'm still where I ought to be.”

He paused.

“Yet if God placed me in Christ, then my position rests upon His action.”

Marcos considered that.

“And that gives faith something entirely different to rest upon.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“Not my success.

“His accomplishment.”

Elías turned to Ephesians.

“There's a passage here that develops this further.”

He found the first chapter.

“Paul says that God chose us in Christ before the foundation of the world.

“He speaks about redemption and forgiveness in Him.

“And throughout the chapter, he repeatedly directs attention to what God has accomplished in Christ.”

He looked around the table.

“Notice the emphasis.

“God is acting.

“He chose.

“He blessed.

“He redeemed.

“He made known His purpose.

“And believers receive those blessings in Christ.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then our position in Christ is not the result of our religious achievements.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's the result of God's gracious action.”

Matías considered the words.

“And that explains why Paul can speak so confidently about what believers possess.

“He isn't describing things they must earn.

“He's describing what God has given them in His Son.”

Adrián had been listening carefully.

“There's something I want to understand.

“If God placed us in Christ, does that mean everything about our Christian life is already complete?”

Elías considered the question.

“We need to distinguish between what God has accomplished and what believers experience as they live.

“Our position in Christ is established by God.

“We have been united with Christ in His death and resurrection.

“We possess eternal life in Him.

“Yet Scripture also speaks about believers growing in understanding, walking by the Spirit, and being transformed in their conduct.

“Those things concern the expression and experience of the life we already possess.

“They don't create our position in Christ.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then spiritual growth is not the process of becoming increasingly placed in Christ.”

“No,” Elías said.

“God has already placed the believer in Him.

“Growth involves coming to know and live in keeping with what is already true.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“That's very different from what I believed.”

The conversation moved toward the believer's life.

Matías turned to Colossians 3.

“There's another passage that belongs here.”

He found the third and fourth verses.

“Paul says that believers died and that their life is hidden with Christ in God.

“Then he says something remarkable.

“When Christ, our life, is revealed, then you will also be revealed with him in glory.”

He looked at his friends.

“We've discussed that expression before.

“Yet I think it becomes even more significant when we understand what it means to be in Christ.

“God hasn't merely placed us in a new religious position and left us to produce a Christian life.

“He has given us life in His Son.

“And that life is Christ Himself.”

Elías nodded.

“That's why the New Testament speaks about eternal life as something found in the Son.

“John tells us that God has given us eternal life and that this life is in His Son.

“He who has the Son has the life.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then life is not something we manufacture through Christian activity.”

“No,” Matías said.

“Life is found in Christ.

“And God has placed us in Him.”

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen seem very thoughtful today.”

“We're discussing where we belong.”

Anna smiled.

“That's easy. You belong at this table.”

Adrián laughed.

“I knew it.”

“Provided you keep paying.”

Marcos shook his head, smiling.

“There's always a condition.”

Anna looked at him.

“Only here.”

She returned to the counter.

Elías had been thinking about something Matías said earlier.

“I think we need to address one more misunderstanding.

“Being in Christ doesn't mean that the believer's personality disappears.

“Nor does it mean he becomes passive or ceases to make decisions.

“Paul speaks about himself living by faith in the Son of God.

“He describes real activity, real responsibility, and real relationships.

“Yet he identifies Christ as the source of his life.”

Matías nodded.

“That's important.

“Because when we say Christ is our life, some people imagine that we're denying the believer's participation in living.

“Yet the distinction isn't between doing things and doing nothing.

“It's between attempting to produce spiritual life independently and living by faith in the One who is our life.”

Marcos considered that.

“Then being in Christ establishes the reality.

“And walking in Him concerns living in keeping with that reality.”

Elías smiled.

“That's well said.”

Matías nodded.

“That's what makes the distinction so important.

“If we misunderstand being in Christ, we may spend our entire Christian lives trying to achieve a position God has already given us.”

Elías looked at the passage in 1 Corinthians.

“And in doing so, we may never learn to rest our confidence in what God has done.”

The conversation grew quieter.

Marcos had been thinking about his own approach to Christian living.

He had always valued responsibility.

He believed people should be dependable, organized, and willing to fulfill their commitments.

Those qualities had served him well in business.

Yet he was beginning to understand how easily they could become the basis of his confidence before God.

He had often measured his Christian life by the things he accomplished.

The ministries he supported.

The people he helped.

The responsibilities he fulfilled.

He had been grateful for the opportunities God had given him.

Yet he had also taken satisfaction in being a useful Christian.

Now he was beginning to see that usefulness was not the source of his position in Christ.

God had placed him there.

And nothing he accomplished could become the basis of that reality.

Matías returned to 1 Corinthians 1:30.

“I think this verse deserves to be remembered.”

He read it again.

“Because of him, you are in Christ Jesus, who was made to us wisdom from God, and righteousness and sanctification, and redemption:”

He looked at his friends.

“Not because of my discipline.

“Not because of my obedience.

“Not because of my knowledge.

“Not because of my ministry.

“Because of God.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“That leaves very little room for boasting.”

Elías nodded.

“Which is precisely where Paul takes the Corinthians.”

He looked at the following verse.

“He who boasts, let him boast in the Lord.”

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Another act of generosity?”

Adrián looked at him.

“I'm beginning to suspect you gentlemen have developed an unhealthy dependence upon me.”

Matías laughed.

“Perhaps you're finally becoming somebody.”

Adrián shook his head.

“After everything we've discussed, I think I'll decline the promotion.”

They laughed.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

Matías looked toward Elías.

“I think I'd like to continue this next week.”

“Of course.”

“There's something else I want to understand.”

“What?”

“If God has placed us in Christ, and Christ Himself is our life, what does it actually mean to walk in Him?”

Elías smiled.

“That's where Paul takes us in Colossians.”

Marcos looked interested.

“Then perhaps we should begin there.”

As Matías drove home, he thought about the words that had occupied him all week.

*In Christ.*

For years, he had understood the expression primarily in terms of Christian conduct.

He believed that being in Christ meant living as a faithful Christian.

And because he wanted to be faithful, he had worked hard to become one.

Now he was beginning to understand that Paul used the expression to describe something God had accomplished.

God had placed him in Christ.

His position did not depend upon his ability to produce spiritual life.

It rested upon God's gracious action.

And the life he possessed in Christ was not something he needed to manufacture.

Christ Himself was his life.

Matías thought again of the passage in Colossians.

*Christ, our life*

He had spent years trying to live the Christian life.

Now he was beginning to understand that the Christian life was not something he could produce.

It was a Person.

And God had placed him in Him.

# Chapter 47
## Walking in Him

Marcos had been thinking about something Matías said the previous Thursday.

*God placed us in Christ.*

He had understood that statement. At least, he believed he had.

Yet the more he considered it, the more he realized how thoroughly his thinking about the Christian life had been shaped by activity.

He knew what it meant to work for something.

He knew how to establish goals, organize people, and measure progress.

He knew how to recognize a successful undertaking.

And for years, he had approached his Christian life with much the same understanding.

A faithful Christian attended church, studied Scripture, prayed, served others, and sought to obey God.

Those were good things.

Yet Marcos was beginning to see that none of them could explain what it meant to be in Christ.

God had placed him there.

And Christ Himself was his life.

That left another question.

What did it mean to walk in Him?

At Deb's Place, Adrián was examining a new sign near the counter.

*Please do not move the tables.*

He looked toward Anna.

“Is this directed at me?”

She glanced over.

“Have you been moving tables?”

“Not recently.”

“Then you have nothing to worry about.”

“I just wondered whether the sign was personal.”

Anna smiled.

“Not everything is about you, Adrián.”

He considered that.

“I'm finding that increasingly difficult to accept.”

Marcos laughed as he approached.

“Good morning.”

Anna smiled.

“Morning, Marcos. Your friend is examining our policies.”

“Has he found anything objectionable?”

“Not yet.”

“Give him time.”

Adrián shook his head.

“It's remarkable how little support I receive here.”

They took their usual seats.

Matías and Elías arrived shortly afterward.

They ordered breakfast, talked about their families, and exchanged news from the week.

Eventually Marcos opened his Bible.

“I've been thinking about our last conversation.”

Adrián smiled.

“Then we're in familiar territory.”

Marcos turned to Colossians.

“Last week we established that God placed us in Christ.

“That's our position.

“It isn't something we accomplish through Christian conduct.

“Yet Paul also tells believers to walk in Christ.

“And I've been wondering how those two things belong together.”

Elías opened to the second chapter.

“There's a passage that addresses exactly that.”

He found Colossians 2:6.

“Paul says, ‘As therefore you received Christ Jesus, the Lord, walk in him,’”

He looked around the table.

“Notice the order.

“They received Christ Jesus.

“Now Paul tells them to walk in Him.”

Matías considered the passage.

“That sounds simple.”

“It does,” Elías said.

“Yet we've spent years making it considerably more complicated.”

Marcos smiled faintly.

“Perhaps because we assume walking in Christ means performing the activities expected of Christians.”

Elías nodded.

“That's a common understanding.

“We speak about walking with Christ or living for Christ, and immediately begin thinking about what we should do.

“Prayer.

“Bible reading.

“Church attendance.

“Serving others.

“Obedience.

“Those activities may have a proper place in the believer's life.

“Yet Paul doesn't say, ‘As you received Christ, now begin producing the Christian life through your own effort.’

“He says to walk in Him.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“And he connects that walk with how they received Christ.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“That's important.”

Marcos leaned forward.

“How did they receive Him?”

“By faith,” Elías said.

“And that's consistent with Paul's argument throughout his letters.

“Believers receive Christ through faith, not through their own religious achievements.

“And now Paul tells them to continue in Him.”

Matías considered that.

“Then walking in Christ cannot mean abandoning faith and beginning a life of independent effort.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Paul is directing believers to continue in the One they have received.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“That's where I've misunderstood the Christian life.

“I believed salvation was by faith.

“Yet afterward, I thought producing the Christian life depended on my ability.

“I needed to become disciplined.

“I needed to develop spiritual character.

“I needed to make good decisions and accomplish worthwhile things.

“And I asked God to help me.”

He paused.

“Now I'm beginning to understand that the Christian life continues on the same basis upon which it began.

“Faith in Christ.”

Anna arrived with breakfast.

Adrián examined his plate.

“Wonderful.”

She smiled.

“I'm glad you're pleased.”

“I'm becoming very easy to satisfy.”

“That's a welcome development.”

Marcos laughed.

Anna returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Matías returned to Colossians.

“There's something about the word *walk* that I think we should examine.

“When I hear that word, I naturally think about activity.

“Walking is something a person does.

“So when Paul tells believers to walk in Christ, I immediately begin thinking about what I need to do.”

Elías nodded.

“And that's where we need to distinguish the action from its source.

“Paul isn't denying that believers live, act, choose, and serve.

“He's describing the sphere and basis of their life.

“They have received Christ.

“They are in Him.

“And now they are to walk in Him.”

Matías considered the explanation.

“Then the question isn't simply what I'm doing.

“It's whether I'm living by faith in the One who is my life.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“That's the distinction.”

Adrián had been listening quietly.

“There's something I've been thinking about.

“We've said repeatedly that Christ is our life.

“Yet I wonder whether we really understand what that means.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“Explain.”

Adrián considered his words.

“Suppose I wake up tomorrow morning and decide that I'm going to live a very good Christian life.

“I'll pray.

“I'll read Scripture.

“I'll be patient with my family.

“I'll treat my employees well.

“I'll avoid becoming irritated with difficult people.”

Anna passed behind him.

“That last one could be challenging.”

Adrián laughed.

“Thank you, Anna.”

She smiled and continued toward another table.

Adrián returned to his thought.

“Now suppose I accomplish all of those things.

“I've had a very successful day according to my expectations.

“Yet what have I actually established?

“That I prayed.

“That I read Scripture.

“That I behaved patiently.

“That I treated people well.

“Those may all be good things.

“Yet none of them proves that I was living by Christ as my life.”

Matías nodded.

“Because the flesh can imitate much of that behavior.”

“Exactly,” Adrián said.

“And that's what troubles me.

“I can perform the activities of a Christian without necessarily depending upon Christ.”

Elías opened to Galatians 2.

“Paul gives us an important distinction here.”

He found verse twenty.

“I have been crucified with Christ, and it is no longer I that live, but Christ lives in me. That life which I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me, and gave himself up for me.”

He looked at his friends.

“We've returned to this verse many times.

“And I think we need to keep returning to it.

“Paul isn't saying that he has ceased to exist as a person.

“He continues by speaking about the life he now lives in the body.

“Yet he identifies Christ as the One living in him.

“And he says that he lives by faith in the Son of God.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Then Paul is describing real human activity that proceeds from dependence upon Christ.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“He isn't presenting himself as an independent source of spiritual life.

“Nor is he describing a man who has become passive.

“He lives.

“He acts.

“He serves.

“Yet his life is grounded in Christ.”

Matías looked at the verse.

“Then walking in Him is not the absence of activity.

“It's the absence of confidence in ourselves as the source of spiritual life.”

Elías smiled.

“That's well said.”

Marcos had been thinking about something else.

“I wonder whether this explains why Christians can become so exhausted.

“We approach the Christian life as an endless series of responsibilities.

“We need to pray more.

“Study more.

“Serve more.

“Become more disciplined.

“Improve our relationships.

“Overcome our weaknesses.

“And because there's always something else to accomplish, we rarely feel that we've done enough.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“I recognize that life.”

Marcos continued.

“Then someone tells us that Christ is our life.

“And we immediately turn that into another responsibility.

“We need to learn how to let Christ live through us.

“We need to become better at depending upon Him.

“We need to develop a more effective method of walking by faith.”

He looked around the table.

“And before long, we've turned dependence upon Christ into another project.”

Adrián laughed.

“That sounds painfully familiar.”

Elías nodded.

“It's an important danger.

“Faith is not a technique for producing spiritual results.

“It's confidence in the One who has already given us life.”

Matías looked down at Colossians.

“Then perhaps we've been expecting the wrong kind of answer.”

Adrián smiled.

“We've become very good at that.”

Matías continued.

“We keep asking how to live the Christian life.

“And we expect a method we can master.

“Yet Paul directs us to Christ Himself.

“As we received Him, we're to walk in Him.

“The Christian life isn't something we manufacture through a collection of activities.

“It's the life of Christ expressed in us as we live by faith.”

Elías nodded.

“And that's why knowing what God has accomplished matters.

“If believers don't understand their position in Christ, they'll continually attempt to achieve it.

“If they don't understand that Christ is their life, they'll attempt to produce that life.

“And if they don't understand the Spirit's work, they'll treat Him as someone who assists their independent efforts.”

Marcos considered the statement.

“Then walking in Christ begins with knowing the truth.”

“With knowing and believing what God has revealed,” Elías said.

“Not because knowledge itself is life.

“Christ is life.

“Yet faith cannot count upon what it doesn't know.”

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen seem unusually thoughtful.”

“We're discussing walking,” Adrián said.

Anna looked at him.

“Walking?”

“Yes.”

“Then perhaps you should try it sometime.”

Marcos laughed.

Adrián smiled.

“I walk regularly.”

“From your car to this table?”

“It's a beginning.”

She shook her head and moved on.

Elías turned to John 15.

“There's another passage that belongs here.”

He found the fifth verse.

“Jesus says, ‘I am the vine. You are the branches. He who remains in me, and I in him, the same bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.’”

He looked around the table.

“That's a remarkable illustration of the relationship between life and fruit.

“The branch doesn't produce the life of the vine.

“It receives its life from the vine.

“And fruit appears through that life.”

Matías considered the passage.

“Then the branch doesn't need to manufacture fruit.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Nor does it become the source of the life that produces the fruit.

“The fruit appears on the branch.

“Yet its source is the vine.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“And that explains why Christian activity cannot become the measure of spiritual life.

“Two people may perform the same outward action.

“One may be acting from self-reliance.

“The other may be expressing the life of Christ.

“The action alone doesn't establish the source.”

Elías nodded.

“That's why we must be careful about judging spirituality by appearances.”

Adrián leaned back.

“There's something else about the vine illustration.

“Jesus doesn't tell the branch to become a vine.”

Matías smiled.

“No.”

“He doesn't tell it to develop an independent supply of life.

“He tells it to remain in Him.”

Elías considered the passage.

“And we should understand that remaining in Him is not the same as achieving our position in Christ.

“God has already placed the believer in His Son.”

Elías looked further down the passage.

“If you keep my commandments, you will remain in my love; even as I have kept my Father’s commandments, and remain in his love.”

Matías looked up.

“Then we can't leave obedience out of our explanation.”

“No,” Elías said. “And He tells them to love one another as He has loved them.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Then remaining in Christ isn't another way of making ourselves acceptable to God.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's living in dependence upon the One who is already our life.”

Matías had kept his finger on the page.

Marcos had been considering how this affected ordinary decisions.

“There's something I want to understand.

“If Christ is our life, what does that mean when we're making decisions?

“Suppose I need to decide whether to expand my business.

“Or how to respond to a difficult employee.

“Or whether to accept a particular responsibility.

“I still need to think carefully.

“I still need to make a decision.

“And I may still make mistakes.”

Elías nodded.

“Of course.”

“Then how does walking with Christ relate to those ordinary responsibilities?”

Elías considered the question.

“I don't think Scripture presents dependence upon Christ as the abandonment of judgment or responsibility.

“Paul made decisions.

“He planned journeys.

“He reasoned with people.

“He worked.

“And he sometimes changed his plans.

“Yet he understood his life and ministry as dependent upon God.

“The distinction isn't between making decisions and refusing to make them.

“It's between treating ourselves as independent sources of life and living by faith in Christ.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then walking in Him doesn't remove ordinary responsibilities.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It changes the basis of our confidence as we carry them out.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“I think that may be one of the most difficult things for me to learn.

“I've spent years believing that the Christian life depended upon making the right decisions.

“If I chose correctly, I was living faithfully.

“If I chose poorly, I had failed.

“And I measured my spiritual condition by the outcome.”

He looked at his friends.

“Now I understand that my life is not found in the decisions themselves.

“My life is Christ.

“I still make decisions.

“I still have responsibilities.

“I still need wisdom.

“Yet none of those things creates the life I possess in Him.”

Adrián nodded.

“Then what we do isn't the source of the Christian life.”

“No,” Matías said.

“It's found in whom God has given us.”

The conversation grew quieter.

Elías looked again at Colossians 2.

“There's something in the following verses that I think we should notice.”

He read the next verse.

“rooted and built up in him, and established in the faith, even as you were taught, abounding in it in thanksgiving.”

“Paul doesn't describe believers as people who must create their own spiritual foundation.

“They are rooted in Christ.

“And their growth proceeds from that relationship.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Then the Christian life is not a movement away from Christ toward increasing independence.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's a deepening understanding and experience of the life already given in Him.”

Matías looked at the words.

“And thanksgiving becomes natural when we recognize that everything rests upon what God has done.”

Elías smiled.

“Yes.”

For a while they sat quietly.

Marcos thought about the years he'd spent organizing ministries and measuring their success.

He had been grateful for the opportunities God had given him.

Yet he had also taken satisfaction in the things he could accomplish.

He was beginning to understand that successful activity could never become the source of spiritual life.

Christ was his life.

And walking in Him meant living by faith in that reality.

Not becoming passive.

Not abandoning responsibility.

Not refusing to act.

Living in dependence upon the One who was already his life.

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Another opportunity to demonstrate your generosity?”

Adrián laughed.

“I'm beginning to think you've developed a very comfortable understanding of grace.”

Matías smiled.

“Perhaps you're finally learning to give without expecting recognition.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Let's not become unreasonable.”

They laughed.

Anna placed the bill in front of Adrián.

“Whatever you're learning, I hope it continues.”

They stood beside their cars for a few minutes.

Marcos looked toward Elías.

“I think we've answered something important today.”

“What?”

“Being in Christ is God's accomplishment.

“Walking in Him is living by faith in the One who is already our life.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“And the fruit belongs to the Spirit.”

Adrián smiled.

“Which means we can't take credit for producing it.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Yet it can genuinely be expressed through us.”

Marcos considered that.

“Then perhaps that's what we should examine next.”

Adrián looked toward him.

“What?”

“What it means for Christ's life to be expressed through believers.

“Not simply performing Christian works.

“Being witnesses of His life.”

Elías smiled.

“That's a good place to go.”

As Marcos drove home, he thought about the passage in Colossians.

*As therefore you received Christ Jesus, the Lord, walk in him,*

For years, he had believed that salvation began by faith and the Christian life continued through disciplined effort.

He had worked hard to become useful.

He had organized ministries, supported good causes, and helped people.

And many of those activities had genuinely benefited others.

Yet none of them could become the source of his life in Christ.

God had placed him in His Son.

Christ Himself was his life.

And the Spirit was the One who produced spiritual fruit.

Marcos was beginning to understand that walking in Christ was not another project he had to make succeed.

It was living by faith in the One who had already become his life.

The distinction was simple.

Yet it changed everything.

Because the Christian life was not something Marcos needed to create.

It was something God had already given him in Christ.

And now he was learning to count upon it.

# Chapter 48
## Witnesses of His Life

Elías had been thinking about a conversation with his wife.

They had gone for a walk after dinner, something they had begun doing more frequently. The evening was cool, and they had taken the longer route through a neighborhood of older homes and large trees.

For a while they talked about their children, an upcoming family gathering, and a friend who had recently been hospitalized.

Then his wife asked him something unexpected.

“Do you think you've changed?”

Elías looked at her.

“In what way?”

“Since you've been meeting with the men.”

He considered the question.

“I've changed my understanding of several things.”

“I know that.”

She smiled.

“You've been telling me about them for weeks.”

He laughed.

“Then what do you mean?”

She thought for a moment.

“You seem less concerned about having the answer to everything.”

Elías was quiet.

She continued.

“You listen longer. You don't always feel the need to explain something immediately. And when we disagree, you seem more interested in understanding what I'm saying.”

He looked at her.

“Was I really that difficult?”

She smiled.

“Would you like the long answer?”

He laughed.

They continued walking.

Later that evening, Elías found himself thinking about what she had said.

He hadn't established a program for becoming a better listener.

He hadn't made a list of improvements he wanted to accomplish.

He had simply been discovering things about Christ that were changing the basis of his confidence.

He wondered whether his wife had noticed something he hadn't been trying to produce.

At Deb's Place, Adrián was standing beside the counter, examining a small display of homemade desserts.

Anna watched him.

“Breakfast first.”

“I was only looking.”

“You've been looking for five minutes.”

“I'm appreciating the craftsmanship.”

“It's cheesecake.”

“Excellent craftsmanship.”

Marcos arrived and laughed.

“Planning dessert already?”

“A wise man prepares for the future.”

Anna shook her head.

“Your future can wait until after breakfast.”

Adrián followed Marcos to their usual table.

Matías and Elías arrived shortly afterward.

They ordered breakfast and spent some time talking about their families.

Eventually Elías told them about his conversation with his wife.

Adrián smiled.

“She noticed you've become easier to live with?”

“Apparently.”

“That's encouraging.”

Matías laughed.

Elías continued.

“What interested me was that I hadn't been keeping score of the changes she described.

“I hadn't made a program out of becoming more patient or attentive.

“I've simply been reconsidering things I believed about myself and my relationship with Christ.

“And she noticed a difference.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Perhaps that's worth examining.”

Elías opened his Bible.

“Last week we discussed what it means to walk in Christ.

“We established that the Christian life isn't something we produce through independent effort.

“God placed us in Christ.

“Christ Himself is our life.

“And we walk by faith in Him.

“Yet that raises another question.

“What does that life look like when it's expressed through believers?”

Matías considered the question.

“That's where we've often returned to lists of Christian activities.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“We begin talking about witnessing, serving, helping others, and doing good works.

“And those things can be genuine expressions of Christ's life.

“Yet the activities themselves cannot establish their source.”

Marcos nodded.

“We've seen that throughout our investigation.”

Elías continued.

“I think we need to distinguish between doing things associated with Christianity and being witnesses of the life of Christ.”

Adrián leaned forward.

“Explain.”

Elías turned to Acts.

“Jesus told His disciples, ‘But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit has come upon you. You will be witnesses to me in Jerusalem, in all Judea and Samaria, and to the uttermost parts of the earth.’

“Notice the emphasis.

“He didn't merely give them a list of religious activities to perform.

“He spoke about the Spirit's power and about their being witnesses of Him.”

Matías looked at the passage.

“Then witnessing is not simply an activity we schedule.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Sharing the gospel is certainly part of bearing witness to Christ. The apostles proclaimed Him openly.

“Yet their witness was not meant to be an independent human performance.

“The Spirit empowered them to bear witness to the One they had come to know.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Then perhaps we've made witnessing another performance believers must produce through their own strength.”

Elías nodded.

“We often speak about it that way.

“We establish evangelism programs.

“We train people to give presentations.

“We set goals for how many conversations they should have.

“Some of those arrangements may help people communicate clearly.

“Yet the program itself cannot produce the life of Christ.

“And it cannot supply the Spirit's power.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“So even witnessing can become another way for the flesh to feel successful.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“If the believer's confidence rests in his ability to perform the activity.”

Anna arrived with their breakfasts.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Wonderful.”

She smiled.

“You've said that before.”

“And I intend to keep saying it.”

“Then perhaps you're finally learning gratitude.”

Matías laughed.

Adrián looked toward the others.

“Apparently my spiritual progress is becoming visible.”

Anna shook her head.

“Don't get carried away.”

She returned to the counter.

Marcos had been thinking about something Elías said.

“I think there's a distinction here that matters.

“A person can do something genuinely beneficial without that action necessarily being the fruit of the Spirit.

“We've discussed that before.

“Someone may give generously because he wants recognition.

“He may serve because he enjoys being important.

“He may teach because he takes pride in his knowledge.

“And he may share the gospel because he wants to be regarded as a successful evangelist.

“The activities may benefit other people.

“Yet the source of his confidence may still be himself.”

Matías nodded.

“That's what I've been discovering about my own life.

“I've done many things that I considered spiritual.

“And some of them were genuinely useful.

“Yet I often measured my spirituality by how faithfully I performed them.

“I wanted to be a good Christian.

“And I believed that doing the right things would eventually make me one.”

He paused.

“Now I understand that Christ is my life.

“And if His life is expressed through me, the source cannot be my independent effort.”

Elías looked at him.

“That's the distinction.

“The believer genuinely acts.

“He speaks.

“He serves.

“He loves.

“Yet the life being expressed is Christ's.”

Adrián leaned back.

“There's something I want to understand.

“If Christ is living in us, how do we know when something is genuinely an expression of His life?”

Elías considered the question.

“I don't think Scripture gives us a technique for examining every action and determining its source with absolute certainty.

“We can recognize conduct that contradicts Christ's character.

“We can examine our confidence and motives in light of Scripture.

“And we can learn to distinguish the works of the flesh from the fruit of the Spirit.

“Yet we should be cautious about making ourselves judges of every hidden motive, including our own.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then the answer isn't to become endlessly occupied with analyzing ourselves.”

“No,” Elías said.

“That could become another form of self-preoccupation.

“Our attention belongs on Christ.

“We know what God has accomplished.
