“We count upon what He has said.

“And we walk by the Spirit.”

Matías nodded.

“Then the fruit is not something we manufacture or constantly inspect to reassure ourselves that we're spiritual.”

“Exactly.”

Elías turned to John 15.

“Jesus gives us a picture that helps here.”

He found the familiar passage about the vine and the branches.

“The branch bears fruit because it remains in the vine.

“The fruit appears on the branch.

“Yet the branch isn't the source of the life that produces it.”

Adrián smiled.

“We've returned to the vineyard.”

Matías laughed.

“It seems we have.”

Elías continued.

“Consider what that means for witnessing.

“The branch doesn't produce fruit by attempting to imitate another branch.

“It doesn't study the appearance of fruit and then manufacture something that resembles it.

“It bears fruit through the life of the vine.

“And the fruit is evidence of that life.”

Marcos considered the illustration.

“Then our witness is not merely what we say about Christ.

“It also includes His life being expressed through us.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And that expression may appear in ordinary circumstances.

“Love.

“Patience.

“Kindness.

“Gentleness.

“Faith.

“Those are not insignificant things.

“They are the fruit of the Spirit.”

Matías looked toward Elías.

“Perhaps that's what your wife noticed.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“Perhaps.”

“You weren't trying to become a better listener through a program.

“You've been learning to rest your confidence in Christ rather than in your ability to be right.

“And she noticed a difference.”

Elías considered the thought.

“I don't want to claim more than I know.

“Yet I do think something has changed.

“For years, I was very concerned about being correct.

“I believed that sound doctrine was essential, and I still believe that.

“Yet I often treated my knowledge as though it gave me a kind of spiritual strength.

“When someone questioned my understanding, I felt compelled to defend it.

“And I wasn't always very interested in listening.”

He smiled.

“My wife has apparently noticed that.”

Adrián laughed.

“After twenty-seven years, I imagine she's qualified to comment.”

“She certainly is.”

Marcos had been thinking about the relationship between witnessing and church activity.

“I wonder whether we've confused being witnesses of Christ with maintaining a busy church calendar.

“We organize evangelistic events.

“We schedule outreach programs.

“We establish ministry teams.

“And we measure participation.

“Again, those things may provide useful opportunities.

“Yet none of them can produce the life of Christ.

“And if we begin treating participation as evidence of spirituality, we're back to the same problem we've been examining.”

Matías nodded.

“A church can become very active without necessarily expressing Christ's life.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“And a believer can be deeply involved in ministry while depending primarily upon his own abilities.”

Elías looked thoughtful.

“That's why Paul speaks so carefully about the source of his own ministry.

“He labored.

“He taught.

“He traveled.

“He suffered.

“Yet he attributed what was accomplished to God's grace.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“Then the problem isn't activity.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's treating activity as the source of spiritual life, or a busy calendar as proof that Christ's life is being expressed.”

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen are quiet today.”

“We're discussing what it means to be a witness.”

Anna considered him.

“A witness to what?”

“To Christ.”

She smiled.

“That's a serious subject.”

“It is.”

She looked around the table.

“Well, I hope you're learning something.”

Marcos laughed.

“We are.”

“Good.”

She moved on to another table.

Adrián watched her go.

“You know, she never seems particularly impressed with our discoveries.”

Matías smiled.

“Perhaps that's healthy for us.”

Elías turned to 2 Corinthians.

“There's another passage that belongs here.”

He found the fourth chapter.

“Paul speaks about the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Jesus Christ.

“Then he says, ‘But we have this treasure in clay vessels, that the exceeding greatness of the power may be of God, and not from ourselves.’”

He looked at his friends.

“That's a remarkable description of Christian ministry.

“The vessel is ordinary.

“The treasure is extraordinary.

“And the purpose is that the power should be recognized as God's.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Then the believer doesn't need to become impressive.”

“No,” Elías said.

“The treasure is not the vessel's accomplishment.”

Matías looked down at the words.

“That challenges so much of what we've believed about being useful to God.

“We've tried to become stronger, more capable, more disciplined, and more impressive.

“Yet Paul directs attention away from the vessel and toward the treasure.”

Adrián smiled.

“That's going to disappoint a few of us.”

Matías laughed.

“Perhaps all of us.”

Marcos had been thinking about his own business.

“There's something about this that I find encouraging.

“I've always assumed that being useful required becoming increasingly capable.

“And in ordinary responsibilities, developing ability can be important.

“Yet when it comes to expressing Christ's life, my ability cannot become the source.

“That means the Christian life isn't reserved for people with exceptional gifts, impressive personalities, or extraordinary knowledge.”

Elías nodded.

“Exactly.

“Every believer has been placed in Christ.

“Every believer possesses life in Him.

“And the Spirit of God dwells in every believer.”

Matías looked thoughtful.

“Then witnessing His life isn't a privilege reserved for the most accomplished Christians.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It's the expression of the life God has given His people.”

Adrián leaned back.

“There's something almost amusing about the way we've approached this.

“We've spent years trying to become impressive Christians.

“And now we're discovering that the whole point is for Christ to be seen.”

Marcos smiled.

“That's one way to put it.”

Adrián continued.

“We wanted to be known as disciplined.

“Knowledgeable.

“Successful.

“Spiritually gifted.

“And perhaps we even wanted people to admire how much we'd accomplished for God.

“Yet if Christ is our life, then the most important thing about us isn't what we've accomplished.

“It's the One who lives in us.”

Matías nodded.

“And if His life is expressed through us, He deserves the recognition.”

For a while the table was quiet.

Elías thought about his wife.

He remembered her saying that he seemed less concerned about having the answer to everything.

He hadn't realized how much his need to be correct had affected the way he listened.

He had believed that defending the truth required him to respond quickly and confidently.

Now he was beginning to understand that truth did not depend upon his ability to defend his reputation.

He could listen.

He could reconsider.

He could admit uncertainty.

And none of those things threatened his position in Christ.

Perhaps that was part of what his wife had noticed.

Not a man who had finally mastered the Christian life.

A man who was beginning to lose confidence in himself and find his confidence in Christ.

Matías opened to Galatians 2.

“I think we should return to this verse one more time.”

Adrián smiled.

“Only one more?”

Matías laughed.

“Perhaps not.”

He read the familiar words.

“I have been crucified with Christ, and it is no longer I that live, but Christ lives in me. That life which I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me, and gave himself up for me.”

He looked at his friends.

“That's where everything we've been discussing comes together.

“God placed us in Christ.

“Our old man was crucified with Him.

“Christ Himself is our life.

“And the life we now live, we live by faith in Him.”

Elías nodded.

“Then being witnesses of His life isn't another performance we produce through our own strength.

“It's what happens as His life is expressed through those who belong to Him.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“And the fruit belongs to the Spirit.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“Not to us as independent producers of spiritual life.”

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Another opportunity to demonstrate your generosity?”

Adrián laughed.

“I'm beginning to think my generosity is being taken for granted.”

Matías looked at him.

“Perhaps you're becoming less concerned about recognition.”

Adrián considered that.

“Let's not get ahead of ourselves.”

They laughed.

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

“Whatever you're becoming, I hope you keep coming back.”

They stood beside their cars for a few minutes.

Elías looked toward his friends.

“There's something I think we should return to next week.”

Matías looked at him.

“What?”

“Daniel.”

The others grew quiet.

Elías continued.

“And the young believers who were told that the gospel they were teaching was dangerous.”

Marcos nodded slowly.

“We've come a long way since we first discussed them.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And I think we understand something now that we didn't understand then.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Why the message of grace could be unwelcome in a church.”

Elías nodded.

“Exactly.”

As Elías drove home, he thought about the conversation.

For years, he had believed that being a faithful Christian meant becoming increasingly knowledgeable, disciplined, and useful.

He had taught Scripture.

He had served in the church.

He had defended doctrines he believed were important.

And many of those activities had been genuinely valuable.

Yet he had often treated his accomplishments as though they were evidence of his own spiritual strength.

Now he was beginning to understand something different.

God had placed him in Christ.

Christ Himself was his life.

And the Spirit was the One who produced spiritual fruit.

His responsibility was not to manufacture the life of Christ.

It was to know what God had done, count upon it, and walk by faith in the One who lived in him.

He thought again of his wife's words.

*You seem less concerned about having the answer to everything.*

Perhaps she had noticed something he hadn't been trying to produce.

Not a new accomplishment.

Not another improvement to add to his list.

Something of Christ's life becoming visible in an ordinary conversation.

Elías smiled faintly.

For years, he had wanted to be known as a man who understood the Scriptures.

Now he was beginning to appreciate something far greater.

That through his life, others might come to know the One the Scriptures revealed.

# Chapter 49
## The Gospel They Found Dangerous

Daniel had not expected to leave his church.

He had grown up believing that the church was a place where believers could open their Bibles, examine the Scriptures, and help one another understand what God had said.

He knew Christians disagreed about certain passages. He had witnessed those disagreements and understood that some questions required patience and careful study.

He also respected the elders.

They were older men who had served the congregation for years. They had taught him, encouraged him, and helped him through difficult periods of his life.

He had no desire to challenge their authority.

He wanted to understand what the gospel meant for his fellowship with God.

And that was where the trouble began.

Elías had arranged to meet Daniel for coffee.

They chose a small café on the other side of Windsor, somewhere neither of them visited regularly.

Daniel arrived first.

He stood when Elías approached.

“Good to see you.”

“You too.”

They shook hands and sat down.

For a while they talked about ordinary things.

Daniel had been busy with work. His family was doing well. He had continued meeting with a small group of believers who enjoyed studying Scripture together.

Eventually Elías asked about the church.

“Have you thought about returning?”

Daniel looked down at his coffee.

“I've thought about it.”

“And?”

“I don't think anything has changed.”

Elías considered that.

“Perhaps not.”

Daniel looked at him.

“I didn't leave because I wanted to.”

“I know.”

“I loved those people. Some of them had known me since I was young.”

He paused.

“I just couldn't agree to stop asking questions.”

Elías nodded slowly.

“I've been thinking about that.”

Daniel looked surprised.

“Really?”

“Yes.”

Elías took a moment before continuing.

“When you first raised your questions about First John, I believed I understood the passage.

“I'd taught it for years.

“And I was concerned that your interpretation might lead people to misunderstand the seriousness of sin.”

Daniel nodded.

“I remember.”

“I still believe we need to examine Scripture carefully. I haven't abandoned that conviction.

“Yet I've begun to question the way we handled your concerns.”

Daniel remained quiet.

Elías continued.

“We should have been willing to examine the passage with you without making agreement with our established explanation the measure of your submission.

“Instead, the discussion became increasingly concerned with whether you would accept what the elders had already decided.”

Daniel looked at him for a long moment.

“That's what I kept trying to explain.”

“I know.”

“I wasn't trying to convince people that sin didn't matter.

“I was asking whether the passage meant that every time a believer sinned, he lost fellowship with God until he confessed it.

“And if that was what John meant, I wanted to understand how it fit with the forgiveness and life we have in Christ.”

Elías nodded.

“Those are legitimate questions.”

Daniel looked down.

“I wish that had made a difference at the time.”

Elías felt the weight of the words.

He remembered the elders' meeting.

He remembered the concern about protecting sound doctrine.

He remembered the discussion about Daniel's influence upon other believers.

And he remembered how little time they had spent examining the actual question once the issue became one of submission.

“I owe you an apology,” Elías said.

Daniel looked up.

“For what?”

“For the part I played in that.

“I believed I was protecting the church.

“Yet I allowed the discussion to become more about defending our established teaching than examining what Scripture said.

“I should have handled that differently.”

Daniel was quiet for a moment.

Then he smiled faintly.

“Thank you.”

Elías nodded.

“I don't expect that to resolve everything.”

“I know.”

“Yet I wanted you to hear it from me.”

Daniel looked toward the window.

“I appreciate that more than you know.”

They spent the next hour discussing Scripture.

There were passages they understood differently.

There were questions neither could answer immediately.

And there were moments when Daniel challenged something Elías had said, prompting him to open his Bible and examine the context again.

Elías found himself enjoying the conversation with Daniel without feeling responsible for producing an immediate conclusion.

He was not there to defend his reputation.

He was not there to demonstrate his knowledge.

He was there to examine the truth with another believer.

And he wondered why that had once seemed so threatening.

The following Thursday, Elías arrived at Deb's Place carrying a small notebook.

Adrián was already at the table.

“What's that?”

“A notebook.”

“I can see that. Should we be concerned?”

“Probably not.”

Marcos arrived and smiled.

“Has he been preparing questions?”

Adrián shook his head.

“That's what worries me.”

Matías joined them a few minutes later.

They ordered breakfast and talked about the week.

Eventually Elías told them about his meeting with Daniel.

The others listened without interrupting.

When he finished, Matías looked thoughtful.

“How did Daniel respond?”

“He was gracious.”

“Did you resolve the question about First John?”

“Not completely. We examined several passages, and I think we both left with things to consider.”

Adrián smiled.

“That sounds like a successful Bible study.”

Elías laughed.

“Perhaps it was.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“And how did you feel afterward?”

Elías considered the question.

“Relieved.”

“Why?”

“Because I wasn't trying to win.”

Matías had been thinking about Daniel.

“There's something about his experience that troubles me.

“He wasn't encouraging people to sin.

“He wasn't denying Christ.

“He was asking questions about forgiveness and fellowship.

“Yet the discussion became serious enough that he eventually left the church.”

Elías nodded.

“Yes.”

Matías continued.

“And I wonder how many believers have experienced something similar.

“They begin understanding grace more clearly.

“They discover what Scripture says about their position in Christ.

“They begin questioning teachings that make their relationship with God depend upon repeated acts of religious performance.

“And suddenly they're regarded as dangerous.”

Adrián leaned back.

“I've seen that happen.”

Marcos looked toward him.

“Where?”

“In more than one church.

“Someone begins emphasizing that believers are forgiven in Christ.

“Someone teaches that the old man was crucified with Him.

“Someone says the Christian life isn't produced through discipline or law.

“And before long, people begin asking whether he's teaching too much grace.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“As though grace were something we needed to ration.”

Adrián nodded.

“Exactly.”

Elías opened his Bible to Galatians.

“Paul faced something similar.

“He had preached the gospel to the Galatians.

“They had received the Spirit by hearing of faith.

“Then others came and began teaching that religious observance was necessary for their standing before God.”

He looked around the table.

“Paul didn't treat that as a minor disagreement about church practices.

“He regarded it as a serious departure from the gospel.”

Marcos considered the passage.

“Because it shifted their confidence away from Christ.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“Toward what they could accomplish through religious observance.”

Matías looked at the letter.

“And Paul asked, ‘Are you so foolish? Having begun in the Spirit, are you now completed in the flesh?’”

Elías nodded.

“That's why Galatia has been so important to our investigation.

“The problem wasn't that the believers had become careless about religion.

“They were being drawn toward greater religious seriousness.

“Yet the source of their confidence was changing.”

Anna arrived with breakfast.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Excellent.”

She smiled.

“Another successful morning?”

“So far.”

“Good. I'd hate to disappoint you.”

Marcos laughed.

Anna returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Then Marcos spoke.

“I think we've discovered why the gospel can become uncomfortable in churches that profess to believe it.”

Matías looked toward him.

“Explain.”

Marcos considered his words.

“A church may sincerely believe that salvation is by grace.

“It may preach Christ crucified.

“It may teach that forgiveness comes through faith in Him.

“Yet the church may also have developed an entire approach to Christian living that depends upon human effort.

“Rules.

“Discipline.

“Accountability systems.

“Religious expectations.

“And the assumption that spiritual maturity is produced through obedience to those arrangements.”

He paused.

“Then someone begins teaching that Christ Himself is our life.

“That God placed believers in Him.

“That our old man was crucified with Christ.

“And that the fruit of the Spirit cannot be manufactured through human effort.”

Adrián looked at him.

“Suddenly the system is being questioned.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“Not necessarily because the person is attacking the church.

“Because the gospel exposes the inadequacy of the very things the church has come to trust.”

Matías had been listening carefully.

“I think that's what makes the situation so difficult.

“The leaders may genuinely believe they're protecting believers.

“They may fear that teaching grace will encourage carelessness.

“They may worry that people will stop attending meetings, serving, giving, or obeying Scripture.

“And those concerns can feel responsible.”

Elías nodded.

“I know they can.”

Matías continued.

“Yet if our answer to those fears is to make believers dependent upon religious pressure, we've returned to the flesh as the means of producing spirituality.

“We're attempting to protect the Christian life through something that cannot produce it.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“And the gospel becomes threatening because it removes the basis of that control.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“It tells believers that their life is not found in the system.

“It's found in Christ.”

Adrián leaned back.

“There's something almost tragic about that.

“A church may spend years teaching people how to become better Christians.

“It may develop excellent programs.

“It may establish strong expectations.

“It may have respected leaders and committed members.

“And then someone comes along and tells the people that Christ Himself is their life.

“You'd think that would be wonderful news.”

Elías smiled faintly.

“It is wonderful news.”

“Yet it can sound dangerous to people who have built their confidence somewhere else.”

Marcos nodded.

“Because the message doesn't simply offer another improvement to the system.

“It questions whether the system was ever capable of producing what it promised.”

Elías thought about Daniel.

He remembered the young man's expression when he'd said he didn't want to leave the church.

There had been no triumph in his voice.

No satisfaction in having challenged the elders.

Only sadness.

And a determination not to abandon what he believed Scripture was teaching him.

Elías looked toward his friends.

“I think we need to be careful about something.

“We shouldn't imagine that every disagreement about grace is evidence of carnality.

“Believers can misunderstand Scripture.

“Teachers can make mistakes.

“And there are genuine distortions of grace that deserve correction.

“The question is whether we're willing to examine those matters through Scripture, or whether we protect our established practices by refusing to consider the questions.”

Matías nodded.

“And whether our confidence rests in Christ or in our ability to maintain control.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“That's the issue.”

Marcos turned to Romans 1.

“There's a passage we've returned to several times.”

He found verse sixteen.

“For I am not ashamed of the Good News of Christ, because it is the power of God for salvation for everyone who believes; for the Jew first, and also for the Greek.”

He looked around the table.

“The message is that Christ died for our sins and rose again.

“We've discussed how churches sometimes treat the gospel as though it needs help to reach certain generations or cultures.

“We've also discussed how churches try to produce spirituality through discipline and religious systems.

“Now I think we're seeing the connection.”

Adrián looked toward him.

“What connection?”

“In both cases, the church begins trusting something other than the gospel.

“One church believes the message needs better methods to become effective.

“Another believes believers need religious pressure to become spiritual.

“And both may be sincere.

“Yet the underlying assumption is that what God has provided in Christ is insufficient.”

Elías considered the thought.

“And that's why Paul refused to move away from Christ crucified.

“He understood where God's power was found.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“I've been thinking about the years I spent trying to become spiritual.

“I believed Christ had saved me.

“I believed my sins were forgiven.

“Yet I thought the rest of the Christian life depended largely upon my ability to become disciplined and obedient.

“And I encouraged others to think the same way.”

He paused.

“Now I understand why the message of grace might have made me uncomfortable.

“If someone had told me years ago that Christ Himself was my life and that I couldn't produce spirituality through my own effort, I might have agreed with the words.

“Yet I don't think I would have understood them.

“And if the person had challenged the disciplines I trusted, I might have regarded him as careless or dangerous.”

Adrián looked toward him.

“Because he was threatening something important to you.”

“Yes,” Matías said.

“My confidence in what I could accomplish.”

For a while the conversation grew quiet.

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen seem serious today.”

“We're discussing why people sometimes reject good news.”

Anna considered that.

“Perhaps they don't believe it's good.”

Marcos smiled.

“That's possible.”

She looked toward Adrián.

“Or perhaps they think there's a catch.”

Adrián laughed softly.

“That's possible too.”

Anna moved on.

Elías returned to Galatians.

“There's something Paul says near the beginning of the letter that I think belongs here.”

He found the passage.

“Paul expresses astonishment that the Galatians are turning away from the One who called them in the grace of Christ toward a different gospel.

“He isn't merely concerned that they've adopted an incorrect religious practice.

“He sees a departure from the grace in which they were called.”

Marcos looked at the passage.

“Then the issue is deeper than the particular rule.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“It's a question of where their confidence rests.

“Christ.

“Or religious achievement.”

Matías nodded.

“And that's why the gospel can become unwelcome in a church.

“Because it directs confidence away from the things the church has learned to depend upon.”

Adrián had been thinking about the young believers who had encountered resistance in churches.

“There's something I find difficult to understand.

“These young people weren't asking churches to abandon Scripture.

“They were asking people to take seriously what Scripture says about grace.

“Yet some were warned to stop speaking about it.

“Others were told their teaching was dangerous.

“And some eventually found themselves unwelcome.”

Elías nodded.

“I've seen that happen.”

Adrián continued.

“Imagine that.

“The gospel is the message the church claims to proclaim.

“Yet when someone begins emphasizing its implications for the Christian life, the church becomes uncomfortable.”

Marcos considered the thought.

“Perhaps because the implications reach further than the church expected.”

Matías looked toward him.

“How?”

“The gospel doesn't merely tell us how to be forgiven.

“It tells us what God has accomplished in Christ.

“It tells us that our old man was crucified with Him.

“That God placed us in His Son.

“That Christ Himself is our life.

“And that the Spirit produces what the flesh never could.”

He paused.

“Those truths challenge every attempt to make human effort the source of spirituality.”

Elías looked at the others.

“I think we're beginning to understand the question that started this entire investigation.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“Why the gospel can be unwelcome in the very churches that proclaim it.”

“Yes.”

Elías continued.

“Not necessarily because the leaders are dishonest.

“Not necessarily because they consciously reject Christ.

“Sometimes they genuinely believe they're protecting the church.

“Yet they may have developed an approach to Christian living that depends upon something other than Christ.

“And when the gospel exposes that dependence, they may resist the message rather than reconsider the system.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“Because the flesh doesn't surrender its importance easily.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It doesn't.”

The conversation continued for some time.

They discussed churches they had known, people who had helped them, and leaders whose sincerity they had never questioned.

None of the men wanted their investigation to become an excuse for despising other believers.

They had all benefited from the patience, generosity, and teaching of Christians who had served them over the years.

They also knew that they themselves had trusted the flesh in ways they had only recently begun to recognize.

Their discoveries had not placed them above the churches they were examining.

They had exposed how much they shared with them.

Eventually Matías spoke.

“I think that's what we need to remember when we finish this investigation.

“We began by examining four churches.

“And we discovered things that troubled us.

“Yet the most uncomfortable discoveries weren't about those churches.

“They were about ourselves.”

Marcos nodded.

“We recognized our own confidence in the things we admired.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“Spiritual experiences.”

“Discipline,” Matías said.

“Success,” Marcos added.

Elías looked down.

“Knowledge.”

They sat quietly.

Then Adrián spoke.

“And all the while, Christ was our life.”

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“Another opportunity to demonstrate generosity?”

Adrián laughed.

“I think we've established that I have a problem with recognition.”

Matías smiled.

“Perhaps we should develop a program.”

“Please don't.”

They laughed.

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

“Whatever you're discussing, I hope it's helping.”

Adrián smiled.

“It is.”

She looked at the four men.

“Then perhaps all these mornings have been worthwhile.”

Outside, the afternoon was cool.

They stood beside their cars for a few minutes.

Elías looked toward his friends.

“I think there's one more conversation we need to have.”

Matías nodded.

“About the churches?”

“About what we've learned.”

Marcos considered that.

“And perhaps about what we still need to understand.”

Adrián smiled.

“That could take another year.”

Elías laughed.

“Perhaps.”

They said goodbye.

As Elías drove home, he thought about Daniel.

He remembered the young man's questions.

His desire to understand Scripture.

His reluctance to leave the church.

And the relief he'd expressed when Elías finally acknowledged that those questions deserved examination.

Elías had spent years believing that defending the truth required him to protect the answers he already possessed.

Now he was beginning to understand that confidence in Christ did not require confidence in his own infallibility.

He could be corrected.

He could reconsider.

He could admit that he had misunderstood something.

And none of those things threatened his position in Christ.

He thought about the gospel that had seemed dangerous to the elders.

The message that believers were forgiven in Christ.

That God had placed them in His Son.

That their old man was crucified with Him.

That Christ Himself was their life.

And that spiritual fruit came from the Spirit rather than human effort.

Those truths did not threaten the Christian life.

They revealed its source.

Elías smiled faintly.

The gospel had never been the danger.

The danger was the confidence they had placed in something else.

And now he understood why the young believers had found themselves unwelcome.

They had brought a message that left no room for the flesh to remain important.

A message that directed every believer's confidence toward Christ.

A message that could not be improved by human effort.

The gospel of grace.

The power of God.

And the life of Christ Himself.

# Chapter 50
## What We Learned About the Carnal Church

The four men had agreed to meet at Deb's Place one more time.

Not because they intended to stop seeing one another. Their friendship had endured far longer than their investigation, and none of them imagined that Thursday mornings would disappear from their lives.

They simply felt that the conversation they had begun months earlier had reached a natural conclusion.

They had started by comparing churches.

Now they were examining something much closer to home.

Adrián arrived first.

Anna was arranging clean cups behind the counter.

“Good morning.”

She looked up.

“Morning, Adrián.”

“Something important is happening today.”

She stopped what she was doing.

“Should I be worried?”

“Possibly.”

“What have you done?”

“Nothing. That's the remarkable part.”

Anna laughed.

“Then perhaps there's hope for you.”

Adrián smiled.

“We're finishing our investigation.”

“Which investigation?”

“The one we've been conducting at that table for months.”

She glanced toward their usual seats.

“Oh, that.”

Adrián looked surprised.

“You knew?”

“Adrián, you've been discussing churches, the flesh, grace, and the cross loud enough for half the diner to hear.”

He considered that.

“Then you've been following our work.”

“I've been serving your coffee.”

“A valuable contribution.”

She handed him a cup.

“Go sit down.”

He laughed and made his way to the table.

Marcos arrived a few minutes later, followed by Matías and Elías.

They greeted one another, ordered breakfast, and settled into the familiar comfort of four men who had known one another long enough to recognize when something mattered.

For a while they talked about their families.

Matías had spoken with his father the previous evening. Marcos was planning a short trip with his wife. Adrián had been invited to another family gathering, where he hoped to recover some of the dignity he'd lost during the earlier argument about arepas.

Elías listened, smiling.

He had begun appreciating these ordinary conversations more than he once had.

They weren't interruptions to something important.

They were part of the life he shared with his friends.

Eventually Marcos opened his Bible.

“Well, gentlemen. Where do we begin?”

Adrián smiled.

“At the beginning, I suppose.”

Marcos looked around the table.

“Do you remember how confident we were when we started?”

Matías laughed.

“Unfortunately.”

“We had opinions about everything,” Marcos continued. “What made a church healthy. What made a church successful. What kinds of problems were most serious.

“And we each recognized something of our own congregation in the churches we studied.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Corinth was rather exciting.”

“Galatia was serious,” Matías said.

“Laodicea was successful,” Marcos added.

Elías smiled faintly.

“And Ephesus was doctrinally careful.”

They were quiet for a moment.

Marcos continued.

“We thought we were comparing four very different churches.

“And they were different.

“Corinth had jealousy, divisions, and serious problems with conduct.

“Galatia was being drawn toward religious observance as the means of spiritual completion.

“Laodicea was confident in its own sufficiency, even while Christ described its true condition very differently.

“And Ephesus had genuine commendations from Christ, yet had fallen from its first love.”

He looked toward Elías.

“What surprised me was how easily we recognized the problems in the churches we didn't admire.”

Elías nodded.

“And how reluctant we were to recognize anything troubling in the ones we did.”

Matías smiled.

“Especially when their strengths resembled our own.”

Adrián had been thinking about Corinth.

“I remember being impressed with how much was happening there.

“The gifts.

“The activity.

“The personalities.

“It sounded like a church where something exciting was always taking place.”

He paused.

“Then we began examining what Paul actually said.

“He didn't deny that God had gifted them.

“He didn't treat their spiritual gifts as imaginary.

“Yet he called them fleshly because jealousy and strife were among them.

“And suddenly I had to reconsider what I thought spiritual life looked like.”

Marcos nodded.

“Because visible spiritual activity doesn't necessarily establish that people are living by the Spirit.”

“Exactly,” Adrián said.

“And that's difficult to accept when you've spent years associating excitement with spirituality.”

Matías looked toward him.

“You weren't alone.”

Matías opened his Bible to Galatians.

“I found Galatia much more uncomfortable.

“At first, I admired their seriousness.

“I've always believed that Christians should be disciplined and committed. I still value those things.

“Yet Paul confronted the Galatians because they were being drawn toward something the flesh could accomplish.

“They had begun by the Spirit.

“And now they were being persuaded that religious observance could complete what God had begun.”

He looked at his friends.

“I recognized myself in that.

“I believed Christ had saved me by grace.

“Then I spent years trying to become spiritual through discipline.

“I established routines.

“I worked hard.

“I measured progress.

“And when I failed, I assumed I needed greater determination.”

He smiled faintly.

“I was trying to produce the Christian life.

“And I didn't understand that the Christian life was Christ Himself.”

Elías looked at him.

“That's been one of the most important discoveries we've made.”

Matías nodded.

“Yes.

“Because it changed the question.

“I was no longer asking how to become sufficiently disciplined to live the Christian life.

“I was beginning to ask what God had already accomplished in Christ.”

Anna brought their breakfasts.

Adrián looked at his plate.

“Wonderful.”

She smiled.

“One thing hasn't changed.”

“What's that?”

“You still haven't tasted it.”

Adrián laughed.

“Some truths are established by long experience.”

“Then I hope your experience continues to be pleasant.”

She returned to the counter.

They ate for a while.

Marcos eventually spoke again.

“Laodicea troubled me for a different reason.

“I understood their confidence.

“I've spent years building a business. I know what it means to organize people, establish goals, and produce results.

“And I've often assumed that success was evidence that something was being done correctly.”

He paused.

“Yet Christ's assessment of Laodicea was entirely different from its assessment of itself.

“They believed they were rich and needed nothing.

“He said they were poor, blind, and naked.

“That forced me to consider something I'd rarely questioned.

“A church can be successful according to its own measurements and still misunderstand its spiritual condition.”

Adrián looked toward him.

“And that applies to more than money.”

“Certainly,” Marcos said.

“A church may have excellent programs, a growing congregation, and impressive ministries.

“Those things may accomplish genuine good.

“Yet none of them proves that Christ is the source of its life.”

Elías had been listening quietly.

When Marcos finished, he spoke.

“Ephesus was the most difficult for me.

“I admired its discernment.

“Christ commended the church for its deeds, perseverance, and refusal to tolerate evil. He acknowledged its testing of false apostles.

“Those commendations were genuine.

“Yet He also said that the church had left its first love and had fallen.

“I used to think that doctrinal accuracy and perseverance were sufficient evidence of spiritual health.

“Ephesus forced me to reconsider that assumption.”

He looked down at his Bible.

“I still believe sound doctrine matters.

“I still believe believers should test what they hear.

“Yet knowledge is not life.

“And even when we defend something true, we cannot assume that our conduct is necessarily proceeding from dependence upon Christ.”

He paused.

“I discovered that in a very personal way.”

The others knew he was thinking about Daniel.

For a while no one spoke.

Then Adrián smiled faintly.

“You know what's remarkable?”

Marcos looked toward him.

“What?”

“What we admired in our own churches made us feel comfortable with ourselves.”

Matías laughed softly.

“Yes.”

“And then Scripture began making us uncomfortable.”

Elías smiled.

“That's one way to describe it.”

Adrián continued.

“We thought we were examining the churches.

“Then we discovered that the same confidence we were questioning in them was present in us.”

Marcos turned to 1 Corinthians 3.

“I think this is where our investigation truly began.

“Paul called the Corinthians fleshly because jealousy and strife were among them.

“We wanted to understand what made a church carnal.

“And we discovered that carnality was not limited to the things we usually associate with serious moral failure.”

Matías nodded.

“The flesh can be religious.”

“Very religious,” Elías said.

Marcos continued.

“It can be disciplined.

“It can be knowledgeable.

“It can be successful.

“It can become skilled at organizing people and maintaining order.

“And it can accomplish things that appear admirable.”

Adrián looked thoughtful.

“Yet none of those things makes it spiritual.”

“No,” Marcos said.

“Because the issue is the source.”

Matías leaned forward.

“That's what changed everything for me.

“For years, I thought the difference between a spiritual Christian and a carnal Christian was primarily what each one did.

“One behaved well.

“The other behaved poorly.

“One was disciplined.

“The other was careless.

“One was committed.

“The other wasn't.

“And I believed that if a person improved his behavior sufficiently, he would become spiritual.”

He looked around the table.

“Now I understand why that approach could never produce what I wanted.

“The flesh cannot produce the fruit of the Spirit.

“It can imitate certain behaviors.

“It can become disciplined.

“It can restrain itself.

“It can even take pride in overcoming its previous failures.

“Yet the fruit belongs to the Spirit because the Spirit produces it.”

Elías nodded.

“And that distinction is essential.

“We must not confuse beneficial conduct with spiritual life.

“A person may do something genuinely useful through human ability.

“Yet Christ Himself is the believer's life.

“And the Spirit is the source of spiritual fruit.”

Adrián smiled.

“Which brings us to sin management.”

Marcos laughed softly.

“We spent quite some time there.”

“For good reason,” Adrián said.

“Once we assume the flesh must become spiritual through improved behavior, we need systems to make that happen.

“Rules.

“Programs.

“Accountability arrangements.

“Discipline.

“And eventually we begin treating compliance as evidence of spirituality.”

Matías nodded.

“Some practical arrangements are necessary. We shouldn't forget that.

“Churches have responsibilities.

“People need protection.

“Serious misconduct must be addressed.

“Yet none of those measures can produce the life of Christ.”

Marcos considered the distinction.

“And that's where carnality management becomes deceptive.

“It may produce order.

“It may produce conformity.

“It may even produce impressive results.

“Yet the flesh remains incapable of producing spiritual life.”

Elías opened to Romans 6.

“Then we came to the cross.”

The table grew quieter.

He looked at the familiar passage.

“Our old man was crucified with Christ.

“Paul doesn't present that as something believers must accomplish through sufficient effort.

“He declares what happened in our identification with Christ.”

Matías nodded.

“And Galatians tells us that those who belong to Christ have crucified the flesh with its passions and desires.”

Elías continued.

“The cross confronted carnality.

“It did not offer the flesh another opportunity to improve itself.

“It was God's decisive action concerning sin.

“And through our death with Christ, sin's dominion over us has been broken.”

Marcos looked thoughtful.

“Then the cross disarmed the claim that sin remained our rightful master.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And Colossians describes the cancellation of the record against us and Christ's triumph over rulers and authorities.

“We must keep those statements distinct.

“Yet together they show us the extent of God's accomplished work.”

Adrián looked down at the passage.

“And the cross still protects us from carnality.”

Elías nodded.

“Because its accomplished work remains the basis of our freedom.

“Believers do not need to repeat Christ's victory.

“They need to know what God has done, count upon it, and walk by the Spirit.”

For a while they sat quietly.

Marcos turned to Romans 1.

“There's another thing we discovered.

“The gospel is the power of God for salvation to everyone who believes.

“Yet churches can begin acting as though the gospel needs human ingenuity to become effective.”

Adrián smiled faintly.

“The gospel that needs help.”

“Yes,” Marcos said.

“We discussed churches that assume certain generations cannot receive serious biblical teaching.

“They shorten the message, replace teaching with activities, and organize events designed to attract people.

“Some of those methods may be useful for communication or fellowship.

“Yet when they begin replacing the gospel, the church can become very successful at attracting people while giving them very little understanding of Christ.”

Matías nodded.

“And the church may eventually conclude that people aren't interested in serious discipleship because it has never given them the opportunity to discover its value.”

Elías considered the thought.

“Paul knew the gospel offended some audiences and appeared foolish to others.

“Yet he continued preaching Christ crucified.

“He understood that the power belonged to God.”

Adrián leaned back.

“Then we found another way for the flesh to remain important.

“By convincing itself that the gospel needed its assistance.”

Marcos smiled.

“That's one way to put it.”

“And perhaps that's why we keep developing systems,” Adrián continued.

“We want something we can accomplish.

“Something we can measure.

“Something we can take pride in.

“Even when we say we're doing it for God.”

Anna returned with coffee.

“Another refill?”

Adrián held out his cup.

“Please.”

She poured.

“You gentlemen are unusually quiet.”

“We're reviewing our investigation.”

“Did you find what you were looking for?”

Adrián considered the question.

“Not exactly.”

Anna raised an eyebrow.

“Then what did you find?”

Matías smiled.

“Something we weren't looking for.”

She looked at him.

“Was it good?”

He nodded.

“Very.”

Anna smiled.

“Then perhaps the investigation was worthwhile.”

She moved on to another table.

Elías had been thinking about the question Anna asked.

“What were we looking for?”

He looked around the table.

“We wanted to understand carnality.

“We wanted to identify what made churches fleshly.

“And we wanted to know how the problem could be corrected.”

Marcos nodded.

“Then we discovered that the answer wasn't a better method of correcting the flesh.”

“No,” Elías said.

“It was what God had accomplished in Christ.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“And grace.”

Elías smiled.

“Yes.

“Grace had made the difference.

“Not because grace gave us a better system for becoming spiritual.

“Because God, in His grace, placed us in Christ.

“And Christ Himself is our life.”

Adrián looked toward Matías.

“I think that's the part I want to remember.”

“What?”

“That life is not something we produce.

“For years, I've associated the Christian life with what Christians do.

“Prayer.

“Bible study.

“Service.

“Evangelism.

“Good works.

“And those things can be genuine expressions of Christ's life.

“Yet none of them is the source of that life.”

Matías nodded.

“Christ is our life.”

“Yes,” Adrián said.

“And that's something very different from treating Him as help for a life we're trying to produce ourselves.”

Marcos opened to Colossians 3.

“Paul says that Christ is our life.

“And in the second chapter, he tells believers that as they received Christ Jesus the Lord, they are to walk in Him.”

He looked at his friends.

“We've spent years treating walking in Christ as though it were another way of describing successful Christian behavior.

“Yet walking in Him is living by faith in the One who is already our life.”

Elías nodded.

“And that's why the distinction between position and experience matters.

“God placed us in Christ.

“That is His accomplishment.

“We don't become increasingly in Christ through improved behavior.

“Yet we can grow in our understanding and enjoyment of the life we already possess.

“We can learn to count upon what God has said.

“And we can walk by the Spirit.”

Matías smiled faintly.

“Not to create life.”

“No,” Elías said.

“Because Christ is our life.”

The conversation continued for a while.

They spoke about their families, their churches, and the people who had helped them through the years.

None of them wanted to leave the impression that everything they'd experienced in church had been wrong.

They remembered faithful believers who had loved them.

Teachers who had patiently explained Scripture.

Friends who had encouraged them during difficult seasons.

People who had served without seeking recognition.

There had been much to be grateful for.

And they understood that the presence of carnality did not mean God had never worked among those congregations.

The Corinthians themselves had received genuine gifts from God.

Ephesus had received genuine commendations from Christ.

The question was not whether anything good had ever happened.

It was whether human effort could become the source of spiritual life.

And Scripture had answered that question.

Eventually Elías returned to Daniel.

“I think my conversation with him may have been one of the most important parts of this investigation.”

Matías looked toward him.

“Because of the question about confession?”

“Partly.

“Yet more importantly because of what happened when he began questioning an established teaching.

“He wanted to understand Scripture.

“We wanted to protect what we believed was sound doctrine.

“And somewhere along the way, our concern about protecting the teaching became entangled with our concern about maintaining authority.”

Marcos considered that.

“Then the gospel became uncomfortable because it threatened the arrangement.”

“Yes,” Elías said.

“And that brings us back to the young believers who were told they spoke too much about grace.”

Adrián nodded.

“Some were warned.

“Some were rejected.

“And some eventually left churches they had loved.”

Matías looked down at his Bible.

“They weren't necessarily trying to change the church.

“They were trying to explain what God had done in Christ.”

Elías nodded.

“And the message exposed how much confidence some churches had placed in human effort.”

For a while the table was quiet.

Then Marcos spoke.

“I think we should remember something.

“We haven't discovered a way to classify ourselves as spiritual while identifying everyone else as carnal.”

Adrián smiled.

“That would have been convenient.”

Marcos laughed.

“Very.

“Yet our investigation has exposed our own confidence in the flesh.

“Each of us recognized himself in one of the churches.

“And each of us has had to reconsider what he believed about the Christian life.”

Matías nodded.

“We didn't become better than those churches by discovering their problems.”

“No,” Elías said.

“We discovered how easily the same problem could appear in us.”

Anna approached with the bill.

Adrián reached for it.

Marcos smiled.

“One last demonstration of generosity?”

Adrián looked surprised.

“Last?”

“For the investigation.”

Adrián laughed.

“Gentlemen, I hope you don't imagine I'm retiring from breakfast.”

Matías smiled.

“Of course not.”

Anna placed the bill in front of him.

“Good. I'd hate to lose my most talkative customer.”

Adrián looked pleased.

“Finally, some recognition.”

She smiled.

“Don't let it go to your head.”

They gathered their belongings and made their way toward the door.

For a few minutes they stood beside their cars.

No one seemed particularly eager to leave.

Marcos looked toward the diner.

“It's strange to think how much we've discussed at that table.”

Matías nodded.

“And how differently we understand some things now.”

Adrián smiled.

“I think we've solved at least a few problems.”

Elías looked at him.

“Have we?”

Adrián considered the question.

“Perhaps we've discovered that we aren't the ones who solve them.”

They laughed.

Then the conversation became quiet again.

Matías looked toward his friends.

“There's something I want to say before we go.”

They waited.

“For years, I believed the answer to my failures was to try harder.

“Be more disciplined.

“Become more committed.

“Take the Christian life more seriously.

“And I did try.

“I tried for a very long time.”

He paused.

“Yet the harder I worked, the more I discovered how incapable I was of producing the life I wanted.

“I could improve certain habits.

“I could become more consistent.

“I could accomplish things.

“Yet I could never produce Christ.”

The others listened.

Matías continued.

“And now I understand something I wish I'd understood years ago.

“God never asked the flesh to produce His Son's life.

“He dealt with our old man through the cross.

“He placed us in Christ.

“And He gave us life in Him.”

He looked down at the Bible in his hand.

“I spent years trying to become what I believed a Christian ought to be.

“And all that time, the life I needed was already mine in Christ.”

No one spoke immediately.

Then Elías smiled.

“That's a good place to finish.”

Marcos nodded.

“Yes.”

Adrián looked toward the diner.

“Although I suspect we'll find something else to discuss next Thursday.”

Matías laughed.

“Almost certainly.”

They shook hands, embraced, and made their way toward their cars.

# Author’s Closing Reflection

I have often thought about the question that began this journey.

Why would the gospel of grace become unwelcome in churches that claim to proclaim it?

At first, I assumed the answer would be found in particular teachings, leadership failures, or disagreements about Christian conduct.

Those things certainly matter.

Yet the deeper issue is the confidence we place in the flesh.

We can believe that Christ died for our sins while continuing to trust ourselves to produce the Christian life.

We can preach salvation by grace while teaching believers to become spiritual through discipline.

We can defend sound doctrine while relying upon our knowledge.

We can organize successful ministries while treating human ability as the source of spiritual fruitfulness.

And we can become so attached to those things that the gospel itself begins to sound dangerous.

Because the gospel directs our confidence somewhere else.

To Christ.

I know something about the frustration of trying to become the Christian I believed I ought to be.

I tried.

For years, I believed the answer was greater effort.

More discipline.

More commitment.

More seriousness.

I wanted to do what was right.

And I failed.

Not once or twice.

Again and again.

There were times when my failures hurt me.

And times when they hurt people I loved.

I thought the answer was to become stronger.

To try harder.

To find a better way to overcome what was wrong with me.

Yet the flesh could never produce the life of Christ.

And God, in His grace, had already provided what I could never accomplish.

The cross was not another method for improving myself.

It was God's decisive action concerning the old man and sin's dominion.

Christ had died.

And I had died with Him.

God had placed me in Christ.

And Christ Himself was my life.

Grace met me where I was.

It did not wait for me to become what I could never make myself.

It directed me to what God had already accomplished in His Son.

That changed everything.

Not because I finally discovered how to live a successful Christian life.

Because I began to understand that the Christian life was never something I could produce.

It was Christ.

And He was already my life.

The gospel had not been powerless.

It had not needed improvement.

It had not required another system to make it effective.

The power belonged to God.

The work had been accomplished in Christ.

And the life was His.

God has already dealt with the flesh through the cross. Yet a church can continue trusting that very flesh to produce spiritual life, spiritual growth, and spiritual fruit.

**That confidence in the flesh is what makes a church carnal.**
